To the memory of my father, Herb Finn. Whether through genetics or osmosis, his gift of humor shaped me as a writer and a personÑ and has made the hard times easier and the good times even better.

FOREWORD 
Ed Asner 
W
hen we started The Mary Tyler Moore Show, little did we imagine that almost fifty years later, people would still be enjoying our efforts. Today, classic TV comedy is huge. And who better to discuss it than the writers of its most popular shows. Their own stories are even funnier and more entertaining than their most brilliantly written scripts. 
IÕve played my share of dramatic roles, and I can appreciate how essential comedy is to even the most tragic stories and characters. Everything has comedy in it; in comedy lies truth. A shared laugh is powerful beyond words. It connects actor to audience, viewers to each other. 
I have the utmost admiration for TV comedy writers. The job takes guts, grit, gumption. One might even say it takes spunk. This book provides an inside look at the lives and work of these brave souls. They discuss backstories of their shows, their creative processes, and coping with personal demons and fears. 
This isnÕt a how-to book per se, but aspiring and established writers canÕt help but benefit from the gems of wisdom and advice it imparts. And, thereÕs no shortage of laugh-out-loud anecdotes. 
ItÕs a veritable fun fest for comedy buffs and anyone fascinated with the cre.ative mind or with the business of writing funny. As a researcher by profession and the daughter of Honeymooners writer Herb Finn, Paula Finn has done an exceptional job of assembling a comprehensive picture of what really goes on behind the scenes of our most beloved classic comedy shows. 


FOREWORD 
Carol Kane 
I 
was so lucky to land in the world of Taxi when I was twenty-eight years old. It was 1980, and at that time, actors who came from theater and film largely did not do television series, and if they did, it was frowned upon. So I had nothing but disdain for everything to do with television. But I had seen an episode of Taxi that Jack Gilford was in, and it was brilliant, so I thought, if he could do one, then I could do one. I was asked to be a guest star playing Simka, a moun.tain woman who came from the same country as Andy KaufmanÕs character Latka. Maybe one of the reasons they asked me was because of my role in the 1975 movie Hester Street, in which I spoke Yiddish, perhaps making me some.what eligible to learn LatkaÕs language. 
I knew nothing about how television worked. I thought the producers of the show meant the sponsors of the show, and I therefore assumed that the produc.ers of Taxi worked for a cereal company, like Nabisco! I certainly did not under.stand that the producers were actually the creators and head writers of the show. 
Taxi was only the second comedy IÕd ever done. The first time I did it, IÕd had no interest in pleasing anybody or being funny for anyone. I just wanted to be pure in terms of my work as an actorÑto play my part honestly, to do the same kind of work I always tried to do on any other movie or theater production. But when I came back to Taxi the second time, I wanted desperately to fulfill the writersÕ expectations, and I tried so hard to be funny. And trying to be funny was the complete opposite of what IÕd done the first time. 
As part of the rehearsals on Taxi, we had a tradition called Òcast to the rail.Ó We would do a scene in rehearsal, and then the stage manager would say, ÒCast to the railÓÑand the cast would run up to where the writers were sitting in the bleachers in front of the stage, our heads craning upward. The audience sat in those bleachers the night we shot, but in the run-throughs, only the writers were there, in the first row. After every scene, we would go to the rail, and the writers would give us notes. And when I first heard about this, I thought it was just horrific; itÕs so condescending. I couldnÕt understand it, and I was offended by it. But as soon as I came to understand how the creative process of the show worked between the writers and the actors (and in the case of Taxi, we also had the genius of our director, Jimmy Burrows)Ñwhen theyÕd announce, ÒCast to the railÓÑIÕd be the first one to run up to that rail! Because when I did, I would have incredibly rich notes given to me by Jim Brooks or Stan Daniels or Ed. Weinberger or Ken Estin or Sam Simon or Jerry Belson. And so for me, Òcast to the railÓ turned from being this horrible, offensive notion to Òoh thank goodness; if I get there first, maybe IÕll get the most notes!Ó The notes were so nourishing and helpful. 
My most significant and intense Òcast to the railÓ experience was on my second episode. After the run-through, I went up to the railÑand Jim Brooks basically took me apart! He said, ÒThis is not it. You are not to try to be funny. If we have written it funny, it will be funny. And if we have not, we donÕt want you to try and make something out of it thatÕs not there. ItÕs not your job to try to be funny. If itÕs not funny, we will fix it.Ó And then, indeed, they would go into the writersÕ room until all hours of the night, order Mexican food from LucyÕs El Adobe CafÑand fix it. 
And that was such a huge revelation for me: I just had to play my part honestly with commitment, and the results would be what they were. If IÕm working with good writers and they wrote it funny, itÕll be funny. If I go out there and say the line in a pure and truthful way and itÕs not funnyÑthat means the writers have to fix it. Or, as in some of the richest episodes of TaxiÑnot all moments are supposed to be funny because the writers are also trying to tell a three-dimensional story! Some episodes of Taxi ended sadly, like in real life. You cannot imagine the gift it was to learn not to force and push too hard because, when you are doing comedy, you get addicted to the laughÑand unfortunately, will do anything to get one. 
That lesson from Jim has stayed with me all my life. I have to revisit it a lot. Once I understood what he was saying, everything changed. And I was fortunate enough to become a regular on the show. 
I feel that IÕve gotten to work with some of the best television writers. I re.member when they wrote the Taxi episode where I got to say, ÒPeel me like a grape so I can get out of here!Ó to JuddÕs character. I was driving onto the Para.mount lot for shoot day, and I distinctly remember thinking, ÒOh my God, they donÕt understand that I would pay them any amount of money in the world to be lucky enough to get to say that line in that situation!Ó 
So, it is the clich Òif it ainÕt on the page, it ainÕt on the stage.Ó I would say the writers are the tree of life in terms of inspiration. The actor, of course, has to give the work their truth and love and skillÑbut the beginning, the roots for me, are always the writing. You can fulfill the writing or notÑbut if you donÕt have the basic inspiration of good writing to stand on, then thereÕs nothing. For me, I know thatÕs true. And I think any good actor will tell you that everything rests on the writing. Sure, we can twist it, we can turn itÑbut if the good writingÕs not there as a foundation, the story wonÕt work. 
I am so happy that Paula has written this book, and IÕm honored to con.tribute. Her father was there from the very beginning of television comedy. And the shows he wroteÑThe Honeymooners, The Gale Storm ShowÑoh my God! I would do anything I could, whatever hypochondriacal thing I could concoct, just to stay home from school and watch that show. Throughout this book, starting with the interview with the great Carl Reiner, I notice that the best comedy writers have in common the notion that their best work comes from drawing on real-life stories founded on real-life emotions. The element of comedy can be as broad as your imagination will allow, and as long as itÕs based on some kernel of truth on the page, it will be OK. As I said, I was so lucky to work with the quality of writers on Taxi. And now IÕm so lucky to work with the great Tina Fey and Robert Carlock. Perhaps I shouldnÕt be tell.ing you this so my luck doesnÕt run out. 
We are so fortunate to have this book because, in these interviews, so many generous writers have been forthcoming with Paula about their top-secret pro.cesses. We get to enter the ever-mysterious writersÕ room and take a seat. 
I, for one, am so thrilled to have this precious information. To me, our writersÑwho, down through the ages and now and in the future, tell us the stories that make us laugh out loud, sometimes until we cryÑare heroes. LetÕs celebrate them! 


PREFACE 
A 
phone call from Bob Hope isnÕt the norm in most homes. But to me as the daughter of a TV comedy writer, it was life as usual. Early on, I realized how many people held my dadÕs job in high regard. Once on a cross-country trip, we got stopped for speeding. The officer asked my father what he did for a living. When he learned that Dad wrote his kidsÕ favorite TV showÑThe Flint.stonesÑhe let him off with a warning. 
One of the best things about having a TV writer for a dad was that, whatever my favorite show, I was invited to a closed set to watch the magic of its creation: Mister Ed, That Girl, The Beverly Hillbillies, The Patty Duke Show. This was the sixties, before multicamera setups allowed ÒliveÓ studio audiences to watch essentially play-like performances of shows. It could take several hours to film a short segment of a show. While it must have been tedium for the actors, to me, every minute was exciting. When I walked back outside, I always felt like I was leaving behind a secret, elite world. 
My dad could find humor in anything and would often jot down ideas and jokes on any available scrap of paper. IÕve been asked if life at our home was non.stop laughs. Not exactlyÑbut his sense of humor definitely made it more fun. I loved his amusing observations whenever we watched TV; heÕd comment hu.morously on the bad acting or weak dialogue or say of an actor wearing a toupee, ÒHe has more hair now than he did ten years ago!Ó Mom and I could always tell when he was kidding us about something by the unmistakable glint in his eye. 
I was proud of his ability to make anyone, anywhere, laugh. As a friend once put it, ÒHe made your heart smile.Ó Once when my father was in Las Vegas, comedian Gabe Kaplan asked him for the time. Dad answered, ÒWith all the money you make, you canÕt afford a watch?Ó 
He wrote his first shows on a 1940s Royal typewriter. He later upgraded to a 1960s Olympia. That, a wheel eraser, and paper were all he needed to produce hundreds of scripts. A reformed chain-smoker, he worked with an unflavored toothpick in his mouth. And he always talked to himself as he wrote: When working on a play set in Ireland, he walked around the house muttering in an Irish brogue. 
Dad was born in Boston, raised in Brookline, and he never lost his Bostonian accent. In high school, he was the fastest runner in the county (ÒFlying FinnÓ) and looked forward to competing in the Olympics. But a track injury curtailed that dream. 
Instead, he joined the Provincetown Players summer theater as a comedic actor. While a member of the troupe, he lost most of his hair, and the remainder went down the drain after a fellow cast member persuaded him to shampoo with coal tar and the remedy failed. It was the beginning of a lifetime of self-deprecating bald jokes. 
One day, Dad heard the DuffyÕs Tavern radio show. Knowing he could do better, he scribbled down a few jokes and mailed them to the showÕs producer, Ed Gardner. Two days later, he received a telegram: ÒSend a script.Ó It was 1946; he was hired on the show for seventy-five dollars a week. A month later, his sal.ary was raised to one hundred dollars a week. 
From there, he went on to write for such old-time radio shows as Sweeney and March, Amos Õn Andy, and Our Miss Brooks. Like many fellow radio writers, he transitioned into TV and worked on the likes of December Bride, The Beulah Show, The Colgate Comedy Hour, and Bachelor Father. 
In 1955, he began his first season on The Honeymooners. In an article for On Cable Magazine, he describes meeting Jackie Gleason for the first time: 
When I showed up for work as the newest writer on The Honeymooners, it was 1955. Eisenhower was President, and gold was $35Ñan ounce. Jack Philbin, GleasonÕs producer and close friend, brought me into an office for a story meeting at the Park Sheraton Hotel, along with writers Walter Stone and the late Marvin Marx. And there was Gleason, seated at his desk, enjoying a manicure and the company of a pretty, young manicurist. At age 39, he was good-looking despite his 300 pounds, and beautifully dressed.1 
Gleason shook DadÕs hand, and everyone was shocked. This was the first time heÕd ever shaken hands with a writer. Apparently, he even avoided talking di.rectly with the writers. 
Dad continues: 
My first clue to GleasonÕs talent and the remarkable simplicity of The Honeymoon.ers came that very first day as Marvin Marx, serving as spokesman for the writers, pitched our story. Gleason hardly appeared to be listening, seemingly more inter.ested in what the manicurist was doing. If he liked what he heard, heÕd nod his head almost imperceptibly; when something displeased him, he would interrupt quietly and point out the problem. That was it. He knew in an instant what was right for the show, and what wasnÕt. 
It was live TV in its purest form: Òno laugh track or canned applause, no tele.prompters or cue cards; there was practically no rehearsal, and no redoing of mistakes.Ó And it didnÕt always run smoothly. One time an actor who played RalphÕs boss, Mr. Harper, became ill just before he was due on stage. Jackie and Art Carney improvised masterfully, saying things like, ÒIf Mr. Harper were here now, you know what heÕd say?Ó The audience never knew the difference. 
Gleason was adamant about not having a phone in the KramdensÕ apartment, fearing that the writers would use it as a crutch. When a call was needed, we had to write it so the Nortons upstairs would lower their phone to the KramdensÕ window below. During one show, a call came in for Ralph, and Norton dutifully lowered the phoneÑbut not quite far enough. Gleason almost fell out the window while struggling to grab it as it dangled inches out of reach. Finally, frustrated but unfazed, he turned to the audience and bellowed, ÒNow I know why they say ÔDonÕt call me . . . IÕll call you!ÕÓ 
In The Official Honeymooners Treasury, Dad recalls how Gleason would communicate with the writers: 
WeÕd get a call: ÒJackie wants to see you.Ó And weÕd say, ÒUh oh, what now?Ó WeÕd go down to his office and heÕd be sitting at his desk, looking out the window, and Philbin would be sitting on the couch, with a very serious face. Gleason would say, ÒAll here?Ó And heÕd look at Philbin and say, ÒWe didnÕt have to have that set. The doctor could have come to the apartment.Ó And he would bawl Philbin out for something we had done. HeÕd never look at us; always at Philbin. And Philbin would take it and take it. 
Walter Stone had a funny line. Walter said Gleason had a dream and a voice 
said, ÒJackie! Jackie!Ó Gleason says, ÒWhoÕs that?Ó And the voice says, ÒCharles 
Dickens.Ó And Gleason says, ÒYou know I never talk to writers!Ó2 
In his first year with the show, Dad was teamed with writer A. J. Russell. The following year, he worked with two different writers: 
Stone, Marx and I delivered scripts for the show every other week, alternating with another team. Walter Stone was a master joke smith with big, clever lines pour.ing from him constantly. Marvin Marx was the plot man, working out the stories. Marvin lived in New Jersey and would think up plots on the train to and from Man.hattan. But most of his best work was done . . . on the toilet. When a story lacked freshness or inventiveness, Walter would say, ÒMarvin, get back on the toilet!Ó 
Dad reflects on the character of Ralph Kramden: ÒThe whole idea of writing a Kramden script was, what can he do that he can get in trouble with.Ó3 He explains, 
Kramden was stupid. Actually, ÒstupidÓ is too harsh a word. He was gullible. Norton would say a stupid thing, and Kramden would recognize it as stupid. But if Norton said, ÒI know a guy who wants to show glow-in-the-dark shoelaces,Ó Ralph would say, ÒHey, thatÕs good. ThereÕs money in that!Ó . . . Alice would always have to bring him back to earth.4 
Apparently Jackie Gleason did everything in a big way. Once after a show, he invited his six writers to go bowling with himÑand they took over the entire bowling alley. In a Museum of Broadcasting tribute to Gleason, my dad noted, 
I donÕt know how much Jackie gave the pin boys as tips . . . but the next day, two of them bought bowling alleys of their own! Jackie was a good bossÑtrusting, considerate, generous. He trusted his writers enough to let them do their work with little or no interference from him. . . . ItÕs been said that no man can serve two masters. Well, maybe so. But when youÕre serving someone the size of Jackie Gleason, you come mighty close! 
Dad moved on to write for such stars as Garry Moore, John Forsythe, and Bing Crosby. In 1962, he started writing The Flintstones, in which the Stone Age characters of Fred and Wilma were actually based on Ralph and Alice Kramden. IÕd notice that sometimes FredÕs personality was a little like my dadÕs, and WilmaÕs words reflected some of my momÕs mannerisms. I guess that made me Pebbles! 
In New York, heÕd worked on staff; in LA, he had a home office, a studio detached from our house. From one wall hung a miniature wooden crutch, a gift from The Honeymooners writers. During his first season on the show, Dad had broken his ankle when he stepped in a hole playing ball with me. Other wall dcor included a glamour 8.10 of my mom from when she was secretary to actor Burgess Meredith. SheÕd worked for many old-time radio stars, includ.ing Robert Q. Lewis; she met my father when he was a writer on The Robert Q. Lewis Radio Show. 
Three of the shows Dad wrote were GilliganÕs Island, ItÕs about Time, and The Brady Bunch, all produced by Sherwood Schwartz. In contrast to todayÕs staff-written sitcoms, Sherwood believed it was better to hire outside writersÑto recruit fresh minds, so to speak. He thought it was healthier for the business because it gave more writers work, and it also varied the kind of shows he could do. And Sherwood looked this up for me: of all his freelancers, my father and his long-time writing partner, Alan Dinehart, wrote the most GilliganÕs Island scripts. 
Alan had a background in variety and quiz shows; theyÕd met in New York and first worked together on The Flintstones. As more of a storyteller, Alan cre.ated the story line for the scripts; then Dad put in all the dialogue and the humor. One Christmas, Alan gave my dad an unabridged dictionary with the inscription ÒHerb, at last I found something to give you that you donÕt have to rewrite!Ó 
Alan would arrive at our home in his white Cadillac every day at 9 a.m. They worked in my dadÕs home office; Alan sat on the couch while my dad typed. They broke for lunch at Studio CityÕs famous Du-parÕs. At five oÕclock, they went for drinks at the popular Tail oÕ the Cock Restaurant, where the bar was always full of writers. 
In the precomputer days, I moved four hundred miles north to the Bay Area. When I began my own writing career, Dad offered to send me my type.writerÑand a wastebasket. When I made my first magazine sale of a technical article on earthquake preparation, he wrote me. ÒA fine, professional-type job, Paula, and IÕm proud of you. I know now what to do before, during, and after an earthquake. As soon as your mother finishes her sewing, IÕm sending her down under the house to look for anchor bolts on the sill. And sheÕd better not come back without finding them!Ó 
And after seeing the newspaper write-ups on my first book sale, he wrote, 
ItÕs really a pleasure to read about you in the papers. I hope youÕre not letting this fame go to your head. I never did when I was basking in the limelight. Although I knew I was undoubtedly the best comedy writer in the business, I was never too proud to admit it. And when I was lauded and extolled, I was always modest enough to accept it as my due. ThatÕs what endeared me to all the other writersÑ and is why I am where I am today. And I wish I were where I was yesterday. So remember, fame is fleeting, and I hope you make a fortune out of it! 
Alan Dinehart Jr. describes my father as not being outwardly funny: 
He didnÕt walk around trying to be funny. But he would look at something, or something would happen, and heÕd come out with a commentÑand it would be hysterical. And then he wouldnÕt say anything for 10 more minutes. He was more quiet and reserved than my dad. But he had that dry sense of humor; when he said something, you listened to it Õcause it was funny. 
Sherwood Schwartz described my father as a 
funny writer who talked funny, too. And thatÕs not always the case. There are some writers who donÕt talk very much in meetingsÑeither funny or not funnyÑbut then theyÕll go away and write a very good script. There are other writers who talk very funny at a meeting, and the script they write isnÕt as good as what theyÕve said. Your dad had a kind of a serious expression; the word ÒdourÓ comes to mind. But the words that came out of his mouth were funny! 
M*A*S*H creator Larry Gelbart, who worked with my dad on DuffyÕs Tav.ern, told me, ÒI actively remember him being entertaining, witty. Comedy writ.ers tend to be fast and funny, but in a broader sense. IÕm not sure what it wasÑ your dad always had a kind of humor . . . it was witty, for sureÑbut there was a bit of a sting to it. ThatÕs something extra. And thatÕs not given to everybody.Ó 
In 1985, DadÕs play The Almoster received a Mobil Playwriting Award. The London Times published this clip: 
They awarded the anonymously-submitted piece . . . to Herbert Finn, a 73-year.old Californian who had never set foot in Ireland and has no Irish connections. Nor has he written a play before, though he once wrote scripts for Bob Hope and worked alongside a young Neil Simon, whom he advised: ÒGet out of writing and into something thatÕll make you a decent living!Ó 
In my dadÕs final days, he was very ill with pneumonia. I asked him, ÒWhere else in the world would you rather be, than here at home with the people who love you?Ó 
I could barely hear his whispered response: ÒIn a pool hall.Ó

warm thank-you to every writer who contributed so generously to this project! For their encouragement and support, I wish to thank Milt Brew.ster, Laura Casserly, Lisa Freedman, Eddy Friedfeld, Joyce Kane, Billy Riback, Sally Shumaker, Vincent Terrace, and Rob Turner. 

INTRODUCTION 
I
Õve chosen to focus on television of the twentieth century because classic TV is currently so popular, and thatÕs where my father and his colleagues shone. My decision to write this book was fueled by a long-standing admiration for and curiosity about this particular breed of creative thinker. I wanted to learn more about the lives and thought processes not only of the people whoÕd worked with my dad but also writers of my favorite shows from the eighties and nineties. 
Forty-one writers generously shared their stories with me, offering a window into their minds and lives. Their accounts are hilarious, poignant, inspiring. I was especially gratified by those who recalled my dad fondly. One-hundred.year-old Bewitched creator Sol Saks surprised me by recounting in detail their cowriting a My Favorite Husband pilot in 1953. (It sold immediately.) 
Dad was one of six staff writers on The Honeymooners. I was struck by how many writers cited that show as an influence on their own writing. Everybody Loves Raymond creator Phil Rosenthal says, ÒItÕs why IÕm here! I owe your father a lot. I have to tell you, this was one of the formative things of my life . . . not just my career. This was huge to me.Ó Soap writer Stu Silver refers to the Honeymooners writers as Òthese guys who basically invented sitcom.Ó 
I was most impressed with the intervieweesÕ candor. Recounts of their self-doubt, regrets, and Òlearning experiencesÓ made clear that the comedy-writing business isnÕt all laughs. Sometimes itÕs vomiting, weepingÑeven Òbleeding from your assÓ (one writerÕs graphic description of how she felt in a poorly led writersÕ room). But in the end, they all say itÕs worth it. 
This book features fifteen Q&As, some of which include relevant ÒBehind the ScenesÓ material from additional writers. A final section, ÒMore Tales from 

Writers for The Honeymooners: Herb Finn, Walter Stone, and Marvin Marx. Photo courtesy of Paula Finn 
the WritersÕ Room,Ó reflects a variety of viewpoints in a discussion format; topics include pitching, celebrity feuding, and a comedy writerÕs unique take on the world. 
The earliest sitcom writing staffs were small. Then they grew, and grewÑand now theyÕre getting smaller again. I happened to catch a Dennis the Menace rerun a few days ago. On the credits, one writerÕs name was given: my dadÕs. In contrast, todayÕs scripts are largely a group effortÑso collaborative, in fact, that typically no one really knows who wrote what. 
The first shows were done on kinescope, essentially taking a picture of a picture, which yielded very poor quality. In the sixties, film was used, followed by tape. Preferred camera setups have gone from single-cam on closed sets for filmed shows to multicam with a live audience and tape and back again to single. Television has evolved from a place where writers were, in Sol SaksÕs words, Òbelow the lineÓÑto where writers themselves are running the shows. 
The language and subject matter have obviously seen changes: In its fourth season, The Flintstones ran an episode called ÒDaddyÕs Little BeautyÓ [S4E12], in which Fred enters Pebbles in a beauty contest. My dad wrote it, inspired by my winning first place in a neighborhood beauty and talent contest. Coinciden.tally, in its fourth season, The Simpsons ran ÒLisa the Beauty QueenÓ [S4E4, written by Jeff Martin], in which Homer enters Lisa in a beauty contest. 
The similarities stop there: The Flintstones episode references musical spoons, hula hoops, and the lost art of conversation due to the popularity of TV. Flintstones commercials featured its characters smoking to promote the showÕs sponsor, Winston cigarettes. The Simpsons story references binging and purging, taping your swimsuit to your butt, the mayorÕs illegitimate sonÑand it satirizes the tobacco industry with a pageant sponsor recruiting new young smokers to replace older smokers whoÕve died. 
Whether the writers create their magic on a 1940s Royal typewriter or an iPad, for kinescope or for film, what hasnÕt changed is that no one can explain the process. Although they agree that certain principles of the craft can be taught, when it comes to pinpointing exactly how they come up with the funny stuff, the most brilliant of them can only say they Òjust do it.Ó 
And arenÕt we glad they do! 
1 

CARL REINER 
C
arl Reiner is one of the worldÕs most beloved comedy legends. In a career spanning more than six decades, he has lent his talents to television writ.ing, producing, and directing and has also written and directed films. Originally planning to be a stage actor, he has performed in Broadway musicals, film, and numerous television shows. HeÕs perhaps best known for creating the clas.sic TV comedy The Dick Van Dyke Show and for playing straight man to Mel BrooksÕs 2,000-Year-Old Man character. He has authored several books and is the recipient of such awards as the Mark Twain Prize for American Humor, nine Emmys, three Outstanding Achievement Awards, a Grammy, and election to the Emmy Award Hall of Fame. 
When we spoke, Carl Reiner was recovering from knee surgery. HeÕd just finished a whirlwind few months of travel and speaking appearances and had recently acted on the TV shows Hot in Cleveland and The Cleveland Show. HeÕd finished writing a novel the year before and was starting work on an.other. At one point in the conversation he apologized to me for hesitating on a response; he said he was playing computer solitaire, and needless to say, he Òloves to multitask.Ó 
IÕll start by asking how your writing career began. 
On Your Show of Shows, I was originally an actor, but IÕd sit in the writersÕ room once in a while just to see if I could still type because IÕd been a teletype operator in the army. So when there was an extra typewriter, I started typing up little things for myself. And I think thatÕs the beginning of my getting ideas because I wrote my first novel not too long after that. 

Carl Reiner. Photo courtesy of Carl Reiner 
I also started contributing ideas for sketches. And the producer, Max Liebman, said, ÒHe stays with usÓÑmeaning that I could stay in the writersÕ room, and from then on, I was a writer. Albeit, I was a Òwriter without portfolioÓÑmeaning I had no creditÑbut I did come up with stuff that allowed me to learn my craft. And it was an opportunity to learn from some of the best writers that ever wrote. We were all of the same age, but some were writers and some were performers. 
Do you have any favorite stories from either of the Caesar rooms? 
There are a thousand of them! [Laughs] But the one that comes to mind is about Neil Simon, who was part of a team with his brother then [on Your Show of Shows]. Neil had the voice of a turtle; he spoke very quietly, and he always had wonderful ideas. And I was sitting on the couch with him, and often heÕd throw in an idea, and nobody would hear it. Because I was the noisy one, I would stop things, and IÕd say, ÒJust a moment, hold it, hold it.Ó NeilÕs name was Doc Simon in those days. IÕd say, ÒDocÕs got it.Ó And I would repeat what Doc just said, and it would get into the script. And so it became a running gag that I would stop people from continuing on if Doc had said something nobody heard. 
Nice of you not to take credit for his jokes! 
[Laughs] Oh no, I would neverÑit never occurred to me! And I remember one time, before IÕd written my first book, I pitched a joke in the room, and it wasnÕt accepted. And Mel Tolkin says, ÒShut up; youÕre nothing but a fuckinÕ actor.Ó This was his line. And then about a year later, I wrote Enter Laughing, and I sent him a copy of the book. He wrote me back a note that said, ÒYouÕre a fuckinÕ good writer!Ó Mel Tolkin was very funny; I loved him. 
So after you left Sid Caesar, you obviously continued both acting and writing. 
I was on The Dinah Shore Show as a writer, and I was also on every other week as a guest actor. So one week weÕd have a special guest star like Yves MontandÑand a Ònot-so-special guest star,Ó Carl Reiner. Being billed as the Ònot-so-special guest starÓ was always such a funny idea to me. 
I wrote that show with one partner, a guy named Charlie Isaacs. We worked together in a room, and then weÕd bring our ideas in to Dinah. And we didnÕt write sketches so much; weÕd only do one sketch a week for whomever the guest star was. For the most part, we were writing lead-ins, or cute ideas, for how to get into her songs. 
As everyone knows, you created The Dick Van Dyke Show. Can you tell me how that came about? 
The year CaesarÕs Hour went off, the sponsors werenÕt interested in situation comedies because that was the time of horses and guns: the westerns. And so, sponsors werenÕt interested in situation comedies. There were a few new sit.coms, and I was offered jobs on themÑbut none of those shows was very good. 
And my wife actually said to me, ÒCarl, why donÕt you write one?Ó And I said, ÒI donÕt write situation comedies. IÕve never written one.Ó And IÕve said this a number of timesÑI remember the moment I found my way. I was on my way home from being downtown; I was on the East River Drive, getting off at 96th Street, and I talked to myself. Incidentally, I tell people to talk to yourself; you might get an answer! I said, ÒWhat piece of ground do you stand on that nobody else stands on?Ó And the answer was, ÒWell, I live in New Rochelle, I have a wife and two kids, I work on a variety show in New York, I commute every day,ÓÑand so that was the genesis of The Dick Van Dyke Show. ItÕs about a writer who lives in New Rochelle with one kidÑI had two kids at the time. And he writes on a show, although I was actually a writer/actor on that show. And because it was a good idea, I wrote up a pilot very quickly, and I showed it to my agent. He thought it was wonderful, and he got it financed immediately. Peter Lawford put the money up, and I financed the pilot for myself. Barbara Britton and I were the actors in it. And a couple of people were interested, but it didnÕt sell. 
I put it aside and started writing movies. I wrote a couple of films, which got done for Universal: The Thrill of It All and The Art of Love. But I had gone ahead and written thirteen episodes for The Dick Van Dyke Show when I thought I would do it myself because I thought I should have a bible for some of the other writers in case I had to act on the show. I knew I couldnÕt write all thirty-nine, but I had thirteen ready. 
And my agent loved those shows. He said, ÒWe canÕt let these die,Ó and he brought me in to see Sheldon Leonard. At the time, Sheldon was working with Danny Thomas; they had the production company T & L Productions and a show called The Danny Thomas Show. And he said, ÒWe gotta do this show.Ó I told him I didnÕt want to fail with the same material. He said, ÒYou wonÕt fail. WeÕll get a better actor to play you!Ó That was the way he put it. And he sug.gested Dick Van Dyke. 
And thatÕs how the show originated, and the first thirteen episodes that aired were the ones that I had written. And actually, the first two years I worked alone; I had no story editor and no producer. I was also rewriting episodes that I had farmed out to freelancers, and IÕd find myself rewriting more than I was writ.ingÑso in those first two years, I think I wrote forty of the first bunch of shows. 
And at that point, I think I wouldÕve died had I not been able to bring in some wonderful writers. We were fortunate enough to finally find writers who under.stood what the show was about because it had been on the air. Other shows had been man versus woman, but this was about a couple who was two against the world rather than husband and wife against each other. And it was Bill Persky and Sam Denoff and Garry Marshall and Jerry Belson who saved my ass by com.ing in and helping me those third and fourth years. 
The Dick Van Dyke Show was a totally different experience from Your Show of Shows. I describe it as being the most exciting job I ever had; it was the thing that actually formed me into what I ultimately became. 
Can you elaborate on how previous shows had been a battle of the sexes and this show was different? 
Lucy is a prime example. She was always trying to fool Desi; she was always plotting against him because she wanted to get on the show or be an actressÑor who knows what. Every week it was some double-dealing; she and Ethel would get together, and they were always plotting against something with their hus.bands in some way. 
A battle of the sexes/husband against wife was an easier way to go, and it had been a sitcom standard for years. Even Burns and Allen, you know, were dumb wife and smart husband. And I decided to write about my wife and IÑand she was much smarter than me! To this day, I attribute everything I have ever done to the fact that she honed me. I married her when I was twenty-one years old and she was twenty-eight, and she knew more than anything about everything. We only lasted sixty-five years. 
Which is quite an accomplishment. So that show is based on your own marriage? 
Yes. We had our arguments, but rather than being two against each other, we were two against the world. So Rob and Laura would argue, but the audience always knew their marriage was strong. 
Did The Dick Van Dyke Show use a three-camera technique? 
Yes. That was actually invented by Desi Arnaz. Jerry Lewis started using it when he was directing his own movies so he could see what he was doing. But at the same time, Desilu came up with the same idea. 
And what were its advantages? 
The fact that you could do it in front of a live audience and you didnÕt have to stop. With one-camera shows youÕd stop and go with interruptions all the time, whereas on a three-camera show, you could actually do each scene like a play. And you could have the audience watching, and you could capture their actual laughter. So the audience laughter you hear on The Dick Van Dyke Show is that of real audiences in the studio when we were doing it. Up until then, theyÕd been using canned laughter on one-camera shows. And that bothered me because I never thought it sounded like real laughter. 
Do you remember what joke you wrote that got the biggest laugh? 
One of the biggest laughs ever was the baby show, where Rob was question.ing whether their baby was really theirs because it didnÕt look like them [ÒThatÕs My Boy??Ó S3E1]. The hospital had gotten LauraÕs name mixed up a couple of times with a Mrs. Peters, a new mother in another room. Laura had gotten the figs that had come for Mrs. Peters. So Rob said, ÒI bet we got Mrs. PetersÕs baby!Ó And at the very end, in walked Mr. Peters, played by Greg Morris, with his babyÑand you realized it was a black baby. That laugh lasted about ten min.utes. Persky and Denoff wrote that script, but I was very much involved with it. 

The Dick Van Dyke Show 
Bill Persky (Writer/Producer): HereÕs the perfect example of what itÕs like to be a comedy writer. Sam [Denoff] and I had an office on Sunset Boulevard. It was so small, there wasnÕt room for both of us in it, so I literally sat out.side in the hallway while Sam was in the office typing. And people would walk by and see this guy in the hallway laughing and talking, and theyÕd wonder who he was talking to! 
Tim Conway had recommended us to do a McHaleÕs Navy episode, which we did. And we went to Universal to meet with the showÕs producer, Si Rose. We were there for literally three hours, and he had notes on ev.ery page. We left for lunch, and I said to Sam, ÒYou know what? I donÕt think we can do this. Obviously, weÕre all wrong.Ó And then we go back, we spend another two and a half hours with this manÑhe was a lovely guyÑbut just everything was ÒWhy did you say and here instead of . . .Ó or ÒWhyÕd you say the or . . . ?Ó It was so awkward and uncomfortable. 
We went back to our office and were really so depressed. And while we were there figuring what the hell we were gonna do, Carl called [about our ÒThatÕs My Boy??Ó submission]. And Sam answered the phone, since obvi.ously I was in the hall and I didnÕt have a phone! And he said, ÒYeah? Yeah? Yeah?Ó He was smiling, and I said, ÒWhat?Ó He said, ÒCarl loves it!Ó And we went in the next day, and Carl said, ÒYou guys can do as many of these as you can handle. IÕll give you an office, and youÕll learn, and youÕll work here.Ó And thatÕs what we did. And it was amazingÑthe yin and the yang of that day. And itÕs funny: Just last week I got a check for thirty-seven cents from Guam for the McHaleÕs Navy that we did. I swear. And of course, I have to send my ex-wife seventeen cents. 
So much of The Van Dyke Show and so much of what IÕve doneÑand what Sam and I did and I continue to doÑhas some personal touch of my own life. So the genesis of the show ÒThatÕs My Boy??Ó was when my own daughter was born, and we got some flowers that were meant for someone else. And I said, ÒHey, how do we know we got the right baby!Ó 
Back then there was no DNA testing. If you thought you had the wrong baby, you could take a paternity test, but for a TV show, we needed some.thing faster to unequivocally say that you were wrong. And the more we thought about it, we realized the only way to do it was to have the child be of a different ethnicity. 
We thought of having him be Asian, which would have been okay because that wasnÕt a big political issue at the time. And then Carl said, ÒLetÕs make him African American.Ó We said, ÒThatÕs great.Ó But the net.work didnÕt think it was greatÑthey said, ÒAbsolutely not.Ó We went in for a meeting with them, and they told us that, in this climate, the world is not ready for a white couple to make a black couple the butt of a joke. And Carl said, ÒNo, you donÕt understand. ItÕs the black couple thatÕs mak.ing the white couple the butt of the joke.Ó And the guy went, ÒWell, weÕre certainly not ready for that!Ó 
We had an alternate ending in case it didnÕt work, and to be honest with you, I donÕt remember what the hell it was. It was a live show with an audience, and everything in it was working great. But if the ending didnÕt workÑI mean, where were we? I was standing next to Carl on the floor, watching the show behind the cameras, and when Dick went to the door and that moment happened and Greg Morris came inÑthere was a deadly silence long enough for Carl to say to me, ÒOh, shit.Ó And then, the laughter started and literally went on for twenty minutes. Every time weÕd go back to do a pickup and start again someplace, the audience would get hysterical. It was like the relief of a fever, you know? It really tapped into all of the tension, and it just made everything okay, and it made people laugh about how stupid it was to make an issue of it! And the show got a lot of attention, and overnight [Sam and I] became major stars. We were Òthe Boys.Ó 
Carl Reiner said those characters wouldnÕt do anything you wouldnÕt do. And so that was the core of it all. Everybody could identify with the people in it and find a piece of themselves. I mean, we were crazy maybeÑbut there was a recognizable human behavior in everything that happened. And then Dick would make it funnier. The stories were never outlandish, and the laughter came from situations and not from jokes. We never wrote jokes. We wrote what had to be written, and then that was it. 
In [ÒThatÕs My Boy??Ó], where they were getting ready for the baby to be born, there was that wonderful thing Dick did where he was able to get out of bed dressed. People do shit like that! Not quite that, but I know when my first child, Dana, was going to be born, I timed the trip to the hospital by four different routes at three different times of the day. And then when the actual moment cameÑI stopped a block from the hospital to fill up with gas. So thereÕs that absurd part of yourself. And The Dick Van Dyke Show didnÕt go past those moments. 
[Regarding collaborating with his partner,] Sam would sit at the typewriter and weÕd talkÑhow about this, oh and then thisÑand it just evolved. ItÕs essentially an ongoing conversation in which certain things are funny and certain things are relevant to where youÕre headed. 
When Sam passed away, I was interviewed by a lot of people for his obituary. And one guy asked me what was the difference between the way I wrote and the way Sam wrote. And I said, ÒWell, I donÕt know, but it just kind of worked out. Sam loved jokes, and I didnÕt really, so I guess through the combination of his looking for them and my trying to avoid them, somehow we arrived at a place where it was a third voice.Ó And later on, the guy asked what do I think Sam and I will be remembered most for. I said, ÒProbably That Girl because it was such an important show and changed an entire gen.eration. To this day, I am a hero to women in their fifties and sixtiesÑexcept for my first wife!Ó And he said, ÒYou wanted to say that.Ó I said, ÒNo, thatÕs a perfect example of what I was talking about. I didnÕt know I was going to say that. I was telling you a fact, and then in order to add something to it, I said it.Ó So thatÕs how I write. Sam might have said, ÒI wanna do something about my ex-wife,Ó and then back up and figure out what it is. 
Carl Reiner is really fearless. He could not suffer fools; he called a spade a spade. And a new guy had been made the head of comedy development and programming at CBS, and he was coming out to LA to read the riot act to everybody and declare himself the new fastest gun in town. And everybody was saying, ÒGod, he was here. HeÕs really a terror. HeÕs this; heÕs that.Ó And finally he came to the Van Dyke Show, and he walked in with his entourage. And he had his jacket over his shouldersÑyou know, with his arms not in the sleeves? Not a good thing, for Carl. [Laughs] And then he sat down, and he put his feet up on the coffee table, which also was not a good thing. He started talking, and Carl let him talk, and then the guy said, ÒAnd now IÕd like to tell you what I think is funny.Ó Well, that pushed it over the edge. Carl got up, walked across the room, took off the guyÕs Gucci loaferÑjust one of themÑand threw it out the window. He said, ÒNow thatÕs what I think is funny.Ó And that was the end of the meet.ing. Even the guy laughed. He realized he was a pompous fool. 
IÕll tell you a story which is a perfect example of Carl. Obviously, I just love him. We talk at least once a week, and I called him last night, and for the first time I misdialed his phone number. And you know, IÕm eighty-one years oldÑand when you do something like that, you get nervous. So when I talked to him, I said, ÒCarl, I canÕt believe it. I just dialed the wrong number for you.Ó He said, ÒIsnÕt that funny? IÕve just been sitting here trying to remember my social security number.Ó And I said, ÒWell, donÕt tell it to meÑbecause next time IÕll dial it!Ó 
What do you consider the funniest joke you ever wrote? 
LetÕs see, it might have been the walnut show [ÒIt May Look Like a Walnut,Ó S2E20]. I was trying to do a takeoff of Invasion of the Body Snatchers, and I did the episode in the style of a Twilight Zone episode. After watching a scary science-fiction show on TV, Dick Van Dyke dreams heÕs being invaded by some.body from the planet Twilo. He thinks everyoneÕs souls had been taken over by walnuts. And at the very end of his dream, he opens a closet door, and his wife comes sliding out on a chute, on top of ten thousand pounds of walnuts. The audience laughed forever on that one. 
Was your portrayal of the writersÕ room on that show 100 percent accurate, or were there some aspects of it that you didnÕt want to show? 
No, it was pretty accurate. The only thing that was different from the Your Show of Shows room was that we had about five or six writers, and I made The Dick Van Dyke Show with only two or three. This was because of the problems of finance; youÕd have to pay for the other writers, and in a half-hour, you wouldnÕt be able to service them anyway. 
How would you say your experience on the Caesar shows influenced your writing for Dick Van Dyke? 
I must say that I owe everything to having worked on the Sid Caesar shows: Your Show of Shows and CaesarÕs Hour. The only difference between the Caesar shows and The Dick Van Dyke Show is that my comedy wasnÕt sketch comedy. But the situation-comedy portions of the Sid Caesar shows were based on rec.ognizable things that happened in the households. Not the movie takeoffs but what we used to call the ÒCommuters.Ó So even though the CaesarÕs Hour show was technically sketch comedy, it was the closest thing coming to being real because it was based on real, observed behavior in peopleÕs homes. So I honed myself on that. If I hadnÕt done Your Show of Shows and CaesarÕs Hour, I prob.ably never would have done The Dick Van Dyke Show. 
YouÕve often mentioned your love of radio as you were growing up, yet speaking as someone who grew up with television, radio seems so limited. 
You know, itÕs funny: When we listened to radio, we saw who we were listen.ing to. The other day somebody asked me what did I watch when I listened to radio. And the answer is, we looked at the radio. We figured theyÕre in there someplace! But it was so well written, and they let you know exactly, very grace.fully, what was happening; the sound effects would let you know where they 

Dick Van Dyke as Rob Petrie, Morey Amsterdam as Buddy Sorrell, and Carl Reiner as Alan Brady in The Dick Van Dyke Show. CBS / Photofest © CBS 
were, doors were opening, and people coming in and out of rooms, so it was kind of magical. 
Did listening to radio influence you as a TV writer? 
It might have. I never thought about what influenced me. I know what influ.enced me mainly was my wife and her advice to write about what you know. IÕd written some short stories very early in my career, back at Your Show of Shows when I was relearning how to type. I lent them to somebody to read, and they said, ÒThese are wonderful!Ó And this friend gave it to another guy, who said, ÒYouÕre a writer. You should write a novel.Ó And he wanted me to have lunch with him. I told him IÕd never written a novel, and I didnÕt know how. I didnÕt go to college; IÕd just gone one year at Georgetown during the war, and I said, ÒI donÕt think I have enough words for a novel.Ó And my wife was the one who said the thing that made all the difference to me; she said, ÒYou have something else. You have feelings.Ó I said, ÒYeah, I do have feelings.Ó And that was a big catalyst in my careerÑjust those three words. 
LetÕs focus on the creativity itself. Do you believe people are born funny, or do they acquire it from experience? 
ItÕs both. I think there are creative genes, you know, that come with the ter.ritory. When youÕre born of very bright people, your chances of being bright are certainly better. But when youÕre born into a household that finds things funny and takes you to movies that are funny and listens to radio programs that are funny and you find yourself telling people about things you saw that were funnyÑthat plays a big part in it, too. And thatÕs the way I became good at it. I grew up listening to Fibber McGee and Molly, Jack Benny, Eddie CantorÑand my favorite movies were comedies. 
When I was a kid, I could always tell a joke as well as IÕd heard it and some.times infuse it with a little extra performance. So it was environmental and genetic. 
Can you define comedy or what makes something funny? 
Yes. If it makes you laugh, itÕs funny. If it doesnÕt make you laugh, itÕs not. And if it doesnÕt make you laugh, it may be you, and it may be the comedy, but we donÕt know. WeÕll never know. Not knowing who you are! [Laughs] 
Do you have any formula or rule of thumb for construction of a joke? 
No, I think the seat of your pants. If youÕre a real writer, you donÕt worry about the technique of it; you go by the seat of your pants. 
My favorite thing that I do, and I have never failedÑI may fail the next time because I havenÕt done it in a few months!Ñbut I have never failed to be funny in front of an audience despite not preparing ahead of time. IÕve emceed the Di.rectors Guild for maybe twenty-five or thirty years, and I donÕt prepare anything before I go and emcee these big events. Of course, you have to have an event that makes some sense. But I think thatÕs my biggest talent: the ability to make fun without having prepared. Sometimes IÕll be preparing in my head as IÕm walking from the toilet to the podium. 
Is there anything that helps you get into a creative mood? 
No, just feeling all right. If youÕre not feeling well, youÕre not gonna be funny. If youÕve got any medical things going on, youÕre probably going to pay attention to them more than anything else. And as you get older, thereÕs more and more of those things that take your time. 
Did you ever have a tough case of writerÕs block or get stuck at some point? 
No. You know what? If I do have a writerÕs block, I start on something else. Right now, I havenÕt written anything since last year, when I wrote a book called Just Desserts. And IÕm stuck on what to do next. And just this morning, as a mat.ter of factÑjust this morning, believe it or notÑI got to my desk, and I started cleaning it out of things that IÕd started. I found like five or ten things, three, four, or five pagesÑjust of fun things. And I threw out the things that made no sense and saved the others, and one struck me as something with potential I may work on. I pulled it up from the computer and started adding to it, and maybe IÕll continue it. ItÕs called Blockbuster. 
Is it a novel? 
ItÕs gonna be! [Laughs] 
What for you is the hardest thing about writing comedy? 
Being funny. If youÕre not funny, itÕs very hard. If youÕre funny, itÕs not as hard. 
How do you keep the writing fresh when youÕre working on a long-running series? 
If you canÕt keep it fresh, you quit. You know, thatÕs one of the things IÕm most proud of. We kept The Dick Van Dyke Show fresh almost Õtil the end, and I knew it was going to be that way because IÕd decided we wouldnÕt go past the five years. I knew five years would give us one hundred and however many shows, and that would be enough for rerunsÑand that was it. And so I had to keep the quality up until we left; I had to be very careful of not accepting anything that wasnÕt up to our high standards. Of all the shows we wrote, there were less than a handful that I wouldnÕt want to see again. 
And how did you come up with ideas? 
TheyÕre just there. On The Dick Van Dyke Show, we had writers coming into the office with ideas to pitch. And even before they did that, IÕd say, ÒTell us what happened in your house lately that you think might have been a pain in the ass or somethingÑand we can turn it into funny.Ó And some of the best things we ever did came from writers who were telling us their story. 
What elements must a good premise have? 
I would tell the writers on The Dick Van Dyke Show that, in the first two seconds, I want somebody to say, ÒHey, May!Ó By that, I meant a guy calling his wife, ÒCome in here, you gotta see this.Ó In other words, youÕre doing something they recognize. And so, anything recognizable as being part of the human condi.tion, their human condition, is what you go for. And if you go for your human condition, you can guess that your human condition isnÕt that much different from anybody elseÕs. And if it isÑthen you shouldnÕt be writing! [Laughs] 
Was it always important to you to convey a basic truth or comment on hu.man nature in your comedy? 
ThatÕs the only thing I knew how to do. I mean, I could have done fantasyÑ but that doesnÕt get people to laugh as much as if you expose what they are to themselves. And then people laugh when they say, ÒOh, thatÕs me!Ó or ÒThatÕs my Uncle Fred!Ó or ÒThatÕs my father or my mother!Ó 
A question about mechanics: Have you always used a typewriter? 
I worked on a typewriter, and now I use a computer. IÕm not computer liter.ate. I use it for very few things, like getting my e-mail. I donÕt know how to do Facebook or any of that stuff. 
If you can write the way you do, Facebook should be easy for you! 
[Laughs] Yeah, I donÕt go into that. I just get my e-mail. 
But did using the computer change your writing at all? 
No, not at all. As a matter of fact, it helped because itÕs easier to correct grammar and punctuation and to make changes. On a typewriter, you had to X out things. 
WhatÕs the most significant change youÕve observed in the industry over the years? 
One of the biggest changes and the thing IÕm most angry about is the deregu.lation of the airwaves, which Mr. Ronald Reagan did very well in the eighties. I canÕt remember the exact year, but I remember that I was furious when it hap.pened. In 1937, whenÑbelieve it or notÑHerbert Hoover was the secretary of commerce, they ruled that the airwaves belonged to the people. They restricted the number of minutes out of every half hour that could be used for commer.cials. I think it was two minutes at the most and fifteen seconds for public service announcements and nothing more. 
And then Reagan came along, and the people at 20 Mule Team Borax and General Electric were his buddies. And they said, ÒDeregulate it. Let them use as much time as they want.Ó And my feeling is that the quality of some television disappeared because of that. 
There still are some wonderful shows being written, and thank goodness we have cable because that still allows us the time to write beginnings, middles, and ends of shows. But a show that had lasted twenty-seven minutes is now down to twenty-two minutesÑgiving us the chance to see ten or twelve minutes of commercials every half hour. Beautiful, lovely fifteen-second commercials packed one after another! And for that you can thank Mr. Reagan. He did it all by himself, this lovely man that everybody loved so muchÑI saw him do that. 
In what ways does the business of TV comedy writing differ from how it was in the fifties and sixties? 
The big difference I notice is the number of writers it takes to write a half-hour. When I started, it was me. For the first two years on Dick Van Dyke, I was in a room alone. I was by myself. I was my own story editor and my own rewriter when other writers came in. And today on the credits, you see fifteen namesÑand not only as writersÑbut as producers, associate producer, assistant to the associate producer, coproducer, co-co-co-co-co-coproducer! That drives me crazy; I think thatÕs nuts. 
I loved it when there was a guy named Reggie Rose writing The Defenders, and Robert Alan Aurthur writing Playhouse 90. They were real writers. Now you see fifteen writers on one show. 
By the way, IÕm doing two shows now; one is Hot in Cleveland, and the other is The Cleveland Show. And they have a bunch of young writers who are so funny and so talented. I go to the writing session for the first reading, and those guysÑthereÕs a slew of them, dozens of them hanging aroundÑtheyÕre all funny. But they do it in a factory style now. I donÕt know why; thatÕs the way it is. 
Nobody trusts someone to do it all by himself anymore. Maybe they think you need helpÑsomebody must, maybe because the executives need help. I donÕt know what the reason for it is. 
What personality characteristics are necessary to succeed in this business? 
Stick-to-itiveness. You gotta stick with it. I mean, first of all talentÑthat goes without saying. If you donÕt have talent, then forget it. But whoÕs going to tell somebody whether they have talent or not? The world will tell you fast enough. If you keep sending things to people and they keep rejecting them, you get the idea that maybe youÕre not talented. [Laughs] But then again, if they donÕt un.derstand you, and youÕve got something very differentÑyou wonÕt stop. 
Do you feel that writers got less respect when you first started in the fifties and that thatÕs improved? 
Writers always got respect from the people who mattered. 
What about censors on your shows? Did you ever have any problems with them? 
I actually quit the second Dick Van Dyke show because I had a fight with the network when they tried to censor me, even though we were a hit show. This was the show that was called The New Dick Van Dyke Show, and we quit in the third year when ratings were in the top ten. That show is very strong in my memory; I had written an episode where Dick Van Dyke is an actor and he goes away for the weekend to perform. He comes home unexpectedly, and his daughter walks in while he and his wife are making love. And it was a very, very well-researched thing about what you would say to a child in that situation. IÕd gotten the advice of psychiatrists on it, and we handled it very responsibly. 
And they said, ÒSome people want to tell their children that a stork brought them.Ó And I said, ÒLet them tell them thatÑand argue with us!Ó I said weÕre tell.ing the truth, and those people have a chance to tell the truth. And if they donÕt want to tell the truth, they can tell their kids what we said on TV wasnÕt true; we made that up. But I was so mad, I quit. I said thatÕs enough, and Dick backed me up. I didnÕt particularly want to continue anyway because at that point I was doing movies and I was happy not to be involved with censors. Or Òcontinuity acceptance,Ó as they were called then. 
What makes a show timeless? 
The casting, or what I call the foreground. ThatÕs always the key. People wonÕt even watch a show if they donÕt like the cast. And then itÕs the writing. 
And itÕs the truth of the material and the truth of the actors. You canÕt beat it. Nothing else. Once in a while, you get a fanciful idea for a show, and sometimes that carries it for a time, but really, thereÕs nothing better than the truth. 
Do you think todayÕs shows lack the qualities that made the earlier ones great? 
Not the good ones. The good ones have it all. 
What do you think of todayÕs comedy shows? 
There are some very good shows, but thatÕs mainly on cable. I love 30 Rock and I think The Office is hilarious. I love Steve Carrell; I think heÕs sensational. And the ones I just mentioned, Hot in Cleveland and The Cleveland Show. Also Family GuyÑthose cartoon shows make me roar! They break all the rules. TheyÕre hilarious, though. 
WhatÕs your all-time favorite comedy show? 
Of those IÕm not involved in, I think All in the Family sure broke everything apart. I never missed that when that was on. And it wasnÕt only because my son was in it; it was just too good. 
Did you think All in the Family ever went too far in terms of political cor.rectness? 
No, never. You never can go too far. If you believe what youÕre writing about and it gets people talking, thereÕs no such thing as going too far. YouÕll go too far for somebody but not too far for yourself. 
Carl, I have a few questions to sum up. What do you feel is the most impor.tant factor in your success? 
You know, sitting down and doing it. Having the patience to sit and do it. My father was a watchmaker; he had a lot of patience. He was also an inventor. And I find myself thinking of myself as my fatherÕs son very often. He used to say, ÒDonÕt force anything. Just think about it. Sit down and think about what the possibilities are.Ó Whatever it is, you know, whether itÕs opening a jar, or whatever. And youÕll figure out how to do it. And I do that every once in a while: I just stop for a second and think and then go forward. 
Looking back, how do you see your career as a whole? 
The experience was wonderful, a lovely memory. 
What do you consider the biggest rewards of your success? 
The greatest reward IÕve experienced through the years is having young writ.ers come up to me and say that, when they saw The Dick Van Dyke ShowÑor the reruns of it when they were twelve, thirteen, fourteen years oldÑÐthey had no idea there was such a thing as writers! But these funny kids saw that show, and they thought, ÒI could do that.Ó In other words, they realized that they could grow up to be writersÑthere was a place for them, a thing for them to do when they grew up. 
And I hear it all the time. As I told you, IÕm doing two shows now, Hot in Cleveland and The Cleveland Show. And I go to these readings with a lot of young writers, and I get more kick out of them coming to me and saying, ÒI wouldnÕt be a writer if I hadnÕt seen The Dick Van Dyke Show as a teenager.Ó 
And the show is still around; it keeps coming back to me. People are still watching it. The president [Barack Obama] wrote about it in his book The Audacity of Hope; he said he got great pleasure from seeing his wife watching The Dick Van Dyke Show reruns. And during the campaign, somebody asked Michelle if she was going to go on any campaign tour, and she said, ÒNo, IÕd rather stay home and watch the Dick Van Dyke reruns.Ó So that gives me more pleasure than I can tell you. 
Finally, whatÕs the most unusual question anyone has asked you? 
You know, IÕve been interviewed within an inch of my life for so long now that I canÕt think of anything that surprised me. [Laughs] HeyÑprobably the question you just asked! 
2 

LEONARD STERN 
R
ecipient of two Emmys and three nominations, Leonard Stern was an in.dustry pioneer. After writing for Abbott and CostelloÕs radio program, in 1953, he moved on to his first experience with live television on Jackie GleasonÕs variety show. That show later evolved into the half-hour Honeymooners, on which Leonard and my dad worked together. 
During his prolific career, Leonard was the creator, writer, director, and/or producer of such shows as IÕm Dickens, HeÕs Fenster; Run Buddy Run; He & She; McMillan & Wife; and The Governor & J.J. He also wrote for Phil Silvers and Steve Allen and was executive producer/writer on Get Smart. He is co-founder of Price Stern Sloan, once the largest trade publisher on the West Coast. In later life, he worked full time writing and editing the famous Mad Libs books based on a game concept heÕd created with Roger Price in 1953. 
Leonard, why did you become a writer? 
It was something I wanted to do since I was in the seventh grade. I was in a French class, and Mrs. BlankensteinÑwhom everybody called Mrs. Fran.kenstein, naturallyÑwas the French teacher, but she also produced an original program in the auditorium every year. And I volunteered to write it. Anything rather than have to speak French poorly! And she and I hit it off well, and I had my first play performed that year. At the time, I realized how lucky I was that I knew what I wanted to doÑthat I had a direction. 
How do you think the experience of growing up listening to radio influenced your television writing? 
I was thinking recently about how important radio seemed to be to me and to most of my writing peers. When you listened to radio, as we all did as young kids, you had to create what the character looked like, so you started to imagine. Then you had to construct the sets in your mind. And then you had to envision the location. And so, when the radio show youÕd heard was over, you were as responsible for many of the elements as were the writers of the show, in your own terminology, so you learned. I remember when Jack Benny went on television, I couldnÕt believe that that was his vault. It wasnÕt the one I had created. But it taught me that I was already working without knowing it. My imagination was stimulated. 
When did you receive your first paycheck for comedy writing, and how much was it? 
It was five dollars from Milton Berle. I wrote a page of jokes for him. I donÕt remember the joke he bought, but I do remember him saying to me, ÒYouÕre older than you look.Ó I said, ÒWhat?Ó He said, ÒThis first joke is one hundred years old!Ó I hadnÕt known thatÑto me it was original. He was a great teacher, and he was one of the comedians that had great respect and admiration for writers. And I became his traveling companion. Whenever he played at an en.gagement where he needed some spontaneous jokes or commentary, he took me along. I was seventeen, eighteen then. And it was so beneficial. 
How did you feel the first time you saw your name on TV credits? 
I thought of my parents. I knew how they would feel. I had very supportive parents and quite marvelous. 
Does seeing your name on the credits ever get old? 
It doesnÕt get oldÑbut you try to appear more casual. 
You started on GleasonÕs variety show in 1953. What do you remember of that? 
There were five writers at that time: Marvin Marx, Walter Stone, Syd Zelinka, Howard Harris, and me. We all worked together and wrote the shows until Howard Harris left at Christmastime. Marvin, Walter, Syd, and I continued to write in concert until we started to lose energy and enthusiasm and literally fall asleep in the office because the show took a full weekÑthe four of us working ten to twelve hours each and every day. And so we decided to risk something and alternate. Marvin and Walter would do a show, and Syd and I would do a show. And that would give us two weeks to write a show. We tried it, and it worked. 

Jackie Gleason as Ralph Kramden, Art Carney as Ed Norton, Audrey Meadows as Alice Kramden, and Joyce Randolph as Trixie Norton in The Honeymooners. CBS / Photofest © CBS 
And we were immeasurably relieved because, if my memory serves me correctly, that first year we did fifty episodes of the Gleason show. And subsequent years we cut back to thirty-nine. 
You were working ten to twelve hours a day, fifty weeks of the year? 
Yes. So I had tickets to many Broadway plays, and I saw none of them. We were doing thirty-nine stories then, and we needed a writer, and Andy Russell joined us. But over the next year and a half, we worked so hard that we were get.ting to be shadows of ourselves, with the exception of MarvinÑwho somehow mysteriously put on weight. And once again we needed help, and when your dad came, we decided Andy and Herb Finn would work as the third team. And Gleason decided to do only a half-hour show and eliminate the variety elements. So we wrote the thirty-nine shows that are now referred to as the classic Hon.eymooners. And IÕve always said, ÒIf weÕd known they were going to be classics, we wouldÕve written them better!Ó 
I think you did all right by them. Do you have any favorite anecdotes from the Gleason writersÕ room? 
On this particular rewrite Saturday, several of our ideas had been summarily and unfairly turned down by Gleason. Howard HarrisÕs tolerance for rejection suddenly came to an abrupt end because the hours were endless and the de.mands were extreme. Pushing back his chair, Howard stood up and memorably declared, ÒI quit. I no longer want to be part of this humiliation festival!Ó He stormed out of the office, stumbling as he crossed the threshold and, by so do.ing, observing a comedy writerÕs rule: Always leave them laughing. 
I remember that Marvin led a rather penurious existence; I guess he had a fearful remembrance of days past where things had been limited at his home. So once a month, he would go to 59th Street across the bridge to the Sunshine Bakery and buy broken cookies for all of us. We were indebted to him and sorry that he had this need, but we were the beneficiaries. 
In the early days of television, was there generally much camaraderie among writers? 
Because there were so many variety shows, you had a large group of people devoted to the creation and the exploration of ideas, and usually within the group, friendships were formed. Your Show of Shows was a remarkable collection of thinkers and unique writers. Every Saturday night after the show, theyÕd go to DannyÕs Hideaway. And Sid, who was uncomfortable being himself, would assume a character at the restaurant. His wife would be with him, and anyone who had a date or a spouse would bring them, and then Sid would decide who they all were. Everyone was welcome to participate. So the night I was there, Sid decided he was a missionary rabbi, and they were going to Alaska to kosherize blubber. And then Howie [Morris], Carl [Reiner], and Mel [Brooks] fell into the roles of the constituents or part of the congregation or the oppositionÑand the whole evening would be conducted as if we actually were those characters. And those of us who didnÕt participate were entertained. And they did that regularly. 
What was it like for you to transition from live shows to filmed? 
I came out here when they shut down live TV in the East and were convert.ing as fast as they could to film. IÕd done nine years on live TV, and Screen Gems wanted me to do a show for them. It may have ultimately become Bachelor Father, if memory serves me correctly. But they kept stressing the fact that, while it was a comedy, it did not have to have jokes. And that was emblematic of what was happening then. They didnÕt want to challenge the performers with material that would require a laugh because they werenÕt experienced at working this way, and there wasnÕt an audience. In retrospect, I feel those shows never deserved a laugh track; at best, they could have a Òsmile track.Ó 
You see, in creating comedy for all the years prior to that, the writers hadnÕt compromised. I donÕt think they could write without the jokes, the humorous asides, the witÑand so they didnÕt make any concessions to the medium. Gener.ally, the shows that were written for an audience were the province of stand-up comedians or established comedian/comediennes, and they dictated the content of the show. And they had standards. Laughter was important to them. And the laughter had to be earned; it couldnÕt be supplemental from a laugh track. They worked to win that audience in the theater, which they felt was representative of the audience at home. But the newer writers were weaned on a different form. And they did not, I think, work asÑassiduously isnÕt the right wordÑbut they didnÕt work to get a laugh. They knew that, if they could entertain and the theme and the characters were clearly defined, that was satisfactory. Laughter became another element. And it was called a Òsituation comedy.Ó 
What was your involvement in Get Smart? 
My dear friend and partner Dan Melnick came up with the idea for the show. Then he hired Buck [Henry] and Mel [Brooks] to write it. Dan wanted to do something that satirized James Bond, and thatÕs why the show didnÕt go over well in England at first. Since they considered James Bond a spoof, they were initially sort of intolerant of Maxwell Smart. I ultimately took over the show and became executive producer and writer. 
Have you had any experiences with censorship? 
Do you remember the show My Favorite Martian? When Sherwood Schwartz and Jimmy Komack were working on that, we all had lunch one day. They told me of a memo theyÕd received from the network which said, ÒPlease change the dialogue on page 14. A Martian wouldnÕt have said that.Ó And we all laughed. I said, ÒMaybe IÕll go back and check Get Smart and see if I have any memos.Ó And sure enough, I did. And then I wrote to everyone imaginable, and everyone had some stashed away. And I put a book out titled A Martian WouldnÕt Say That. 
And Herbie Baker sent me one heÕd received on the Fred Astaire specials he did which said, ÒToo much dancing.Ó HereÕs one from The Honeymooners: ÒWhen Ralph says, ÔYouÕre going to the moon, Alice,Õ it may be the wrong des.tination. The moon is generally regarded as romantic. Could Ralph send her to Mars?Ó Or ÒOn page 7, Norton says Ôva-va-va-voom.Õ Before we can give it clear.ance, what does it mean in English?Ó What makes it such an indictment is that theyÕre authentic! ItÕs such incriminating evidence against those in charge. And they are not dullards, but theyÕre not qualified because theyÕre not visionaries. 

Barbara Feldon and Don Adams. CBS / Photofest © CBS 
Besides the teams on The Honeymooners, did you ever collaborate with a partner? 
I started out as a collaborator. I decided to team up with a schoolmate at NYU named Marty Ragaway, and we did radio and then came out and did films. And at one point, we both realized that we didnÕt know if we could write alone, and we had to find out. So each of us tried it, and we both did well enough to gain confidence. Once you know you can write alone, you can work with anyone. 
What did you contribute to your partnerships? 
I was always innately giftedÑthatÕs too vain a wordÑI mean gift in the sense that somehow story was always part of my concept. And I donÕt know where it came from; I was always a comfortable storyteller. So I would invariably concen.trate on the story in any collaboration. And until I was satisfied with the story, I didnÕt think it advantageous for us to get involved in a script. Once you start to put dialogue down, the story is more difficult to change. 
I was going to ask where your ideas for stories come from. 
ItÕs unexplainable, but IÕve always thought that way, and I still do. And IÕve figured, I have to be thankful and stop questioning how it happens. [Laughs] 
Just enjoy it. 
IÕll tell you a funny story about the first time Mel Tolkin and I worked to.gether. When I sold IÕm Dickens, HeÕs Fenster, I asked Mel to join us. Before we started writing together, he said to me, ÒDo I have the privilege of being stupid?Ó And I liked that because he was saying, ÒWeÕve both been head writers, and now here we are working togetherÓÑand if we have to wait until a gem occurs to us, thereÕll be a lot of silence in the room. 
This show was mine and my creation, and in the early days of creating ad.ditional scripts, there was really nobody involved in it other than us. As Mel and I were writing, we kept saying, ÒThey wonÕt let us do this. TheyÕll object to this. This isnÕt their type of comedy. They wonÕt . . .Ó And one day Mel said, ÒLeon.ard, they is us!Ó We realized we had nobody to report to. 
And what happened then wasÑwe couldnÕt write! We werenÕt effective. Until Mel came up with the solution: Carl Reiner had the office next door, so we wrote against Carl. Once we said, ÒCarl wonÕt like this, Carl wonÕt think thatÕs funny,Ó we were back to functioning. So my theory is you need that impetus: ÒIÕll show them, IÕll prove . . .ÓÑyou know, an internal pep talk. 
After weÕd been writing against Carl for about three or four weeks, there was a knock on the door. And Carl says, ÒHi guys. You wanna have lunch?Ó 
And Mel says, ÒFuck you!Ó So then we had to explain to Carl what was going on. And he understood. 
How funny! Do you have a definition of comedy? 
I imagine thereÕs an element of surprise. Let me put it in a way that you might find interesting. I wondered why I was equally at home with writing mysteries as I was at comedy. Because I had had success with McMillan & Wife, which was a basic mystery, although humor was certainly incorporated in almost each and every scene. And then I realized that comedy and mystery have something in common. And itÕs very strong: In both, you mislead. And in both, you surprise with the ending. And so literally comedy is taking somebody in a direction, guiding their thoughts, and then surprising them with where youÕve gone. And mystery is not very much different in the basic element thatÕs required. 
Can you explain how you come up with jokes? 
ThereÕs a formularization for many jokes, but itÕs very hard to explain. Sud.denly you have that humorous insight into something. IÕve discussed this very often with Larry Gelbart, who is probably the most gifted writer and satirist by nature. HeÕs extremely articulate, and he couldnÕt stop the flow of humor. He often said, ÒI wish I could just write this straight. IÕd like to see how I thinkÓÑ because his writing always had that surprising twist. And he himself was sur.prised by the direction his thoughts took him. So itÕs always been hard to define that, that odd perception or perspective of life, the capturing of a moment of absurdity. I never could define it; I just knew it existed. 
ThatÕs not to say there isnÕt some methodology IÕm practicing uncon.sciously, but IÕm unaware of it. I just know something funny will come up. I know to have patience. Also, when I write, I generally put down all my thoughts, even if they donÕt seem to be associative, until I have a page full of what looks like disconnected insights. And suddenly a format develops on that page; I see where they fit with each other. And IÕm truly not conscious of that in the beginning; IÕm just free-associating. 
Did you always use a typewriter? 
I leaned heavily on a typewriter. I made notes on paper and then would type them. Often when my partner Arne Sultan and I went somewhere together, IÕd do the driving. And whenever an idea came to me as I was driving, IÕd take a pencil out of the glove compartment and start writing. And Arne would get totally unnerved! So my first tape recorder was a gift from him. And now I rely heavily on tape recorders. And I guess I would still be with a typewriter if left to my own devices. I miss the sound. ItÕs strange with a computerÑyou donÕt know if anybodyÕs working!
