Were you always working on jokes and ideas in your head, even when you werenÕt writing? 
Yes. When my children were growing up, at dinner they used to say, ÒWhatÕs wrong, Dad?Ó IÕd say, ÒNothingÕs wrong. IÕm thinking.Ó And eventually, they would look up and say, ÒDad, stop thinking!Ó And my wife Gloria would say, ÒLeonard, stop thinking!Ó And IÕd say, ÒOh, I didnÕt realize it.Ó 
Do you think people are born funny, or is it environmental? 
I think itÕs innate, or it might even be genetic. My family appreciated humor, but nobody ever said anything uniquely funny or had an odd perspective on life. So I donÕt know where it came from. And I donÕt know why. As some theorists say, it may have been a defense mechanism. ItÕs a way of getting even for lifeÕs many injustices, whatever they may be. I think I have a list somewhere. [Laughs] 
WhatÕs your thought on current television? In what ways are todayÕs shows better than the Golden Age? 
Technically, theyÕre more proficient, but I donÕt know if you can match one against the other. I think what has hurt the shows is that theyÕve cut the time down, and the seven minutes usually devoted to exposition, plot, and character were eliminated. So you had to have a new form of comedy, a kaleidoscopic presentation, that had lots of physical movement and short scenes. Seinfeld was a masterful example of being equal to the demands of the medium. 
Also, what we have to consider is that no previous audiences have been ex.posed to this much television. Just the fact that itÕs been sixty yearsÑitÕs very hard upon writers today to create something that hasnÕt been done before. So when anything succeeds today, if I still wore a hat, which also disappearedÑ I would doff it. 
IÕd like to wrap things up with some reflections on your career overall. What do you feel was the most important factor in your success? 
It was a belief in myself. And that was important because it taught me to reject rejection. 
What was the most frustrating aspect of your career? 
My frustration is that you have to deal with people who shouldnÕt be in the job. Or should recognize that they need a creative equivalent of them in those meetings and give him or her equal rights and say. 
And the most rewarding? 
The most rewarding are the relationships IÕve enjoyed over the years and the magnificent, marvelous, highly electrical charges I receive from our friendships. 
Leonard, IÕd be remiss if I didnÕt ask you about one of your most successful endeavors, the creation of Mad Libs, a highlight of many of my own birthday parties! 
I think my love of words led to the creation of Mad Libs because it came about when I was literally searching for a specific word to describe Ralph Kram.denÕs bossÕs nose. Roger Price came in, and I told him I needed an adjective. And before I could explain further, he said, ÒNaked and crazy.Ó And I started to laugh because a naked nose sounded worthy of a pulp-fiction detective storyÑ ÒThe Naked NoseÓ! 
We started to work on it, and weÕd take it with us to play at parties. And five years later, when I was the head writer at The Steve Allen Show, we put it on the air. And it was an instantaneous success. We allowed the audience to give us adjectives and nouns and adverbs and verbs. They didnÕt know what they were writing, and it turned out to be the introduction of the guest stars. And I remember the first one, Bob Hope. The introduction was, ÒAnd now ladies and gentlemen, here isÓÑthe audience said scintillatingÑso we said, ÒHere is the scintillating Bob Hope.Ó It got a mild response. But when it got to Òwhose theme song is Thanks for the CommunistsÓÑbig roar! Laughter was a crucial element in Mad Libs. 
It was a brilliant idea! Lastly, whatÕs something most people donÕt realize about a comedy writerÕs career? 
How surprisingly serious the business of comedy can beÑ24/7. 
Leonard had a reputation for being classy. As we talked, he asked if he could think about some of my questions and get back to me. A few days later, I received his insightful responses in the mail, written on elegantly embossed stationery. Sadly, he passed away in 2011. His memorial had a huge turnoutÑtestament to the strength and longevity of the friendships heÕd treasured. As part of the eulogy, his son Michael led us all inÑwhat else?Ña game of Mad Libs. 
3 

NORMAN LEAR 
S
tarting as a writer on early TV shows like The Colgate Comedy Hour and Ford Star Revue, Norman Lear went on to develop several groundbreaking series, including All in the Family; Good Times; The Jeffersons; Mary Hartman,  Mary Hartman; One Day at a Time; and Sanford and Son. He is also the creator of Fernwood Tonight and Maude. 
When Norman Lear and I spoke, he had just written his memoir, Even This I Get to Experience, despite a bout of carpal syndrome, for which he had to learn to type with only his left hand. He was heading out to a stag dinner that night with a bunch of guysÑand was looking forward to seeing The Book of Mormon for the fourteenth time. A complete list of his creative and philanthropic achieve.ments can be found at www.normanlear.com. 
Norman, before we start, how much time do we have? 
We have three days. 
That will barely scratch the surface! 
[Laughs] 
Can you talk first about your fatherÕs influence on you and how that led to some of the most poignant scenes in your shows? 
You know, I just finished my memoir last Friday and sent it east to the publisher, so oh my God, have I covered that questionÑbecause that is the central question of the book. But IÕm happy to talk about it. I spent my life un.consciously seeking to make up for a father who let his son and everybody else down. He was a rascal; he went to jail because he was such a rascal. And IÕm using the word rascal because I adored him! [Laughs] So I donÕt want to call him a crook. But I couldnÕt stop loving him, so I fought all my life to make up for him. ThatÕs about it in a nutshell. 
I was a kid of the Depression, so I watched my father and his brothers go belly up. There was never anything. And my own father was one of these guys who was gonna have a million dollars in ten days to two weeks. And I did a show about that called P.O.P. with Charlie Durning, who played my father. The theme also came up in an All in the Family episode where Mike and Archie are locked in the basement of ArchieÕs Place [ÒTwoÕs a Crowd,Ó S8E19]. And for the first time, Archie talks about how his father abandoned him. He says to Mike, ÒHe goes out, works his fingers to the bone to put food on the tableÑand you think heÕs a bad guy?Ó Yeah. That was me. [Laughs] 
Did you write that particular scene? 
I worked on all of the shows; I donÕt remember who got writing credit on this episode, but that exchange between Mike and Archie was solid out of my background. 
And how did All in the Family come about for you originally? 
I had read about the English show ÕTil Death Do Us Part from some publica.tion, I canÕt remember the name of it. I saw some reference to it, and I thought, ÒHoly shit. I grew up with that! How could I never have thought of that!Ó Be.cause my father used to call me the laziest white kid he ever met. And I would scream at him, ÒYou know youÕre putting a whole race of people down just to call me lazy?Ó And heÕd say, ÒThatÕs not what IÕm doing; youÕre the dumbest white kid I ever met!Ó 
How would you describe the character of Archie Bunker? 
He was afraid of the future, afraid of the new, afraid of what disturbs what he knows. 
LetÕs talk about the writing. In general, what kind of guidance did you give your writers? 
What I used to say to the writers working on all the shows was, ÒPay a lot of attention to whatÕs going on in the house, to your families. And read at least two newspapers a day.Ó I suggested the New York Times and the LA Times. And the Wall Street Journal if you can make a third, just so you know whatÕs going on. Because weÕre going to be writing about whatÕs affecting us in our lives, so start 

Clockwise from left: Rob Reiner, Sally Struthers, Carroll OÕConnor, and Jean Stapleton. 
CBS / Photofest © CBS 
with the relationships. The expression I always used was, ÒScrape the barrel of your experience.Ó And thatÕs whatÕs gonna feed us. 
And also, for every character that entered, I wanted the writer to know what was on that personÕs mind: what was making them unhappy, what was making them happyÑwhether the person had to tie a shoelace or had indigestion or didnÕt get enough sleep last night or needed to fart or was upset with his child orÑI wanted the writer to know what was on the characterÕs mind, not just what they said to the convention of speaking as they were entering a room but what was behind that. In other wordsÑsomething is on his mind, whatever it is. Know whatÕs on the characterÕs mind. 
Do you believe that humor comes from pain or is a defense against it? 
I think, when itÕs best, humor comes from some understanding of the foolish.ness of the human condition. ThereÕs a wonderful Huffington Post piece tonight by Marty Kaplan, who runs the Lear Center. I just read it a little bit ago. He writes about a game called Flappy Bird that so many people were playing, and it was the biggest success. Someone in the Far East put it online, and then they took it down, so there was a big ruckus about that. And Marty wroteÑand IÕm adding the word foolishlyÑabout the amount of time that people foolishly spent on that game. Missing the opportunity to see everything that was in their life at the moment and if what is surrounding all of us isnÕt of more value than the time we spend on these games. ThatÕs his suggestionÑthat thereÕs more just in nature than playing a game like that. 
So to answer your question, I think humor comes from a basic understanding of and ability to see the foolishness of the human condition. How much so many kids and others are missing in their immediate surroundings because theyÕre glued to a TV set or an iPhone or to a game. 
Do you think humor is related to anger? 
Well, a lot of humor is used to tear something down, to tear a point of view down, tear an opinion down, tear a person or a cause or idea down. 
How do you see the world differently as a comedy writer? 
I saw the world differently, which is why I became a comedy writer. 
In what way do you see it that others donÕt? 
I have a better understanding perhaps of the foolishness of the human condi.tion! [Laughs] 
You started writing television in the fifties. IÕve heard that the pressure was very hard for you. 
Well, in television, the clock is ticking. And you better get that bloody thing finished. [Laughs] The early years of my career were filled with an incredible amount of fear and anxiety. I always wrote in a clutch. By one oÕclock in the morning, I had to start writing or else. I remember sitting on the phone with a shrink after vomiting and literally weeping for hours. I had four hours, five hours to get a monologue in. For years I was stopped by fear. I used to call it Òshit in the headÓ; I called it that because I couldnÕt identify it. But it was a fear, I guess a fear of not making good. 
I started to wear a hat because I would sit all those hours and I would pick my head. I was bald. And it was my wife who threw a hat on me one day and said, ÒDonÕt take this off, or youÕll lose your scalp.Ó 
Do you still have doubts when you sit down to write? 
No. No. Somewhere along the line, I began to understand myself. And youÕll see a lot of that in my memoir. Because that inward path is our most important, our most arduous of journeysÑjust getting to know yourself. 
A psychiatrist gave me a great piece of advice when I had shit in the head. And the one piece of advice that workedÑand it came years into the process of having thatÑwas to imagine fifty people in a room, and somebody shouts, ÒFire!Ó And thereÕs one small door, and when everybody rushes to the door, everybodyÕs not gonna get out. Some of them are gonna be burned. And you think of your ideas that way. It doesnÕt matter in what order those people get out; you sort them out afterwards if you wish to. Just get Õem out. ItÕs the same thing with your ideas. Good, bad, indifferent, they fit, they donÕt fitÑyouÕll sort that out after theyÕre out the door. It was a great piece of advice, and IÕve given it ten thousand times. 
And at some point, when I was letting all the ideas out when there was a fire in a crowded room, I picked up a tape recorder. Because that was the easiest way for me to do it. Because then I didnÕt see them when they left the room. It didnÕt matter who went first! [Laughs] So I started to dictate out of that, and just like the other metaphor, the rewrite was a lot easier. Everything was out. 
Do you have any quirks that help you to write? 
No. I often play music but not always. 
Can you put into words what you feel when youÕre writing most creatively? ThatÕs the payoff, I guess, for all the torment. 
ItÕs like having great sex. When I was younger, IÕd need to masturbate when ideas were flowing; the whole body was flowing, so you either woke up your wife or jerked off. IÕve also compared it to somebody climbing a mountainÑthe greatest high in the world. 
Did your writing style change over the years? 
Somebody else has to be the judge of that. My memoir is entirely different from anything else IÕve ever writtenÑso IÕve learned a lot about myself and my ability to write. I learned how hard it is to be a human being. 
If you do it right. 
However you do it, itÕs hard to be a human being. And I discovered that the learning curve never ends. 
What is the hardest thing about writing comedy? 
I think knowing where the comedy is. And that harks back to understanding the foolishness of the human condition. The better you are with that, the more you know itÕs there, everywhere. I donÕt care what the scene is, you knowÑthe starkest moment, the most pitiable momentÑthereÕs a laugh there somewhere. 
Do you have a theory about what makes things funny? 
The closest I come to a theory of what makes things funny isÑand this explains a lot of the kind of shows we were doingÑwhen you had an audience caring about something, they laughed harder. The more they cared, when some.thing funny occurred, the harder they laughed. Even just between Archie and Edith; if they were on EdithÕs side when she got a laugh, it was far bigger than if they were caring about Archie. 
Were social relevance and meaning always an important part of your writing? 
I can tell you a story which answers that; I remember this because I just re.searched it all for my memoir. IÕd written a sketch for The Colgate Comedy Hour with Martin and Lewis. And I was worried that the scene was blown because the guys went out of character; they left the script, and you know, they just behaved the way they behaved. And it broke my heart. 
But the interesting thing is, that scene was still on target enoughÑor angry enough or whatever it wasÑthat it caused a big reaction. IÕll tell you what the scene was. After the war, when television was coming along, the motion picture industry was afraid of television; they feared it would hurt films or it would maybe kill films. And so, they had an advertising campaign called Movies Are Better Than Ever. It was a big campaign that all the studios participated in, and it was hot at the time. We were doing our first show, and in the very first scene we did, Dean was with an actress whom I think was Marilyn Maxwell, who was gorgeous. And he managed a theater, and she was an usherette, and the two of them were trying to get people into the theater to see a movie. And this was a satire on Mov.ies Are Better Than Ever. And Jerry was just a feckless guy walking along, and they stopped him. And she, as a pretty girl, tried to lure him, and Dean tried to talk him into it. And they finally went into the dark theater, and that was the scene. But it caused a huge ruckus among the studios, and they asked for an apology. 
And an hour after the show, I was unhappy over dinner. And then three days later, there was publicity everywhere about the motion picture industry asking NBC to apologize for the sketch. 
Interesting foreshadowing of the impact youÕd have later. Norman, how would you explain the popularity of All in the Family? 
The four actors were heaven sent. The way they worked together and off of each otherÑin every direction, whether itÕs Edith and Gloria, Edith and Archie, Archie and Edith, Archie and Mike, Mike and GloriaÑthe four of them, the chemistry was just explosive. And thatÕs a gift of the gods. So that was the biggest factor. We wrote well, but that cast was miraculous. 

All in the Family 
Phil Doran (Writer): We worked out script ideas in NormanÕs office. We called it the Richard M. Nixon memorial recording system; weÕd sit around this large conference table, and literally microphones were on, so you had to be careful about your language or saying something nasty about anyone in the room. The recording was so that nothing was lost because we had a room full of writers, and people were pitching stuff fast. We had some great joke writers on that show, really heavy hitters. And you didnÕt want to miss a punch line. So some poor assistant had to sit there with earphones and type every goddamn thing we said. Huge transcripts would arrive, thick as a dictionary. And then as we left, the writers for the next show would come in, and theyÕd do the same thing. 
The show did stick to a real sense of reality, and this is where the actors held our feet to the fire. I remember a whole conversation I had with Jean Stapleton about Edith making toast. Jean said, ÒWe donÕt have a toaster.Ó I said thatÕs okay. She said, ÒNo itÕs not. Unless weÕve said we have a toaster and theyÕve seen a toaster, I canÕt make toast.Ó And this is the level of real.ity they dealt with! So we had to write something like Archie brings home a toaster, which was ridiculousÑjust for that day. And so all right, weÕll make it something else. How about boiling eggs? Yeah, you can boil eggs. 
Mort LachmanÕs style of running the All in the Family room was vague. We called him Captain Vague. HeÕd come in and say, ÒWell hereÕs, you know, this scene is like, it has to uh, you know, andÑOkay? Get it to me when you can.Ó And Doug [Arango] and I would look at each other and say, ÒWhat the fuck did he say? What are we doing here?Ó 
He was artfully vagueÑabout everythingÑand so it was a very interest.ing management style. But weÕd say, ÒAll right, letÕs just do what we think is right.Ó And we did, and sometimes it worked, and sometimes it didnÕt. 
Doug and I could really turn it out, but it was a very hard job of all the shows I worked on. This was the sixth and seventh year of the show, and we were competing with the best episodes from the early seasons, when it was fresh and new and you could get all the low-hanging fruitÑthe easy stories that are more organic to the show. We were in the boiler room shoveling jokes into the furnace all day, all night, every day, weekendsÑI mean, we never went home; there was enormous tension and pressure. We were routinely kicked around by everybody. WeÕd get one set of scenes with NormanÕs notes on it, one set with Carroll OÕConnorÕs notes, and one with the networkÕs notes, and weÕd say, ÒWell, this says this, and this says thatÑand oh yeah, by the way, itÕs gotta be funny.Ó So we had to reconcile everybodyÕs perception of what this particular show was and somehow make it amusing. It was terrible. 
I learned a valuable lesson from Norman. At some point on All in the Family, the monumental size of the audience and everything got to me. This was back in the day where we routinely had audiences of 40 million every week. And I remember waking up in a cold sweat, thinking, ÒHow the hell am I gonna do this? What do I have to say to 40 million people? This is just overwhelming.Ó 
And I was talking to NormanÑnot about that, since I didnÕt want to confess to the boss that I was having an anxiety attackÑbut in one of our conversations, he said, ÒWhat I do is, I just write what I think is funny. And I think IÕm normal enough that, if I think something is funny, you will, too.Ó And I thought, ÒBy God, heÕs right.Ó So thatÕs all IÕve ever done is write what I think is funny. And for the most part, other people have agreed. 
And what made Maude work so well? 
You could say the same thing about Bea. Bea Arthur made me laugh in places in my body that I didnÕt know existed. Nobody ever was as mad as Bea ArthurÑ mad in the most extraordinarily funny way. 
ÒMaudeÕs DilemmaÓ about abortion was a landmark episode of the show [S1E9Ð10]. Was that your idea? 
IÕm thinking the idea may have come from Irma and Austin Kalish, but the decision for her to abort was my decision. I was the only one who could make that decision and fight it out with the network. 
Was there any backlash once it aired? 
Oh, there was enormous backlash. There were two CBS stations that refused to run it all together, the first time. And when it came into reruns, the religious right didnÕt know what was happening, of course; nobody did, except a couple of the stations. But once the religious right had it, they werenÕt going to let it go. So by the time it came into May or so when it was going to be rerun, they were prepared to lie down in front of William PaleyÕs car, in front of my car. Only the network knows how many letters they received over it. 
Do you have a favorite anecdote from the Maude writersÕ room? 
We did a fiftieth birthday on Maude; it was WalterÕs fiftieth birthday [ÒWal.terÕs 50th Birthday,Ó S1E15]. Walter had always told stories about this kid that he loved when they were about eight, nine years old. And they used to do a little song routine together. And Walter would do that routine every once in a while for Maude. So she knew there was this special, special kid in WalterÕs life, but they hadnÕt been in touch in forty years. So to surprise Walter on his birthday, she found this guy, and he was due any minute. And when the doorbell rang, everybody left the room in order for Walter to be alone to answer. He yells, ÒCome in,Ó and the door opens, and this guy comes in. And from twenty feet away, Walter saysÑI donÕt remember the first name, but letÕs say itÕs HenryÑhe says, ÒHenry?Ó And ÒWalter?Ó ÒHenry?Ó ÒWalter?Ó TheyÕre ready to explode. And then one of them starts to do that little routine, and the other guy follows, and now theyÕre confident that each is, you know, Walter is Walter, and the other guy is Henry. And Walter says, ÒOh my God, I canÕt believe it.Ó And the other guy says, ÒWalter, gosh! IÕm so happy I couldÓÑand he falls and drops dead. 
And thatÕs the end of the first act; he just falls behind the couch, dead. The second act opens with Maude on the phone saying, ÒWhat do you mean the box arrived without the body?Ó And it got a huge laugh. But what makes it most memorable is that a couple of the writers said the audience will freeze. ThatÕs the scariest moment, and theyÕll freeze; youÕre never going to get a laugh in the second act. They begged me to change it, and I wouldnÕt change it. And finally, I said, ÒPut it in writing that the second act isnÕt going to work following this death. Give me a letter.Ó And I got the letter. Well, the second act worked great. And of course, they admitted they were wrong because I had it in writing! [Laughs] So thatÕs what came out of the writersÕ room. 
What was your style of running the room? 
YouÕd have to ask the writers. You probably talked to a couple. 
Yes, Elliot Shoenman and Charlie Hauck. 
Oh my God, Charlie Hauck. One of the greatest, Charlie Hauck. And Elliot, too, a lovely writer. But Charlie, oh shit. This year my wife called Charlie Hauck and said, ÒInstead of a Christmas card, youÕve been sending out a letter for thirty years. Please tell me you kept those letters. I have to have a copy of those letters!Ó We couldnÕt find ours. And he had them, thank God. Because it was so funny, she just had to have them. 

Maude 
Charlie Hauck (Writer/Producer): Whenever we broke a story, weÕd go in and pitch it to Norman; sometimes heÕd have story ideas, and heÕd read scripts. The joke was that his ultimate praise of a script was, ÒIt will rewrite well.Ó And when he bought a copy of the Declaration of Independence, we joked that he bought it because it would rewrite well! 
Norman would always come in and pitch stories based on something that happened to his wife, Frances. And she had manic depression, so we wrote a two-parter, where Maude had depression. And it was really hard to avoid making the comedy silly. 
But one night, we were there at around 12:30, and we were struggling, trying to do it. We really had a big problem with it. And [former I Love Lucy writer Bob] Weiskopf said, ÒWhy canÕt Frances just lose her wedding ring in the birthday cake, like Lucy used to do?Ó 
The conference rooms were below the stage, down in the basement. And very early on, right after a taping, I started down the stairs, and Bea Arthur was on the stairway, so I felt like I had to say something. I was very intimidated, and I said, ÒYou were very good.Ó 
And she stopped, she took my hand and said, ÒReally?Ó Like she really wanted reassurance. I said, ÒYes!Ó And suddenly, I had all this confidence when I realized she trusted my judgment because Norman hired me, so I must know something. That was a very touching moment for me. 
Elliot Shoenman (Story Editor/Writer): I was twenty-six, and I got a job as story editor. We were at CBS, across the hall from All in the Family, and between the two shows, the next-youngest guy was fifty-four. I was with all the veterans of early TV, and they all were writing from middle-age experience. At one meeting, they were talking about a possible show where Maude gets a hysterectomy. They were all very intense and excited about it, and could Norman get it done through CBS and everything. So I joined in and pitched and said, ÒI think itÕs a great idea.Ó And somebody suggested making it a two-parter, and I said, ÒGreat!Ó And then I went home and looked up what a hysterectomy was. So it was really by the seat of your pants, and on the other hand, I got an intense and amazing education in how to do it. 
I think I can say honestly, because I was young, I was the hotheaded one. I had a few arguments with Rod when I felt he had wavered or changed his mind. One time we did a script about MaudeÕs mother, and Norman originally had Shirley Booth in mind to play her. I wrote the script over the holidays and worked very hard on it. I gave it to Norman, and he really liked it. And then he said, ÒBut I donÕt know if we can get her; does it have to be her mother? Maybe it could be her best friend or her cousin.Ó In other words, itÕs fantasticÑbut make the boy a dog. It was a total rewrite. 
And if I remember correctly, I ended up changing it and then changing it back to MaudeÕs mother, and then casting somebody who wasnÕt that great. In retrospect, I get why that happened because, if it wasnÕt going to be some great actress, it wouldnÕt work that well. But at the time, I just saw it as ÒHoly shit, I did all this work and now . . .ÓÑI always call it Òmake the boy a dogÓÑmeaning we love it, we love it, but why does it have to be a boy? 
The two Bobs [former I Love Lucy writers Weiskopf and Schiller] were always going back and forth, grumbling with each other. I remember pass.ing their office, and they were arguing about whether Maude would say, ÒGodÕll get you for that, Walter!Ó or if she should say, ÒWalter, GodÕll get you for that!Ó Whether Walter should be at the beginning or the end of the sentence. And the next time I passed their office, they were still arguingÑ but the positions were reversed, so they would just go on like that. 
This was just this Christmas. NobodyÕs funnier than Charlie. And he worked with the guy who hired him, Rod Parker, and the director of that show; it took a great director. I mean, they were the best. They were just the best. 
After all these years, do you see any signs that your shows have changed peopleÕs minds? 
I know that The Jeffersons and Good Times helped a lot of African Americans, especially kids, because they saw themselves on the tube where they hadnÕt ever before. What that meant to them I donÕt know, you have to ask themÑbut I hear that all the time from African American people. People now in their fifties will say, ÒI grew up on that.Ó So thatÕs wonderful to hear. 
When IÕm asked, ÒDid you change anything?Ó I always repeat the story my grandfather taught me. My grandfather said, ÒWhen you throw a stone or a rock into the ocean or the lake, the level of the water rises. YouÕll never see it. What you get to see is the ripple.Ó So I use that metaphor. What you get is a ripple. 

Sherman Hemsley, Norman Lear, and Isabel Sanford. CBS / Photofest © CBS 
Of all your characters, which do you love the most? 
Maude because of the character in Bea Arthur; well, all of themÑEdith and Archie, and Mike and Gloria, tooÑI mean, they brought so much to those char.acters. And Sherman. [Laughs] He was wonderful. The Jeffersons was on the air for weeks before I found a father; it was just an offstage voice until I remembered IÕd seen this guy in a show years before. And on Mary HartmanÑoh my God, Louise Lasser. ThereÕs only one Louise Lasser. 
What was your goal for that show? 
I wanted that show to say a lot about what the media was doing, its impact on an average housewife. ThatÕs what that whole series was about. And you know, she went mad at the end and was in an institution. 
You thought that was the best-performed scene on TV. 
Ever, ever, ever. I believe you can find it on YouTube now. That was live; it was not edited or cut. That was one performance, and it was fabulous. I mean, she really looked like she was going from sane to mad. 
What do you think of todayÕs shows? 
Well, today is the Golden Age of television, certainly for drama. God, the amount of shows that people say to me, ÒHave you seen? You gotta see.Ó 
And what about comedy? 
The shows I think are the funniest are the animated shows, like South Park. And theyÕve got something on their mind. If you asked me what show IÕd most want to write for other than my ownÑpast and presentÑIÕd say South Park Õcause itÕs hilarious. And meaningful. 
Which shows do you think will be timeless? 
I guess The Simpsons is already timeless. [Laughs] ItÕs not a question of which one will beÑit is. 
Can you think of something from your shows that still makes you laugh today? 
Oh, I can think of a lot of things that still make me laugh today. I mean, my God. And all I have to do is go to YouTube and I can find thirty or forty of Õem. 
What are you most proud of that youÕve done in your life? 
Stayed sane. 
What in life do you find most rewarding, professionally or otherwise? 
My family. IÕve got six great kids. I have daughters from nineteen to sixty-seven. So thatÕs a lifetime of joy. 
Do you have any words to live by? 
IÕve always liked a talmudic story that says a man should have a jacket with two pockets. In the first pocket, a piece of paper, on which is written, ÒI am but dust and ashes.Ó In the second pocket, a piece of paper, on which is written, ÒFor me, the world was created.Ó 
IÕd be remiss if I didnÕt ask how you broke into television. 
I managed to reach Danny Thomas on the phone by pretending that I was a reporter writing an article, and I sold him something he needed. He needed something new and something short because he had to learn it quickly and do it for a show business audience who knew all his material. And this was the day before, and what I had for him was short, and he could learn it quickly. And he did it. And I got a phone call off of that because, when he performed it the next night, it was a smash. And David Susskind called and asked me to write some sketches for a show he was repping. 
Two last things. Norman, congratulations on finishing your memoir! 
Now I go on with the rewrite. You know, nothing is ever written. ItÕs rewritten. 
And what do you feel is your greatest strength? 
I think conviction. I didnÕt lack convictionÑeven when I was wrong. [Laughs] 
4 

AUSTIN AND IRMA KALISH 
A
ustin ÒRockyÓ and Irma Kalish were not ones to shy away from controver.sial subjects. Not only did they contribute to such groundbreaking shows as All in the Family and Maude, they also pitched ideas for and wrote some of those seriesÕ most controversial episodes. Together, their almost-forty-year career includes writing and producing such shows as 227, The Bob Newhart Show, The Facts of Life, Good Times, Family Affair, and My Three Sons. Theirs is a story of hard work and remarkable successÑand the mutual support and humor that saw them through it all. 
How did you break into TV writing? 
AK: I was writing for radio first and submitting scripts and things like that to different people. I knew Danny ThomasÕs family very well, and I did some writ.ing for him. And then I wrote in the closet for Martin and Lewis for years. That was also for radio, once upon a time. 
Can you explain what Òwriting in the closetÓ meant? 
IK: It means you didnÕt get name credit. The people who did get credit wanted the sole credit, but they needed help. People were willing to do it because you got paid. 
AK: I was writing half a script for Norman Lear. He and his partner Ed Simmons were working as head writers for Martin and Lewis. And when that converted to The Colgate Comedy Hour on television, I was on staff for them for three years, first in the closet, so nobody knew. And I came out of the closet because they saw that I had value. It was really through Dean Martin; he was the guy that broke that part of it loose for me. In the room sometimes, only he would hear me pitch something, and heÕd say, ÒHey, tell them what you just said.Ó So, Dean listened. Dean was a very, very nice guy. 
Do you have any recollections from that writersÕ room? 
AK: I remember Dean coming in one morning and saying to us, ÒI just fucked my wife goodbye.Ó He was divorcing his first wife, and thatÕs exactly how he said it. That was Dean. 
And so when did your husband-and-wife writing collaboration begin? 
AK: Basically when we made our stance. I was playing softball every weekend with some people in the business, and one of them was Ed Hartmann, the producer of My Three Sons. And because Irma was a writerÑshe was always a writerÑI asked Ed if she could come in with me and pitch some stories. 
IK: Rocky already had written shows with other writers along the way, and when he and his last partner broke up, they had outstanding orders to do certain shows. And one of their shared commitments was with Ed Hartmann, so thatÕs when Rocky asked if I could pitch a story with him. 
AK: And Ed said, ÒSure. Give it a shot.Ó 
IK: It was very courageous of Ed to allow me to do that because women were not regarded as comedy writers. A common belief in the industry was that women could bring tears to your eyes, but they couldnÕt bring laughter. We went in with fifty-two premises. We worked hard. And Ed was just amazed because none of those premises had ever been used on any of the shows heÕd done before. And we scored right away; we didnÕt even have to do a rewrite on the show we sold. If Ed Hartmann had been anyone but the nice guy he was, when Rocky asked if he could bring his wife, he would have said, ÒCan she type?Ó Because thatÕs what they thought the women did. 
AK: We know of stories where a woman was supposed to be a writer and the producer didnÕt believe it, so he asked the woman to come in and sit at a type.writer and write something funny to prove it. Very ugly! It was a while before the climate changed. 
AUST IN AND IRMA KALISH 
Can you tell me a bit about how the two of you collaborated? 
IK: How we did it, and still would do it, is initially weÕd drum up ideas together. And thatÕs easy; we could do that while weÕre driving or even on vacation. WeÕd talk about ideas and pitching. Then weÕd do a story outline together. That, too, was easy because itÕs a matter of back and forth, pitch an idea and a reaction, what happened next. Then IÕd sit at a typewriter and do what was essentially a first draft by myself. Then Rocky would take that first draft, and heÕd rewrite it. HeÕd add humor and make changes because he has a directorÕs eye. Then heÕd come back with a second draft, and then he and I would go over that word for word and rewrite it together. Then weÕd turn it in as a Òfirst draft,Ó but in es.sence, it was the third draft. 
In what ways are the dynamics different for a husband-and-wife writing team? 
IK: One of the dynamics is that the arguments you have are not just writing arguments. You have arguments based on your life together. So those issues are thereÑbut you donÕt let them affect the writing. You separate it. 
And you write humor in spite of it. 
AK: You write humor with tears in your eyes. 
How did you usually get hired? Was it through your agent or word of mouth? 
AK: After we presented ourselves to Ed Hartmann, we got chased down by agents. 
IK: We always wrote for shows we liked. Once we were given an assignment on a show called Hank, and we didnÕt particularly want to do it, so we asked our agent if we could get out of that one and be transferred to F Troop, which we thought was a fun show to write. So we traded that assignment for one on F Troop. 
AK: And we became the fair-haired writers of F Troop. We wrote a lot of them. They gave us an assignment of anything that we wanted, as many as we wanted. 
IK: Looking back, F Troop was one of our favorites. 
AK: Because it was pure comedy. The showÕs humor was broad, and we could do satire. We came up with a wide range of ideas. And none of our ideas were inspired by something done on any other show. 

Stanley Livingston, Barry Livingston, Don Grady, and Fred MacMurray. CBS / Photo-fest © CBS 
What can you tell me about My Three Sons? 
AK: It was just a pleasant family show. 
Did the show have a formula for story? 
AK: I could describe our premises as pulling something apart, spreading it, and then making it all come back together to what it was to begin with. And we didnÕt plan it, but it developed that there was always a moral ending of some kind. 
Can you explain what the MacMurray method was? 
AK: That was a certain type of shooting. The production manager set it up because Fred only wanted to work thirteen weeks of the season. So all of FredÕs scenes were shot in those thirteen weeks, and for the rest of the season, which was another twenty weeks, we shot scenes without him in which weÕd just refer to him. Or we did the reverse camera views with someone else standing in for him. They did that with all the lead actors; thatÕs how they persuaded them to take the role because they only had to work thirteen weeks, and they could go off and do motion pictures the rest of the year. We did it with Brian Keith in Family 
AUST IN AND IRMA KALISH 
Affair. They did it with John Forsythe in To Rome with Love and with Henry Fonda in The Smith Family. 
IK: ThatÕs how they got those wonderful actors. 
You mentioned that all your premises for F Troop were unique. Did you ever 
recycle stories for shows? AK: No, we never went back and tried to say, ÒThis would work for here,Ó and then use it. 
IK: Never. AK: We see a lot of our premises on shows today. A lot of stuff is stolen. IK: We were once writing The Joey Bishop Show, and we went in to pitch shows 
to the story editor. AK: We pitched five shows to him. IK: And he said, ÒThose sound like good ideas, but you know, I have to pitch 
them to Joey first.Ó And then our agent called and told us he got word that Joey didnÕt like any of those ideas. Fine. AK: Five weeks laterÑweek after week after week after week after weekÑ 
IK: Our ideas came on. AK: Our ideas were stolen. Eventually that guy, the story editor on The Joey Bishop Show, came to us for a job. Needless to say, what goes around comes around. 
Had he forgotten what heÕd done? AK: Of course. Or maybe it never registered with himÑwho knows. 
So what did you tell him? AK: WeÕll think about it. WeÕll think about it. 
What do you feel was the hardest show to write? 
AK: I think probably All in the Family was the toughest, but it was also a good show to write. It made you think intellectually. 
IK: It was difficult in that you could write very serious, relevant themes such as breast cancer, which we did, or Gloria getting attacked. Those were serious themes, and yet you had to set them in comedy. And you donÕt make comedy about cancer, but we were able to do it in the surrounding story. You canÕt just write a drama for a half-hour. 
AK: Norman [Lear] was OK. Irma was still his friend, but I became his enemy because he took what I said as a challenge. IÕm not afraid to open my mouthÑI wasnÕt then, and IÕm not now. But during a show, he said, ÒWe have to rewrite it,Ó and I said, ÒYou donÕt have to rewrite it; just have the actors do it the way it was written.Ó It pissed him off that I dared question his judgment. 
IK: I would say he was a nice man and encouraged a lot of writers and started them off on their careers. He was a great mentor, not just to me, but to a lot of women. 
AK: Still wears that same stupid hat. 
How did you come up with the idea for ÒEdithÕs Christmas StoryÓ [S4E15]? 
IK: We thought it was a topic that was important. People knew about breast can.cer, and they talked about breast cancer, and we thought, ÒWhy not do it?Ó And toward the end of the year, we pitched the show to them, and they didnÕt want to do it. So the following year, we went back again to pitch some more ideas. We asked them what shows they were already doing, and they said, ÒWeÕre going to do a show where Edith thinks she has breast cancer.Ó We said, ÒYes, we know youÕre gonna do it because that was our idea.Ó They acquiesced, and we did the show. 
AK: They said, ÒOh yeah, thatÕs right. Yeah, you were the guys that did that.Ó I mean, they really were stealing it. It happens. 
Was it difficult to adapt from tamer shows like My Three Sons to something as hard-hitting as All in the Family? 
AK: Not for us. We adapted; we felt we were equipped for anything. We never had a doubt about any aspect of what we were doing. And we knew we had to keep within the confines of the series itself and the budget and all the other things that are involved with any show. 
For part of your career, you worked freelance at home. Was it hard to remain disciplined? 
AUST IN AND IRMA KALISH 

Clockwise from left: Ralph Carter, Bern Nadette Stanis, JaÕnet DuBois, Esther Rolle, Jimmie Walker, and John Amos. CBS / Photofest © CBS 
IK: Probably it was; I wasnÕt the kind who said you have to get up at six and write until ten. So we didnÕt have a set schedule. I remember working at home, and we were writing something, and a neighbor came over and rang the bell. And when I opened the door, she said, ÒAre you busy?Ó I said, ÒWeÕre writing.Ó She said, ÒOh good. Then youÕre not busy!Ó 
What experiences did you have with censorship? 
AK: When we did The Colgate Comedy Hour, we were allowed to say one hell and two damns during a show at eight oÕclock. If we had a show at 8:30, we could have two hells and two damns. I donÕt remember any censorship on that show because we wrote it pretty clean. 
IK: We had an interesting experience on Too Close for Comfort. The mother in the show was an artist, and she got a job photographing a nude man for a maga.zine pinup [ÒCenterfold,Ó S1E15]. And he was going to be sitting in a chair, covering his privates with the book he was reading. And the joke was, ÒWhatÕs the name of the book?Ó The censor wouldnÕt allow us to say it was Moby Dick, or it could have been Raising the TitanicÑthere were all of those kinds of things. 
We put up a big fight for it. We said it was harmless; it was just a book he was reading. And they kept saying no. So we used a different one. I forget what it was. However, on the night of the show, we stopped the telecast at that point, and we told the audience we had that problem with censors. And we asked them to suggest names for the book. 
How fun. 
IK: So the audience made up their own names of books, and we wrote them all down. And finally, they let us use The Big Red. It was an actual book about an army. But it was crazy. We also did a show where the girls had had enough of their parents interfering with their lives, and they moved out into an apartment in Oak.land [ÒTenants, Anyone?Ó S1E6], which was the worst possible neighborhood. 
AK: When they turned on the water, it came out of the pipes brown. 
IK: And they said, ÒOh look, itÕs clearing up!Ó And when the parents came to visit, they brought their hubcaps upstairs with them; they carried them in their hands! They wouldnÕt leave them on the car because everything always got stolen. 
AK: And the city of Oakland came after us. They told us theyÕre going to recom.mend that nobody ever watch this show. Oh yes, crazy time. 
When you got your first producing job, Irma, how many other female pro.ducers were there? 
AK: She was the first comedy producer. 
HowÕd you get that job? 
AK: Hard work. 
IK: I kept going, writing, and then there came a time when Rocky wanted to do some directing, and I was on my own. And they let me be on my own. 
The Facts of Life was the first show you produced by yourself. Can you remember what your first days on the job were like, adjusting to doing that by yourself ? 
IK: To me it wasnÕt a question of ÒadjustingÓ because IÕd already played a lead.ership role in several other organizations before then. So it was something that 
AUST IN AND IRMA KALISH 
came naturally to me. The first days were fine. The people accepted me, the director accepted me, and I just went on from there. 
Switching gears, do you feel characters should always evolve on a long-running series, and can you give any examples of that? 
IK: I think The Facts of Life is a good example of a show that allowed for the charactersÕ growth and advancement in schooling and in life. I did the last two seasons of it. The characters started out as young women in a girlsÕ school; then they got to be a little older, and then sex came into the picture. We wanted to do an episode about one of them actually having sex [ÒThe First Time,Ó S9E16]. 
AK: Big fight! 
IK: It wasnÕt such a big fight with the network. They were willing to do it as long as you said somewhere that it was safe sex. However, one of the actresses refused to appear in that episode if the other character was going to have sex in it. I told her she could do it, and weÕd write her point of view; itÕd be an element in the story. No. She wouldnÕt do it. They couldÕve docked her pay for that, but I spoke against it and said, ÒItÕs all right. WeÕll write her out of the episode.Ó Which we did. 
What do you think of todayÕs television comedy? 
AK: ItÕs broader; thereÕs more sex than we could show or even allude to, but the stories are somewhat the same. I donÕt think itÕs better than before; some people who donÕt know anything about the fifties, sixties, and seventies may think todayÕs shows are the answer to what comedy is, but we were in the beginning. We started thisÑwe planted the seeds and watered the ground that led to these people making millions of dollars. 
What do you think is the biggest mistake writers are making? 
IK: When you talk to young writers, you find very often that they only write one script. And then they show it around and try to sell it, and it doesnÕt work. And they despair. You canÕt just write one script. I tell that to young writers all the time. 
AK: You write and keep writing. 
IK: I donÕt know where this story came from; it wasnÕt ours. But itÕs about an American traveler in the Indian lands early in the twentieth century and how amazed he was that, every time the Indians did a rain dance, it rained. And he asked the chief, ÒHow is it that, when your braves do a rain dance, it always rains?Ó And the chief said, ÒThat is because we never stop dancing until it rains.Ó So we tell young writers donÕt stop writing until the sun comes out, if you will. 
What do you feel are each otherÕs strengths as a writer? 
AK: Irma had and has the ability to never reach a tired place in writing. Never. I mean if you were in a room for twelve hours trying to get something, she never tired. Guys might fall asleep; she never fell asleep. She always kept up that won.derful drive she had. She was very specially gifted. 
IK: We were a good team. Our characters meshed and complemented each other, and we made it happen. 
AK: And our comedy meshed. 
IK: We both thought the same things were funnyÑand thatÕs the secret of a suc.cessful marriage, as well. 
How do you see the world differently from someone whoÕs not a comedy writer? 
AK: A comedy writer looks at the world as the world isÑwe see what is there, and we put a bend in it to make comedy. In order to get things said and under.stood in a way that doesnÕt sound bitter, we develop a comedic approach to it. WeÕre pretty straight-thinking people, but we have to put a bend in our thinking to write humor. So we are really two people in ourselves. And I think that hap.pens with all comedy writers. 
Do you think it can be taught? 
IK: You canÕt teach comedy writing. You can teach the format of scripts, and thatÕs about it. 
AK: But you have to have that instinct. That gut instinct that can take you around that bend. Away from the straight and narrow. I was just doing some improv here the other day. They wanted an impromptu monologue. Well, my head thinks funny, and I got laughs right along. ItÕs what I do. 
AUST IN AND IRMA KALISH 
Do you still write? 
AK: Every day. IÕm writing my memoirs, and when Irma reads a section, she always says, ÒI didnÕt know that!Ó Because I did a lot of things in life, all kinds of things that are wild. I once had to spy for the United States in China and the Philippines. IÕm a guy from the Bronx, from George Washington High School. How does this happen? It happens. ItÕs like writing comedy: You never know where the subject will take you because you donÕt start out having an ending. YouÕre at the beginning, and you have to go someplace in the middle, and from the middle itÕll take you somewhere else. 
What would you say are the most rewarding and most frustrating aspects of your career? 
AK: The frustration is that youÕre always fighting network, and network was a strange place. Network was always filled with people who thought they were writers. And they would suggest jokes that you might put in. And they were never very good. Or useable. 
The most rewarding thing for me was the fact that Irma was running for president of the Writers Guild. I was very proud of her because I had to do a lot of fighting in order to get her recognized. 
IK: He encouraged me not to just be Rocky KalishÕs wife but to go out on my own and do things. When I ran for president, I got a death threat in an anony.mous phone call. Can you imagine that? 
AK: If she ran. 
IK: But I did run, and in the end, I lost by what has now become very famous in the guild as the Òfour fucking votes.Ó I lost by four votes. 
Rocky received so many wonderful rewards, but last year he got the one thatÕs most meaningful to him. When he graduated high school, heÕd gone to NYU, but the day after Pearl Harbor, he left college and enlisted in the army. He never went back to NYU and never got a BA degree, and that always bothered him. So last year, we wrote the president of NYU and told RockyÕs story. 
And there was no reply, but then one day, this box came in the mail, and in the box was the mortarboard hat for graduation with the tassel; there was a cer.tificate of BA in effect, and there was a present of a little gold clock from Tiffany. And he officially became a member of NYUÕs class of 2011. 
AK: It was very exciting, I was in a hospital bed at the time; I took the hat off and threw it in the air. Seventy years later, I was a graduate of the school. That was a very touching and comforting feeling for me. 
Any final thoughts? 
IK: When I received an award at a Women in Film event, Hal Kanter once introduced me by saying, ÒIrma is a writer, her husband is a writer, her son is a writerÑand her daughter is happy.Ó But the truth is, weÕre happy. I wouldnÕt have had life any other way than the way we did it together. 
AK: ItÕs been a great life, and sheÕs been a great support for me in this situation with my health. A year and a half ago I nearly died, but even then, I had no nega.tive thoughts; I have a very positive life outlook. And so, humor comes to me through tough times. And I can make humor out of things I see on the news in order to make them more palatable for me to digest. Interesting concept of what writers can do. Not just for yourselfÑbut for humanity. 
How would you sum up your contribution to television history? 
AK: I was there. 
IK: And I was there with him. 
5 

JAMES L. BROOKS 
J
ames L. Brooks is an industry legendÑbut heÕd be the first to tell you how much the phenomenon of luck has played in his success. Jim believes in character comedy, an interest he attributes to reading a lot of plays as a kid. Surprisingly modest, he will gladly expound on his creative process or the shows, colleagues, and communities heÕs loved throughout his careerÑbut one subject he shies away from is the profound, game-changing impact his work has had on the comedy television landscape. He created Room 222; cocreated Mary Tyler Moore, Rhoda, Taxi, and The Tracey Ullman Show; and helped to develop the longest-running American sitcom of all time, The Simpsons, with which he is still actively involved. JamesÕs film credits include writer/producer/director of Broadcast News, producer/director and screen.play writer of Terms of Endearment (adapted from the book by Larry McMur.try), and writer/producer of The Simpsons Movie. 
When asked what for him is the hardest part of sitcom writing, he says, ÒBe.ing good.Ó Clearly, heÕs equal to the challenge. 
Jim, the first comedy you wrote was My Mother the Car. WhatÕs one good thing you can say about that show? 
That they employed me. I was so happy to get a job writing for television; it was extraordinary for me. And by the way, Allan Burns created that show with Chris Hayward, and they were a top team. They did many great shows. Dave Davis was associate producer, and he later went on to the Newhart show. Rod Amateau went on to do many successful shows. So they were great; they gave me my break. 
Were your earliest jobs all freelance? 
I think I had one staff job as story editor someplace. I didnÕt freelance that long, maybe for a season, a season and a half. 
And how would that work? 
At the beginning of a season, youÕd come in, and youÕd pitch ideas. And de.pending on your idea, you got a job or you didnÕt. I got my jobs usually because people read something I did and they gave me a job. I donÕt know whether I ever got one from pitching an idea; whenever I went in to pitch, I wasnÕt so success.ful. But then if you got the job, youÕd write it at home. And I was always long, meaning I always wrote more pages than I was supposed to. And it would take me I guess a couple weeks to do the first draft. Then youÕd get notes, and theyÕd ask you to do a second draft. And youÕd get rewritten. ThatÕs the tough thing on the outside: You get rewritten. 
And did you watch your shows on TV? 
You bet. 
Were you ever surprised by how much theyÕd changed? 
Yes, sometimes grievously so. Especially if it was an audience show, and youÕd go see it with somebody you really cared about. IÕd go with my sister, whom I really cared about, and the worst thing was, through the whole show, sheÕd ask, ÒWas that joke yours?Ó ÒNo.Ó ÒWas that one yours?Ó ÒNo.Ó ÒWas that one yours?Ó ÒNo.Ó ÒWas that one yours?Ó ÒNo!Ó 
How did your collaboration with Allan Burns begin? 
We didnÕt put ourselves together. We were friends, and Grant Tinker put us together to do Mary Tyler Moore. 
And how did you adjust to writing with a partner? 
We just did. I think we were a good team; we worked very easily together, and when youÕre a good team, you donÕt have absolute sense of who does what. But certainly, he was the more grown-up of the two of us. I mean he was this really responsible, great guy who was married. I was single at the time. 
What guidance or instructions did Grant Tinker give you for creating Mary Tyler Moore? 
Basically, do your best. He was not allowed to run the company at that point because he was employed by another company, so basically, we built the show 

from the ground up with some people looking over our shoulder. Later we learned that the network had wanted to fire us. Grant secretly saved our jobs. 
What was your intent for the show? 
We wanted it to be true, and we wanted it to be funny. At the time, we rebelled against situation comedy, and weÕd get pissed off if anybody called it that. We felt we were doing character comedy. 
Did you feel That Girl influenced the show at all? It was also about a single woman. 
Not at all. Except that I had done That Girl, and we all admired the guys who did it; they were sort of setting the model. They were the same ones who did The Dick Van Dyke Show, which was the gold standard. 
How was Mary Tyler Moore fresh and different from sitcoms before it? 
I donÕt know how to answer that question. I guess we were lucky enough to have the show taking place during the feminine revolution, and so that gave us some of our best shows, stuff that was going on in the culture around us. I donÕt think it was in MaryÕs character to be a feminist, but the big thing was, when youÕre doing a television show in that time and womenÕs rights are such an is.sue, people wanted to politicize the show and get their hands on the steering wheel. And our job was to keep everybodyÕs hands off the steering wheel and let the show be true to its characters. We very much wanted women writers on the show, and we had some great ones. And a lot of them were very involved politically in what was going on, and certainly that fed into what we were doing. 
Did the idea of work colleagues being family come from your own life? 
I think it did, but IÕm not the first person to have that experience. I think itÕs common. But it was certainly in the nature of the pilot script that Mary would have a home life and a work life, and her work was important to her; that was the model we had in the pilot. 
Can you characterize the overall feel behind the scenes of that show? 
It was an experience that was informal and loose and catch as catch can. It was a show that Allan and I got to run to an extraordinary extent, thanks to Grant. He just gave us our freedom to a remarkable degree. We actually hired the accountants! It was the inmates running the asylum. And we got used to it, and I think everybody who worked on the show was spoiled in a great way. 
So we ran our own shop. There was a blowup with the network, and they came at us. They didnÕt want us to do the episode with Nancy Walker; it was about four shows into the first season, and I forget their reason. One person, not Òthey.Ó A ÒheÓ did that. And we held our ground, and of course Grant always supported us, and we did the show anyway. And then the show began to get noticed more and moreÑand then we didnÕt have to submit story outlines any.more. At a certain point, they didnÕt require it. 
What happens is a culture springs up, and it was fun and great. It was a show that really cared about all its writers; it was a show where everybody contributed. And we got to make the rules of work, and I think they were good rules and right rules. 
ThereÕs always something democratic about a show. Television was and is a writerÕs kingdom, you knowÑweÕd be the final judges of what got cut and what got in. WeÕd all discuss staging; Jay Sandrich was the director, whom we loved, but thereÕd always be an ongoing conversation about how it would go. It was just a model of people respecting each other and having fun together. And we goofed around; we were funny. And thereÕs something very democratic about writing a joke. I mean, when youÕre in a writersÕ room, the room laughs democratically. It doesnÕt laugh according to who said the joke. But somebody does have to run the room, and we ran the roomÑbut everybody really respected each other. 
Would you want to comment on how the show redefined the American sitcom? 
No. 
Am I correct in assuming that you donÕt like to think about how influential your career has been on television? 
Yes, youÕre correct! 
Were you aware that you were doing something that could potentially shape the future of sitcoms? 
I donÕt agree with that premise, but we were very aware that people were seeing our show. We were very aware that some people wanted to politicize it, and we were very aware when we did one that wasnÕt so hot and when we did one that was so hot. We had standards, we kept on working hard, and it was a beautiful way of working. I think we started with thirty-three, thirty-four shows a year, and then everybody took a vacation for two months. And then we came back, and we knew we had those jobs, and we got ready for the next season. I think maybe in those days I was too nave to fully appreciate it, but I did, enormously. I mean it was unreal. And maybe it was so unreal that I had a job like that, that it got in the way of my appreciating Taxi. All of us, to the man and woman, appreciated what we had going for us while we had it going for us. And I think thatÕs true of The Simpsons today. I think we know we have creative freedom. We know we get to do it. WeÕre serving something bigger than us. It keeps us properly humble. 

Why did you decide to do a spin-off of Mary Tyler Moore? 
Because Valerie was going to leave the show, and that was the only way we could sort of keep her. 
And what was your reason for RhodaÕs divorce? 
It wasnÕt quite working; the chemistry was off. The balance was off. It was hard to generate stories, so we did it. And I think it was hard to recover from. Story is important. And we were finding it difficult to come up with stories with what we had going for us, and we wanted to get back to dating Rhoda and take her as somebody for whom life handed a hard one. 
Were you worried about making the show too depressing? 
Not enough, I guess! ItÕs a valid point youÕre making. But your point is based on a belief that you have to be safe; better safe than sorry. ThatÕs a very valid position, but I donÕt think the only position. 
Switching to Taxi, what are some positive memories you have of working on that? 
Taxi days were wilder. The early eighties were wild; the Paramount lot was segregated between movies and television in every way possible. If you worked in television, nobody would hire you for movies, but we were having all the fun. We drove into separate gates. A bunch of the people who were the first to make it over the wall to movies happened to be working there, restricted from television at the same time though loving itÑand having a great time. You know you always read about the way they made movies in the forties and how people would go from stage to stage and visit other people? We hung out together; we visited each otherÕs shows. Taxi had a great party every Friday night, a really great party, and it was amazing. And again, we had a wonderful feeling of com.munity on that show. 
By the way, in each case, the frustrating thing about all this work is that none of it happens unless the right person walks in the door, for the right part. And sometimes you get lucky, and sometimes you just stay in the casting process longer than the world wants you to. And for every show IÕve done, it was the group of actorsÑin each case, a crazily extraordinary group of actors. And in each case, actors from different disciplines who got there in different ways. 
I was about to ask you about the role of luck in your career. 
Recently I heard this guy talking about his career, which is a good career, and nowhere in it did he credit anybody else. And I thought, ÒWhat a crazy person.Ó Like peopleÕs marriages, thereÕs a moment in everyoneÕs life when she walks in or he walks in. And people talk about that lore, and sometimes it works out; sometimes it doesnÕt. People have a lot of names for the romance of that. You can have the same screenplay, and somebody comes through the door that makes itÑyou know, if Holly Hunter hadnÕt happened, Broadcast News wouldnÕt have been distinguished at all. If she hadnÕt walked in the door. And the only thing I take credit for is holding the door open until people were screaming, and she was the last one to come in. And IÕll never stop being grateful to my casting director then, who instead of just saying, ÒI canÕt believe this guyÕs calling me again,Ó kept on going through who was aroundÑthat times a million, you know? Or Grant Tinker, at that point in time. Gene Reynolds. You could go crazy with every show. Everything that worked involved luck. 
And the reverse is probably trueÑbut you donÕt look at it that way. What happens when you cast wrong, the wrong actor is there and you cave, or you defend the wrong thing? I always say that an important part of life is to not be the guy or woman in the bar telling the story of the mistakes that killed you. And part of not having that mistake happen is luck. By the way, thereÕs no way you can create it, except you can extend the enterprise youÕre working on to give it more chance to happen. 
And luck was a factor in creating Taxi. 
Oh my God! Louie DePalma. If I hadnÕt been lucky enough to see a dis.patcher accept a bribe from a cabdriver, we wouldnÕt have had Louie DePalma. That gave me the whole idea for his character. 
The basis of Taxi was that everybody was only driving a cab temporarily, and they really wanted to be something glamorous. And that came from a magazine article weÕd read about a cab company in New York where none of the drivers was happy; they all had dreams of doing something else. So for research, we all went to New York to visit that company, to talk to the people, to observe. And within twenty-four hours, we had our show. Sure enough, all the cabbies told us how discontent they were being stuck in their jobs and that they dreamed of something greater. But if the cabdriver who was the most charismatic person in the garage hadnÕt come in at seven oÕclock in the morning after driving fourteen hours, the show would have been completely different. This guy rolled in, and we asked him what he wanted to be. And he said, ÒMe, IÕm a cabdriver.Ó And that defined the hero that Judd played. I didnÕt have a hero until I heard that line. I didnÕt know what made a cabdriver a hero until I heard that line. 
What do you think were some of the best Taxi episodes? 
We didnÕt do many two-parters, but we did a show where AndyÕs character was freezing to death, and the only way he could survive in his stranded cab was to have sex with another woman [ÒScenskees from a Marriage,Ó S5E4Ð5]. His wife noticed it right away, and then we got into one of my favorite things, which was inventing things about AndyÕs culture. And there was one about Alex having a gambling addiction, which I liked a lot [ÒAlex Goes off the Wagon,Ó S5E3]. But IÕd say if you can get four, five, six really terrific shows a season and no more than one or two that you wish you hadnÕt done, you feel great. And I think we had a great batting average on Taxi. 
What about The Simpsons writing are you the most proud, Jim? 
I think that our ambitions are great. The show allows us to go in so many dif.ferent areas: We can be realistic and have a story with emotion, or we can be wild and crazy. Or we can be musical. Even though we did set limits at various points because the first thing we wanted when we created the show was it could not be a cartoon. We didnÕt want to be a situation comedy depending on the situation for the jokes; we didnÕt want to be a cartoonÑwe wanted to treat our show the same way we did with live action because a lot of us had worked together in live action. And that was our big rule; we were really tight-assed about that rule. And then I think one of the things that gave the show the vitality is that we held the rules, we held the rulesÑand then we gave up the rules. But we didnÕt just go crazy at once; we kept on increasing the areas we could go into. I think weÕre still doing it. 
Do you have a favorite Simpsons episode or premise that you contributed? 
You donÕt keep score like that; you all write the stuff. I go in, I give notes on every show, and then you pitch key parts of it, and then it goes to a table, and every script is rewritten and rewritten again. But I tend to like the shows that look back to Homer and MargeÕs youth; I have a soft spot for those. And weÕve done some black comedies; most of our Halloween shows are black comedies, and thatÕs sort of fun because it has you looking for the joke in an area which is not considered fertile for comedy. 
And at a certain point, the continuum is what youÕre doing instead of the epi.sodes. You know how they talk about running a marathon, and you have to get past the ten-mile barrier, when youÕre really hurting? I think it was right about our tenth year when we just thought, ÒHow do we avoid repeating ourselves?Ó And then in some miraculous way, we broke through that. Though it always happens with us, and we can do it. ItÕs not a burden anymore; somehow weÕre solving it, and I donÕt know how because we really donÕt repeat ourselves. 
Do you have an idea for how youÕd like the show to end? 
LetÕs see, we came close to the end a couple of times and then had resur.gences. But no, I donÕt. With Mary it was a great ending, but we closed the world that we wrote about. We just closed that world out, said goodbye to it. I donÕt think it would be an ending like that for The Simpsons. I think they will continue; they will continue after we fade out our last show. 
But itÕs one of the hardest things to do. I canÕt imagine a harder job than to bring home a show thatÕs been successful where everybodyÕs looking at your finaleÑand it rarely is terrific. ThatÕs really hard! The batting averageÑI mean the world is not good at that one. 
To what do you attribute The SimpsonsÕ success? 
I think passion. First of all, thereÕs a weird thing that happens when you enter the culture and where suddenly youÕre ubiquitous and thatÕs what people are talking about. And thatÕs a wild ride, and itÕs crazy. And thatÕs only happened to meÑwell maybe more than once, I donÕt know. And then when that passes, then where do you go from there? And that depends on the group thatÕs doing it. People talk about how to use failure and how to learn from it, and I think itÕs all valid. But there are scars to failure, so healing becomes part of it. The right way to use success is to take chances and to feel a sense of service and to use the success to be creative. Use it for the work instead of for yourselfÑwhich ends up being for you, the best thing you can do for yourself. 
My dad wrote The Honeymooners and The Flintstones andÑ 
Wow. Two great shows. 
So IÕd like to ask if you think Homer is a descendant of Ralph Kramden. 
Oh, letÕs see. HeÕs a lot happier than Ralph was, and heÕs not as frustrated with his lot as Ralph was. But a working man where familyÑwell heÕs a father, thatÕs a big difference right away. And he doesnÕt work hard, and he doesnÕt have a grueling job; he goofs off at his job. No, I donÕt think he is, actually. I covered the ground! 
Do you think heÕs a descendant of Fred Flintstone? 
Not at all. I mean, Fred was dumb, and Homer is dumb, but I donÕt think in the same way. But they were each simple men with simple needs, which I guess means that each had the secret to life because, whenever you come up with the answer to life, itÕs always if you can get it simple. And these guys were born to keep it simple. 
IÕd like some insight into your creative process, Jim. Can you describe how you feel when youÕre in the zone? 
ThereÕs a couple ways. ThereÕs a thing everybody talks about, which is Òyou hear your characters talking to you, and youÕre taking dictation.Ó Everybody talks about that. But thereÕs another thing, especially when writing a movie, where so much energy goes into pushing the story out there, pushing the narra.tive, trying to push the charactersÑand at a certain point, if youÕre lucky, youÕre not pushing; youÕre being pulled. And you donÕt always understand whatÕs pulling you, but youÕre being pulled. Suddenly the whole nature of what you do when you sit down and write changes. 
You know how people talk about process? ItÕs a real thing; itÕs a real word for a real thing. And what process I think simply means is a tremendous preoc.cupation with the work. Tremendous, where everything that happens in your life begins to go through a filter about whether itÕs of any use whatsoever in what youÕre working on or not. 
And I think if you do get into process like thatÑthe great thing is that, when youÕre not working, youÕre working. And that when youÕre avoiding, itÕs OK. EverythingÕs OK. DonÕt tell yourself youÕre in process when youÕre not, but if youÕre really in process, you can take those few days and let it cook; you can do anything you want. You treat it like thereÕs a permission to assume that, no mat.ter what youÕre doing, itÕs righteously about the work. 
Did you ever have a serious case of writerÕs block? 
ThereÕs no not-serious case of writerÕs block! Every case of writerÕs block is serious. Yeah, I think the worst was Terms of Endearment when I was writing it. It took a long time, and they had optioned the book, and then at a certain point they had to buy the book. And they bought the book. And now I felt tremen.dously responsible. And I was stuck. I was stuck in my script, and I couldnÕt go backwards, and I couldnÕt go forwards. And I spent every day blushing. IÕd liter.ally be blushing. It still happens sometimes. It was just intolerable. And I went out one night, and there was a concert pianist there who did pretty well all over the country, but he had never played New York. And he had a fear of what that would be if he played New York. And I described what was happening to me, the blushing and stuff. And he said, ÒOh, thatÕs a state of shame.Ó And it helped me enormously that there was a name for it, which meant I wasnÕt the only one in the world who ever experienced it. And I donÕt know what happened from there; I know I went to Hawaii and had a small room at a friendÕs house, and I had the illusion that I had cracked the whole thing. And I had one of the most euphoric moments in my life. It turned out I hadnÕt cracked the whole thing. But the feeling that I had cracked the whole thing released me from all the tentacles of that writerÕs block. 
What about television. Were you ever blocked there? 
ItÕs not allowed! [Laughs] IÕve always said the bad thing is you have to do it again; the great thing is you get to do it again. 
Besides community, whatÕs your favorite part of writing for sitcoms? 
Well, the golden key is self-expression. I mean, thatÕs the best thing you can get out of anything. I used a weird thing that happened with my alcoholic father for at least two Taxi episodes. You could have the worst thing in the world hap.pening to you, and you say, ÒI got a story.Ó ThereÕs nothing like it. 
Grant Tinker used to say during The Mary Tyler Moore show, ÒWhen you fly over the U.S. on a Saturday night, you could jump out of the plane and very likely land on somebody watching your show.Ó And thatÕs amazing. 
But I love that I still get to sit in a room and pitch jokes for television on The Simpsons. Because to me, that keeps you honest. I always think everybodyÕs lucky to be at the party, but they shouldnÕt forget why they were invited to the party. And I think IÕm invited to the party because I was that funny outcast in high school. So you have to be careful to take yourself seriously and not too seriously. 
In general, do you have a preference for writing workplace ensemble sitcoms over domestic comedies? 
I donÕt think that way. IÕve written different kinds of shows at different times. I think different things take you to different places at different times. I mean, we did Taxi based on a magazine article. We did The Simpsons based on fifteen-second shorts. 
Briefly, what elements are essential to a successful sitcom? 
I think it has to be believable; it has to be funny. And as I said, I think good story is very important. Story always drives you nuts. If you have a good story, other people can make it funny invariably. A good story is really hard; a really good story is just sort of amazing. But with animation, you could have a beautiful visual sequence, an imaginative visual sequence that really tests the imaginationÑ sort of like a good story, even if you only get it for a hunk of the show. 
Do you use any particular techniques for brainstorming premises? 
I donÕt think there is a technique for that; I think you just open your mouth and see what comes out. Or sit and think of a story that youÕre going to bring in. Or try and construct it. 
You know, you do something enough, and you have craft. But you still need inspiration, and you still need looseness, and you still need to surprise yourself. And most writing rooms are like that. The great things in the night are the sur.prises, where youÕre trying to work on a moment and then the room breaks up. ItÕs a surprise, and itÕs great, and itÕs a relief. 
LetÕs talk more about the room. WhatÕs the worst experience you can have in one? 
The worst experience is at 4:00 a.m. when you have to look at the food con.tainers. ItÕs work, but itÕs work that everybody loves doing even though youÕre crabbing at the time, and you go nuts if you canÕt get it. I mean, everybodyÕs do.ing what they want most to do in the world; thereÕs a certain atmosphere to that. 
Do you have any memorable anecdotes from any room? 
I donÕt have a handy story; IÕve worked with great people, and I have a great fondness for being around other people and them being terrific at the work, and that makes you better. Going from show to show is like having a lot of college lives. 
Do you have a preference for quiet, thoughtful rooms over loud rooms? 
ItÕs not supposed to be quiet. ItÕs supposed to be people pitching. Pitching is saying a joke out loud and seeing if people laugh. There are long silences if nobody has it, but everything gets boisterous when thereÕs a run going. 
Switching gears, can you think of something you wrote that still makes you laugh today? 
ThereÕs a lot in my mind, when I think about it. ThereÕs a monologue that Reverend Jim had on Taxi in the gambling show where he talks about getting stoned with a shaman that I just thought was a great joke. And the longest laugh anybody ever got was also Reverend Jim on Taxi when Jim asks Bobby for help on his written driverÕs license test [ÒReverend Jim: A Space Odyssey,Ó S2E3]. He asks, ÒWhat does the yellow light mean?Ó And Bobby says, ÒSlow down.Ó Then Jim repeats the question, more slowly: ÒWhat . . . does . . . the . . . yellow . . . light . . . mean?Ó 
[The director] Jimmy Burrows felt that it was a bigger joke than that, and he gave them the go-ahead to keep doing it until the laughs faded. And they never faded! They would have laughed forever, as long as we did it. Jimmy cut it at the correct point, but the audience would neverÑGod knows what would have happened. We would all have been ground to dust just continually going slower and slower. It was amazing. 
Who are some of your favorite comedians, past and present?
