Lenny Bruce was staggering. Nichols and May were staggering, staggering beyond belief. Newhart was, is, extraordinary. And the great working-class woman, Roseanne, was a revolutionary female stand-up, and people forget it. I remember seeing her in a club, and you just never saw a woman do jokes like that on a stage. Never. 
How has television comedy changed since the early days of your career? 
I believe in character comedy. I think thatÕs important in a half-hour show. I think we still have a lot of it; I donÕt think thatÕs changed that much. Certainly, what you can do and the society that youÕre inevitably reflecting has changed so dramatically. ItÕs a bolder and edgier, sexier society. Dick Van Dyke and his wife slept in twin beds, you know. Television is great today because writers are in charge, and their voices rule. I think itÕs the great writersÕ place. 
What do you think is the best way to learn the craft of comedy writing? 
ThatÕs obvious: ItÕs just doing it. USC film school is putting a tremendous concentration on comedy right now and teaching it. I think they have quality people there. 
And I think itÕs important to appreciate that, in most of the profession in movies and TVÑacting, directing, writing, producingÑwriting is the only one where you get to be in a room alone and you get to do it. Nobody can stop you from doing it. 
Now for a few personal questions. Have you always been confident you could write funny? 
Oh, man, yeah. I donÕt know whether confident is the word, but IÕve sure seen a lot of my comedy work. 
What keeps you humble in the face of your success? 
The current job. 
Do you have a favorite philosophy or words you live by? 
Listen to what you say; you may mean it. ItÕs very important to listen to whatÕs coming out of your mouth and get informed about yourself from it. I also think the key is to try to be on your own side, which is trickier than it should be. 
Which of your shows is most personally meaningful to you? 
Boy, itÕs so hard. You know, you really give everything to every show. I always have said the best job in the world is a television show thatÕs working. It really is. Because movies are great, but then everybody blows apart; the things that made them great are spread over a lot of other things. But in televi.sion, you build a community that can go on forÑwell, in the SimpsonsÕ case, twenty-five years! But in all cases, with shows that are working for five, six, seven, eight years, it becomes this life. People get married, they have children, peopleÕs lives change as you work together. So IÕd say Taxi because of the time it was at Paramount. And because of that street where five shows with great people on them and great people behind them were all gathered together, and everybody just really had a great time together. I think I enjoyed that show the most. And the Charles brothers were there, Sam Simon was there, Jimmy Burrows was thereÑa lot of the next decade of television was in that staff. And the show dealt with existential stuff, which is fun. 
What do you want people to take from your work? 
I forget who said it, but somebody terrific: The purpose of popular culture is to let people know theyÕre not alone. 
Have you received any feedback on your career that was particularly mean.ingful to you? 
I got to have very open and close conversations with Mike Nichols, and I think that was most meaningful to me. Some of itÕs personal; it was all very wise, and it was all very witty, and sometimes sublime, sometimes very deep. He was most remarkable. IÕm one ofÑGod knows what the number is, maybe four fig.uresÑwho just loved the guy and worshipped him. 
Did you always feel that you had to be a writer, or could you have found fulfillment doing something else? 
I canÕt imagine having done anything else. I never had the ambition to be a writer because it seemed impossible. And what I just said is a common thing be.cause IÕve talked to others about how many years it takes of making your living as a writerÑuntil, when somebody asks you what you do for a living, you can say Òa writerÓ without your voice catching or rising. Because itÕs just an amazing thing. 
6 


TREVA SILVERMAN 
A 
versatile writer whose work ranges from off-the-wall hijinks for The Mon.kees to the depth and realism of Mary Tyler Moore, Treva Silverman was one of the earliest women TV writers and the first to win an Emmy in comedy as a woman writing alone. Some of her other credits include That Girl, He & She, Captain Nice, and Room 222. 
In our conversation, Treva articulates vividly the joys and struggles of her creative process: the excitement she feels when a script is going wellÑÒAnd when itÕs not going well, itÕs torture, the worst feeling on the face of the earth!Ó 
She apologized to me profusely for having postponed our meeting because, in her words, 
I got caught up in an absolutely wonderful, startling thing where I started rewrit.ing something and I just let everything drop. It was like being in the middle of a good tornadoÑdiving into a different world, where I was seeing new things I hadnÕt ever seen. And it was wonderful to be surrounded by these characters that I love. I didnÕt make that many changes, but I was so delighted to be able to take my ÒnowÓ self and put new stuff in and be chortling away and making myself happy. Those things donÕt happen a lot, and when it grabs you, you have to do it. ThatÕs what youÕre here for. But I broke appointments, and now IÕm writing apology letters to everybody! 
When asked how she had felt starting out in a manÕs industry, she says, ÒI al.ways felt that I had to be better. Whether it was a manÕs world, a womanÕs world, a hermaphroditeÕs worldÑI always felt that I had to excel.Ó And clearly, she has. 

Treva Silverman. Photo courtesy of Treva Silverman 
What led you to become a writer, Treva? 
From the very, very beginning of my life, I never wanted to do anything that didnÕt involve expressing myself. At five, I was a child prodigy in music, so I started writing music, then writing poetry, then setting the poetry to music, then writing little plays. I felt isolated and not understood, and writing was a way for me to get in touch with myself and my thoughts. 
My mother took my three sisters and me to everything that was artistic and creative: Broadway plays, musicals, ballet, opera. At home, we were all reading constantly, all taking piano lessonsÑI was taking singing lessons, balletÑall that creativity was very much in the air. I would have said zeitgeist, but I didnÕt know that word then! 
My parents talked a lot in clichs, which kind of annoyed me. And I think part of my humor came from my practice of secretly doing little comebacks in my head, which I would never say out loud. You didnÕt contradict or correct your parents in those days. 
Also, in my era, you didnÕt talk about feeling different; you didnÕt talk about feeling bad. Everybody was supposed to be cookie cutter. And even my name gave me away: IÕm different, IÕm different, IÕm different. What a terrible era to be different in, the Eisenhower era. It was in theater and movies and reading that I found people who matched me more than those around me did. So the world of creativity was really my savior. 
How did you get your start in television? 
I had started out as a songwriter and sketch writer, and at night I earned my living singing and playing show tunes in New York. Some of my sketches ended up in a musical revue, Carol Burnett saw them and hired me for a variety show called The Entertainers. 
At that point, how were women writers regarded in the industry? 
OK, letÕs talk about the women thing. They werenÕt really regarded in the industry, mainly because they werenÕt in the industry. There were a few women writing comedy with a male partner, but when I worked on The Monkees, I was literally the only woman writing episodic comedy alone. I was written up a lot because it was such an anomaly. When I was story editor on Mary Tyler Moore, I was the first woman executive on a comedy show. And when I won the Emmy for Mary Tyler Moore, I was the first woman writer who wrote alone to win an Emmy for comedy. 
I remember going to a producerÕs house with Peter Meyerson, my writing partner whom I met on The Monkees. This producer had heard about us because we were kind of the new kids in town, and when we were introduced, he pointed to me and said, ÒYou do the story, and Peter does the jokes. Right?Ó And Peter, who was so wonderful, said, ÒAbsolutely not. We both do both.Ó And then he gave a long, beautiful feminist speech about me. With that producer, it was an assumption that women werenÕt funny. Another producer who hired me was talking about comedy scripts, and he said, ÒI donÕt like itsy-poo.Ó And I said, ÒYeah, well, I donÕt either. IÕm curious: Why do you bring it up? Did you have a bad experience with some guy or something?Ó It was assumed that a woman would write jokes about gardening, I guess! 
Did anyone ever discourage you from this profession because of your gender? 
Well, my parents didnÕt, which was great. They absolutely believed I could do anything I wanted to. But I had an interview with Johnny CarsonÕs people to write for The Tonight Show, and the guy I met with called my agent and said it was a great interview, Òbut we could never hire a woman.Ó That was in 1964. 
You mentioned The Monkees. How did you get hired for that job? 
My agent asked me to take what was the best of my sketches, and he submit.ted them to The Monkees. The next thing I know, IÕm called in for three inter.views. The MonkeesÕ producers had told all the agents they didnÕt want to hire Hollywood writers because Hollywood writers had learned a lot of bad habits. They wanted the New York kind of writer, what they called Òthe New York head,Ó the quickness of the New York mind. And after three weeks of holding my breath, I was picked for the staff. 
About writing for The Monkees: IÕve learned over the years that three kinds of writing come naturally to me. The first is real life, how people really are, behavioral things, the charactersÕ real feelings, their idiosyncrasies, how theyÕre trying to cover up what they actually feel but you can tell whatÕs really going on underneath. The second kind is larger than life, a little over the top, more satiri.cal. And the third kind is totally off the wall, insane, non-sequitur, sort of Monty Pythonish, which, of course, was right for The Monkees. But I had never written that third way beforeÑand I didnÕt know at that point if I could do it. 
The day we got there, they gave us guidelines because we were all so new. Like ÒInt. RestaurantÑDayÓ and ÒCut to.Ó Really basic. And then they gave us specifics. I remember there was a hand-drawn thing of the MonkeesÕ Òpad.Ó I didnÕt let them know I had no idea what a ÒpadÓ was. 
IÕm guessing it was a relatively easy show to write. 
Looking back now, I would say yes. Because compared to other shows, they werenÕt looking for depth. Depth would have been out of place because they werenÕt actors who wanted to show their range; they wanted to be a bunch of crazy kids who were intense about their music. It was the first episodic comedy show I wrote, so it was really a perfect first show for me because I just had to worry about story and off-the-wall stuff and making it funny. 
Then how were you hired for Mary Tyler Moore? 
I had known Jim Brooks for several years. In New York I was singing and playing show tunes at a club in the theater district, and Jim was there. We started talking about Salinger and became friends from that. By the time he created Room 222, we had become very close friends, and I wrote some episodes. And although they had never written together before, Jim and Allan Burns were asked to create a show for Mary Tyler Moore. A while later, Jim called me and said, ÒWhat are you doing?Ó I said, ÒIÕm just about to wash my hair.Ó He said, ÒNo, no, I mean with your career, with your writing. Because we just got green-lit, and we want you to write as many Marys as you would like to.Ó 
What wonderful news! 
Wonderful! And I thought, ÒJesus, I would love that,Ó because I loved JimÕs writing and I loved AllanÕs writing. They had written two scriptsÑthe pilot script and one otherÑand when I read them, I was elated because it was exactly the kind of stuff that I love to write. I canÕt say anything comes easily to meÑbut if something did, it would be that kind of writing thatÕs about relationships and behavior. I ended up writing sixteen episodes. 
They had nine monthsÕ lead time, and Jim and Allan have often said that having this long lead time helped them enormously because it gave them a chance to really know what they were doing and think about the show. So it hadnÕt been aired, but it was starting to get negative buzz at CBS. CBS put all the writers in a far-off facility that was sort of broken down and not where people really wanted to work. I was oblivious to all this because IÕm writing characters and I love the characters and IÕm having this great time and ev.erybodyÕs smart and funny and nice, and I didnÕt know what was happening around me. But I found out later that only one executive at CBS liked the scripts. Everybody else was saying it wasnÕt going to get picked up, and thatÕs why we got put in this crummy facility. 
At this point, my agent said, ÒYouÕve written two or three scripts there, and thatÕs fine, but at this point itÕd be good to spread yourself around so that other studios, other networks, know your work.Ó And I said, brave little thing that I was, ÒThis is the only show I want to write. ItÕs exactly my kind of writing, the thing that I love, and IÕm having a great time.Ó 
Everybody there was so good that you felt a kind of an alertness; you didnÕt have to argue with anybody. You didnÕt have to say, ÒWhyÕd you cut this line?Ó because they would just know what was good. And the actors were so extraor.dinary that I thought, ÒWow! This is one of the first times IÕm getting back more than IÕm giving.Ó It was this wonderful community of like-minded people, and I think we all inspired each other, raised the bar. I think you only learn when youÕre with writers as good as you or better. 
I also felt there was no demarcation between the people who were running the show and me. I had worked on a show where I knew the producer loved my work. And one time I had a deadline, and the story editors were busy, and I remember going into the producerÕs office and saying, ÒI hate to bother you, but IÕm having a problem with my story. Could you spare a few minutes?Ó And this unnamed Mr. X producer said, ÒTreva, IÕm right in the middle of a million things. Why are you coming to me? With all due respect, itÕs like your typist coming to you.Ó ThatÕs a direct quote IÕll never forget. 
Terrible. 
Terrible! And my God, this guy loved my work. Imagine if he didnÕt like my work! 
Do you think he would have said that to a male writer? 
No. Great insight! ThatÕs so interesting; I never thought of that. I just thought, ÒThe guyÕs a sleazebag.Ó But one of the things that made Mary Tyler Moore so wonderful was that pecking order didnÕt matter. It was just whoever came up with the best line, the best story point. If it was a writer who was just doing one episode or if it was Jim or Allan or Ed. WeinbergerÑnobody cared. It was just whoeverÕs brain came up with it. 
YouÕve been credited with substantially shaping some of the characters on that show. Do you feel that you did? 
Well, Jim and Allan told me thereÕs a lot of me in Rhoda. And I wrote a script that aired right after the pilot, and other people mentioned something which I hadnÕt really realized: that I had softened Rhoda. That she initially had more of a harsh edge to her, and I sort of rounded her out a little. Because thatÕs how I saw her. 
Was it Valerie HarperÕs idea for Rhoda to call Mary ÒMairÓ? 
I actually wrote ÒMairÓ the first time. I wanted to kind of deformalize her name, make her more casual. 
Did Mary and RhodaÕs friendship mirror any of your friendships? 
Yes. The idea that women can count on each other. I have three sisters; maybe thatÕs a part of it. But you always put in your imagination, your own Òwhat if ÕsÓÑwhich is what you live with twenty-four hours a day. IÕm always observing situations and wondering, ÒWhat if ?Ó I donÕt think I could write a black person from a ghetto or a refugee, people where so much was different from their mo.ment of birth. How could I know anything about their inner life? A writer can always write generically, but it shows. 
You created the character of Georgette. IÕm wondering how you go about creating a brand-new character. 
LetÕs see, how to explain it. Sometimes as IÕm trying to create a character, a line or an attitude occurs to me, and I donÕt know where it came from, but it starts to make me laugh. Like IÕll actually laugh, just a little sound. I created a character called Sparkie for a Mary Tyler Moore episode. And she said, ÒMy real name is Frances, but they call me Sparkie because they say I just sparkle and twinkle.Ó And she started really amusing me. And she stayed in the scene longer than I expected her to. 
In the case of Georgette, we knew we wanted someone who worked with Rhoda. So I thought, ÒWell, Mary is the straight one, Rhoda is the side-kick funny one, Phyllis is the self-involved one.Ó So I looked for someone different, a brand-new colorÑand I came up with ÒAh, how about a spacy one, a ditsy one?Ó So I was thrilled when Georgette popped up. 
ItÕs hideous when youÕre trying to create a new character, and theyÕre just not really very interesting. And youÕre trying to pull it out: ÒBe interesting!Ó And they go, ÒNope! Not me. Sorry.Ó ItÕs just so horrible; itÕs the worst feeling in the world. And you try over and over, and you just hope somebody more interesting comes into your brain. Please! 
Can you articulate your process? 
Some writers say it helps to write out the background of the person. What college did they go to, what their parents were like, et cetera. For me, thatÕs a head trip that only takes me someplace intellectually; it doesnÕt bring me any closer to who they are. OK, she graduated from Berkeley, and her parents are divorcedÑbut itÕs only when I get somebodyÕs voiceÑthen I know them. Then 

Georgia Engel, Valerie Harper, Mary Tyler Moore. CBS / Photofest © CBS 
I can start to fill in the rest. ThereÕs no foolproof way to get the voice, but if I can get maybe three or four lines that sound like herÑthen for the rest of the script, I donÕt have to worry; I get her. I know her. I love her. I donÕt know how to write anybody without loving them. And so once I get her voice, which is this magical thing that you canÕt run afterÑit has to pop in your headÑthen IÕm home free for her. 
Did Mary Tyler Moore have a writersÕ room? 
It was very different from what it is now. We would have very, very small gath.erings, where you would work out the story with one or two other people. You never left until you had an attitude towards each scene. Then youÕd go home and write up the story by yourself. YouÕd get that approved and then write the first draft. YouÕd meet on your first draftÑagain it would be just two people, three people. YouÕd write the second draft, and then you were invited to the table read. After that, youÕd all go back to the office, and everybody would throw in lines. So, no, there wasnÕt a writersÕ room as itÕs known now. It was much more personal. 
Do you have any favorite anecdotes from working on that show? 
I wrote a script I was really fond of, in which Phyllis wanted Mary to teach Bess about the facts of life. And when we were talking about the script, Allan came up with a line for Rhoda. It was something like, ÒI donÕt want Bess to get all confused and think she has to swim up the Columbia River!Ó And after we filmed that episode, everybody came over to me and said it was a really wonder.ful showÑbut what they loved most was that line about the Columbia River. And with reddened face, I would say, ÒI loved that line, too, but actually it was Allan BurnsÕs.Ó This went on a couple of times, and Allan was standing near me, and he came over. He said, ÒTreve, you know, you donÕt have to say it was my line. All they care about is that it was your script and that line made them laugh, and they want to tell you about it. But it embarrasses them because then they feel maybe theyÕve stepped on your toes.Ó And I thought, ÒWhere else are you going to find a writer who doesnÕt want to take credit for what was the best line in my show?Ó [Laughs] ThatÕs how wonderful Allan is. 
IÕd like to touch on the showÕs cultural context, as it coincided with the womenÕs movement. Did you do any research on that subject? 
No. I was living it. And Valerie was a big influence because she was so beauti.fully wise about it. And youÕre right in the middle of it, and you have what Ms. magazine calls the ÒahaÓ moment of recognizing, ÒOh my God, not only am I feeling this, but other women are feeling this, too.Ó And you couldnÕt help but bring it to the show. 
Have women told you personally their lives were changed in that they were either inspired by your career or by the Mary Richards character? 
Both. A surprising number of women have come up to me and said they had watched the show as young girls and had realized for the first time that a girl could become a comedy writer. Sometimes I get almost moved to tears when they tell me that. Also, women have told me how much it meant to them that it was the first show where a woman really cared about her careerÑthat it wasnÕt just something to do until the right man came along. 
I understand you left the show to go to Europe. Why was that? 
Because I realized that, every time I went to Europe, I came back a little changed. I was seeing different ways of life, different attitudes, different types of relationships, different everything. And I felt that I didnÕt want to be a writer who was getting into a rut of only really knowing New York and LA. IÕd been giving out and giving out creatively since I was a little girl at the piano, and I wanted to start taking in so I wouldnÕt become a writer who writes the same thing over and over because thatÕs all they know. And so I left, and I ended up living in Paris and Copenhagen and Amsterdam for two and a half years, and it was soul enriching. 
LetÕs talk more about creativity. YouÕve been quite open about your use of marijuana in the sixties and seventies. Did it help? 
A lot of writers I knew in the sixties and seventies smoked grass. It was part of what was happening in America and western Europe, and I loved it. Creatively, it helped my mind explore different areas it might not have explored otherwise. ThatÕs an awfully formal way to put it; let me explain a different way. Whatever the chemicals are when you smoke grass, they would heighten different parts of your brain, and ideas would flow in and out. I would love getting stoned with other writers. WeÕd laugh a lot, and some stupid things weÕd say seemed funnyÑ but then again, so did some brilliant things. Defenses would be eliminated, and youÕd all feel part of a wonderful group and then laugh and talk some more. Damn, IÕm still not getting it right. Maybe I have to be stoned to explain it! 
Does anything else help you get in the mood, like cigarettes or coffee? 
Not really. I used to smoke, but I quit. When youÕre smoking and writing, smoking and writing, you think your talent is in the cigaretteÑbut in fact, I found that it helped to not be doing anything else when I was writing. I have certain rules. One rule is that I donÕt read e-mail, at all, ever, before I start to write because then, ÒOh no! Now I have to e-mail this person back.Ó Your brain is just going around too much. Another rule is not to eat while IÕm writing because sometimes, if you feel the urge to eat when youÕre writing, what youÕre really feeling is a frustration. ItÕs very frustrating to write, so then you go and get something to eat. But itÕs not really that you want food; itÕs that you want to end the frustration. ItÕs really very complex. You have to acknowledge that youÕre frustrated because youÕre writing and that only writing well will resolve the frustration, nothing else. 
A writer friend told me that she masturbated in the middle of writing. And we decided she absolutely shouldnÕt masturbate because then sheÕs relieving all the tension that really should go into the writing. ThatÕs how it should beÑonly writing well can cure it. Because masturbating or foodÑor food while mastur.bating, if youÕre athletically inclinedÑisnÕt the point. The point isnÕt relieving your frustration; the point is to get your satisfaction from writing whatever youÕre writing. 
Can you describe how you feel when youÕre writing most creatively, when youÕre in the zone? 
ItÕs like being in a trance. And IÕm so excited, I canÕt wait to get to the next line. When IÕm in a trance, it means generally that I love everything that IÕm writ.ing. I canÕt imagine where it came from; I feel like IÕm cheating almost because itÕs just coming so easily. I feel I can do no wrong, and IÕm usually laughing as I do it. And it keeps going and goingÑuntil it stops. And when it stops, you say, ÒOh, no, come backÑIÕll do anything!Ó 
How do you get yourself into that state or get inspired if youÕre not? 
If IÕm not inspired, if I absolutely canÕt hear the characterÕs voice, if IÕm go.ing crazy from wanting to get a voiceÑI start talking into a tape recorder, sort of stream-of-consciousness as the characters. And sometimes IÕll get the voice, and thatÕll be great, but sometimes the voice I get wonÕt interest me, so IÕll say, ÒI had no idea you were so boring! So letÕs look for a better voice, thank you.Ó That will work more times than not because itÕs fooling your mind; your mind is saying, ÒAll IÕm doing is talking. I mean, I talk all the time, right? IÕm not doing anything hard like writing, God forbid.Ó So your mind can feel freer because youÕre not under the gun; you donÕt have to turn out something right there. 
And IÕll go and go until I feel IÕve gotten something, and sometimes I donÕt get something, so then IÕll try it again. And usually IÕll get something. I shouldnÕt say that because the god of inspiration will hear me right now and say, ÒOh re.ally? IÕll tell you when youÕre getting something, young lady!Ó 
IÕll put it this way: You hear me, god of inspiration? If in fact IÕm lucky enough to get something of the voice, and if I find it funny, IÕll put everything aside until the next day or sometimes two days later. I want to totally forget what I said, and I want to hear it like a stranger. And usually, by two days, I wonÕt remember anything. So IÕll listen to it then, and if I find myself laughing, IÕll say, ÒOh thank God, thank God.Ó 
And if not? 
IÕll say, ÒOh shit, oh shit.Ó If it wasnÕt the person, then IÕll try again, or IÕll go to another part of the script that seems less daunting. I think everybody gets incredi.bly nervous writing the first scene because in your head the message is pounding, pounding, ÒThis is the first thing ÔtheyÕ are going to see! TheyÕre gonna decide right now if they like it! It better be good!Ó And you get so nervous and you say, ÒWhat I think IÕll do now is just tiptoe gently over to the third scene. The third scene doesnÕt have to be so damn goodÑright, third scene?Ó And the third scene says, ÒRight. I donÕt have to be that good.Ó And so you relax a little bit. 
Leaving talent out of the equation, I think 99 percent of writing, for me, is the confidence that I can do it. And if IÕm starting something and I get a few lines that I like, then I have confidence to go on. If I donÕt get a good line, I feel itÕll never happen. But then I think, ÒYouÕve said this a million times before, and it is gonna happen.Ó And of course I say, ÒHa! But not this time!Ó 
One thing I do if IÕm not writing well is to take out an example of a first stab at something I wrote that I ultimately ended up liking. And I say to my unconscious, ÒYou see how awful this was when it started? So even though what IÕm writing is awful now, that doesnÕt mean itÕs not gonna end up being good. Right?Ó ItÕs a source of inspiration, a reminder that you start from aw.fulness. I have this ritual: When IÕm starting to write something brand new, I type at the top of the page, ÒThis is crap. Total crap. ThatÕs all itÕs expected to be.Ó It seems to help. 
Is writing with a partner less of a struggle? How would you compare col.laboration to working alone? 
Psychologically, itÕs kind of comforting to write with someone. It does away with a lot of anxiety, knowing someoneÕs there to energize you when you need it and vice versa. 
On an emotional level, though, nothing compares with writing alone. Given the choice of a good script I wrote alone and a good script I wrote with somebodyÑthe satisfaction is just so much greater when I know itÕs all mine. All mine! 
Of course the advantage of a collaboration is sometimes your partner is better at something than you are in some way. Or there will be times when theyÕll say a line thatÕs really good, and then you pile on a line on top of that, and they pile on a line on top of thatÑand youÕre both laughing, and you feel so wonderful to.gether. ItÕs like sex without the sex. Also, you practically never get writerÕs block when you have a partner. WriterÕs block hits you when youÕre just you, praying for inspiration to enter your empty, gray, dull mind. 
When I was writing The Monkees, I would get sick every day before writing. I mean, really physically sick. In my early twenties, I was hospitalized for incipient ulcers, which obviously came from tension and anxiety and fear. 
And has that gotten better? The fear? 
Yes. IÕve seen enough self-proof that IÕm capable of writing things that I really like. Plus, 723 years in therapy has been a little bit of a help. And luckily, thereÕs medications now. [Laughs] 
Really, itÕs the fear of not doing your best and knowing that your best is inside you, and it exists, but youÕre not finding it. When I was first starting out, I read a magazine article about George S. Kaufman that said, every time he finishes something and he thinks itÕs really great, he says, ÒWell, whatÕs the good of that? IÕll never do it again.Ó And that was so inspiring to me! That it was George S. Kaufman who would say that! I used to carry that article in my wallet until it got yellowed edges because, when you admire somebodyÕs work, you always think it just springs out perfectÑand there was Kaufman with his fears. 
I get the sense youÕre very hard on yourself. You lack the faith. 
No, see, itÕs not that I lack the faith. I just donÕt know if the abilityÕs going to be there that minute. ThatÕs the frustrating thingÑI have a confidence in my talent, but I canÕt just grab it at will. I canÕt say that at exactly one oÕclock in the afternoon IÕm gonna write something thatÕs gonna be good. ItÕs not at my command. You wait for it to come, or you try for it to come, and if it doesnÕtÑitÕs torture. 
But IÕm absolutely delirious when I write something I like. You fall in love with your characters and you want them to come to life. Occasionally I think maybe I should write fiction or something, but I say, ÒNo because then the char.acters stay on paper.Ó I just love seeing them alive. You want to see what actually sprang from your mind. And you want to be around them. 
7 

KEN ESTIN 
N
o one can accuse Ken Estin of being heartless. In fact, the Taxi staff used to tease him for handing in scripts that reflected exceptional poignancy. He actually earned a reputation for writing episodes Òwith heart.Ó Remember the scene in Taxi when Reverend Jim stuns with his brilliant piano playing? Or in Cheers, when the coach tells his daughter how beautiful she is? Ken EstinÕs writing brings chills (the good kind). 
His rsum lists him as writer and producer of Taxi, Cheers, and Almost Perfect and cocreator and producer of The Tortellis and The Tracey Ullman Show. Ken also tells an interesting story about his involvement in the origin of The Simpsons. 
Ken, letÕs start with how you became a writer. 
I was writing short stories in college, and a teacher told me thereÕs no market for short stories or novellas, but if I was looking for short-form storytelling, I should consider writing television. So when I graduated, I wrote a spec Bob Newhart and sent it to Glen and Les Charles at The Bob Newhart Show. It turned out it was the end of the showÕs last season, and I found out later that they loved the script, and they kept it and waited until they had another show. I always wondered why they didnÕt call and tell meÑit wouldÕve been a nice thing to do! About a year and a half later, they did Taxi, and they called and asked if I was interested in pitching for it. 
IÕd only written one spec script, and I didnÕt realize at the time how unusual that was. I teach writing, and I tell students you have to write many spec scripts; even Glen and Les Charles wrote a dozen or more before anyone invited them in to pitch. 
Glen and Les actually didnÕt like what I pitched, but they liked the Bob Newhart script so much they wanted to give me a chance. So they pitched an idea of theirs to me and asked if I wanted to write it. I did, and it became ÒThe Reluctant FighterÓ [S2E15]. I wrote three more scripts for them freelance, and then they hired me on staff. I was on the Taxi staff my first year in the busi.ness, and by the third year, I was running the show; I took over for Glen and Les when they left to do Cheers. I always tell my students that, besides talent, you have to have luck to make it in show business. I was in the right place at the right time when Glen and Les went on to do Cheers, so I was able to take over for them. 
What do you feel you learned from Glen and Les Charles? 
ÒThe Reluctant FighterÓ won the Writers Guild Award, so obviously I didnÕt need anybody to teach me how to be funny or how to tell a story. My sense of humor was the same when I started on Taxi as when I left, so it wasnÕt that they taught me comedy. 
But in the beginning, I was flying by the seat of my pants. I didnÕt really understand how to lay out a half-hour in a logical, systematic way. IÕd just write as the thoughts came to me and feel my way through, based on my own experi.ences. But Glen and Les taught me basic structural stuff, like how to think in terms of a plot and how to break it up into scenes within the context of a story line. If you canÕt think of four scenes, you donÕt have enough to tell in twenty-two minutes. If you have to tell it in eight to ten scenes, you wonÕt be able to do it because thatÕs too many. So, you think all that through in advance. 
When I first started, IÕd write out the script from beginning to end, and then IÕd go back and rewrite it, and then IÕd go back and rewrite that. Then IÕd put it away for a while to get some distance from it, and then IÕd go back and keep rewriting it again and again until I found all the pieces. And the main thing Glen and Les taught me was how to do it quickly and efficiently, so that I could be part of a group that turns out twenty-two to twenty-six quality episodes a season. 
The other thing they taught me was the amount of laughter thatÕs required in a show like a Taxi or a Cheers. My first episodes didnÕt have enough jokes, and the half-hour format required a certain number of laughs per page. And you had to have the biggest or one of the biggest jokes at the act break in the middle before the main commercials. They also taught me how to write a tag at the end of the show. So, they give you all these limitations, and then within these limita.tions, you try to create something really special. 

Clockwise from left: Andy Kaufman, Carol Kane, Danny DeVito, Judd Hirsch, Christopher Lloyd, Marilu Henner, and Tony Danza. NBC / Photofest © NBC 
IÕd like to ask about some of the Taxi characters. How did you see Louie DePalma? 
When I started on the show, I was told he was pure evil. They said donÕt write like he has a spark of goodness here and there. But they werenÕt able to do that for an entire five years, and as the series progressed, he did have humanÑand even kindÑmoments, or softer moments. In fact, I think, well into the second year, we were already having Louie occasionally do something that had a touch of decency. 
Was that a conscious decision on the writersÕ part to show that he had some goodness? 
I think the writers just realized it was impossible to make somebody so one-dimensional. And I think, when they were proposing the series, they were trying to make things seem extreme since, when you sell a series, youÕre looking for a hook. And people laughed at the idea of Louie being pure evil. But when we got around to executing it, we realized that, if we kept him that way constantly, we wouldnÕt develop the character well enough, and weÕd lose a lot of good opportunities. 
The original script had an older Irish guy with an accent running the cage as a dispatcher. And then Danny DeVito walked into the audition as Louie De-Palma with the script in his hand, and he threw it on the floor and said, ÒWho wrote this garbage?Ó or something to that effect. ÒThis is crap. But letÕs just start.Ó He was Louie from the beginning, and he just knocked them out. What I liked about him was that he was so mean and powerfulÑand little. The first time he walked out of that cage and we saw that he was only five feet tall was a great moment because he was a tough guy whoÕd already been kicking everyoneÕs ass. 
What about Alex? Did you see him as a tragic character? 
ThatÕs interesting. I would say no because, for whatever reason, he was con.tentÑor at least, he convinced himself that he was content to be a cabdriver and that he had no ambition. But again, we had moments where we just had to play that maybe he did have some ambition. We did an episode where he was willing to be a gofer for a chance to be in theater because he loved it so much. And one time, we showed that he had a gambling addiction. His relationship with his daughter and with his father were tragic relationships, and he had no family life. So, there were times when he did seem pathetic. But the original concept was that he wasnÕt tragic at all; he was content to be what he was. 
What was Reverend JimÕs backstory? 
He was an accident. In the first episode he appeared in, they needed Latka to marry a prostitute so he could stay in the country, and they were looking for a reverend or an official to perform the service. And Reverend Jim had a credential he got in the sixties when he was a hippie. The showÕs creators wanted a person whoÕd burned himself out on drugs but who still had moments of brilliance be.cause he had once been brilliant. So, although we played him stupid, we could play him with moments of depth. 
He wasnÕt intended to be one of the regulars. When Chris Lloyd came on and did the part, he was so good that they all got together and said, ÒWe gotta write this guy into the series.Ó So right there that week, they offered him a job. And in my mind, the Reverend Jim character was the best of everybody. 
Do you remember any examples of wisdom you gave him? 
I wrote an episode called ÒElegant IggyÓ [S4E20], and this isnÕt necessarily an example of wisdom, but he had an ability to play the piano brilliantly when he had to. He went to a party with Elaine where she needed to impress people. The pianist didnÕt show up, and Jim said heÕd play, and naturally Elaine thought there was no way he could do it. And the audience believed that, too; we gave no indication Jim had that talent. And here he was on this date that was possibly the most special night of his life, and he was gonna blow it by making a fool of himself playing the piano. And instead, he played it beautifully. 
Where did you get the idea for that episodeÕs premise? 
That story was inspired by fantasies IÕd had of being in a moment where you think you canÕt pull something off, yet you do. The only time you can control the present, past, and future is when youÕre writing fiction, and I wanted to put Jim in a situation where heÕd have to do something that he shouldnÕt be able to succeed at, and he doesÑbecause I always wanted to be able to do that myself. And heÕs the only character who could get away with that because he can have his moment of brilliance. 
Andy Kaufman created Latka. How much of Latka was his stage character Foreign Man? 
It was all him. They saw Andy perform and loved the character and made a deal with him to come in and basically be that foreign man on Taxi. We de.cided heÕd be somewhere from Eastern Europe, but weÕd never identify where he came from because we didnÕt want to offend anyone since his country was so backward and ignorant. And Andy invented the language that Latka spoke; that was all his own. WeÕd write in English what we wanted him to say or weÕd write as a stage direction, ÒLatka answers in his gibberish.Ó And heÕd answer in his language. Andy taught Carol Kane the language, and then sheÕd make it up like he did. So when they spoke together, there was a logic to it, but it was something only the two of them knew. Andy never told us, and we never wrote it. ItÕs interesting. 
What would you like to say about the infamous Tony Clifton? 
In the first season, we did an episode called ÒA Full House for ChristmasÓ [S1E13], where LouieÕs brother comes. And Andy said he wanted a friend of his named Tony Clifton to play the part, and he wouldnÕt do it unless they hired the friend. Latka wasnÕt in the episode, and Andy showed up as Tony Clifton. He put on a big fake gut, and he was obnoxious. He was chauvinistic and made rude remarks to women; he was very lewd and crass. I think he actually grabbed MariluÕs butt a couple times. Everyone knew it was Andy, and he wouldnÕt admit to it. So they told Andy they had to fire Tony, and Andy said, ÒYou canÕt fire him. IÕll talk to him.Ó And so they went through this whole charade with Andy for several days. Finally, the guy was so obnoxious and so difficult to work with that they fired him anyhowÑeven though it was AndyÑand they brought in another actor to play the part. 
Any behind-the-scenes Andy stories? 
I remember one time Andy dated a friend of mine. He took her out some.place, and then he staged this thing where one of his friends came at him with a gun and shouted at him threateningly. Andy hurried her out, and they got in the car and drove away. And then they went somewhere else and the guy showed up again, Õcause Andy had told him where they were going to be. And she told me she was scared to death for Andy. When Andy was leaving to go home, she told him, ÒThis guyÕs been following you, heÕs been finding you, I donÕt know what youÕre gonna do. You canÕt go home!Ó And at that point, Andy did admit that it was all just a joke on her. 
How cruel! 
And Andy told me one time that he and a friend saw a young woman hitch.hiking on Sunset, and they drove past her, and he let his friend off. Then he went around the block and came back and picked up the hitchhiker. And then his friend was hitchhiking up ahead, and he picked him up. And it was a similar scenario in which the friend tried to kill Andy; he tried to strangle him and got physical with him and scared the girl to the point where she jumped out of the car while it was still moving. And when Andy told this story, he thought it was hysterical; I remember him laughing and thinking it was really funny that they scared this girl so much. 
Anything you remember about him on the show? 
Andy was very cooperative at work. But one time we couldnÕt find him, and the show was supposed to start, and the shows are very expensiveÑevery min.ute countsÑand we had to wait like a half hour, and people were searching for him. Then he just showed up and said, ÒOh, I was meditating in my car in the parking lot.Ó 
How did BobbyÕs departure affect the show? 
It didnÕt really. It was frightening how easily the show continued without him, even though he was a major character and a major in the original pitch of the show. When he left, it was a simple transition to continue without him. 
So the writers didnÕt consider replacing him with another cabbie? 
No, because weÕd added the Reverend Jim, who hadnÕt been in the show originally, and the feeling was that we had enough characters. They decided they just didnÕt need another mouth to put words into. But itÕs sad to think he was that disposable. 
Do you think the garage was a character in itself ? 
ThatÕs interesting. I never thought of it before as being a character, but yes. I would say it was. I loved the garage. But it was a very somber, morose, melan.choly character, and I donÕt necessarily think thatÕs a good thing to put into a comedy. Personally, I like the combination of dark and light, and I tend to be a little morose myself. But in making a successful sitcom, I think the lightness has to prevail. Incidentally, in Cheers, the bar was a character, too; it was very attrac.tive, very alluring, and very loving. 
How would you characterize the dynamics of TaxiÕs ensemble? 
They had a relationship I envied. When you do a workplace series, itÕs always important that your workplace people feel like a family. I learned that from both Taxi and Cheers. They both felt like a surrogate family. If anyone left, youÕd feel the loss. 
Except for Bobby! 
Yeah, thatÕs the weird part. ThatÕs why I say it was sad because I did feel the loss when Jeff [Conaway] left, but at the same time, we were all relieved because it made the writing easier. 
I was on Cheers when the actor who played Coach died. Originally, they werenÕt going to replace him with a similar character; they wanted to go completely another way. They tried and tried to think of something other than a dumb as.sistant bartender, and they couldnÕt come up with a character that gave them what they thought they needed. Stupid people are very funny when you love them and donÕt feel sorry for them. So they ended up saying, ÒWe gotta do the same charac.ter, but weÕll make him young.Ó 
More about Taxi. Did it have a formula for writing the story? 
Yes. Jim Brooks believed you had to have one strong plot, and we didnÕt call it an A story. The plot had to be strong enough that you could spend the whole twenty-two minutes on that one situation. 
At the same time we were doing Taxi, Barney Miller was on. And that show used to have three stories: one that was about the guys, one that was about crime, and one that was about somebody in a cell. And it was so hard coming up with some of the stories that we wanted to tell; often weÕd pitch a story to Jim Brooks, and there just wasnÕt enough there for a whole twenty-two minutes. WeÕd say, ÒCanÕt we put two or three of these together like they do on Barney Miller? TheyÕre all small, but theyÕre funny.Ó And he would always say no. And IÕm telling you, we had so many good ideas that Jim wouldnÕt let us do because we couldnÕt think of four scenes. It was very frustrating. 
Did the show have a formula for humor? 
It had to be a laugh-out-loud show. If a joke was humorous, it wasnÕt good enough. If a joke made the audience laughÑas in make a noiseÑit could stay in. If the audience just thought it was cleverÑno. 
You wrote ÒAlex Jumps Out of An AirplaneÓ [S2E21]. What inspired that? 
Not that I ever jumped out of an airplane or wanted to, but I used to sit down and make lists of what I thought were interesting stories based on movies and TV IÕd seen and then try to adapt some of those ideas to our characters. ÒFish out of waterÓ is a fairly common device used in comedies, and I remember thinking that Alex was such a mild-mannered person that seeing him exert himself would be fun because we always saw him playing calm and rational. And then I thought, ÒWhatÕs the most outrageous thing I can come up with for someone to do whoÕs kind of a quiet, rational person?ÓÑand thatÕs to jump out of an airplane. 
At the end of the episode, Alex delivers some powerful lines describing his experience. Did you write those? 
I did. And I knew from the beginning thatÕs how IÕd end the script. To me, that was a chance to get inside AlexÕs head and really feel what he felt. It was a moment where I could write a little bit of poetry, which you never get to do in sitcoms. I took advantage of it, and I spent a lot of time on that little piece. I remember sitting there and thinking what motivates somebody to jump out of an airplane and imagining what youÕd feel while youÕre coming down. They didnÕt touch that little speech, and I loved it. 
What did you want that episode to say? 
That we are what we are. We can get excited about things, but after the new.ness wears off, we go back to our old selves. 
What do you remember about writing ÒLouie Goes Too FarÓ [S4E10, writ.ten by Danny Kallis]? 
ThatÕs the one where he has a hole drilled in the womenÕs bathroom, and he watches Elaine change her clothes. I think it was Ed. Weinberger who said we need something to make Louie sympathetic. HeÕs just done something terrible. How can we possibly feel sorry for him in this situation? So they called Danny in; I was in the room when he came in. They asked him what could he recall as the most demeaning thing that ever happened to him because of his size, that maybe could happen to Louie to make the audience have sympathy for him. And Danny told the story of how he actually had to buy his clothes in the husky boysÕ section, and people would stare at him and comment. He said it was humiliating. So that came from Danny. WeÕre all good writers, and maybe if weÕd thought about it long enough, we might have come up with it on our own. But Ed. said letÕs bring Danny in and see if he has something very real. 
Do you have any unusual backstory of an episode? 
I remember when some guy in prison sent Ed. an interesting pitch for a story that Ed. didnÕt want to do, but because he wanted to help this guy out, he gave him an assignment to write the episode. The guy never delivered anything, not even an outline. Sam [Simon] and I wrote the script and gave him a ÒStory byÓ credit. We didnÕt want to cut him out entirely because he was in there for armed robbery, and we were afraid heÕd come shoot us! 
Did you base any premises on your own life? 
No, they were just stories that I made up. We did do one episode inspired by an experience Jim BrooksÕs wife had; he came in one day and said his wife had a really bad haircut, and sheÕd come home crying because sheÕd been intimidated by a very well-known hairstylist. He wanted to write an episode about it, and we wrote a show about Elaine getting a really bad haircut. But we pretty much just tried to invent stories. I hear of people coming into the room and talking about something that happened to them that week that suggests an idea, but except for the haircut one, I donÕt remember anyone ever saying, ÒHereÕs something that happened to me. LetÕs do it.Ó 
And when you make up a story, it just comes to you out of thin air? 
Yeah, sort of. You think about the characters, and you try to imagine them in a situation that would be interesting. And maybe you do think about things that happened in your own life, but then you find a variation. As an example, I had a very old dog, and I used to worry about him dying. And I wrote the episode called ÒAlexÕs Old BuddyÓ [S5E14]. It wasnÕt about my dog dying, but he was getting old, and I remember thinking, ÒWhat if Alex had an old dog, and how would he deal with its death if it died at the end of the episode?Ó The story itself was funny: him getting the dog and his reaction to the dog and Louie not want.ing the dog in the cab and Alex taking the dog with him in the cab. But in the end, the dog does die, and Alex deals with it. And we wanted it to be uplifting, but we couldnÕt think of a funny joke. 
I donÕt see how a dogÕs death could ever be uplifting! 
One of the tricks the dog always did was to play dead. We thought of hav.ing Alex tell the dog to play dead, and then when heÕd say, ÒOK, get upÓÑthe dog wouldnÕt get up. Somebody thought that was funny, but when we actually tried to act it out, it was just very morbid. And then we didnÕt have a joke for the ending. 
So we had the dog die offstage. Alex comes in with the leash, and Louie says, ÒWhereÕs the mutt?Ó and Alex says, ÒHe passed away.Ó So the last scene in a sitcom is Alex sitting there with tears in his eyes, looking at the leash that used to be on his dog. It was really a weird episode. 
Since Jim Brooks demanded the show have big laughs, how do you explain that episode? 
Ed. Weinberger, who loves dogs, was in charge that week because Jim was off doing Terms of Endearment. I donÕt think in a million years Jim would have allowed that story to develop the way it did. 
Some viewers have complained that there were unresolved story lines on Taxi. Were you aware of any? Like Tony was going to adopt a kid? 
The first episode I wrote for Taxi was about Tony trying to inspire a kid in a wheelchair by boxing against a champ who was making a comeback. And Tony [DanzaÕs] son played the part, and everyone loved him and decided he should be in the series. And we actually did a second episode with him where it looked like Tony was going to adopt him, and then we never spoke of it again. They decided they didnÕt like Tony having a son because, story wise, they thought it would affect his ability to date and have relationships. 
Do you remember the unresolved story line where TonyÕs girlfriend accepts his marriage proposal and then disappears? 
Yeah. That was bad. Everyone on the show decided they just didnÕt like that relationship enough to continue with it, and instead of having them break up, they never bothered to bring her back. 
ItÕs funny, but Sam and I once met with Steven Spielberg, and Sam said to him, ÒIn Raiders of the Lost Ark, thereÕs a scene where they come out of one of the pyramid bases, and they go right past an unconscious guy. It was never explained why he was there.Ó And Spielberg said, ÒIn one version they knocked him out to get in, but in a later version, we decided not to bother with that, so we just had them get in without any resistance. But when we were editing it together, the only shot we had of them coming out was with the guy at the exit, and I didnÕt care. YouÕre the first person who ever asked me about that.Ó And this is Steven Spielberg! And thatÕs how Jim Brooks and Stan Daniels and Ed. decided to deal with those things; they said just go past it; people arenÕt gonna care enough to ask or pursue it. 
What about the Taxi writersÕ room? Anything come to mind about your experience there? 
One time we had a scene where Tony was advising another boxer how to get inspired for the last round of a fight he was in. Ed. said we need a funny sound to his advice; like if he could say, ÒDo it for the duck,Ó weÕd get a laugh. And we said, ÒHow are we gonna do that?Ó Well, weÕd been there for hours trying to think of a way to end the scene, when I said, ÒI can do that. I can get there.Ó And David Lloyd said, ÒThen do it.Ó And I just came up with this thing about Tony remembering he had a little duck, and the duck got hit by a car, and he had it in his hands, and he was running and trying to get to a vet to save its life. He was running out of energy, but he kept telling himself, ÒDo it for the duck. Do it for the duck.Ó And they put that in the script, and it worked. 
What I thought was fun about it was that Ed. just said, ÒHereÕs a funny last line,Ó and we had no way to get there. And I said we can get thereÑand in front of everybody, I invented a way to do it. David Lloyd applauded when I did be.cause I was young enough and new enough to the business that I thought you could create anything if you put your mind to it. And the others were maybe more jaded by that point. 
Glen and Les were perfectionists. The hours were long, but when I was running Taxi, my hours were even longer because we knew that, if we turned in a script without a big joke at the end of every scene, Jim Brooks wouldnÕt accept it. Glen and Les wouldnÕt, either. So you could finish at ten oÕclock and still be there Õtil one oÕclock because you had to make bigger jokes. I remember spending hours trying to find one joke for the end of a scene. Sometimes weÕd get tricky; if we couldnÕt find one big enough, weÕd steal one from earlier in the scene and find a way to rewrite it so that the joke came up at the end. Or maybe in the subplot or the runner or the business, there was a funnier jokeÑand weÕd move that around and stick it in at the end. WeÕd do that when we got really worn out! 
Do you remember any jealousy among the writers? 
I remember someone winning an award for their script and other people being angry because they said it had been rewritten by the staff and that person didnÕt deserve the award. And I remember the reverse case, where we rewrote a script and it won an award. Instead of being grateful, the person who won the award said we robbed him of the satisfaction of it being his own script. He was very angry about it. 
You never know. IÕd like to ask about a Cheers episode thatÕs been widely praised for its emotion. What was your inspiration for writing ÒThe CoachÕs DaughterÓ [S1E5]? 
I remember thinking that the coach was really a beautiful person, and it would be wonderful if he in fact saw things differently from everyone elseÑthat what they call being ÒdumbÓ or ÒstupidÓ created a simplicity that was a positive quality. It would be good if he could look at somebody and see what was lovable about them rather than their physical traits. So I wanted to write a story where Coach thought his unattractive daughter was the most beautiful woman in the world. I wanted him to look at her and really believe it. And I didnÕt know if I could get away with that. And Nick Colasanto did it brilliantly; he actually cried, which I did not see coming. He had tears in his eyes when he said, ÒI didnÕt realize how much you look like your mother,Ó and we knew that what made her beautiful to him had nothing to do with her physical appearance. Then she said, ÒI look exactly like her. And Mom wasnÕtÑÓ and you thought she was going to say, ÒMom wasnÕt physically appealing.Ó But she saw his expression and changed it to, ÒMom wasnÕt comfortable with her beauty.Ó That was my favorite scene. 

It was a very emotional episode for me. I didnÕt see it shot because, at the time, I was running Taxi, and we were shooting the episode I wrote about JimÕs father dying. So on two stages, next to each other, episodes I wrote for the two top shows on television were shooting on the same night. 
Can you think of any entertaining behind-the-scenes stories from Cheers? 
What was interesting was that Ted Danson and Shelley Long were so much into their on-screen relationship that sometimes theyÕd lose track of when they were in character and when they werenÕt. For example, Shelley was pregnant in the third season, and it was hidden on camera. And Glen and Les wanted to show the pregnancy, but Shelley and Ted were really upset that it wasnÕt SamÕs baby. They couldnÕt get past the idea that she could have a baby with anyone else, and they said they couldnÕt play it. 
But Glen and Les didnÕt want to make it SamÕs baby because, once Sam had a baby with Diane, you could no longer accept SamÕs philandering. HeÕd have to commit to Diane, or youÕd destroy the Sam Malone character. But they said they couldnÕt do it. So we had to do all sorts of things to keep her from showing her pregnancy; she either wore the apron or she stood behind the bar. One time we had her stuck in the air conditioning vent. 
LetÕs talk about The Tracey Ullman Show. How did that get started? 
Jim Brooks had met Tracey and wanted to do something that showed off all her talents, which were singing, playing many characters, acting, and dancing. Our first thought was we couldnÕt do a sitcom and have excuses for dancing and singing and different characters unless she was doing a show, and we felt that was hokey and people had done that before. So we decided to do a program with vignettes, a variety show of sorts. And we wanted to have bumpers to separate the sketches. And we were thinking of all sorts of weird things for the bumpers. 
We actually wanted to have an animated talking bear or a guy in a bear suit whoÕd say, ÒWhat did you think of that? That was pretty badÑbut we got some.thing better coming up next!Ó We all laughed when we thought about a bear being our emcee. But we finally decided that would get old pretty quickly. And weÕd considered a cartoonist who did a psychiatrist character. Somebody had given me a gift of a cartoon that Matt Groening drew, and I said, ÒI wonder if this guy could just do little short cartoons in between our sketches.Ó But Matt wouldnÕt do it because he was merchandising his Life in Hell characters for a living, and he didnÕt want to give up any of the merchandising to Fox. 
I was running the show with Heide Perlman, Jim Brooks, and Jerry Belson. And I said I thought MattÕs stuff was really funny, and instead of letting him walk 

Cheers 
David Isaacs (Writer/Coproducer): Ken [Levine] and I were there with Glen and Les, who were the guiding force and the creators of Cheers, along with Jim Burrows. In terms of the writing, we were working for them, with them, and being guided by them. They had nurtured that idea since they left Taxi, and they knew exactly what they wanted, and we just followed along. The Charles brothers were steeped in the MTM style, and they had cut their teeth on Taxi as well, so I think they wanted to do a show that had a strong ensemble. 
They originally kicked around an idea of a woman who inherits a hotel and an old ballplayerÑa football player, I think, before it was a baseball playerÑwho owns a piece of the hotel. They always had the idea of that kind of romance, but that slowly evolved into the idea of the bar. And in the pilot, Cliff was not really considered to be a character moving forward. And there was an older woman in a wheelchair who was a regular at the bar; she was a conservative type and liked to have a drink every day. That got cut out of the pilot and didnÕt go any further, even though we had some threads of that in episodes that had already been written. 
The brothers were very good at adapting to what they saw that was strong. They realized very quickly that the money was Sam and Diane: that they should be the sun and the moon, and everything else should somehow orbit around them; that no matter what story you didÑa Carla story or a Norm storyÑsomehow it had to get back to Sam and Diane and the tension between them. 
The ÒNorm entranceÓ was just the idea that he was a regular whom everybody knows. It kind of reflects the theme song: a place where every.body knows your name. Those were very difficult to write because they were stand-alone jokes, you know: ÒWhat are you up to, Norm?Ó ÒMy ideal weight if I were eleven feet tall.Ó Ken wrote that one, by the way. The beer jokes idea was in the pilotÑI mean, Norm loves beer. His ambition is to drink beer all day. It simply came out of the character. 
Phoef Sutton (Writer/Executive Producer): The problem with writing Norm was that he didnÕt want anything from life; he was perfectly happy. So weÕd have to construct a story in which heÕs not able to sit at the corner of a bar and talk, and then he gets back to the corner of the bar and talks. I remember George Wendt saying to me, ÒI donÕt think Norm should get off the bar stool.Ó And at the time, I thought he meant in the scene. But in retrospect, I think he meant ever! He felt that Norm should always be sitting there, just commenting on what goes on around him. 
And the Norm entrance was our bte noire. We didnÕt have one in every show, and we got to the point where we would give anything not to have a Norm entranceÑbecause they got harder and harder to come up with since each one had to top the one before it. And what I didnÕt realize at the time was the thing audiences loved about Norm entrances was everybody yelling, ÒNorm!Ó They didnÕt really pay much attention to the joke that fol.lowed it. But we used to pay a lot of attention to it! I donÕt know at what point they realized they had to do it almost every time, but that must have been a chilling moment. 
People want to know why no one seems to pay for their drinks [on Cheers]. ItÕs just boring to watch people take wallets out and pay for the drink. ItÕs the same way people never say goodbye when they hang up the telephone on a TV show: ItÕs just filler, and itÕs just fluff, and itÕs boring. 
Fans ask me how Cliff would be drinking when heÕs technically still on the job. My feeling was that Cliff was never on the job when he was in the bar; heÕd always gotten off work, and he just hadnÕt changed out of his uni.form. IÕve been asked if we ever talked about making Cliff Ògo postal.Ó We never really thought about it since this was before the clich of the crazy postal guy coming in and shooting everybody had taken hold. Believe me, we would have done jokes about that if it had. 
People want to know if the beer was real: There was never real beer on stage. George Wendt told me, the one time they tried to use real beer on stage, everybody got drunk and couldnÕt do anything! So they used Near Beer, and he said it was the worst thing you could possibly taste. So he just nursed the beers along. 
On Cheers, we actually wrote in two different rooms. Most of our work was done downstairs in the main office. We didnÕt have a table; everybody sat on the sofas and just talked. It was kind of like a party. Nowadays, everyone in a room sits around a table and writesÑand that really feels like work to me! Cheers was fun. Now, sometimes it was a bad party, where all the conversation had died and everybody was just staring at everybody else, trying to figure out what came nextÑbut it was basically like a party. 
In the beginning of the season, weÕd break the stories in GlenÕs office, which was upstairs. And that room was laid out in pretty much the same way except it was very, very dark. It was like an old-fashioned pool room or gentlemenÕs club, with dark green wallpaper and dark shades, and youÕd be struggling to stay awake. And Glen and Les would smoke cigars and drink scotch. They knew how to live! My most vivid memory from the room is seeing David Angell hang himself by the cord of the blinds. He always used to make a noose out of it and hang himself when things were going slowly. That was an ongoing joke; itÕs rather bleak humor. 
because he wonÕt do his Life in Hell, maybe heÕs done some other characters we could use. HeÕs a cartoonist, after all. And I know people sometimes tell this story differently, but I specifically remember saying that to one of the executive producers, Richard Sakai, and Richard going to Matt and asking him if he had any other characters that he would share his merchandising from. And Matt cre.ated the Simpsons instantly, and it became the bumpers for the show. 
So are we saying the whole Simpsons series owes its existence to you? 
I say that! [Laughs] But nobody else would say that. TheyÕd say I remember it differently. IÕm positive I told Richard to see if Matt had other characters. 
Ken, IÕd like more insight into your creative process. Do you use any specific brainstorming techniques? 
Often the way I find the jokes and the story is by writing stream-of-con.sciousness. Sometimes IÕll sit, and I just donÕt feel funny. I canÕt think of where the sceneÕs going; I feel like IÕm at a dead end. IÕll start writing the scene anyhow and forcing myself. IÕll have the character talk, then the next character talks, and somebody answers him, and somebody answers that. I just make myself do it. 
It rarely comes as inspiration. For me itÕs labor; itÕs forcing myself to put the words on the page. Then if I put enough words on the page, I always find that I can hone it down to some really good stuff and then work from that. 
Do you follow any rules of thumb for joke construction? 
IÕve read rules, but I never worked that way, and IÕve never known anyone who did. We all just go by what our gut tells us. I donÕt think you can do it by mechanical means. You have to do it by instinct and experience and intuitionÑ and all those kinds of vague feelings you have as a human being. When I write a scene, I have to put myself in the situation. And although I wonÕt laugh out loud, I can feel the difference between something thatÕs funny and something that doesnÕt sound quite right. The formulas donÕt really work because comedy is based so much on rhythms. Sometimes just the right word is funny, and youÕre not sure why. 
So to answer your question, IÕm sure there are people who can come up with a very good explanation of the structure of a joke, but I canÕt work that way. ItÕs visceral for me. 
What do you feel is the hardest thing about writing comedy? 
Being funny while telling a story because very often the laughs can interrupt the storytelling. Sometimes the jokes came naturally; sometimes they fit just right. But sometimes you really had to manufacture a joke to fit the story you were telling. The hardest thing for me were the jokes; putting heart into the show was the easiest. 
And what do you teach your students about the importance of heart in a story? 
I tell people that, if the show has heart, if it has a soul, if it has those human elements that are so precious to usÑitÕll be a better episode. I always thought about finding a really human moment, a really touching moment. 
But you canÕt do it all the time because then people start predicting it. It starts getting old if someone does something sweet in every episode. We used to do about twenty-five episodes a season, and most of them would just be good, funny stories, and then thereÕd be maybe three that had heart. 
It sounds like those were all yours! 
The truth is they used to kid me about that because IÕd always come in with stories that had moving moments. When I was writing ÒCoachÕs DaughterÓ and ÒElegant Iggy,Ó I thought, ÒGee, I wish I could write movies because in movies you could just go for the heart all the time.Ó 
A few last questions. YouÕve said Jim Brooks was your mentor. What do you feel you learned from him? 
From Jim, I learned that no matter how brilliant you are, you can write some things that donÕt work. And that was encouraging to me. One day we were all struggling with a scene for Taxi, and then Jim sat down and wrote it without any help. He just kind of dictated it, and boom, there was the scene. And everyone in the room loved it and said, ÒIsnÕt this guy amazing! We sat here working on it for hours, and within twenty minutes, he wrote the whole scene.Ó Then we did it on stage, and the audience didnÕt laugh at it; it was a complete flop. And IÕve seen so many brilliant things from Jim Brooks that it just reminded me why you have to be a little bit humble. 
How would you characterize his comic sensibility? 
Jim adds depth. He brings a certain profoundness to comedies because he thinks beyond the sitcom moment, beyond the funny moment or the light dra.matic moment. Also, he looks for the unusual. If two people pitched the same joke in a room, he wouldnÕt do it. Even though everything we do in sitcom is really a variation of something thatÕs been done already, Jim always wanted it to feel unique in that moment, and he was good at that. 
Who are some of the funniest writers youÕve worked with? 
Jerry Belson, David Lloyd, and Glen Charles. If you had any of them in the room, you knew youÕd get the joke, and you knew youÕd get it pretty fast. Jerry Belson used to complain a lot. He was often sick, and heÕd moan and actually had pains. He always seemed a little bit sad and depressed, but heÕd come up with funny, clever jokes and pitch them almost deadpan. He wanted his tomb.stone to say, ÒI did it their way.Ó 
Glen Charles was kind of an enigma. He wouldnÕt say a whole lot; heÕd just do his funny jokes. He was kind of a craftsman; he knew what he needed to do, and he did it. David Lloyd was kind of a crazy man who always had energy in his jokes and his manner. Out of context this might not be that funny, but he once pitched a line that stuck in my mind because itÕs so weird: One character asked another to do something and he said, ÒIÕd rather be a janitor at the homeless shelterÑthe night the custard went bad!Ó 
8 

MATT WILLIAMS 
A
s creator and former executive producer of the 1980s sitcom Roseanne, Matt Williams is a survivor of the infamous ÒMatt/RoseanneÓ battles. In this chapter, he talks candidly about the experience and how he laid the founda.tion for a landmark series that ran nine seasons. 
He formed Wind Dancer Production Group in 1989 and, with partners Carmen Finestra and David McFadzean, created and produced such shows as Thunder Alley, Buddies, and Home Improvement. The latter ran for eight seasons, becoming a Òglobal phenomenonÓ as the number 1 show in several countries and one of the biggest syndication shows worldwide. 
In this chapter, Matt articulates in detail his creative process, including his periodic stays in Òthe tunnel.Ó He discusses what he feels are the crucial ele.ments of a successful showÑa topic heÕs obviously well qualified to speak on. 
Your first job was story editor on The Cosby Show. HowÕd you break into TV? 
I was trained as an actor and a director. And after college, I went to New York to direct, but because I couldnÕt afford residuals for a play, I started writing so IÕd have something to direct. I wrote one full-length play that was eventually published and played at the Kennedy Center and then a bunch of one-acts. And those one-acts are how The Cosby Show discovered me because they all hap.pened to be funny. 
What contribution to The Cosby Show are you most proud of ? 
I think I brought a sense of character and humanity to it. IÕm not a joke writer; IÕm not a ba-da-bingÑyou know, two nuns and a penguin walk into a barÑIÕm not that kind of writer. I just canÕt do that. And so what I do is createÑI hopeÑ characters that have extreme points of view or fun attitudes. And I have a strong sense of story. Bill Cosby didnÕt care about story. So I think what I contributed to his show was more a story sense because of my training as an actor and a di.rector. In other words, whatÕs the thrust of the episode, what are the characters doing, what are they playingÑas opposed to what are they saying. I would say story was probably my biggest contribution. 
IÕd like to get a picture of your involvement in the creation of the Roseanne show. 
I was ready to leave The Cosby Show even though it was a huge hit because I knew, if I continued on the show, I wouldnÕt get stronger as a writer. As I said, I think my strength there was story: knowing how to structure an episode. When I left the show, I developed a better sense of creating multidimensional charac.ters with distinctive points of view and a specific drive, so the characters were creating the story. 
I also left because I knew I wanted to create my own show. And Marcy [Carsey] and Tom [Werner] said, ÒWhat ideas do you have?Ó I went in and pitched I think three ideas for a television series, one of them being three women working in an assembly line in a blue-collar town; one was married with kids, one was divorced with a kid, and one was single. This was inspired by my wifeÕs best friend, who worked in the factories up in Detroit. I said, ÒI really want to explore these women who are working class, who go to the Dunkin Donuts and kind of serve as each otherÕs psychiatrist or babysitter and support each other. These are women who play softball on the weekend, and then stay up at night and do homework.Ó And I actually went and interviewed all these women in the Midwest who worked in factories; some of them married, most of them single moms, and some single without children. And when I pitched that, Tom and Marcy came back and said, ÒWhat about Roseanne Barr? Have you seen her?Ó I didnÕt have any idea who she was! So that idea melded with Roseanne, and I said, ÒWell, sheÕs acerbic; her voice is a little irritating.Ó [Laughs] 
A little? 
But sheÕs definitely funny. And I said she could be the married woman of the three. So that was the original impulse for the seriesÑto do a realistic working-class show about these three women. And then through the casting process, we found John Goodman. 
Remember Moonlighting, the Bruce Willis show years ago? I had seen an episode of it, and I thought the guest star in that episode would be great as RoseanneÕs husband. I called Tom and Marcy and said, ÒPlease find out the name of this actor. This guy is RoseanneÕs husband.Ó And Caryn Mandabach, who was working with Carsey-Werner at the time said, ÒNo, no. I found the guy. HeÕs down in La Jolla, heÕs doing Shakespeare, and heÕs perfect for the role.Ó 
I can tell where this is going. 
And of course, we were both talking about John Goodman. And later, when I had written a couple of audition scenes, Roseanne was sitting in a rehearsal room, and John walked in. She looked up at him, and he looked at her; they didnÕt say a word. And then she says, ÒWhat the fuck are you lookinÕ at?Ó He says, ÒMove your fat assÓÑand GodÕs truthÑsat down next to her and started doing the scenes. And she just lit up and started laughing at him, and they started teasing. Tom and Marcy and I looked at each other and went, ÒHoly shit. You canÕt write this kind of chemistry.Ó It was there. So that became the center of the show, and then Jackie and Crystal and the other women became secondary to that primary relationship. 
What did Jackie, the sister, add? 
Jackie was not originally conceived as a sister but as a best friend. And then, because of her own sister, Roseanne wanted a sister. The sheer weight and 

Roseanne Barr, Sara Gilbert, Michael Fishman, Alicia Goranson, and John Goodman.

volume of Laurie Metcalf Õs talent changed everything; sheÕs one of our great ac.tresses. That role was somewhat underwritten, and she brought it to life. I think she brought a sense of verisimilitude and authenticity to it; she didnÕt seem like a Hollywood actress playing at being a factory worker. She embodied the world we were trying to create. 
And the three kids. Did Roseanne have three kids in real life? 
I canÕt remember if she had three or four. But we always knew we wanted two girls and a boy. Dramaturgically, I made the two girls older because they would Òdo battleÓ with their mom, butt heads on issues. The boy was conceived as the youngest so he would be DanÕs Òlittle manÓÑthe baby of the family, the target of the girlsÕ teasing. 
Did you have any input in designing elements of the ConnersÕ home, like the afghan on the couch? 
Completely. That afghan comes from my grandmotherÕs house. I flew our production designer to Evansville to go to my grandmaÕs house and take pic.tures. And the louvered windows above the kitchen sink, the couch, the caftan on the couchÑall of that was very carefully designed. And since I very much wanted a sense of life in the house, we splayed the sets about twenty degrees, so if RoseanneÕs standing at the kitchen stove cooking, the camera could shoot over her, through that archway, all the way to the living room, to the front door. The whole house was designed on an angle and opened up, so if you were sitting on the couch and you were shooting, you could see someone in the background on the phone in the kitchen.
