So with that design, we would consciously write kids in the living room wrestling on the floor while other characters were talking in the other room. ThereÕs a sense that the house feels lived in. 
What about the opening scene? How much of that was your idea? 
IÕll tell you the most important lesson I ever learned on The Cosby Show. On my third or fourth week on the job, I was standing next to Bill, and we were watching a scene with Rudy and Theo. And I felt this elbow in my ribs. And I turned, and it was Mr. Bill Cosby. He leaned over and he goes, ÒHey man, if you were home right now, wouldnÕt you want to be a part of this family?Ó And I went ÒDing.Ó I thought, ÒBoy, thatÕs his genius. ThatÕs what he understands about television.Ó With movies, you go into outer space or to the center of the earth or to the Wild West, right? With television, the viewers get to be a part of their charactersÕ lives; they invite you into their house. So in creating the Roseanne opening, I said I want the family around the table and I want the camera to rotate around the table and, on an unconscious level, just kind of invite the audience to pull up a chair and join the family. Because where I grew up in the Midwest, thatÕs what you did. The adults would put a pot of coffee in the middle of the table and sit there and tell stories, and as a little kid, IÕd sit under the table and listen to the stories. And so that all fed into that. 
I worked with the director on that opening breakfast scene to have Darlene running under the phone and packing up for school. That was all intentional because we wanted to create this explosion of activity that most families have every morning. Draw from real life. Cosby would always say, ÒDonÕt start with whatÕs funny; start with whatÕs real. And then weÕll make it funny.Ó 
How much did Roseanne contribute to the pilot? 
I met with her, and I met with her husband at the time, Bill Pentland. I asked her everything about her background; I do this with every person I work with. Then I took all these ideas, and Tom and Marcy pitched a lot of ideas, and then I went off and did what every writer does: I presented an outline to Tom and Marcy; they gave me notes, and then I wrote a draft. 
With the exception of one scene that we ended up replacing, the story of the pilot stayed exactly the same throughout the whole process. After they read the first draft and before Roseanne had seen it, Tom and Marcy said to me, ÒWe love the characters. We think itÕs funny. But we donÕt think the show has a point of view thatÕs specific enough. You have created, basically, a harried housewife.Ó 
And that was interesting because the premise statement I was using as I wrote the pilot was, ÒA woman has to be all things to all people.Ó She has to be cook, shrink, chauffeur, Madonna, whore, psychiatrist, you know? So with their notes, I went back and really looked at RoseanneÕs stand-up and realized it had to be, ÒI should be goddess of the universe because I have all the answers!Ó So her domestic goddess. It truly was a collaboration; without RoseanneÕs extreme feminist point of view, this series would have been a nice series. It would have been funny. But it wouldnÕt have been what it is. As I told her, I didnÕt create domestic goddess; I donÕt take any credit for that. But the world around herÑ the characters, the factory, her husband, and the kids, all of thatÑthat had to be given structure, thematic unity, a point of viewÑknowing what we wanted the series to doÑnot just what it was about. Those are all the underpinnings that go into creating a successful show. 
And here was the key to this showÑand I say this honestly and openly. First off, donÕt condescend to your audience; donÕt make fun of working-class people. These are the people I grew up with. This is my grandmaÕs home. This is every.one in my family, OK? So that was the foundation. 
Secondly, we knew RoseanneÕs character was going to irritate a lot of people. But if Dan is in love with her, and we see her through his eyes, then weÕre gonna love her. So when you look at the early episodes, Dan is a romantic. He thinks sheÕs the sexiest thing in the world. He adores her. And literally, on a very subtle level, the audience got to see this couple through his eyes, and I think they fell in love with them as a couple. 
Do you have any favorite scenes with the two of them? 
One of my favorite scenes in the pilot is where the kids finally get off to school and itÕs just Roseanne and Dan sitting at the kitchen table. Dramaturgically, nothing major happens there, but it completely solidifies their relationship. And in fact, this is part of David [McFadzeanÕs] and my lexicon now: We call it a butter beat scene. It was inspired by something Roseanne Barr had told us. She said, ÒI drive Bill crazy when I leave toast crumbs on the butter.Ó And he said, ÒI really canÕt stand it.Ó 
So I took that one idea and built a whole scene around, ÒThatÕs disgusting. You leave crumbs on the butter.Ó ÒWell, you leave jelly in the peanut butter jar.Ó ÒThatÕs completely different!Ó Then he says, ÒAre you sorry you ever married me?Ó And she says, ÒEvery second of my life.Ó So even though itÕs not a story point, I think that scene was the key to emotionally investing the audience in them as a couple. 
What do you think of sarcasm and yelling and insults in comedy? 
I think it can be funny unless it becomes painful. I think it was Moliere or another of the giants in theater who said for comedy you have to ridicule without pain. So you can tease, you can be sarcasticÑbut the second you inflict pain, it stops being funny. So banteringÑwe call it Ògiving shitÓÑmost people I know do that. They tease; they ridicule. But what matters is the subtext, whatÕs the intent behind the line. If the line is said to honestly cause harm or inflict painÑit ainÕt funny. 
How do you balance humor with sadness or blend comedy with drama? 
Write human beings as opposed to just writing jokes. Because if you can get the audience to emotionÑIÕm not talking about the sentimental ÒawwÓ moment, the ÒawwÓ in sitcomsÑbut IÕm talking about just landing occasionally on a real emotional moment, then the joke following it is going to be twice as funny. One of my complaints with some shows is itÕs like being machine-gunned in the head with jokes. I just wanna go, ÒWould you please fucking stopÑand talk to each other for a second!Ó 
Because I think the audience is intrigued with human behavior. ThereÕs an adage in the theater that you buy a ticket to see a play so you can get to watch the characters think onstage. And I always remember that. ItÕs not just say the joke. Jokes are funny; I appreciate funny jokes. I enjoy slapstick. I enjoy it all. But the real engaging of the audience is watching those characters think, make discover.ies, deliberate, make decisions. And the behavior thatÕs occurring between them. 
Not to bring up a sore subject, but what was RoseanneÕs opinion of the writers? 
She hated everyone and everything. She hated everything we wrote. She wanted credit for everything. And she had no idea what she was talking about. 
IÕll give you an example because Òthe battle between Matt and RoseanneÓ has taken on this mythical proportion. NoÑit was just constant insanity, and you just had to constantly keep saying ÒnoÓ to the insanity to keep the show afloat. ThatÕs all you had to do. It wasnÕt any one thingÑit was everything. So three episodes in, she says to me, ÒI wanna stop the scene and talk to the camera, talk to the audience.Ó I said, ÒYou canÕt do that.Ó ÒFuck you! What do you mean I canÕt do that?Ó I said, ÒYou canÕt do that because that would completely break the fourth wall and destroy the verisimilitude of the show. ThatÕs not our show.Ó So thatÕs one tiny example. 
I was going to ask you to characterize the creative differences between you and her. 
There were no creative differences! It was pure insanity and sanity. ThereÕs no creative differences. [Laughs] IÕll give you another example. She came into the writersÕ room, threw her script at one of the writers, and cussed us all out and said, ÒYou motherfuckers, all youÕre doing is writing me jokes. I ainÕt a fuckinÕ joke machine. IÕm an actress. I need some meat; I need somethinÕ to act. Quit giving me these goddamn jokes, and write me something!Ó So, I was stupid enough at the time to stay there all night with the staff. We stayed until five in the morning. We went through the script. We divvied out the jokes. We rewrote them in John GoodmanÕs voice and in Laurie Metcalf Õs voice, and we gave her a nice speech. Went home, got two hours sleep, came back, and the first thing is, ÒRoseanneÕs pissed off; sheÕs walked off the set.Ó WhatÕs wrong? ÒYou mother.fuckers, I donÕt have a single joke in this episode. Goddammit, you gave all the jokes to John Goodman!Ó 
And at some point, someone has to say, ÒIÕm sorry. WeÕre in rehearsal. WeÕre actually taping a show this week. This is what itÕs gotta be. LetÕs move forward.Ó 
I read there was a particular punch line Roseanne found offensive that she refused to say. 
ThatÕs every day. Yes, there was one line. I said something about she made fun of John Goodman or she made some kind of reference about his manhood, and she says, ÒI ainÕt gonna say that; thatÕs too fuckinÕ mean.Ó And in the same episode, she ad-libbed a joke about pulling a gun out of a locker and blowing a womanÕs head off. So OKÑweÕre living in a purely subjective world where the inmate wants control. ItÕs miraculous we got the show that we got. And this isnÕt just me. You can talk to anyone on the staff. 
Was there a specific conflict that led to you leaving the show? 
It was everything. Every time I said ÒnoÓ to something or I insisted they start rehearsals on time, I was the bad guy. ItÕs because I didnÕt like women. Or I didnÕt like fat people. Or I didnÕt like her because sheÕs Jewish. Or I didnÕt like her becauseÑRoseanne! ItÕs a TV show. You just have to show up, and letÕs do our job. [Laughs] 
How would you compare writing The Cosby Show to writing Roseanne? 
The Cosby Show was very tough to write because Bill didnÕt want any conflict or animosity. So you were constantly finding ways to tease or playfully torment without any real animosity or anger. And he didnÕt want anything that sounded like a hard joke. He would go through the script, and if it sounded like da-ta.da-ta-punchÑif it sounded like a rhythm jokeÑhe would throw it out. And his thing was, What is the behavior? A good example is when Theo gets his ear pierced, and heÕs got an earring. And they do a two- to three-minute scene where all BillÕs trying to do is talk to him and lean around and see where the earring is. And TheoÕs trying to keep him from seeing it. ThereÕs not a single joke in the scene, and itÕs hilarious because itÕs all behavior. 
I enjoyed writing Roseanne because that sensibility, the world I grew up in, in southern IndianaÑthat was more from my DNA. It just was what it was because of her personality. 
After you left Roseanne, you teamed with Carmen Finestra and David Mc-Fadzean. Had you known them before? 
David was my roommate in college, and we studied in the same theater pro.gram. He was the first writer I hired on Roseanne. I knew he knew his craft. I also knew I could trust him. I had met Carmen on The Cosby Show, and IÕd been trying to get him off that show to come and join me. And then when I signed the deal at Disney, I finally convinced Carmen to come out and join forces to create 

The Cosby Show 
Elliot Shoenman (Writer, Co-Executive Producer): When I came on the show, there were four very cute kids: Rudy, Theo, Vanessa, and Denise. And mid- to late first season, Bill said, ÒI want to do the show where my daughter comes back from college and brings her laundry home. I want the doorbell to ring, and sheÕs there, and IÕm glad to see her. And then thereÕs all these bags of laundry, and thatÕs the joke.Ó Fred Silverman and I said, ÒThereÕs no daughter in college.Ó Bill said, ÒWell, weÕll get another daughter. There was a question of whether I was gonna have four kids or five kidsÑso letÕs add another kid.Ó I said, ÒWhat do you mean? How can we add another kid at this point; itÕs never been mentioned.Ó He said, ÒSo itÕs never been mentioned. You donÕt mention everybody every time.Ó 
But within a few days, we were casting for an actress to play the girl coming home. And the episode was her bringing her laundry home [ÒBon.jour, Sondra,Ó S1E10, written by Jerry Ross and Michael Loman]. And that was the kind of story meeting youÕd have, and things like that would hap.pen all the time. I have no memory of what that episode turned out to be, but it evolved from him picturing this scene with a role we had no one to playÑso we suddenly created another character. 
One day at a meeting, Bill said he was thinking about all the terrible FatherÕs Day presents his kids had given him. And he wanted to do an episode about terrible FatherÕs Day presents. We said, ÒThatÕs all great Bill, butÑÓ; it was nowhere near FatherÕs Day. And on our schedule the show would air in two weeks; it was that frantic. So we said, ÒWe canÕt do that because itÕs not FatherÕs Day.Ó He said, ÒIÕm giving them six monthsÕ notice that they have to buy my presents now, so they have time to return them.Ó And we said, ÒIt doesnÕt make any sense.Ó He said, ÒWell, just try it; go do it.Ó John Markus and I wrote that one together, and it turned out to be a terrific episode [ÒFatherÕs Day,Ó S1E13]. But we went up to the office and looked at each other like God knows. 
Bill wanted to have an episode about him taking Rudy to the dentist [ÒThe Dentist,Ó S2E16]. He said, ÒI want some great old comic actor to play the dentist.Ó He first wanted Red Skelton, who I think at the time was sick, so then he wanted Danny Kaye. So Tom Werner and I were out in LA, and we had to go meet Danny Kaye and talk him into being on the show. Danny Kaye was incredible with kids, but heÕd seen the first seasonÕs episode where Bill and RudyÕs friends had a sleepover, and he didnÕt think he could top it. He agreed to do it, fairly reluctantly. 
If I remember correctly, I wrote the script, and then we were in New York doing our rewrite. ItÕs Sunday night; weÕre starting rehearsals Monday. And Bill calls and says, ÒI think somebody else should take Rudy to the dentist.Ó I said, ÒWhat do you mean?Ó He said, ÒI think Danny Kaye should just have the stage to himself for three-fourths of the show, and I donÕt think I should be in any scenes with him.Ó We said, ÒYou canÕt do that! We got the guy flying in; we talked him into this.Ó And he said, ÒI donÕt think so.Ó 
On the sleepover show, weÕd had a little neighbor boy who didnÕt say anything. And Bill says, ÒYou know the fat little boy? Why doesnÕt his father take Rudy to the dentist?Ó We said, ÒWhat father? WhoÕs the father? Bill, itÕs Sunday night. We canÕt cast it by then.Ó He said, ÒWell, the casting direc.torÑhe kind of looks like he could be the father. HeÕs kind of a squat guy, so let him do it.Ó ÒAre you kidding? Is he an actor?Ó He said, ÒI donÕt know. HeÕs in casting, so how bad can he be? And the bit will be he doesnÕt talk much, like the boy.Ó We said, ÒWe canÕt do this.Ó Bill said, ÒThatÕs what I wanna do.Ó 
So number 1, I have to call the casting director and say, ÒGuess what. YouÕre gonna be in the show tomorrow.Ó The guy was dumbfounded, but he did it. Then I have to call Danny Kaye and tell him thereÕs been a slight change and weÕre rewriting the script. Cosby isnÕt bringing the kid to the dentist; the neighbor is. AndÑdead silence on the phone. Then he starts yelling that we brought him to New York for this and how could I do that to him. I apologized; it was awful. We stayed up all night, we rewrote the script and went to the table the next morning. Cosby was sort of distant to Danny Kaye, and Danny Kaye was furious at Cosby. 
We got through the week somehow. When weÕd see Danny Kaye com.ing, weÕd dive under a table and get out of the way because we were so embarrassed that weÕd done this to him on behalf of Bill, and we couldnÕt explain it to him. 
But why he wanted to change like that, what he did, and how he did itÑit was stuff like that that happened all the time. And it was just crazy. 
On The Cosby Show, we worked seven days a week and easily one-hundred-hour weeks. It was unbelievable. In the beginning, we were in an orthodox Jewish neighborhood, and you couldnÕt get food brought in on Friday night or Saturday, so weÕd have to send out in the neighborhood, and it was a nightmare. I insisted to Tom [Werner] and Marcy [Carsey] that we couldnÕt work there, so after a few weeks, we ended up renting a suite at the Mayflower Hotel on Central Park South. They would move the typewriters and the whole office there on Friday morning, and weÕd work in the hotel all weekend for the food and for the atmosphere. Then weÕd move back. And weÕd trash the hotel rooms like a rock band; we were just insane because it was so intense and so difficult. 
We were in the living room in the middle of the night one night working on something, and I was a complete zombie. I said, ÒWhat if Bill saysÑÓ and then I passed out. They attended to me and got me water and got me back to consciousness, and one of the writersÕ assistants took me home in a cab and got me in bed. I came in the next morning, and everybody said, ÒAre you OK?Ó And I said, ÒYeah, IÕm OK. I just was exhausted.Ó And John Markus says, ÒBy any chance do you remember what you were gonna pitch?Ó And I said, ÒNo.Ó He says, ÒFuck. We never came up with that line.Ó But I had passed out in midjoke. 
Because [The Cosby Show] was such a cause clbre, whatever we did was acclaimed. And some of it rightly so, and some of it not rightly so. But we could kind of do whatever we wanted, and we could end acts on just a straight line. And then youÕd read in the New York Times what courage we had ending a scene on Bill saying, ÒIÕll see you later!Ó 
And the story behind that is that Bill was going out the door at the end of the act. And it was a terrible, flat moment, where the jokes didnÕt work at all, and we wanted to come up with something. Bill said, ÒDonÕt worry about it. IÕll ad-lib some stuff.Ó 
Whatever he ad-libbed didnÕt work whatsoever. So the act ended on him saying, ÒIÕll see yaÕ later.Ó And the New York Times reviewed it, and they praised the show for its courage in ending by just saying goodbye. And it was totally not a courageous thing at all; it was because Bill came up with stuff that wasnÕt any good! 
Wind Dancer Productions. And that was the best collaboration ever because we have the same sensibility, but we all think differently. So where IÕm always think.ing story, DavidÕs thinking characterÑwhy would the character do this? And CarmenÕs thinking the joke. And it just fit together perfectly. 
Do you and your partners all write in the same room together? 
Yes. I think IÕve spent more time with David and Carmen than my wife of twenty-seven years. 
LetÕs talk about Home Improvement. What was its genesis? 
Jeffrey Katzenberg, God bless him, kept insisting that I meet Tim [Allen]. And I kept saying I donÕt want to work with another stand-up. And he said, ÒGo meet him,Ó and I said, ÒNo.Ó And finally he said, ÒMatt, IÕm not asking you to marry the guy. IÕm asking you to have lunch with him.Ó So David McFadzean and I went and had lunch with Tim, and in five minutes, we just clicked. Because heÕs from the Midwest, heÕs from a large family, he was married at the time, and we started laughing and telling stories about our crazy brothers in the Midwest. IÕd worked construction and tools when I was a teenager, and he was shocked that I knew about tools! And we just started riffing at the lunch. I walked away and turned to David and said, ÒIf we do this show, itÕs gonna be a top ten show.Ó You just knew. And we very deliberately took TimÕs character, TimÕs point of view, and his stand-up routine, and we built a world around that. 
We spent a lot of time in preproduction; we spent nine months conceiving and thinking about it and asking, ÒWhat is this series really about?Ó Because you canÕt just blow up a toaster every weekÑso what is it about? And when we landed on that premise, and we found the Wilson character; we went, ÒOh, OKÑwe can explore masculinism, and we can really look at what it is to be a man.Ó And that was a fun thing to do. 
You mentioned the Wilson character. WhereÕd he come from? 
The gimmick came from Tim. Tim said, ÒIÕve got a neighbor in Michigan, and I never see his face.Ó He always goes, ÒHey Tim, howÕre yaÕ doing?Ó And Tim was pitching this frantic kind of nosy neighbor. But we said, ÒIf weÕre go.ing to do this, you canÕt have a hundred or two hundred episodes of a gimmick. What is his dramatic function in the role?Ó And thatÕs when we read Iron John, a book by Robert Bly. And he said every man needs a man to teach him how to be a man. Every man looks for that mentor and that father figure. Whether itÕs a bio.logical father or a surrogate father or a shaman, he needs that male figure to teach him how to navigate through life. So thatÕs when we went, ÒOh, clickÑthatÕs our guy.Ó So this is the ultimate mentor. And you take that, and you exaggerate: He can carve a canoe, and he canÑyou know, he does all these crazy things. But his dramatic function was the ultimate mentor to teach Tim how to be a man. 
Do you have any favorite episodes or scenes from that show? 
One is where heÕs out drinking with the boys, and unbeknownst to him, Jill has prepared this very romantic dinner with candlelight and has put on a slinky black dress. Now, men and women speak different languages, right? So sheÕs talking about him coming home, and he says, ÒOh yeah, IÕll be right there.Ó Well, he stays at the bar for three hours. So when he comes in, sheÕs pissed off, the din.nerÕs cold, and they get into an argument. ÒYou said IÕll be right there.Ó ÒIf youÕd said it like that, I wouldÕve known what you were doing!Ó Women are subtle. 

And they give hints. Men need to be hit on the head and told blatantly what to do. So that was a really fun episode exploring the semantics of male/female. 
How did you find premises? 
Everything on Home Improvement usually started with, ÒOh God, youÕre not gonna guess what happened in my house today.Ó Or ÒDo you know what Angeline and I fought about last night?Ó It usually started with some incident that we would then turn into a story. And the process was generally, ÒWhat is this episode about? What are we trying to dramatically illustrate?Ó And then, ÒHow do we activate our characters and keep them funny?Ó What drove 201 episodes of Home Improvement was not Tim blowing up toasters. The premise of the series for David, Carmen, and myself was, ÒMen and women should never live together. Ever. But they doÑand every week weÕre going to explore how a male and a female live together.Ó Because Deborah TannenÕs book had come out at the time, You Just DonÕt Understand. And its thesis was that men and women speak a different language, and they think differently; men are hierarchal, and women are connective. And when I read that book, I understood every argument I had ever had with my wife. So that was our premise. And when you look at Home Improvement, the tools and the grunting and all of thatÑall from TimÕs actÑthat was the icing. It wasnÕt the cake. The cake was, ÒTake anything on earth and run it through that premise of male/female.Ó ItÕs just a constant conflict of two worldviews that you can make funny. 
Generally speaking, what do you feel are the elements of a strong episode premise? 
ItÕs gotta be active. And itÕs gotta be specific. So letÕs say the premise for an episode is, ÒPaula wants the world to be happy.Ó OK, thatÕs general. ÒPaula is determined to give her mother the best day possible.Ó You see how active that is? YouÕre determined to give your mother the best day of her life. And of course, if weÕre going to find comedy, then itÕs got to be the worst morning your motherÕs ever had in her life, right? So the more difficult your quest or objective is, then the more active you becomeÑbecause youÕre going to have to work overtime to get Mom happy, and sheÕs going to resist it because she doesnÕt wanna be happy. SheÕs pissed off about this morning. And so you automatically have these two characters locked into a situation where youÕre active, as opposed to a general ÒPaula wants everyone in the world to get along.Ó So itÕs directed, and itÕs intentional that way. 
And then itÕs figuring out what the arc of that is: Where do you end up? And then it becomes ÒI know what Paula wants,Ó and the fun now is, ÒHow does she go about getting it?Ó And at what point do you lose your shit and tell your mom youÕre done? Or trick her. Or whatever. So that to me is what triggers a premise that you can actually write from as opposed to thematically discuss. 
YouÕve created several other shows. What do you think made the difference between success and not? 
Not every show ran for one hundred episodes; some only ran for three epi.sodes, and some ran for twenty-threeÑand I always take the blame. And I would say those shows failed because I either rushed the process or I didnÕt spend the time really thinking about the foundation of the showÑwhat the show was really aboutÑthe way I did on Roseanne and Home Improvement. In creating a show, most people think, ÒOh, you have a work environment. Throw some wacky people together, and you got a sitcom!Ó WellÑno. Cheers ran for eleven years because they had specific characters, and they had a worldview, and they were exploring something. And even if the audience isnÕt aware of what the premise or the intent of the series is, they will feel it. When a program knows exactly what it wants to be and what itÕs trying to say about the human conditionÑpeople respond, relate, and become more emotionally involved. 
One of our intents on Home Improvement was to celebrate an American fam.ilyÑand specifically, a marriage. To show that these two people were always go.ing to work through their problems because this was a marriage that was sound. That was just one of the multilayered intents of the show. In the shows that failed, it was usually because youÕre too distracted or youÕre too busy juggling too many projects to put in the think time to figure out what are you trying to say about the human condition. And you go, ÒItÕs only a sitcom!Ó YesÑbut the ones that surviveÑthe Dick Van Dykes, the Mary Tyler Moores, the FriendsÑthey have a special ambiance; thereÕs something about them. So thatÕs part of it. And the other is that sometimes itÕs just dumb luck. Because you may write the most bril.liant script on the planet, and if you canÕt cast it right or you miscast one person, the whole show is off. So it really is like this weird chemistry set where first and foremost you must have the idea, the concept, and the characters, and you must put that on the page. And the second most important thing is finding the right cast. And then thereÕs also the whole look of the show. Even the wardrobe of the show. 
Which of your shows are you most proud of ? 
Home Improvement. I know the critical acclaim goes to Roseanne, and Cosby was groundbreaking for all the obvious reasons. But Home Improvement was shunned by the critics, and the highbrows rolled their eyes at it. I think they thought it was stupid because he grunted and he blew shit up. But if they look at it, and if they really look at the Wilson scenesÑwe were dealing with some very profound issues of what it is to be a man looking for that father figure in life and what it is to be married. We always found the funny, of course, but the show was smarter than what I think people gave it credit for. Now toward the end, when youÕre in your sixth or seventh season, you go, ÒWeÕve done that, weÕve done that, weÕve done that. OK, I guess we can blow up a truck!Ó But look at those first three seasons. It truly embraces humanity in a way thatÕs very life affirming, and IÕm proud of that. And it was a global phenomenon. 
Did TimÕs stand-up comedy ever touch on those heavier themes? 
He mainly dealt with the wild man grunting and howling at the moonÑmen do crazy shit. But any comic will tell you, thereÕs always pain. He lost his dad in a car wreck when he was eleven or twelve, so he grew up without a father. He was longing for that, so it had to inform his stand-up. And his time in jail certainly informed him. You donÕt want to wallow into Long DayÕs Journey into Night in a twenty-two-minute sitcom, but you can stop occasionally and have a moment of real vulnerability or humanity and then get back to your comedy. Which is what we really tried to do during those first three years. 

Home Improvement 
Elliot Shoenman (Executive Producer): Home Improvement was a pretty basic Midwestern show. And if you got too clever, it wasnÕt what the audi.ence wanted and wasnÕt what Tim particularly liked. We did a show about the oldest son, Brad, hiding some pot in the backyard. I was working with Marley Sims, and she came up with one of the greatest jokes I ever heard. It was very smart, and we wanted it in, no matter what. It got a decent laugh from the audience but not even close to what I thought it deserved. The joke was that Tim or Jill finds the bag of pot buried in the backyard, and the other one says, ÒYou think it belongs to one of the kids?Ó And the line was, ÒEither that, or we have a chipmunk with glaucoma!Ó But that was on the level of as smart a joke as you could get. And then a more basic joke was where Brad tells Tim heÕs bringing the pot to a party, and Tim says, ÒSo youÕre the supplier.Ó He says no, everybodyÕs bringing some. And Tim says, ÒSo basically itÕs a potluck.Ó I thought that was a really basic good Home Improvement joke. So that was the difference between the two levels. 
They actually had the most amazing people doing stunts on that show and the prop people. We did a show where Jill gets a scratch on her car, and Tim berates her. He takes the car to the body shop, and he drops the beam on the car. We used a real car. We had one duplicate of the car that somebody found in a body shop. And the guy said he could drop this five-ton beam or whatever it was on a dime. And he did. And it split the car almost in half and made it look like a hotdog bun. We reveal the car and that got an unbelievable laugh Õcause it just looked funnier than anything we could have imagined. 
Tim and Jill were typical of the TV marriage: she being the voice of reason and him being the goofy, childlike dreamer. And you always have to go back to, ÒBut underneath it all, they really love each other.Ó And Pat [Richardson] was very strong with him yet likeable enough. Part of the formula is the boyishness of Ralph Kramden/Bill Cosby/Tim Allen. And I think thatÕs why theyÕre better when theyÕre younger because, as they get olderÑthe same thing with LucyÑit ainÕt as funny. Like I always say, ÒYou donÕt wanna see your grandmother with her head caught in a fishbowl!Ó 
In the key episodes, Tim fucks something up by overdoing it, and JillÕs embarrassed and comes down hard on him. We did one where they were honoring her at some charity, and they wanted a blowup photo of her for a poster as a surprise. He was supposed to get it, and he waited until the last minute, and he snuck out her driverÕs license picture. Which in tiny form wasnÕt so bad, but when they blew it up, it was horrendous. We got the actress to take this terrible picture. And then as a presentation to her, they un.veil the picture, and itÕs the worst picture of her in the world. So the episode was about him screwing up, although he meant well. And that was the kind of episode that always worked for us. And after a while, when we were doing twenty-eight or more episodes, the formula started to come in pretty handy! 
IÕd like to discuss some creative aspects of your work. What kind of humor do you most enjoy writing? 
What I find most satisfying is when you get the joke on behavior, and you get the laugh between the lines. So Tim and Jill could be talking, and he could be saying, ÒOh yeah, that attractive woman,Ó and sheÕs over there cutting carrots, and suddenly, how she cuts that carrot starts getting a laugh. ItÕs not the joke; itÕs her thought process coming out in behavior thatÕs funny. 
WhatÕs the hardest thing for you about writing comedy? 
I think the hardest thing is truly the final polish, and this is where Carmen Finestra is brilliant. I was trained as a director and an actor and not a writer. So I understand the impulses and the beats and whatÕs being played, but when I get down to that final polishÑwhere you change one wordÑand the line is twice as funny? You change your verb, and all of a sudden, the line comes to life? ThatÕs what really takes me time. 
Do you have any quirks or superstitious rituals that help you to write? 
IÕve got a few. I donÕt light candles and go into a trance or anything like that, but every time I start a project, I find an excuse to totally clean and rearrange my library. I have to go through every notepad and every bookÑand I know itÕs procrastination, but itÕs also like a baseball pitcher warming up. And then thereÕs a handful of books that IÕll go back and reread, and some are on writing, some are on directing, some are on acting. So even at this ripe old age, I go back just to refresh myself. And there are probably some books IÕve read ten times just to kind of jump-start and make sure the craft is still there. 
Do you have any specific techniques for brainstorming? 
I do everything on a yellow pad. IÕm visual, not auditory; I see the characters and see the world, and then I hear them. So what IÕll do is sit with a yellow pad and just start drawing circles. And IÕll go, ÒOK, hereÕs Tim Allen. And he is all masculinism, right?Ó And then IÕll think, ÒWhat is the exact opposite of that?Ó And draw another circle and go, ÒOK, itÕs gotta be female if itÕs the opposite.Ó And then IÕll say, ÒHow does this person impact?Ó So IÕll have a yellow pad that looks like the scribbles of a madman with all these arrows and dashes and bubbles. Then in the margins IÕll just write, ÒRemember your premiseÓ or ÒOb.sessive about germsÓÑor whatever it is thatÕs going on with the character. And once I do all that downloading on a yellow pad, I never throw it away because thatÕs got the initial energy. And no one on earth can read it but me, but anytime I get stuck writing, I go back and flip through that pad, and I get back to the ex.plosion of thought that was splattered on that pageÑand then it kind of reminds me of what the original impulse was. 
But do you use a computer at any point? 
I do, but I want to just pour all these ideas out on the page and scribble them as sloppily as I canÑlet them all fall out and not censor myself and just see whatÕs inside and whatÕs stirring. And once I fill that yellow pad up, then I sit at the computer and start giving it organization. If I start writing on the computer, I start worrying about punctuation, and, ÒOh wait, is that the right verb I should use?Ó You know, and I donÕt want to do that at the conceptual stage. I want it just to be any kind of explosion of thought, and then IÕll give it structure. 
Your assistant told me you were in the writerÕs tunnel. What is that? 
ThatÕs usually when I say, ÒDonÕt interrupt my day with three ten-minute calls,Ó since every time I interrupt my day for a ten-minute call, it takes me forty-five min.utes to an hour to get back to where I was when I was writing. And once I go into the tunnel, let me stay in the tunnel. At the end of my day, when I know IÕm not going to do heavy writing tonight, then I can stop and talk as long as people want. 
ItÕs where youÕre in that dreamlike state and the only thing youÕre hearing and seeing are your characters and the world youÕre creating. And the real world disappearsÑor at least goes into soft focusÑand your imaginary world is in sharp focus. And that imaginary world becomes more real than the real world. And you live in that creative tunnel for minutes, hoursÑand sometimes many hours. And then you have to pull out of it, and you come back. And when you stop writing, the real world comes back into focus, and it feels as if youÕve been in a tunnelÑthis very myopic world, removed from reality. 
ThatÕs painful, to pull out of it. 
Yes, it is. It really is. 
Have you ever been stuck with a particularly tough case of writerÕs block? 
My problem is the opposite. If I get blocked, itÕs because IÕve got so many sto.ries going on in my head and I want to write all five of them at once. My blocking is stopping and putting on the back shelf of my mind these other ideas so I can concentrate on the one. If I sit still, I start hearing voices and seeing things in my imagination, and people start talking. And they start either creating a scene or sce.nario or maybe one image. IÕve got a play or two in my head that have been bounc.ing around in there for ten years or more, and at some point, IÕm going to have to write that sucker! And also, you always hit that wall at some pointÑboy, that other idea is far better than this one. ItÕs so much easier to abandon it, and then you start writing on that one, and then you hit the wall on thatÑbut thatÕs not as good as the other oneÑand so youÕre going to have fourteen unfinished scripts. So I say to the writers, ÒEven if itÕs really shitty, you gotta finish it. Finish it! Ó 
How do you recharge creatively? 
Reading and exercise more than anything. 
Have you always been confident that you could write well? 
Never. I donÕt think any writerÕs confident. If youÕre confident, youÕre prob.ably not a good writer. I think you constantly question and doubt. And every time you sit down to write, you start from scratch and go, ÒOh hell, do I even know how to do this? How did I fake them the last time!Ó 
Lastly, what is the most rewarding aspect of your career? 
Some of the most joyful moments were on Home Improvement just because the cast and crew got along. We had the same crew for seven, eight years. And I love the process of creating; I just love production. I love that you imagine something; itÕs invisible, but youÕre imagining it and seeing it, and you put these little black marks on a piece of paper, and then you hand that piece of paper to people, and they build a set, and they say the wordsÑand it comes to life. I still get excited about that process. Despite insanity and temperamental people, it is a wonderful, exciting process to be able to tell stories. 
9 


DAVA SAVEL 
I
n her LinkedIn profile, Dava Savel distinguishes herself as a ÒJewish writer in Hollywood.Ó Her photo is a picture of actor Peter Dinklage. ÒPeople are like, ÔThatÕs you? Why would that be there?Õ I say, ÔÕcause I donÕt care about LinkedIn. ThatÕs why I wrote a Jewish writer in HollywoodÑthereÕs a gazillion Jewish writers in Hollywood!ÕÓ [Laughs] 
DavaÕs excitement is contagious as she describes the high of winning an Emmy for the groundbreaking coming-out episode of Ellen. Also palpable is the stress she went through in running a show she knew would make history. She recalls that year as literally the best and worst of times: ÒSo much fun stuff. ItÕs like childbirth. You only remember the good. [Laughs] All the painful shit you kind of let goÑDid that really happen?! There were some bad times, really bad times, there was a lot of stress, a lot of anger, a lot of screaming.Ó 
Dava believes that humor finds its own level: ÒUnderneath the skin of any situation, serious or not, thereÕs a level of funny. ItÕs just about tapping that source. Sometimes you can find real comedy gold. Sometimes not. IÕve never heard a good joke about the Holocaust.Ó 
Dava, IÕll start by asking, what led you to become a TV comedy writer? 
I was part of a comedy team. For years, I used to write and perform in a New York cabaret. One day a William Morris literary agent from LA came to see us. He gave me his card and said, ÒYouÕre a good writer; you write all this funny stuff. If you ever just feel like writing, call me.Ó 
I was six months pregnant by the time I met with this agent. I called him, and I said, ÒLook, IÕm so fat. No oneÕs gonna hire me. So whatÕs this whole thing about writing? Can we talk?Ó So we discussed it at lunch, and I ended up writing a pilot. 
Because of that pilot, William Morris signed me. And it was like someone handed me a key to two doors: You can either continue being an actor, or you can do this thing called writing. I thought, ÒWell, maybe I should be a writer.Ó And thatÕs how I came into it. 
LetÕs skip to the highlight of your career: writing the coming-out episode for Ellen [ÒThe Puppy Episode,Ó S4E22Ð23]. Was it EllenÕs idea to have the character come out? 
Yes. In EllenÕs fourth season, Mark Driscoll and I were running the show. And Ellen had everyone come to her house. WeÕre all having a little something to eat, and she says she just wanted to welcome all the new writers. And then she says, ÒI wanna make an announcement. This year, IÕm coming out on televi.sion.Ó And I remember thinking, ÒThis is either gonna be the greatest thingÑor the end of the show.Ó 
It could be disastrous. 
Exactly. But I wasnÕt trepidatious as much as I was excited, Õcause I thought it was awesome. It had been really difficult to write the show up to that point because you couldnÕt really write any dating stuff because she didnÕt want to date. So what was the show? We kept reinventing. It was like, ÒHow much could you write?Ó 
You run out of ideas. 
You run out of ideas. 
What were your earliest intentions for the episode? 
We knew the arc of where we wanted to take her, and we knew we wanted her to keep seeing therapists until we got her to the place where the audience would believe that she could tell someone what she was feeling. Oprah was the obvi.ous choice, since she was so nice and warm and nonjudgmental. We had a lot of gay people working on that show, and we asked everybodyÕs coming-out story to see if we could hear something that weÕd never heard before and to see what the commonalities were. We wanted to make sure we were realistic and honest and yet completely original in our storytelling. Not every day a lead in a sitcom comes out as a lesbian. You only get one chance, and you wanna do it right. 
And we wanted to be tremendously respectful because this was not a joke; this affected so many people. It was funny, but we didnÕt want to make fun of anybody. This was such a tender subject. And it was so tender to Ellen. 
It must have been fun coming up with the hints leading to it. 
I didnÕt write this, but I remember one that was really good. Somebody had her walk into the room, and she was singing from West Side Story, ÒI feel pretty, oh so pretty, I feel pretty and witty andÑHey! WhatÕs this thing doinÕ over here!Ó It was great. Initially we had the idea that every time there was a hint, something would ding, like a toaster oven or a doorbell. We wanted to have fun with it because the audience was so excited, and they were looking for clues. And then we thought that would get old real quick, so we threw it out. 
I wrote an episode called ÒGive Me Equity or Give Me Death,Ó where sheÕs sitting with a Realtor whoÕs showing her homes [S4E1]. The Realtor has these little puppets, and she goes, ÒHereÕs you, and youÕre gonna come home, and hereÕs your husband, and heÕs gonna come home, and youÕre gonna come home to your new house!Ó And Ellen says, ÒUh, I donÕt know about the house, but I can tell you right nowÑheÕs in the wrong puppet show!Ó So that kind of thing. 
Whose idea was it to get Oprah? 
Mine. I made that suggestion. You know the only reason I remember that? ÕCause I was looking at the TV Guide article that was done on us, and it quoted me as saying, ÒIf Oprah says itÕs OK to be gayÑthen AmericaÕs gonna say itÕs OK to be gay.Ó And they did. 
What do you remember about Oprah being on the show? 
To see Oprah Winfrey sitting in producer Vic KaplanÕs office was amaz.ing. She was inches away from the television, watching all the footage that had already been shot before she did her stuffÑand she was crying. Cry-ing! To see Oprah crying? And she thanked us up and down for letting her be a part of this piece of history. And then I said, ÒOprah, would you do me a favor? Would you present the cake after we wrap?Ó This huge cake, I think it said, ÒIÕm gay, IÕm here to stay,Ó I donÕt rememberÑbut she wheeled it out and presented it to El.len, and it was just so classy. 
Your first draft was rejected because Disney said you didnÕt go far enough. 
The problem with our first draft was we assumed the episode would be how all the characters related to her coming out. But Disney didnÕt care about the other characters. Their executive Dean Valentine sat us down, and he said, ÒYou see this area? This is her coming to terms with her being gay. I want you to blow this out to this.Ó He literally put his hands together, facing each other, then spread them far apart. ÒThrow everything else out. ThatÕs the episode I want. I donÕt care about any of her friends. Fuck her friends. Fuck her family. I care about one person: Ellen. Spread that out, and give me her and what sheÕs going through.Ó 
It was a two-parter, and I was surprised they wanted to explore it that much and go that deep with it. I think the only joke we did about the other people was everybody sort of paying up a bet after she came out. And DeanÕs advice was brilliant because thatÕs what made the episode. It was exploring her demons, her inner struggle. You loved her when that happened. There was a great moment where she has ice in her glass and sheÕs eating her ice and sheÕs going, ÒThis is not lesbian ice, you know,Ó or whatever sheÕs sayingÑbecause she was so nervous being around Laura Dern. ItÕs pure Ellen. She couldÕve done twenty minutes on the ice. 
Was that improvised? 
So much of it was. There were certain key lines that she went to, that she knew she was heading towards. 
So what did you want the episode to say? You wanted people to see the struggle she was going through? 
I really did. We were very conscious that we could never let the character know more than the audience. You had to keep the character vulnerable, just like Ellen was vulnerable; thatÕs why she was so emotional during that time. She wasnÕt empowered; she was scared. And you want to show that. You wanted the audience to go, ÒYouÕre gay! We see it, and weÕre OK with it.Ó But have her still not see it, her still not accept it, her still be frightened to death to come to terms with it. And needless to say, ABC and Disney were over the moon with how it all came out. 
What can you say about the airport intercom announcement? 
It was genius. We knew we wanted it to be a big moment. It wasnÕt gonna be like, ÒOh, by the way, IÕm gay.Ó It had to be this crazy moment. We wanted it to be really loud. And thatÕs what she said. She goes, ÒBoy, that felt really good. And that felt really loud!Ó And then cut to all these faces of people reacting to a woman who just yells out on a loudspeaker ÒIÕm gay!Ó 
Priceless. 
It was priceless. And you should have seen the audience. Stood up, scream.ing, screaming. 

The showÕs title was ÒThe Puppy Episode.Ó Had someone actually sug.gested that EllenÕs character get a puppy? 
It had nothing to do with a puppy. It was a bullshit title set up to throw off any indication that she was planning on coming out on the show. As IÕd told you, we were limited in writing that series because we couldnÕt deal with her having a relationship; we couldnÕt do romantic arcs in any episode. So it was always like, ÒEllen Buys a New Couch,Ó ÒEllen Gets a New Apartment,Ó ÒEllen Goes on a Road Trip.Ó 
So originally we said, ÒWell, weÕll just call it ÔEllen Gets a New Puppy.ÕÓ There was a white board up in the writersÕ room with all the episodes listed and who wrote them and directed them, et cetera. But because the episode was so hush-hush, we couldnÕt even write the Òcoming-out episodeÓ on it. Just said ÒThe Puppy Episode.Ó IÕm not sure who came up with the title, but it stuck. 
We werenÕt allowed to have anything written down on paper. When we were writing the episode, everything had to be run off on crimson red paper, which canÕt be copied, so nothing could leak. It was really hard to read. We werenÕt al.lowed to take anything home. It was all very top secret. And right before, when we were ready to shoot the episode, the script was stolen. One of our writers heard the radio station say, ÒWe have the Ellen coming-out script, and weÕre gonna be reading it.Ó [DisneyÕs] attorneys shut it down. Is that crazy? 
WeÕd done an earlier episode about Ellen going to rock Õn roll camp [ÒEllen Unplugged,Ó S4E16]. Aaron Neville and Bonnie Raitt and David Crosby and Sheryl Crow all came and did cameos for us. At the end of the episode, theyÕre all playing, and everyoneÕs singing and riffing, whatever. EllenÕs singing, and she gets on the microphone, and sheÕs like, ÒYeah, IÕm gay! IÕm really gay!Ó The audience went nuts. And ABC went ballistic. They were like, ÒGet that out of there.Ó They were so scared that that would be recorded, that it would somehow get out to the press that sheÕd come out before she was supposed to. 
How would you characterize the atmosphere in the writersÕ room that season? 
It was a very hard year. We were under a lot of stress. Tremendous, tremen.dous stress. We were dealing with a very personal issue for Ellen herself, we were dealing with Disney down our necks and ABC down our necksÑthis was a hit show they had. ItÕs like if Ray Romano decided he was gonna say he was gay in the middle of his show. Who does that? 
One of our writers had cystic fibrosis and was dying, so he was coming to work with an IV. Right at the eleventh hour, he got a pair of lungs, so heÕs fine now. But there was so much. We had late hours; we had to split the rooms, and we dealt with B stories in one room. We called it the Tsunami Room. It was fast and furious. And I canÕt say that everybody was in a good mood. 
Ellen is really a brilliant comic, but sheÕs also very emotional, and a lot of things annoyed her. And so youÕre dealing with trying to handhold and trying to be understanding. And IÕm a straight woman, so itÕs hard. We got a tremendous amount of hate mail and threats, and Disney really protected us from it. I remem.ber at one point I was handed stacks of tremendously negative press, and I saw letters from people threatening us that would make your hair curl. We had to get bomb dogs the day we were shooting. I was scared. I mean, you had your whole gay contingency in the audience, and they were so excited for usÑbut thatÕs not middle America. This was a time when it wasnÕt OK. 
After the episode aired, there was so much press. At the Emmys, we were walking from backstage across to the pressrooms after weÕd won, and all the waiters stopped and gave us a standing ovation and just stood there applauding us. These were all gay waiters. It was the most incredible thing. I remember seeing Bette Midler, who gave me a big hug. I saw Carl Reiner; it was like, ÒWow, you kids did something big!Ó And I got so many flowers, so many gifts, from Norman Lear, everybody. Eileen Heisler, who ran Ellen before I did, sent me flowers, and on her card it said, ÒOh, sureÑfor you, she comes out!Ó [Laughs] 
I take it that was the proudest moment of your career. 
Well, if you had to win an Emmy for any episode, you can bet your bottom dollar everybody knows this episode. People say, ÒYou won an Emmy? WhatÕd you win it for?Ó ÒRemember when Ellen came out of the closet and said she was gay?Ó ÒOh, my God, yesÑyou wrote that?Ó But if you said, ÒYou remember when Tony Danza took a trip with his kids andÑI won an Emmy for that.Ó You know what I mean? ItÕs the greatest thing to win an Emmy for. That episode was put down as one of the one hundred greatest moments of television. WeÕre in history books. So I have to say I hit the jackpot on that. 
Some viewers felt that, after the coming-out episode, the show became too gay. IÕm sure youÕve heard that. 
Yes. We had two or three episodes past the coming-out episode, and then we were told that all of us were being let go. Ellen didnÕt want any of us backÑin.cluding her brother, who was a writer on the show. She fired everybody. 
What was she so unhappy about? 
She was empowered. And she was on top of herself; she wanted things her way, and she wanted a brand-new staff. I remember ABC calling my office and saying, ÒWeÕre so happy with how the show came out, and we want to have a big meeting. LetÕs talk about your thoughts for next year.Ó And I said, ÒYouÕre talk.ing to the wrong person. IÕve just been fired. Every one of us is gone.Ó 
What I said at the timeÑand I still maintainÑis that EllenÕs character was a woman who was three steps forward, two steps back. ThatÕs what made her so funny. SheÕd do these comedic rants where sheÕd sort of backpedal on things, and that was her character trait. So even if you announce youÕre gayÑ 
Your personality doesnÕt change. 
Ding! Exactly. And thatÕs what I would have done if I were running the show; I would have made sure that the next year sheÕs dating women, but she still has the same issues, that she doesnÕt change the character. But she did change because El.len changed. She became more sure of herself, more empowered, more in control, not scared. And that often happens with actors portraying characters with the same name as their real name; everything sort of melts into each other, and thereÕs no separation of church and state. And IÕm not surprised that that got criticism. 
You mentioned the ice. How much improvisation did Ellen do on the show? 
Constant. She could do five minutes of shtick; the audience is going hysteri.cal laughing. Why would you cut that? ThatÕs gold. You canÕt write that stuff; itÕs brilliant. Everything else suffered. Then your C runner is gone. Now your B story gets thinnerÑtinyÑboopÑgone. But you donÕt do that for everybody. You do that when theyÕre good, when they know what theyÕre doing. 
How would you characterize the showÕs humor? 
The thing about Ellen is she really loved dorky jokes Õcause she could sell Õem. I did a joke where Ellen sees a woman wearing a handmade scarf. And the line was, ÒYou wove this?Ó ÒI wuv this.Ó ItÕs such a stupid line, and I remember writing it, and somebody saying, ÒThatÕs not gonna fly.Ó I said, ÒJust let me see. She may like it.Ó She loved it! 
Creatively speaking, would you rather be on a controversial show with so much riding on it? 
Yeah. After Ellen, I was very at odds about what I was gonna do next be.cause I wanted to do something meaningful. It didnÕt have to be groundbreak.ing; it just needed to be thought provoking, as opposed to just chuffah. ThatÕs comedy filler. A show has to have peaks and valleys, the highs and the lows; it canÕt just be chuffah. 
So what did you do? 
I went right into running a show on Lifetime called Maggie, with Ann Cu-sack, and it was one of the most delightful shows IÕve ever been on. It was just very calming, and it wasnÕt frenetic. 
Switching gears, generally speaking, how would you characterize the writ.ersÕ rooms youÕve worked in? 
TheyÕre so collaborative. And there are times when you think you wrote a joke. And then someone says, ÒNo, you didnÕt write that. I wrote that.Ó ÒYou did not! I wrote that.Ó IÕve been on shows where somethingÕs really funny, and the executive producer goes, ÒWho wrote that?Ó We all look around, and weÕre like, I think I did. 
I remember one executive producer who would always say, ÒCan you top it?Ó when a joke was thrown out to the room. And it becomes like a volleyball game. You pop it off with your joke, then IÕll tip it one more time with mine, someone else will tip it, and then somebody will hit it right over. And the Òright overÓ is the joke you wanna go for. But it took you three pops to get to it, so everybody really contributed. And by the way, the writersÕ room is very sacred in that sense. And youÕd be hard pressed to find writers whoÕd take credit for anything. Unless theyÕve got issues. [Laughs] ItÕs really like a secret society. 
Please say more. 
ItÕs a bunker mentality. ItÕs the writersÕ room against the network, the room against the star, the room against the studio. And youÕre in there, and a network can give you notes, and they walk away, and they go home to their kids. And you sit there, and you look at each other and go, ÒOh my God.Ó And in comes the PA with the menus, and they go, ÒWhat do you want to eat for dinner tonight?Ó And your whole heart sinks because you know youÕre gonna be there Õtil three in the morning. 
In the room, everybody gets their seat. You donÕt change seatsÑoh no, you do not! DonÕt change seats; youÕll fuck everything up. ThereÕs a lot of cruelty in writersÕ rooms. What I donÕt like about a comedy writersÕ room are the jabs and sparring. 
Any examples? 
Gonna pass on that! ItÕs a hard concept to understand unless youÕre a com.edy writer. Taken out of context, itÕs very hard to imagine. Think of a really nasty roast. Funny barbs, but still barbs. 
IÕve been in rooms where IÕve heard the most derogatory things about women, and I would say, ÒTime out. IÕm not sittinÕ in this room and hearing you calling everybody a c***.Ó 
IÕve been in rooms where IÕve had big fights coming to the defense of young writers trying to say things and not being given the time. 
That brings us to the subject of women in the business. 
HereÕs a good story. Alan Zweibel, the showrunner for ItÕs Garry ShandlingÕs Show, called me in to write an episode. I pitched a whole bunch, and they loved one of the ideasÑa lotÑand they hired me. I wrote the episode, and they loved the first draft. And that first draft subsequently became my calling card; it got me a lotta work. But anyway, Alan calls me in and says, ÒWeÕre so happy with this, and we want to hire you.Ó He wanted me to meet everybody, so I went from writer to writer and saw everybody, and IÕm like, ÒOh my God,Ó because I was a huge Garry Shandling show fan. I got the job. 
But in the same afternoon, Garry comes in. He sits down with us, and he really likes how I wrote, but he has a question for me. He says, ÒI hear that you have a baby.Ó I said, ÒYeah, I do.Ó I had a six-month-old daughter. He goes, ÒI canÕt work with someone whoÕs got a baby.Ó I said, ÒI donÕt have to be home. My husband watches the baby. IÕm fine.Ó And Alan Zweibel says, ÒGarry, she can get the work done.Ó He goes, ÒI donÕt know,Ó and theyÕre going back and forth like IÕm not even in the room. And I saw this job pass before my eyes. I remember Alan put his arm around my shoulder and walked me to the elevator. He goes, ÒIÕm so sorry. At the very least, IÕd love you to do another episode for us as a freelancer.Ó And I realized I lost a job because I had a baby! 
It was hard. I mean, look at the people who came before me, like Linda Bloodworth-Thomason and Diane English. They all worked with their hus.bands. They didnÕt have any kids. This is what was before us. To have a childÑ you just didnÕt talk about your kids. You didnÕt leave because your kid was sick. 
ItÕs different now. 
Oh God, yes. When I gave birth to my second kid, I didnÕt even have any kind of maternity leave. I was just let go. And when you came back, you got paid again then. I think it was a tremendous struggle for women. And for my own kids. I remember coming home one night from Grace under Fire; it was about three in the morning. I quietly got into bed, and I heard these little feet, and it was my daughter. She must have been four, and she said, ÒMommy, where were you?Ó 
I said, ÒI just got home from work, honey. IÕm sorry. Go back to bed.Ó And she said, ÒOh Mommy, havenÕt you made enough money yet?Ó ThatÕs how it was, you know? 
You do what you need to do. On Roseanne, people used to have cots in their office to sleep. Every show has horror stories. But the writers would support each other because it was like all we had. We were fighting a war, a war on TV. [Laughs] Comedy is ugly. 
Are women writers more accepted today? 
Yes, though I still donÕt think there are enough women showrunners. I re.member on Ellen the year before I ran it, the two women showrunners came back from a meeting where theyÕd learned that Disney was upset with them be.cause they didnÕt smile more in the meeting. I remember them saying, ÒDo you think they would ever tell a man, ÔYou didnÕt smile enough at that meetingÕ?Ó ThatÕs how it was. And the sexual harassment; IÕve been through a lot of that, too. I remember being on a show for Fox, and one of the executives comes into my office and says, ÒSo, Dava, I hear youÕre married.Ó I said, ÒYes, I am.Ó And he goes, ÒJust how married are you?Ó [Laughs] OK! 
Are women still thought to be less funny than men? 
Oh, yeah. I once got pulled into The Jackie Thomas Show because I was a woman writer, and they wanted more relationship stuff in the show. They had the funnyÑnow, whereÕs the emotion? So you were brought in to bring the show some heart Õcause men wouldnÕt give the show heart. 
And I was on another showÑgranted, I had my period, so I was really testyÑ but they were dividing the script up into scenes. And they gave me a scene that was all heart. It was what you call the moment of shitÑwhich is the scene where someone has a mea culpa moment; itÕs realizing I made the mistake, and IÕm sorry, or I love you. And they were dividing up the room, and these guys got jokes, and that funny scene went to him, and that funny scene to himÑand ÒDava, weÕre gonna give you this scene Õcause itÕs a nice moment, and we really want it to be written well.Ó 
And that had happened to me so often, and I guess I was just so hormonal that I started screaming. And I screamed, ÒI am so sick of being given these stupid fucking scenes that are about nothing! Why canÕt I ever get the jokes? IÕm funny!Ó And they were open-mouthed, staring, shocked. IÕm like, ÒIÕm really sorry. Really sorry I said that. IÕll just do the scene. IÕm sorry, IÕm really sorry.Ó 
ThatÕs a sitcom scene in itself ! 
Absolutely. On Grace under Fire, Dottie Dartland and I were given a very heartfelt scene to write because we were the two chicks. And we went out, and we were like, ÒFuck this. WeÕre not gonna write a heartfelt scene!Ó We decided to write a filthy pornographic scene and just hand it in like we had finished writing something really poignant. They put the whole script together, and then Chuck [Lorre] is reading the script, and he gets to our scene. And the opening line was something like, ÒAre you OK?Ó ÒYeah. I gave Jimmy a blow job. I donÕt think I did it very well.Ó Chuck thought it was great. Because we were so sick of writing justÑyou know, the chick scenes. 
ThatÕs understandable. What advice do you have for young writers? 
They think, because they watch television, that they know television. But the trick is to read television: read the scripts because thatÕs what people doÑthey read your scripts. That script that I wrote for ItÕs Garry ShandlingÕs Show? At the next job I got, Allan Burns told me that he was in the bathroom on the toilet reading my script. And he was laughing so hard, his wife knocked on the door and asked whatÕs going on in there. He says, ÒIÕm reading a great script.Ó And it doesnÕt get any better than that. 
And you want to know something interesting? That episode was stolen. I got a call from a friend who worked at Fox, and she said, ÒDava, the weirdest thing. Your episode of ItÕs Garry ShandlingÕs Show is on my desk. But itÕs not your episode. It says based on a teleplay by Dava Savel.Ó Somebody had taken the script, rewrote it with my story, and gave me credit for the teleplay. 
Amazing they gave you credit. 
ItÕs amazing. So my agent called his agency. I know the guy. He still works, to this day. ItÕs crazy, crazy stuff. And all the starsÑI was doing an audio ses.sion with Buddy Hackett when I worked on the show Dinosaurs. I was in a booth with him, and he starts hitting on me. He starts taking my leg and putting it on his, and IÕm like, ÒBuddy, you canÕt do that! Buddy!Ó Finally, he says, ÒCÕmon, whatÕs your name. WhatÕs your name?Ó And I said, ÒMy name is Dava.Ó He goes, ÒDava! What kind of a name is Dava?Ó I said itÕs my Hebrew name. He says, ÒYouÕre a Jew?Ó I say, ÒYeah.Ó He goes, ÒWhat the hell am I hittinÕ on you for? IÕve got one of those at home!Ó But being in a booth with Buddy HackettÑthatÕs insane. 
So many stories in the big city! And so much of it has nothing to do with the writing part of it. I remember at Grace under Fire, Chuck [Lorre] was running one room, and I was running the other. I was on the second floor of a building, and I had a bathroom in my office, and I had a big sign outside the bathroom that said, ÒNo Number 2s.Ó I didnÕt wanna deal with anything like that in my office. One of the writers went into my bathroom, and something like twenty minutes later, he comes back through the front door of my office. And none of us really put two and two together. And then all of a sudden, we stopped. And someone said, ÒWait a minute. You went into the bathroom, and you just came in through the front door. What the hell!Ó And this writer says, ÒIÕve been waiting for someone to notice.Ó He had hidden a rope in the bath.room and he climbed out the bathroom window and rappelled down the wall at Carsey-Werner Studios and got to the bottom, pulled down the rope, and walked all the way, and just walked in like nothing happened. And he thought people were going to notice right away. And nobody did. But it was like that kind of stuff. Just a lot of fun little things that happened and some really hor.rible things that would happen. Horrible. 
Like? 
Just stars throwing complete tantrums and throwing scripts at you and say.ing, ÒThis is the biggest piece of shit, and I want it rewritten now!Ó Ellen never went down that road. Many times, IÕd walk into the offices and see people look.ing at me and IÕm like, ÒWhat?Ó TheyÕd say, ÒEllen wants to see you. SheÕs in her trailer, and sheÕs not happy.Ó And sheÕd be really upset with me, and I didnÕt even know what I did. 
Would she tell you? 
Eventually. And it wasnÕt about my writing; it was something I said. SheÕd be crying, ÒYou did this, and youÑÓ She took tremendous offense at half the things I said. And yet, we got along great. And yet, so many hugs and kisses. And when we did the coming-out episode, we were just so happy for each other. 
What trends do you see in the industry? 
I think now weÕre at a place where thereÕs so much youth coming up, and the people who are running the networks are on their last legs. So I think thereÕs gonna be a big shift in terms of whoÕs gonna run things. I think itÕll become this mesh of social media and television and everything streaming. The network sensibility is old-school sensibility. Be interesting to see. 
ItÕs funny, I saw Norman Lear at this dinner party. He says, ÒHow you doinÕ?Ó I said, ÒIÕm good. Do you remember the pilot we did together?Ó He goes, ÒWhat was it about? Was it good?Ó And heÕs ninety-one, you know! And IÕm like, ÒWell, it was about this, this, and this.Ó And he says, ÒThatÕs a great idea! Why didnÕt we sell it?Ó ÒHow the fuck do I know, Norman? I thought weÕd sell it, too!Ó He says, ÒYou know what? LetÕs go back out and pitch it!Ó I mean, thatÕs a show.man, man. ItÕs in your blood. 
But you have sold several pilots. 
But I have yet to get a show on the air. IsnÕt that weird? HereÕs a funny story for you about a pilot. I knew Sherwood Schwartz for thirty years, and he once asked me, ÒYou know how I sold GilliganÕs Island?Ó I said, ÒHow?Ó He said, ÒI wrote this song, and IÕm on my way to the studio. I stop to get my gas filled, and I say to the gas station guy, ÔCan I sing something to you, and you tell me if youÕre gonna watch this show?ÕÓ So he sings the whole thing, and the guy says, ÒThat sounds like a good show to me.Ó Sherwood goes, ÒThatÕs what I thought!Ó So thatÕs how he sold it. And I thought, ÒWow, I guess you gotta sell shows by writ.ing the opening song.Ó 
So when I pitched my first pilot, I wrote the theme, and my ex-husband, whoÕs a Broadway orchestrator, did the whole soundtrack. When I went in, I brought a boom box, and I started pitching, and then I hit the button and sang the whole opening to the head of development at CBS. And he was open-mouthed, staringÑand I was thinking, ÒOh my God, I shouldnÕt have done this.Ó But he loved it and called the entire staff into his office to hear me sing it again. I didnÕt sell the show, but they loved the song. It was so embarrassing Õcause I immediately figured out that singing the theme song at a pitch wasnÕt exactly the norm in Hollywood! 
So do you really want a show on the air, Dava? Is that everyoneÕs dream? 
I really wanted it, and maybe it will never happen. I donÕt want to work on shit; I donÕt care enough. I wanna do something that makes me think. I have a pilot right now that Susan SarandonÕs reading. ItÕs about a mother and daughter, and itÕs for her and her daughter. ThatÕs what I would be proud ofÑwriting a womanÕs show about something that matters. So weÕll see; thereÕs still time. But if not, then IÕm content knowing that what I did do was as memorable as they come. 
A wonderful place to be in life. 
IÕm so honored. I really am. 
10 

LARRY CHARLES 
A
s a writer/producer/director, Larry Charles is best known for working alongside Larry David on Seinfeld and Curb Your Enthusiasm. He also directed Curb, as well as the films Borat and Bruno and the documentary Re.ligulous, starring Bill Maher. 
In this chapter, he reflects on the influence of drugs on his early years and how selling drugs actually helped launch his career in comedy. His first TV writing job was for the 1980s late night comedy show Fridays. Subsequent credits include The Arsenio Hall Show, Mad about You, Dilbert, The Tick, and Entourage. In 2015, he helped develop and executive-produced the TV series The Comedians, starring Billy Crystal. When we talked, he had just finished that show and was glad Òto have a minute to breathe.Ó 
Before we talk about your writing career, Larry, is it true you once thought of being a rabbi? 
When I was a kid growing up in Brighton, there was a period of my life, that very gullible period, when a lot of people probably do cross over into a more orthodox religion, whether it be Islam or Judaism or ChristianityÑjust at that moment when youÕre vulnerable to God. I got very deeply into it, to the point where I was wearing tzitzit and was thinking seriously about becoming a rabbi. But the rabbis at my Hebrew school were so discouraging as people that they virtually turned off that entire generation to Judaism. 
Then how did you end up in comedy? 
I dropped out of NYU film school after a semester and drove out here on kind of an impulse without really knowing what to do or knowing anybody. I had read as a kid that my idol, Woody Allen, was from the same neighborhood as me in Brooklyn, and we have the same birthdayÑso I felt very inspired, muse-like, about him. And also, IÕd read that one of the first things he ever did was to sell jokes to people. So when I came out to California, I got a job as a parking valet, and I started writing jokes. This was before computers, and IÕm talking about handwritten jokes because I didnÕt really type. IÕd fill up a piece of paper with jokes. And then I thought, ÒIÕll just go stand in front of The Comedy Store, and when I see a comedian I recognize, IÕll say, ÔDo you wanna buy a joke?ÕÓ 
So I stood in front of the Comedy Store, and very quickly, all these comedi.ans stopped and checked out my jokes. They were all sort of starting; Jay Leno and David Letterman actually were the two kings, so to speak, of The Comedy Store at that time. They were equally popular, equally funnyÑitÕs ironic how their destinies entwined from that moment forward. But theyÕd look at a joke and say, ÒYeah, I think thatÕs funny. If I can try it out onstage, and it gets a laugh, IÕll give you ten bucks.Ó So it was like a consignment situation. And I remember standing outside and looking through The Comedy Store window, watching them on stage doing my joke, getting a laughÑand knowing IÕd made ten bucks! And so from that, I started writing for comedians. And I got to know a comedian named Daryl Eigus, who was cast on a show called Fridays, and he recommended me for the show. 

Larry Charles. Paramount Pictures / Photofest © Paramount Pictures 
Did David or Jay buy any jokes from you? 
Jay Leno was always supportive and encouraging. He bought material from me, and weÕve remained on good terms all these years. I always thought he was a very cool guy; thatÕs the only way IÕd ever describe him. He actually bought one of my first jokes. It was something like, ÒDelta Airlines: the only airline run by professionals. So whatÕs on the other airlinesÑvolunteers?Ó I think he even did it on The Tonight Show. 
I heard that you were once heavily into drugs. How would you say they in.fluenced your comedy and your writing? 
I think drugs have influenced everything, actually. One of the things that sep.arates me from Larry David to some degree is the ten or so years between us. At some point after heÕs an adult and IÕm still a teenager, drugs become a pervasive part of the culture. I mean, people were taking various drugs, not even caring what they were, quite frankly. They didnÕt come in a bottle with a Rx on it; they were something that was sold in the street. And you hoped you got lucky. And the experience of drugs definitely expanded peopleÑand certainly me being one of themÑin different directions than had previously been explored. 
And youÕve compared your selling jokes to selling drugs? 
At one point, I sold loose joints at concerts at Madison Square Garden, and I was pretty good at that. And I used some of those sales techniques. It was something I knew how to do, and I was able to transpose the skills from one product to another. 
Could you comment briefly on what you learned from working on Fridays and how that job influenced your later writing? 
The great thing about Fridays for me was that you were put in charge of your own sketch and thus had to be responsible for shepherding it through the process and on the air. This was an education better than any film school could provide. And since there were no rules, you learned very early that the only thing you could trust were your instincts. 
You mentioned rules. What rules of comedy do you follow, if any? 
I think the only rule in comedy thatÕs really worth adhering to is that there are no rules. As soon as you start to codify it, itÕs no longer funny. I think what makes someone actually erupt with that sound of laughter is still pretty much of a mystery. And it comes from all kinds of sources, in all kinds of ways, on all kinds of levelsÑand itÕs very hard to say, ÒWell, hereÕs a rule.Ó The best comedy has been about breaking those rules. Every great comedian you love or who has influenced you, every great comedy, and every great funny thought really comes out of nowhereÑthereÕs no preparation for it; thereÕs no minor league for com.edy. ThereÕs no school or college for it. ThatÕs why you see such a random group of people attracted to it and magnetized by it. 
And an absence of rules certainly played a part in the development of Sein.feld. HowÕd you become involved with that show? 
I had met Larry on Fridays, and we became very close, and he was like a mentor to me. And when he was preparing Seinfeld as a pilot, he gave me the first few scripts to read. I thought they were brilliant, like nothing IÕd ever read. He asked me if IÕd work on it. He said, ÒWeÕll do thirteen shows, and itÕll prob.ably get canceled, but weÕll get paid.Ó And it turns out, it went on longer than thirteen episodes. 
Can you characterize the showÕs logic? 
This is from all of our lives basically, all the people who worked on that showÑwe were basically people who said, ÒWhy?Ó When we were told things that were status quo in our lives, many times we would just go, ÒWhy?Ó And youÕd find out there really is no Òwhy.Ó [Laughs] That this is all a big front, these rules and these regulations, and all these kinds of things. If you just question them, suddenly the whole thing collapses. And that was a main philosophy of Seinfeld: Look at things the way nobody else looks at them. And I think that was something Larry and Jerry naturally brought to the show because thatÕs the way they were. 
Can you explain the Seinfeld structure? 
I think there are a lot of factors that went into it. It started with Larry Da.vidÕs basic idea of not really having any structure beyond just the mundane events that might occur during the course of doing something, like going to a Chinese restaurant. The original conceit was just really comedians hanging out and how they get their material; you see the mundanity of their lives and how that would be turned into comedy material. So thatÕs the initial source of it, which is very short-story like. ItÕs almost more of a one-camera idea, if you think about it, at a time when the three-camera live-audience form was still very economical and popular. 
I think Larry resisted the idea of a plot, quote unquote, because the plots of sitcoms are generally lame; theyÕre sort of moralistic, and theyÕre not really funny. And we were really interested in doing a laugh-out-loud funny show, and we drew our inspiration from shows of previous generations, like The Abbott and Costello Show, which had a level of funniness that made us laugh out loud as kids. We wanted to recapture that on some level. But very early on, Larry had pressure on him from the network to give the episodes some structure, give them some plot, give them some storyÑuse the form a little bit more. 
Now in a way, he was breaking the form right there by not really having any of that, and that show would have been great also. But he finally figured out a way to tell a story with these characters and this kind of humor that would in and of itself be funnyÑso that the twists and turns of the plot would also be funny. And a lot of time was spent experimenting with various methods of doing that, intersecting stories and building stories, and taking them to absurd places and absurd climaxes, and trusting our instincts along the way of what worked, what made us laugh. Keep in mind that this was all done while the show was not really successful. We felt the show probably wouldnÕt make it. [Laughs] And in a way, it gave us an abandon when we approached the creativity of these questions; we did exactly what was funny to us. And Larry and Jerry both sort of led the way in terms of, ÒWeÕll just do this, weÕll do it exactly the way we want itÑand whatever happens, happens.Ó Once you have that attitude, itÕs liberating. And I think that led to the first couple of episodes with really funny stories, where the twists and turns were so crazy and yet believable at the same timeÑand it started to find its Seinfeld-ness, if you know what I mean. It started to become the show that people are familiar with. So it was a bit of an experiment, which is cool. ItÕs great when you get a chance to experiment. The first few episodes were really experiments in trying to figure out what the formula for the show was; how do you crack the code of the show? And it took a while. 
Did you have a system for interweaving the stories?
