For a while we had a board up on the wall with all the charactersÕ names, and we didnÕt feel we had an episode unless we could check off each characterÕs name as having a story they were driving forward. If all four of them had a story, then we knew theyÕd intersect in a funny way, and all those accidents and coin.cidences and funny twists and turns would occur. 
And in the early days, we were all guys doing the show, and we were sort of immature, and we really didnÕt know how to write for women at all. And Julia was getting a paucity of good, strong comedy material. We didnÕt even know her full potential. I remember her coming into the office upset about the lack of good stuff. And we thought, ÒYeah, we were squandering her because we were incapable of writing that material.Ó 
And George had a story about a girl from out of town that heÕd been dating, and she came over to stay with him. And while she stayed with him, the relation.ship sort of soured for him but not for her. When it came time for her to leave for the airport to catch her plane, George was very relieved. But she decided instead that she was going to stay. And so then he had to figure out a way to get her to the airport. And normally that would have been a perfect George story, and a decision was made to give it to Julia instead and see what sheÕd do with it. Just give a guyÕs story to Julia. And that worked great. The story was super funny, and she was fantastic in it; she tapped into stuff she hadnÕt had a chance to do and opened up that character in a way none of us had seen before. And suddenly we realized, you write for her the same as you write for everybody else. And she started to flower under that system. 
But in later episodes, did you have a system for integrating stories? Did you eventually get it down to a science? 
I donÕt think comedy writing can ever really be a science, and especially a show like Seinfeld, where everything underwent a certain scrutiny. You couldnÕt just take the lazy way out. A lot of sitcoms over the years reach a point where they know they have go-to material. They have go-to characters and go-to hooks, and they start relying too heavily on those, and the show loses the edge that made it funny in the first place. And with Seinfeld, Jerry wasnÕt going to allow that to happen, and Larry wasnÕt going to allow that to happenÑso there was al.ways a heavy-duty scrutiny on every piece of material and on how it fit together. On each episode of Seinfeld, we tried to spend the kind of time thatÕs now spent on a cable episode. Our goal was to make each one great. 
I think you succeeded. Did Larry have a system for it? 
No, because each episode presented its own set of challenges, and again, this goes back to having rules. You couldnÕt say, ÒOK, it worked there; letÕs just do it again here.Ó Sometimes thatÕll work, but sometimes it wonÕt, and there are a lot of contingencies to all those kinds of choices. But we had a smart enough group of people to embrace a new set of challenges with each episode. And thatÕs what kept the show fresh, too. 
How did you hit upon the showÕs framework of short, quick scenes? 
That had to do with the fact that weÕd never written a sitcom before; we didnÕt know what the rules of the sitcom were. Again that word rules. So we wrote shows that we thought were funny, and as it turned out, those shows had many shorter scenes, more cinematic-like. And we found a way to do that in a three-camera live-audience format. Up until then, shows like All in the Family or Sanford and Son had six scenes basically: three scenes, an act break, and then three more scenes. And most of it took place in the house. People would come into the living room with a story: ÒYou wonÕt believe what just happened to me out there! I got hit by a truck, and blah blah blah.Ó We started to think about showing it. And that led to many, many more scenes. And I think today youÕll see most sitcoms have many, many more scenes as a result. 
Can you describe the interrelationships among the Seinfeld characters? 
I think they were very, very close friends. TheyÕre people who have known each other for a very long time. They love each other on a certain level, they need each other on a certain level, and after that many years of friendshipÑthey resent each other on a certain level, as well. I think itÕs a very real portrayal of the dysfunctional/functional dynamic of friendship. 

Julia Louis-Dreyfus, Jason Alexander, Jerry Seinfeld, and Michael Richards. NBC / Columbia TriStar Television / Photofest © NBC / Columbia TriStar Television. Photog.rapher: Wren Maloney 
Why do you think narcissism is so funny? 
Narcissism is one of the many characteristics that might be funny if executed well. No particular characteristic is inherently funny unless itÕs carried out in a humorous way. The same characteristic, say narcissism, could be executed in a way that would make you weep. It really depends on your approach to it, which is true about all subject matter. 
Tell me something about Larry David. YouÕve obviously had plenty of chances to observe him writing. 
Absolutely. Hours and hours and hours; we also wrote together on Fridays, so I watched him write, and I sat with him when he wrote, and we wrote to.gether. IÕve experienced almost every permutation of that scenario. He has a yel.low pad, and he just scrawls and scrawls across that pad. When heÕs writing, itÕs like a mad-genius type of thing in a movie, like a Mozart or a Beethoven seized with a symphony. HeÕs just gotta get it down on paper. ThatÕs how he writes. 
What would you say is the essence of his genius? 
The essence of Larry DavidÕs genius is he has a completely unique perspec.tive on life, which might qualify him as genius and brilliant. These words are obviously bandied about a lot now, and because of it, their meaning becomes very diluted. But heÕs somebody I really believe falls into that category. He just sees things that nobody else sees. He just thinks about things that nobody else thinks about. And then when he verbalized it, he suddenly realized he had tapped into this deep reservoir inside of everybody. ThatÕs a powerful thing. He communicates on a very primal, visceral level, tapping into the darkest recesses of our consciousness. And not on purpose, not that heÕs calculated about itÑit just is who he is. And thatÕs part of the mystery of it. His thoughts resonate with people. And you canÕt predict that. This goes back to the rulesÑyou canÕt predict a Larry David or a Seinfeld. The predictions that would come out of what had previously existed would never lead you to them. So thatÕs whatÕs cool about it. You donÕt know whatÕs going to happen next, so your rule is going to be rendered moot by the next person, by the next Sam Kinison or Richard Pryor or George Carlin or Lenny Bruce or whoever it is. 
Do you have any favorite anecdotes about Larry? 
I think my favorite is a very Larry David anecdote that to me illustrates who he is and why heÕs such a unique American original. We were on Wednesday nights at 9:30 for a couple of years, and NBC decided they wanted to move us to Thursday night, the powerhouse night, after Cheers. They went to Larry and presented this to him, and he said, ÒAnybody who doesnÕt watch us on Wednes.day night can go fuck themselves.Ó And he refused to allow the show to move to Thursday night. And Jerry and all the other producers and all the Castle Rock people just inveigled him finally to relent. And of course, it moved to Thursday nights and became the massive hit that it became. 
Did you witness Larry and JerryÕs process together? 
I did witness that on occasion as well, sure. And sometimes they would act it out and read it as they were writing it. But I think what was cool about that show was there was very little that separated working from our normal mode of hanging out and just being funny and just riffing. There was a certain focus to it when it was work, but it was pretty much the same process as life. And I think that was reflected in the show. 
Writer Peter Mehlman said you used to argue with Larry. YouÕd have shout.ing matches? 
[Laughs] My memory is not of us really shouting at each other so much but often us shouting at somebody else. And particularly back thenÑmuch more so back then, I would sayÑwe were very volatile Brooklyn guys who didnÕt really know how to channel our emotions, and we definitely had temper tantrums occa.sionally. I know Larry hasÑand IÕm trying toÑgrow up. So weÕre doing our best. 
Who would you yell at? 
Mainly at God, I would say. [Laughs] 
IÕve also heard that you used to hang out at JerryÕs Deli after the show. Do you have any particular memories from there? 
I remember Andy Kaufman working as a busboy there. The only moments I really remember vividly are racing with Jerry from JerryÕs up Laurel Canyon to Mulholland and losing virtually every single time. I had a Saab, and he had a Porsche. HeÕd give me a head start, and IÕd see him coming up in my rearview mirror. He always passed me at Mulholland. 
What do you feel distinguishes the Seinfeld episodes you wrote? 
I was encouraged to use my own voice within the Seinfeld characters. So although mine are totally Seinfeld episodes, they may go to darker places, and there may be a little bit more reliance on, say, Kramer in some of them. I killed Jerry in an episode, you know. I was looking for ways to experiment with the form, and they very much encouraged me to do that. I think thatÕs what probably distinguishes my episodes especially because IÕm mainly there at the early part of the formation of this. 
And of the ones you wrote, which do you think best defines the series? 
I think ÒThe OutingÓ [S4E17] is like that. That was the season we won the Emmy for best show, and there were just a lot of great shows from it. Another of my favorites, ÒThe Bubble BoyÓ [S4E7], is also from it. Larry and I wrote that together. And I think the greatest moment of the show might be in ÒThe ContestÓ [S4E11],1 with Kramer storming into JerryÕs apartment with his cash and slamming it on the counter and saying, ÒIÕm out.Ó ThatÕs the one that pops into my head. 
I was going to ask you about ÒThe Outing.Ó Do you remember where the idea for it came from? 
There were always these weird rumors about Jerry that were silly, and he was very good humored about them. And weÕd talk about how Abbott and Costello slept in the same bedroom with the door closed, and we donÕt know what was going on thereÑweÕd just have fun with that subject. And then I thought, ÒWhat if Jerry and George were mistaken for a gay couple, and what level of media would that attract?Ó and all that stuff. And we talked about it a lot for a while, as we had ÒThe Contest.Ó Both of those episodes were sort of bandied about the season before, even. And no one was quite ready to do them, for a variety of reasons. In the case of ÒThe Outing,Ó the question was, ÒHow do you glean humor from this without being offensive? Is there a way to do this?Ó And I re.member being in the room with them, but I donÕt remember if this was already in the script once or if we were just talking about it thenÑbut I think it was Jerry or Larry who said itÑthe phrase Ònot that thereÕs anything wrong with itÓ came up at that moment. And it seemed like, ÒYou know what? If you could just keep repeating that, almost like a joke mantra, that might allow you to do whatever you needed to do in the episode.Ó And indeed, that was liberating, that was the key that unlocked the door to that particular episode. As Òmaster of my domainÓ was the key that unlocked the door to ÒThe Contest.Ó 
Why do you think one of the earliest episodes, ÒThe Chinese RestaurantÓ [S2E11], is considered such a classic? 
If I recall correctly, thatÕs the episode where we had a big discussion about the fact that we needed a story. We thought, ÒWe canÕt just have them waiting for a table the whole twenty-two minutes.Ó And of course, we could have; it wouldÕve been greatÑbut it was like the line in the sand had been drawn, and the network was insistent on some kind of story. And we finally put a clock on that episode, but itÕs still a pretty minor part of the story. In essence, the story still is the three of them waiting for a table. And so what you have there in a three-camera sitcom is a naturalistic show thatÕs more like a one-camera typeÑalmost like a Paddy Chayefsky short or somethingÑabout three people waiting to get a table at a Chinese restaurant. And it was an incredibly fresh idea at that moment; there was nothing being done like that. Everything was heavily plotted, heavily contrived, very moralistic at the end, very broad humorÑand here was this very finely wrought short story about waiting for a table at a restaurant. 
Tell me about ÒThe Marine BiologistÓ [S5E14]. 
First of all, that was a great episode. But what really made it is the two sto.ries merged in a beautifully poetic way. I mean, Kramer hitting the golf balls, and George then telling that salty tale. ThereÕs something very inspiring about GeorgeÕs speechÑeven the way he pulls out the golf ball with a kind of a flour.ish to show it off. ThereÕs just something very transcendent about that moment. And that happens sometimes; everything just merges together so beautifully you donÕt have to force anything. ItÕs a very organic kind of process, and you just bow to that; you just acknowledge that. 
Was his speech planned from the beginning? 
I donÕt think it was. But it depends how you define the beginning. There are a lot of beginnings in things like this. Sometimes ideas kind of bounce around for months, if not seasons, before someone figures out a way to execute them. 
ÒThe Bubble BoyÓ is another classic. What inspired the idea for it? 
Like with ÒMarine BiologistÓÑand which is often the caseÑa series of disparate elements was being bandied about: How can those elements merge together into a story? One of the writers, Bill Masters, was telling a story about playing Trivial Pursuit, and he was making some point in it. And he mentioned that there was a typo on the card, and so instead of saying moors, it said moops. And everybody went, ÒWait, forget about the other partÑthatÕs the story, thatÕs a funny story!Ó And then it became, ÒOK, how does this moops thing happen?Ó And I had been playing around with the idea of a bubble boy. Somehow, a bubble boy. I just liked that image in a comedy; it seemed interesting to me. And the story of the autograph Jerry wants back and canÕt get backÑso suddenly there are a lot of elements, and you start to see a flow to itÑand suddenly these pieces start falling together. And itÕs again this process that leads to a coherent story in some way. 
It must be so satisfying when you get to that point. 
ItÕs fantastic, yes. I think everybody would agree that to have this job is to be very lucky, to get paid for your thoughts and to be able to have that kind of a resolution thatÕs so exhilarating. Yes, thatÕs the part of the experience that should be savored because itÕs an incredible, special thing. 
Did you ever think the show went too far, as in poor taste? 
Oh my God, no. Never. And as I indicated before, I think the key to some.thing being funny is not so much the subject matter; weÕve proved both on Seinfeld and Curb that virtually any subject matter can be funny if you find the right angle. ItÕs all about your way in. 
In your opinion, did the show have any weaknesses? 
I wouldnÕt say it was a weakness, but I felt it was a limitationÑand that was the form itself. As it turned out, it was a great show, and it worked in that format, but I think a lot of the ideas were somewhat inhibited by the form. It had to be done in front of a live audience, although increasingly, as time went on, a lot of it was not done in front of a live audience. And I would love to have seen a one-camera version of Seinfeld. I think that vibeÑwhich is more evident in a show like Curb and other shows subsequentlyÑwould have been a cool thing for Seinfeld, too. The year we were able to have the Seinfeld cast on Curb was one of the most thrilling things for me because it was great to be able to revisit those characters in this much more modern, more three-dimensional context. Seeing them as themselves versus their personas and how they played off of that with the awareness of the camera that they werenÕt allowed to have on the showÑit was like exploring Seinfeld on another level. 
Can you comment on SeinfeldÕs place in TV history? 
The Yale professor Harold Bloom has a book called The Anxiety of Influence, which talks about how all writers are sort of made anxious by the presence of Shakespeare hovering over them. And I think thatÕs how Seinfeld is: ItÕs like itÕs the anxiety of influence in that itÕs very hard to reach the level of writing and performing that Seinfeld reached. ItÕs a very, very singular show. ItÕs not the only show thatÕs done it, but itÕs a very, very singular example. And I think in that way, itÕs had an impact. People have tried to copy it, and certainly people have been derivative of it, but the difference is very clear between it and its imitators. 
And I think for executives to a large degree, it was not a good role model be.cause it did break so many rules. And it meant that things could be out of control and succeed but still be out of their control. And I donÕt think that was a message 

Seinfeld 
Steve Skrovan (Writer/Program Consultant): My favorite memory from Seinfeld was hanging out with the other three guys who were hired at the same time as me: Bob Shaw, Jon Hayman, and Bill Masters. The three of us hung out in this one room and just spent all day telling each other stories. 
So one day I come into work wearing a Brazilian soccer shirt my sister-in-law had given me as a gift. It was black and red and had a square neck with a collar. I walk into LarryÕs office to ask him something, and the first thing he says is, ÒHey, what about that shirt?Ó And I say, ÒWhat about it?Ó He says, ÒHas Jerry seen that?Ó ÒNo, I donÕt think heÕs in yet.Ó ÒBetter not let Jerry see that shirt.Ó ÒWhatÕs wrong with it?Ó ÒItÕs a weird shirt.Ó 
I told the other writers about that exchange and how Larry is sure Jerry will make some snide comment about my shirt. So we made five-dollar bets on how long it would take for Jerry to say something. And we just waited for him to come into our room. He comes in, and Bill starts a stop.watch, so thereÕs this whole other thing going on among us, as the clock ticks down, that Jerry doesnÕt know about. And if he doesnÕt mention the shirt within three minutes, I win the bet. A minute goes by, and he doesnÕt even look at me. A minute and a half goes by; he may have glanced around but doesnÕt say anything. HeÕs about to leave maybe, and somebody else says something to keep him in the room talking. 
IÕm getting cocky, and so I say something, just to make sure Jerry looks at me. He looks at me and responds. ItÕs 2:45, heÕs heading out the door, and I actually get up and make myself visible to him. HeÕs walking out the door, and he turns aroundÑand at the 2:55 mark, he looks at me and says, ÒSo whatÕs goinÕ on with that shirt?Ó And we all explode in laughter, to the point where JerryÕs startled, and he jumps back. He was like, ÒWhoa!Ó And then we filled him in on the backstory about all the bets on how long it would take him to mention my square-necked soccer shirt. 
We thought of using an idea like that in a script, and it didnÕt go. But a few years later, Larry told that story to Charlie Rose on CharlieÕs show, probably in response to a question like, ÒWhat was it like behind the scenes?Ó I always wondered if ÒThe Puffy ShirtÓ episode [S5E2] came out of LarryÕs remembering that story. 
What I remember about ÒThe ContestÓ is that we had a bunch of ideas we wanted to pitch to Larry, and it was hard to get his attention because he was so busy. He was helping direct on floor, he was writing, he was doing everything. So we suggested having dinner with him at the Daily Grill in Studio City. And at dinner we pitched a bunch of ideas, probably all of which he didnÕt care for. And as I recall, Bob Shaw had some idea about masturbation. And Larry says, ÒIÕm already doing that show. I want GeorgeÕs mother to catch him masturbating.Ó And this is very, very edgy for 1992; this is coming off the Cosby era of television. As far as I know, masturbation had never been dealt with in a sitcom. But that was Larry, always looking for the thing he hadnÕt seen before. 
The bet is not a unique structure of a show; a lot of television episodes involve people betting and some sort of contest. What made this unique was this was a contest about masturbation. And the ingenious thing Larry did was that he never mentioned the word masturbation. He came up with that brilliant euphemism master of your domain. 
Bill Masters (Writer/Program Consultant): Larry came in and said heÕs doing an episode about a contest, and he needs an Elaine story. We all said, ÒWhy canÕt she be in the contest, too?Ó And he said, ÒGirls donÕt do that.Ó We said, ÒYes, they do, and itÕs funny.Ó He goes, ÒWho would women fantasize about?Ó And Skrovan said, ÒJFK Jr.Ó And Larry starts laughing just at that because JFK Jr. is a New York persona. He just loved that idea. So he gave up the idea of having an Elaine story, and he put her in with ÒThe Contest.Ó 
[One day] Skrovan and I were going into LarryÕs office to pitch an idea for a show, and his assistant said, ÒLarry is sick as a dog.Ó So we say, ÒOK, weÕll pitch it when he comes back.Ó She says, ÒNo, no, he wants you to come over to his house and pitch it.Ó And we didnÕt want to go up to a sick guy, you knowÑbut she says we have to do it. 
When you pitch an idea, especially when itÕs you and another writer, and itÕs a comedyÑyouÕve worked out different things like, ÒIÕm gonna say this, and then youÕre gonna come in with this punch line, and itÕs gonna be really funny!Ó And since weÕre former stand-ups, weÕre gonna go in there and just blow Larry away. We started pitching this idea, and in the middle of it, Larry got up, went into the bathroom next to the room we were in, and we could hear him vomiting. 
And I said to Skrovan, ÒSoÑhow do you think itÕs goinÕ?Ó And Skrovan started laughing. Larry comes out, and heÕs all apologetic: ÒIÕm really sorry, IÕm really sorry.Ó Skrovan tells him what just happened, and he starts laugh.ing and says, ÒThatÕs really good, thatÕs really good!Ó And we say, ÒYou mean the story idea?Ó And he says, ÒNo, no, thatÕs shit. WeÕre not gonna do that storyÑbut weÕre gonna do this.Ó 
And then he wrote that into the pilot episode where Jerry and George are pitching to the head of NBC, and the guy has food poisoning [ÒThe Shoes,Ó S4E16]. And that to me was the perfect Seinfeld moment because the show was so great at taking real things and making them happen to the characters in a realistic way. 
ÒThe PickÓ [S4E13] is the episode where Kramer takes a picture of Elaine for her Christmas card and her nipple is showing. A guy came in to pitch that, and he showed us the picture, and it was a picture of a man in a bathrobe totally naked underneath. HeÕs next to the Christmas tree, and his legs are open, and you can see everything. And kids are opening up gifts. And obviously the person who took the picture didnÕt see everything until it came back from the Fotomat, and then they said, ÒOh my God. Look!Ó 
It doesnÕt matter to Larry what the guyÕs idea for the story is. What Larry wants is that moment. So he has to work to figure out who that person is. And then he comes up with ElaineÕs nipple, which KramerÕs responsible for, and the intricacies of that take so much time, plus at the same time, it has to work into the year-long story line of George and Jerry trying to write a show: George is starting a relationship with a woman from NBC, whom heÕs later going to kill with the envelopes; her father is a closet homosexual who has letters from John Cheever that get burned in the fire. All these things that happen are so intricate and complicated, and they have to be woven in. If you had a regular TV show, the next weekÕs episode could be a stand-alone show about your charactersÑthe clich is, ÒJerry has jury duty.Ó You can just plug that in, and itÕll give you a week where you donÕt have to work as hard on all the other stuff. But you couldnÕt do that on Seinfeld. 
Peter Mehlman (Writer/Producer): I would say the best kind of moments were just sitting around, talking stuff. Like when I walked into Larry and JerryÕs office the morning after spending my first night in my new house, and I said, ÒI donÕt see how I could ever get married. I love this house so much. How could I share it?Ó Larry says, ÒThatÕs why the key to a good marriage is having a huge house.Ó And Jerry says, ÒNahÑthey find ya.Ó 
My first year on Seinfeld, there was a lot of nervousness among some of the other writers they hired. And out of nervousness, people would say stupid things, and Larry would never forget. [Laughs] Since Larry was re.writing all these scripts from A to Z, he felt he should have half-credit on them. HeÕd never been on a sitcom before, so he didnÕt know that kind of work is supposedly part of your job. But one of the other writers got very angry that Larry was going to do that. And the funny thing is, I didnÕt know what I was doing, eitherÑand I told Larry, ÒOh, you should get half. Why shouldnÕt you get credit if youÕre writing the whole thing!Ó 
I think the other guy was penny-wise, pound-foolish at that point be.cause he was gone. And in fact, none of the other writers who thought they knew so much and talked too much got rehired. So at that point I decided my motto should be, ÒShut up and learn.Ó And I think thatÕs a good way of approaching anything new: Just shut up and learn. 
One day I was at my health club talking to a friend, and this girl walked by with ample breasts. And I happened to look at her, and my friend said, ÒOh God, come on, theyÕre fake.Ó And at first, I was just thinking that would make a great little moment in a show; the characters could have that little run of dialogue and then go back to whatever the discussion was. But I suddenly realized that it could be a whole story lineÑtrying to find out if they are or theyÕre not. And that became ÒThe ImplantÓ [S4E19]. 
Part of it is not so much achieving the final product; itÕs how things led up to it. When I wrote ÒThe Implant,Ó I had a bunch of really disparate funny scenes, not even story linesÑjust four or five scenes that always seemed like theyÕd be really funny. And somehow, I managed to squish them all together into four stories. And I said, ÒWow. IÕve nailed it! ThereÕs no stopping me now.Ó It never worked again. The process never worked the same way twice. 
ÒThe HamptonsÓ [S5E20] was inspired by the summer IÕd shared a house in the Hamptons with a friend, and his girlfriend went topless in front of everybody. And I just remembered thinking, ÒGod, all that work heÕd put in to get thereÑand then she just gives it to the rest of us for free!Ó That probably occurred in 1984, so it was nine years later that I happened to think, ÒOh, that could be a good episode.Ó 
In this episode, Larry came up with the idea of George coming out of the pool just before JerryÕs girlfriend sees him naked. He said to me, ÒAnd the pool is cold.Ó And I said, ÒOh, you mean like shrinkage?Ó And he goes, ÒYeah. Use that word a lot.Ó 
they were all that thrilled with! It was a very subversive show; it kind of came out like a weed through the cracks in the sidewalk, and people found it on their own and responded to it very, very authentically. And at that time everything was very manipulated to elicit a certain response, and here was something that didnÕt fit any of those boundaries. 
Along those lines, IÕd like your perspective on how sitcom writing itself has evolved. 
I think there are a lot of different schools of sitcom writing, and theyÕre all fascinating to me. You have Norman Lear, who turned sitcoms into these hilari.ous political polemics, and that was unprecedented at the time. You look at All in the Family or Maude or Good Times or any of those showsÑtheyÕll blow your mind with the subject matter theyÕre dealing with. Incredible. 
You also have the Jim Brooks strain with Mary Tyler Moore. Suddenly people werenÕt doing so much the setup and punch line; they were conversing in a funny way and getting big laughs in front of an audience. And the Jim Brooks line includes Jim Burrows as a very seminal director of that style of humor, which leads to Friends and things like that also. ThereÕs also the hook-laden sit.com like Get Smart, which is a kind of a concept comedy. That was very popular for a while, and every now and then, a new one comes along thatÕs based very heavily on concept that winds up hooking the audience for a while. 
ThereÕs also another and very important one, and maybe the one that con.nects most to SeinfeldÑand thatÕs the autobiographical one. And maybe that starts with Jack Benny or George Burns and works through Lucy and comes out through the sophistication of The Dick Van Dyke Show, written and created by Carl ReinerÑwhoÕs obviously an influence on Jim Brooks and those shows. So that strain or thread of the real person speaking to you, the audience, is also a very rich thread that had its influences on Seinfeld as well. 
What do you feel is the most rewarding aspect of your TV writing? 
The most rewarding was when it worked, when you had a script that was produced, and it did great; it killed. If you have a live audience, you want the live audience to go crazy. And when they did, that was a great feeling. The other side to that is there was a lot of pressure and anxiety leading up to it. It didnÕt just come; itÕs something you had to constantly excavate your soul to get to and then pray that someone else agreed. And there was a lot of pressure with that. 
As a sitcom writer, were you always confident that you could write funny? It sounds like you werenÕt. 
No, not at all. Not ever. Not from one day to the next. 
Have you ever really been stuck? 
I have. Seinfeld was a show that had a tremendous amount of pressure and rigor, and trying to come up with a story was a challenge sometimes, absolutely. But everything IÕve ever worked on, if IÕm doing it right, is hard. You know, it shouldnÕt come that easily. Sometimes it does, and thatÕs great, and you just ac.cept that. But sometimes itÕs very hard, very frustrating. 
How do you break through writerÕs block? 
I think itÕs a natural part of the process; you just have to stay with it. You have to keep putting stuff down on paper; you have to keep doing exactly what youÕve been doing. Just like if a batter has a slump, he pretty much has to work through the slump. And sometimes you come out the other side much better for it. 
And how do feel when youÕre in the zone, writing most creatively? 
When youÕre in the zone, youÕre not thinking or feeling. You just are in the zone. And thatÕs not really of the earth. ItÕs like youÕre somewhere else; youÕre there, not here. So any feelings you might describe arenÕt really relevant until you come out of it. 
How does being a comedy writer inform your view of the world? 
I think itÕs the other way around in some way; I think my view of the world is what led me to be a comedy writer. 
And how would you characterize your view of the world? 
Skeptical. Bleak. Scary. Overwhelming. Crazy. Absurd. 
Larry, what do you consider the best work of your career? 
Wow. IÕm very proud of my writing on Seinfeld. IÕm very proud of my direct.ing of Curb. IÕm very proud of Borat and Bruno and Religulous. IÕm super happy with all those. You could always look at everything and wish you could do it all over again, but those came out pretty good. 
And what do you want people to take away from your work these days? 
I just want people to be excited and to have their minds blown by whatever I do. I try to make something thatÕs gonna change everythingÑand see how close I get.

DAVID LEE 
D
avid Lee is a multiple Emmy AwardÐwinning writer/producer/director who broke into the business writing for The Jeffersons. He went on to cocreate the series Wings, Encore! Encore!, and the long-running hit Frasier. He learned of his Peabody Award for Frasier on a day that was particularly memo.rable: Within an hour of seeing his name in lights on a sign in Times Square congratulating him, a friend arranged for him to meet and chat with artist Roy Lichtenstein in his studio while he was painting. Of the experience David says, ÒIt was one of those days that you sort of pinch yourself. I had to take to my fainting couch afterwards!Ó 
The common denominator of all DavidÕs shows is his commitment to quality. The philosophy that drove him? ÒHowever good you think it is, you can always make it better.Ó 
David, IÕll start by asking what your background was for this field. 
I never took a writing class, but I knew I wanted to be in show business from a very young age. When I was ten, an adult cousin took me to see the national tour of My Fair Lady at the Biltmore Theatre in downtown LA. And I have such a clear, vivid memory of sitting in the audience, watching this thing that was like nothing I had ever seen before, and having the thought, ÒI want to do that.Ó I guess thatÕs whatÕs called being bitten. So thatÕs what I pursued. In college, I majored in music and theater, and my earliest job was with a theater company in Iowa. 
And how did you first get together with Peter Casey, your collaborator? 
Way back in the olden days, TV shows would send the scripts out at night to a company that typed and mimeographed them and then delivered them back to the studio in the morning. Peter and I met when we worked for a company like that; I was a proofreader and delivery person, and he was a typist. 
My star as an actor was not rising particularly fastÑor at allÑand we were chatting one day, and he told me he wanted to get into TV writing. And I went, ÒWow.Ó And he asked if IÕd ever thought of doing that, since I had a background with actors and that sort of stuff. I said, ÒWell, IÕll give it a shot. Do you want to try writing something?Ó So we just gave it a shot. We started writing hour-long cop dramas, and then at some point, somebody said, ÒYou guys are pretty funny. You should try writing a half-hour.Ó But we struggled for three and a half years, writing a lot of spec scripts with a lot of frustration before things finally started breaking loose for us. I think we wrote eleven spec scripts. 
And what was your first break? 
We wrote a Barney Miller, and our agent sent it to Barney Miller, and they hated it, which is not uncommon because usually the show you send your spec script to rejects it because they know the show so well that they immediately see all the errors. So then our agent sent it to Rhoda, and the Rhoda people liked it and called us in, and we had a pitch session. When we left, we went, ÒOh boy, weÕre on our way, weÕre on our way!ÓÑand almost before we got back to our apartment, the news came down that Rhoda had been canceled, so we were just suicidal. 
And then our agent said, ÒI got another call, and I sent your script over to The Jeffersons. They liked it, and they want you to come in for a story-pitch meeting.Ó So we got some ideas together and went over, and they liked one of our ideas and hired us to write it. So that was how we finally made our first sale in sitcom writing. And when we wrote that, we thought, ÒWeÕre on our wayÓÑ and that was the end of the season, so it was several months before they were hearing pitches again. And that was sort of the nadir of my existence because IÕd written a TV script, it had been made, it was on the airÑand I had to go back to my then-part-time job at a catering company! But the next season rolled around, and they asked us if we wanted to write another one, so we did. And then they asked if we wanted to be on staff, and we said, ÒWe donÕt know what that is, but I guess so.Ó So we became uncredited staff writers on The Jeffersons. And then they asked us if we wanted to be story editors so weÕd actually get screen credit, and we said, ÒWe donÕt know what that is, but sure, letÕs do that.Ó 
How would you characterize the tone of the Jeffersons room? 
It was very casual, very free for all; it was run by Michael Moye and Ron Leavitt, who would go on to create Married with Children. There was a raucous.ness to it, and it was fun. We had a great time. They were very supportive, and it was very funny, but there were a lot of pencils thrown into the acoustical ceiling. 
Why do writers do that? Because theyÕre bored? 
Yep. ItÕs something to do while youÕre sitting around for hours and hours. See, you have to know that a writersÕ room is a lot of sitting around. Hour after hour after hour. And so certain kinds of writers put the baseball cap on backwards and toss pencils into the acoustical ceiling and then go play teenage basketball over in the corner or a game of foosball out in the hallway, just to oc.cupy their mind. People say, ÒIt frees my mind so I can think of something else.Ó But I believe the sort of activity like playing little games in a writersÕ room is just messing around. 
The Jeffersons was a good place to get an education and learn how to do the day-by-day stuff, and it gave me several years of employment when I was first starting out. But it was also one of those shows where you went home at six oÕclock every night, no matter what shape the script was in. Which meant I had quite a shock when I got to Cheers, and we went down and saw a run-through and they said, ÒOK, I guess weÕre gonna order dinner tonight.Ó I went, ÒWhat?Ó ÒYeah, weÕre gonna order dinner and stay and rewrite the script.Ó So I kept checking my watch, and it was getting to be ten, eleven, twelve at night, and weÕre still only on page 30, and IÕm going, ÒWow, itÕs now two, three in the morn.ing, and weÕre still here?Ó And they said, ÒYeah, this is what we do.Ó So on that show, I learned that, no matter how good you think it is, you can always make it better. And most of the time that meant staying long hours. 
And then why did you leave The Jeffersons? 
After being there for four years or so, Peter and I felt we needed to try to move on with our career. At the time, Family Ties was very popular, and we shared the same agent with its creator, Gary David Goldberg. So the agent sent Gary a Jef.fersons episode that Peter and I had written. It was a perfectly good episode, and he refused to read it. He said he couldnÕt tell how good somebody wrote from a Jeffersons script. And our agent said, ÒThis is going to be a problem because, from a writing standpoint, The Jeffersons wasnÕt considered a good script to take out into the marketplace.Ó 
About the same time, Cheers came on the air. After meeting Glen and Les Charles at a TV conference, I told Peter we need to write a spec Cheers because nobodyÕs gonna read our Jeffersons script. And maybe the pivotal change in my life was writing that spec script because Glen and Les liked it, and that got us an opportunity to pitch for their show. 
How did you come up with the premise for the first Cheers script you sold? 
I recall going in to pitch ideas to Glen and Les Charles, and we sat in their officeÑand we could not come up with a story! I mean, this was gonna be our big chance to write for Cheers, and we couldnÕt come up with an idea. Produc.tion was over, and the guys were already on vacation and were really looking not to be doing this anymoreÑbut they were graciously taking this meeting with us. And after the first day, they said, ÒWell, come back. LetÕs try it again tomorrow.Ó And I canÕt remember exactly, but we may not have found the story even that next day. And then we went back a third day. But the answer to your question of ÒHow did we come up with the story?Ó is we sat in a room for two or three days and worked really hard and thought about it with Glen and Les Charles until we came up with something! [Laughs] 
How much were you involved in the genesis of LilithÕs character? 
She was in a script that Cheri Eichen and Bill Steinkellner wrote. We were introducing a character who we thought would be a new love interest for Frasier, played by Jennifer Tilly. She was just gorgeous but a little bit ditsy. Her name was Candhi with an h, like Gandhi. And the character turned out about as funny as that joke! [Laughs] We began the episode with Frasier coming in from a bad date with another psychiatrist, and the gang was going to tell him he needed to date someone who was the exact opposite of him. So then heÕd date this bombshell woman instead of another psychiatrist. We hired Bebe Neuwirth to play this part that we named Lilith, and it was only a one-page scene where they were coming in from a date, bickering at each other at the bar before she took off. And it scored so well, and Bebe was so hilarious, that we thought, ÒThatÕs the relationship to followÑforget about the Jennifer Tilly character.Ó 
So we wrote another episode that featured Lilith and abandoned the whole idea of Frasier having this other girlfriend. And Bebe came out and did it, and she scored again. I just remember the main scene from it where they went on a talk show together, and Lilith was coming on to Frasier on camera. It was hilari.ous. And when you see the glimmer of gold, follow it. When Bebe Neuwirth is giving you gold, donÕt say, ÒYeah, but weÕre gonna follow Jennifer Tilly.Ó YouÕd be surprised how many writers dig in their heels and do that. 
How would you sum up your experience on Cheers? 
I enjoyed working on Cheers. It was wildly difficult, it required writers who wrote at the top of their game, and Glen and Les Charles demanded the best of us. But it was a hard, high-pressure job, and a lot was expected of us, and at times I wasnÕt sure that I could deliver. So it was highly stressful. Stressful but fun at the same time, if there is such a thing. 

You and Peter created Wings with a third partner, David Angell. How did you come to collaborate with him? 
David Angell was already at Cheers when we got there. Three or four years into the show, he approached us and said, ÒListen, I think itÕs time that I do my own series, but I donÕt want to do it by myself. You guys and I seem to get along well, and weÕre able to come up with stuff. Do you want to form a production company?Ó And we said, ÒSure, why not?Ó 
Where did the idea of building a series around Frasier come from? 
Kelsey Grammer knew Cheers was ending, and he came to us and asked us to develop a series for him. And because heÕs such a brilliant actor and a skilled comedian, we thought, ÒWhy not?Ó 
Please share some of the thoughts that went into the showÕs creation. 
We had this idea of a Malcolm ForbesÐtype character, sort of a wild, rich industrialist who rode around on a motorcycle. And he got in a motorcycle acci.dent and became a paraplegic and had to have people help him. So this guy who had never been dependent on anyone in his life suddenly has to be dependent on others for everything. The main relationship was going to be between him and his home health care workerÑwhich obviously surfaced in the show we ended up doing. Paramount hated it, but they were also supportive of us and said, ÒIf thatÕs what you guys wanna do.Ó Then when Cheers ended and the idea was floated that maybe Frasier should be spun off, they said, ÒAre you guys sure you donÕt want to do this?Ó And we resisted the idea of a spin-off because we wanted to do something new and different. And finally, a guy named John Pike who was the head of Paramount Television at the time really prevailed upon us to see the wisdom of developing a series based on Frasier. And God bless him! [Laughs] Because I think that other series may probably not have stayed on the air for eleven seasons. 
ThereÕs not much comedy in paraplegia. 
Well, you never knowÑit was about someoneÕs struggle. And I think you can get comedy out of that. I mean, it would have been dark. 
But at first, we were going to set it in just the radio station with interesting characters, and he was going to be a radio psychologist. But the more we worked on it, the more it started to feel like WKRP in Cincinnati. 
Around that time, my father had had a stroke, and I was talking to my part.ners about the amount of care he was needing. And we started thinking, ÒWhat if something like that were going on at home for Frasier?Ó so we could get away from the radio station a little bit. What if Frasier had a dad, and he was somehow suddenly responsible for taking care of him? You can sort of hear the ripples of the themes from our original idea for the show. 
And my partner PeterÕs father had been a cop in San Francisco, and we said, ÒWhat if FrasierÕs dad is a cop, and what if he got injured in action, and all of a sudden Frasier had to take him in?Ó And then we thought, ÒOh, and he could have a home health care worker.Ó So yet another ripple from that other show. And about that time, the assistant casting director, Sheila Guthrey, came in with a picture and said, ÒI donÕt know what you guys are thinking of for the new series, but if you need somebody who looks like a brother to Kelsey, you should look at this guy.Ó And she held up a picture of David Hyde Pierce. And we went, ÒOh yeah, itÕs an interesting likeness, but what has he done?Ó So she sent over an episode of The Powers That Be that David had done and we went, ÒWe want to work with this guy!Ó And then we went, ÒOh wait, we just did a series with brothers in it.Ó And then we went, ÒNo, we really wanna work with this guy!Ó And so we added FrasierÕs brother to it. 
YouÕre proud of Frasier being atypical among sitcoms. In what ways did you try to make it different? 
Wings was a case of the three of us being tentative and not wanting to stray too far afield from the Cheers model. So this time we thought, ÒOh, what the hell. LetÕs reexamine everything.Ó And we didnÕt want to be different just to be differ.ent; we all agreed that thatÕs a path a lot of people take, and I think itÕs a foolÕs path. But to reexamine every convention in the sitcom and if itÕs something that serves you and you can do it well, then you can hold onto it. But if the answer to why youÕre doing any particular convention is, ÒThatÕs the way itÕs always been done,Ó then you really need to think about getting rid of it. So we used that ap.proach to a lot of the elements that went into it. 
At the time, the trend in sitcoms was to make scenes shorter and shorter and shorter; we always joked and called it Òshort-attention-span theater,Ó where you zoom in for a scene and you get one or two jokes, and then thereÕs a quick lick of saxophone, and then youÕre off to the next scene for the next joke. And for our purposes, that didnÕt serve us very well because we prided ourselves on being good storytellers and subtle storytellers at times and also unconventional storytellers at times; weÕd want to do farces in the tradition of Feydeau or dramatic episodesÑand you canÕt do those in sound-bite scenes. So we decided one of the goals for Frasier would be to write the longest scenes we possibly could. And basically, that was something we always adhered to during the showÕs eleven years. Of course, sometimes thereÕd be short scenes, or weÕd just pop in for a joke or something, but we always let the story we wanted to tell dictate the form that it took. And with an emphasis on trying to make the scenes as long as possible. 
Another thing we considered was, ÒWhy do you need music?Ó And I could be wrong because I havenÕt followed whatÕs happening lately, but I think to this day Frasier remains the only sitcom that doesnÕt have music between scenes. And then from that, the question that comes up is, ÒWhat will we put there instead?Ó And we said, ÒWell, weÕll just put the exteriors of the buildings.Ó And then we thought, ÒThatÕs gonna look weird without music!Ó And then we said, ÒWhy do we need pictures of the exterior of the building? DonÕt we think our audience is smart enough to know that, if youÕre in FrasierÕs living room, that heÕs actually in a building?Ó And so we thought maybe we just eliminate those exterior establishing shots before every scene, which we did. But then the next question was ÒOK, then what do we do instead?Ó And someone else came up with the idea of doing these black cards with white writing that would tell us where we were or otherwise give some sort of explanation of what was going on. 
And we were sort of intrigued by this idea of the cards because part of writ.ing a pilot is the necessary yet sometimes really difficult exposition: explaining how everybody got where they are and why theyÕre there. So we thought, for instance, ÒWhy not just put up a card that says, ÔThe brotherÕ before a scene that introduces Niles?Ó That way you avoid that sort of fakety-fake dialogue where you go, ÒWell, Niles, as my brother, you should be smarter than that!Ó 
What did you want the relationships among the three Cranes to look like? 
The series was really designed to be about the relationship between Frasier and his father, and Niles was a supporting character that we were going to use for comedy. And very early on we saw the gold in David Hyde Pierce and the relationship between Niles and Frasier. And God bless him, John Mahoney did, too. He basically said, ÒI know the show was originally pitched to me as being a relationship comedy between father and son. But I see the gold you have here, and I think we all will benefit if you follow that vein.Ó And he was right; we all did benefit, including him. 
Why do people like the Frasier character when heÕs so pompous and in many ways unlikable? 
Because ultimately, heÕs a good, kind man who, most importantly, is trying his best to do the right thing. 
Where did the character of Eddie come from? 
The dog pound. [Laughs] No, I was joking. 
I meant, why have a dog? 
Sort of cynical. On Wings, the opening credits were to a Schubert sonata, which the studio hated, and we said we liked it, so they did some testing. We had to stand behind a glass wall and watch people turn the knobs when they liked something. It was basically an afternoon of pure torture to have to watch that. We 

Clockwise from top left: David Hyde Pierce, Jane Leeves, Peri Gilpin, Moose, Kelsey Grammer, and John Mahoney. NBC / Photofest © NBC. Photographer: Chris Haston 
were talking with the testing people, and they told us there were some things that make the dials go up no matter how crappy the show is. ÒOh, what are those?Ó They said, ÒA baby makes the dials go up, and a dog makes the dials go up.Ó And so when we were thinking up Frasier, we went, ÒYou know? WeÕre puttinÕ a dog in this.Ó To make the dials go up. So thatÕs why we did it. And IÕm glad we did. 
Where did you get the name Maris? IÕve never heard that before. 
Maris is a dear old friend of mine. She used to complain that people would say just what you said, that theyÕd never heard that name before. But since Fra.sierÕs been on the air, they donÕt say that anymore! 
And the name Niles? ThatÕs unusual, too. 
I remember distinctly that David, Peter, and I were sitting in the office writing that character, thinking, ÒWhat should his name be?Ó And somebody, I donÕt think it was me, pulled that name out of thin air. And all three of us went, ÒPer.fect!Ó And I donÕt know why, but picking character names tends to be a highly irritating thing amongst writers. So when you get three guys who say, ÒPerfect,Ó you think, ÒOK, that really is perfect,Ó because usually you spend an hour argu.ing over whether thatÕs the right name or not. 
What specifically did you contribute to the show? 
I came up with the idea of using actual celebrity voices calling into his radio show. Again, that came from the idea of, ÒLetÕs try something different.Ó Because originally, we were going to hire actors and actresses to do it, and I thought, ÒMaybe we can have some celebrities, just to give us something people can write about in Entertainment Weekly!Ó 
How soon into the show did you decide on a DaphneÐNiles romance? 
They didnÕt meet in the pilot, so that wasnÕt even a consideration when we were developing the show. I think they met in the second episode; they just happened to cross paths. And it was nothing we had planned. But there was just something about the electricity between the two of them, and of course, they both were such gifted comedic actors that we thought, ÒHereÕs something we can follow.Ó Again, itÕs like when you see the beginning of a vein of gold, youÕd be a fool to say, ÒWell, I wasnÕt looking for gold; I was looking for silver,Ó and abandon it. What you need to do is start chipping away at the gold and see whatÕs there. And again, in the case of Niles and Daphne, it was a rich vein. Oh God, IÕm beating this metaphor to death! But so be it. 
Were you aware that, once Niles and Daphne were married, their personali.ties changed? 
They had to, which is also why we put that off for a long time. That was an ongoing discussion every season about how many times can we tease the audi.ence with this relationship without finally going, ÒOK, weÕve teased you enough. Here!Ó And we got to a certain point where we felt weÕd just be embarrassed to tease them again and that we had to go through with this. At that point, I think we knew we were within sniffing distance of the end of the series, so we said, ÒLetÕs just do it and see what happens.Ó 
A question about process: With three partners, what did your collaboration look like? 
Peter and I would still write our scripts together, and David would write his script by himself. But Cheers was so collaborative that the three of us would do rewrites together. It worked the same way on Frasier; the three of us wrote the pilot together, but when it came to writing individual episodes, David would write by himself. The one exception was ÒMy Coffee with NilesÓ [S1E24], where I was busy doing something else, and Peter and David wrote that together. 
And of course, DavidÕs death changed everything. 
Oh, it was horrible; I donÕt know what else to say. The gaping maw of televi.sion stops chewing for no man, and so we just had to keep churning it out. And after a while weÕd go, ÒOK, weÕre getting through it,Ó and it got easier and easier. But his contribution was sorely missed. 
What about the writersÕ room? How did you lead it? 
Well, IÕm not sure who spread it, but thereÕs a rumor going around that the room was very quiet and you had to have fully formed ideas. And that just wasnÕt true. But we didnÕt run the kind of room where guys sit around with backwards baseball caps and throw pencils into the acoustical ceiling and shoot hoops over in the corner. That wasnÕt our style. So the room reflected our style, which was, ÒYou come in and you have intelligent discussions amongst really funny people.Ó As opposed to having a roughhouse, kind-of.clichd writersÕ room. But a lot of great work was done, and a lot of good times were had, and a lot of smart things were said; a lot of really funny things were said. But also, we encouraged people to be quiet and thoughtful, if thatÕs what it took to get them to the really funny stuff. 
Generally speaking, what elements are most essential to a successful sitcom? 
Great actors, great writers, great directing, great scene design. But even as I say that, I realize I can probably think of examples that donÕt have any of those things yet have been successful, soÑbut youÕre not getting names out of me. 
Is there a common denominator that you bring to all your shows? 
ThereÕs a sensibility to all the shows weÕve done that, no matter how good you think it is, you can make it better. 
What do you feel your shows have accomplished? Have they changed TV comedy? 
Well, clearly notÑif youÕve seen whatÕs on the air lately. [Laughs] ThatÕs so hard to say. I donÕt think Wings was earth shattering because, as I said, it was a show that came out of guys not wanting to venture too far from the Cheers model. IÕd like to think that Frasier will hold a spot in the annals of situation-comedy history as being up there with the best, which is something of an accomplish.ment. The one thing about Frasier that I really likeÑwell, I like a lot of things about FrasierÑbut one thing that I find really curious is that itÕs not been imi.tated. So I guess you can extract from that that itÕs an original. And I guess thatÕs not bad for a situation comedy. 
12 

PHIL ROSENTHAL 
P
hil Rosenthal is best known as the creator/writer/producer of the hit series Everybody Loves Raymond. In this chapter, Phil reflects on his nine years with a show that received sixty-one Emmy Award nominations and thirteen wins. He discusses his intention to create a classic sitcom with traditional values by emulating earlier shows that were Òset on planet earth,Ó and he shares his secret ingredient to a successful writersÕ room. 
Author of YouÕre Lucky YouÕre Funny: How Life Becomes a Sitcom and a self-proclaimed student of television, Phil has contributed his expertise on several documentaries. When we spoke, he was working on the Òthird actÓ of his lifeÑa documentary series titled IÕll Have What PhilÕs Having, which combines two of his favorite subjects: travel and food. 
I first want to mention that my dad wrote for The Honeymooners. I know you have a particular interest in the show. 
Are you kidding? ItÕs why IÕm here! I owe your father a lot. I have to tell you, this was one of the formative things of my life, not just my career. This was huge to me. 
And youÕve said you wanted to be Art Carney? 
I felt an affinity for him. I felt like he was an underdog type of character, and I was skinny like him, and I could do him; even at three years old, I could do him. I would do the things with the arms, you know, as he prepares to write a check or something, right? I could do all that, and I could talk like him, and I made my parents laugh, and I made their friends laugh with this. And I swear I know every single episode by heart. 
What in your view made The Honeymooners such a classic? 
I guess it goes without saying: It was hilarious. It was just hysterically funny to me. I also loved that it was very simple. It all took place in that tiny set. And you would notice that today, but as a child you donÕt notice it. 
Show over substance. 
You got it. ItÕs that style-over-content issue weÕre always fighting. If weÕre writers, if weÕre actors, if weÕre directors, the content is the most important thingÑand not the style. And many times, it gets blown out the other way be.cause, once in a while, the show with the style succeeds, and then the people in charge think thatÕs all you need. But itÕs that way in every profession; the cereal box is more important than the cereal, you know? TheyÕre trying to sell it; we gotta get it out there. It doesnÕt matter what the content is if nobodyÕs buying it. 
Then how was Everybody Loves Raymond influenced by The Honeymooners? 
Everything I just told you had a profound influence on me because this was one of the benchmarks. And then I was attracted to other shows like it, shows that took place on planet earth, with stories that were rooted in real life and real characters. Now, The Honeymooners had a certain amount of exaggeration to it but not enough that it took me out of the believable situation. On Raymond we had one rule in the writersÕ room, and that was, ÒCould this happen?Ó 
And The Honeymooners and the shows I tried to emulate after itÑlike All in the Family, Mary Tyler Moore, The Odd Couple, Roseanne, and The Cosby Show from the 1980sÑhad real people in real situations. And I use the word real to mean not actually real the way it would happen in real life all the time, but believ.able. Obviously, some shows are set up to be unbelievable from the get-go, and you accept them on those terms, and thatÕs fine. But the shows that purport to be real life and oftentimes are nothing like itÑthose are the shows I wouldnÕt know how to do because the values are different. 
ThereÕs a scene in the ÒPal oÕ MineÓ Honeymooners episode [S1E8] when Ralph thinks Norton was injured. I know you find this particularly touch.ing, Phil. Can you elaborate on why itÕs so meaningful to you? 
It was the unspoken love between [Ralph and Norton]. That was, for me, a gigantic moment where something beautiful was expressed between these two guys. We laughed with them, and we only saw that Norton bugged the hell out of Ralph most of the time, right? So for Ralph to acknowledge this and in such a touching and unexpected wayÑand it really came out of left field in that sceneÑI just thought it was the most beautiful thing. And sometimes that can happen in the least likely places, which makes it more special. And it grounds them as characters; it grounds them as believable because weÕre not just ha-ha funny all the time. 
What would be an example of an emotionally resonant scene or moment in Raymond? 
Off the top of my head, I can think of a moment in a farcical episode we did [ÒThe Canister,Ó S5E19]. Marie could swear a canister was left at DebraÕs house, and Debra swears that Marie took it back. And then one of the children brings the canister in after sheÕs told Marie off in a very strict way, so Debra was wrong. 
But now it becomes, ÒI canÕt let her see I was wrong; IÕll never hear the end of it.Ó So the rest of the show is a farce in which weÕre going to try to sneak the can.ister back into MarieÕs house. And Marie enters, and Debra thrusts the canister into FrankÕs hands because she canÕt be seen with it. So now Frank is standing there, and Marie says, ÒThatÕs my canister! Frank! Where did you get that? IÕve been looking all over for it.Ó 
And thereÕs a moment, and Frank says, ÒI was hiding it from you. I thought itÕd be funny.Ó So weÕve seen Frank take the bullet for Debra. And Debra says to him, ÒWow, Frank, thank you. Why did you do that?Ó And he goes, ÒI donÕt know. I guess I didnÕt want her to do that to you.Ó And he says, ÒYouÕre like my daughter.Ó 

Brad Garrett, Peter Boyle, Doris Roberts, Ray Romano, and Patricia Heaton. CBS / Photofest © CBS 
So in the middle of this crazy farcical thing, in the most unexpected moment, he confesses something he would never, ever say in just regular life. But when pressed, why did he save her life? Suddenly that comes out. So it makes you do a couple of things. First of all, I hope it touches you in an emotional way. But it also makes you love him in a way you didnÕt two seconds before. 
Is there an art to balancing the humor with poignancy? 
I think, to do it well, there is. I think to do it so the audience doesnÕt think that was sappy or that was unearned or that wasnÕt believable. I think anybody can do that. But maybe there is an art to doing it in a way that you go, ÒAww, that was sweet.Ó Genuine. 
Backing up a bit, what were some of the specific challenges of writing a pilot? 
ItÕs the hardest episode of all to write. To create a world, where none existed? ThatÕs very difficult because youÕre starting from nothing. So I started with a very good source material, which was Ray RomanoÕs actual life. He actually lived in Queens, if not Long Island; he lived with the two twin boys and the older daughter. And the parents lived close by, and they were bothering him, and that was pretty much all I had. And then I created a world and a story from that and used my own family to flesh out the characters because IÕd never met his family. I just liked the situation that he was in, in real life. Plus, I had a practical reason for doing this: He had never acted before. He was a stand-up comedianÑ a great stand-up comedianÑso why not surround him with something familiar? And I saw immediately that the parents living close by was a metaphor for how your parents are always in your life. And that he would be a man in the middle; heÕd be a man in the middle of being a husband, a father, a son, a brother. And heÕd be torn at from all directions. 
And the phrase ÒEverybody loves RaymondÓ was very specific; it was how the brother felt about him, so it gave you a point of view. And for the show, ÒEv.erybody loves RaymondÓ would be a blessing and a curse. But I do love that the word love was in the titleÑbecause, when all was said and done, thatÕs what the show was about. 
Ray Romano told you his brother said that phrase. But did you know that was the title when you first heard it? 
I didnÕt. I thought it was just a phrase in the show. I didnÕt have a title, and my wife saw the line written in the script and said, ÒI think thatÕs the title.Ó And I said, ÒYou know what? I donÕt have anything better.Ó 
Who was your favorite character to write? 
I think Frank was fun because there was no filter on him. And for comedy writers that was fun because it was just jokes, jokes, jokes. You could think of the worst things to say to your wife [Laughs], and Frank could say them. But to be honest, we loved writing for everybody. And the characters were pretty well delineated, so it wasnÕt like that joke would work for this guy or that ladyÑthey were very, very specific. I think thatÕs what we liked most about them. 
Did you think that DebraÕs anger was always justified? 
Always. YepÑalways. We worked very hard at that because, otherwise, she would just be a shrew. And listen, there are people who thought she was a shrew anyway because they donÕt like a woman telling a man what to do or complain.ing about anything or being in a bad mood. 
Why is it funny when a woman calls a man an idiot, but it wouldnÕt be so funny the other way around? 
I think you call Raymond an idiot because his character is an idiot, and so he deserves it, right? You couldnÕt call Debra an idiot because she was never one. You know, the couple of times that she was wrong and Ray was right were giant moments in the show. And Ray would then go, ÒOh ho!Ó But he still wouldnÕt say anything because he doesnÕt want to get killed. But we know what heÕs think.ing: ÒWhoÕs the idiot now? Maybe IÕm not so stupid. Maybe I know something.Ó 
How would you describe Frank and MarieÕs relationship? 
Contentious. It reminds me of my parentsÕ relationship. Bicker and argue all the timeÑbut they would never dream of a world without the other. They understand that they have mated for life and that divorce is not even a possibility. 
How did the characters evolve over the series? 
ItÕs like anything else. You start with one idea of what the character will be, and you try to be as specific as possible. Character equals the writer plus the actor. And so the character evolves as the writer sees what the actor brings to it. We made conscious choices to dimensionalize the characters as the episodes went by. So, for instance, if Brad GarrettÕs character Robert is one thing in the pilot, and heÕs getting jokes and getting laughs from being that one thing, then we show additional facets to his behavior or personality in subsequent episodes. What did they do with Norton to dimensionalize him? Well, they gave him that serious moment where he wasnÕt just a fool, but he was a loving, caring brother to RalphÑand it dimensionalized Ralph as well. So you could say that moment we just talked about was a milestone in the evolution of those characters. They now have a depth and a dimension that they didnÕt have before that episode. 
In All in the Family, look at just the way Edith Bunker spoke in the first few episodes. That voice evolved over several episodes to become what it would be famous for. Edith evolved from just a dingbat to a three-dimensional character with a gigantic heart and a mind and will of her own. She saw the goodness inside this ignorant man and made you truly understand, not only who would marry a bigot like this, but a philosophy of love and acceptance of everyone, even bigots. 
Who do you think should have picked up the suitcase in that famous episode ÒBaggageÓ [S7E22]? 
[Laughs] ThatÕs a very good question. The idea for that episode actually stemmed from a weekend vacation weÕd all had together. We worked for HBO, and they were going to telecast a Rolling Stones concert up in San Francisco. They asked if we wanted to go, and of course, we wanted to. So we went, and a couple weeks later, Tucker Cawley came into the room and said, ÒI gotta tell you, I think IÕm having kind of a fight with my wife.Ó I said, ÒThatÕs why weÕre here; tell us about it.Ó And he says, ÒYou remember the trip? Well, I put the suitcase on the thing, and it hasnÕt moved yet.Ó And the one single guy in the room said, ÒWhy donÕt you just move it? What do youÑÓ And the rest of us, all the married guys, in unison said, ÒYou do not touch that suitcase!Ó So all the married guys were completely on the other married guyÕs side. And if you ask me, IÕm afraid IÕm siding with Raymond. IÕm not saying itÕs right; IÕm just saying that certain aspects of marriage are war. [Laughs] You donÕt give up so easily. Ray had a very logical reason why he didnÕt move it. He schlepped it around all week; he carried it here; ÒSo what if itÕs there now? YouÕre gonna do the laundry in it anyway! While I go to work, right? So, move the stupid suitcase. What? YouÕre waiting for me to move it the one extra foot?Ó This is the logic. IÕm not saying itÕs right. 
What did you want the finale to say? 
I wanted it to be a half-hour and not a bloated one-hour finale, which IÕve seen way too many of. I wanted it to be a typical episode and yet be atypical just because the audience is aware that itÕs the final one. So two years earlier, IÕd had the idea that Ray would have a minor operation on his adenoids. And thereÕd be a scare that he wasnÕt coming out of the anesthesia. Just a scare, like the mo.ment we were talking about with Ralph and Norton: a scare. ThatÕs where the idea came fromÑthat Honeymooners episode. And in that moment, youÕd see suddenly this isnÕt funny; suddenly I could lose this husband that is a pain in my butt. And what would that mean? And the comedy is that Ray can never learn about this because heÕll never let us forget it! But then he does find out. Then he asks what people did, and he realizes that his wife was upset. And then, for me, the biggest moment is Ray expressing to Debra what she means to him. Because the name of the show is Everybody Loves Raymond, but now in this final episode, how does Raymond feel about everybody? So we loved that. 
And we think we ended just in the perfect way for us, with the family all gath.ered around a tiny kitchen table thatÕs way too small for everyone, but theyÕre eating, and theyÕre talking and making fun of each otherÑand we just pull back and fade out to black, and you still hear the sounds of breakfast going on. IÕd always felt that life is hard enough without having to say goodbye to the fictional characters that you like! We came in in the middle of their livesÑso why not leave in the middle of their lives. And let this feeling of family and togetherness and the bond between us and you, the viewer, continue. And I never could have predicted it, but thatÕs actually what happens today; weÕre still in the top ten of all syndicated shows in the country. 
You deserve to be. 
Well, thank you. But again, we wouldnÕt be here without your dadÕs work. 
What do you remember as one of the showÕs funniest scenes? 
I think the biggest laugh ever in the show was when Frank and Marie drove a car into the front of Ray and DebraÕs house, and first Frank got out of the car on the passenger sideÑand then the driverÕs side opened, and Marie got out. That was gigantic [ÒThe Wallpaper,Ó S5E3]. 
Was that your idea? 
I think so. I know that story idea came from Lew Schneider, who said some.body had backed out of their house and into the car across the street. I said, ÒWeÕre gonna do this and have Frank and Marie back, not into the car, but right into the front wall of the house.Ó Because that happens; people drive into peopleÕs houses. And what better metaphor for intrusion than that! So it was my idea based on something Lew said. 
LetÕs talk about behind the scenes. How did you and Ray mesh creatively? Writer Steve Skrovan told me there was creative tension between you. 
There would be sometimes, mainly because weÕre too different people with two different ideas. But it was never contentious; it was two people trying to have what was best for the show. I had been writing sitcoms for a few years before then, and so I had a kind of sitcom vocabulary that in certain cases was right for the show, and Ray needed convincing of that. And in certain cases, it was too jokey for the show, and Ray convinced me of that. And so we had this wonderful place of agreement where our sensibilities overlapped and became the sensibility of our show. 
I know you placed emphasis on being free to say whatever in the room. Was your room more free or the things you did more outrageous than most? 
No, I think we were tame by comparison. And I say Òtame in comparison,Ó yet we still said the most shocking things that you should never say in public. But thatÕs part of it. In fact, the comedy to comedy writers is saying the most shocking, wrong, politically incorrect thing for the shock value alone. But we also knew this was for the room only. The problem is when people take that and put it on the air! [Laughs] Those jokes were to make us laugh in private, and the fact that theyÕre shocking and wrong is actually whatÕs funniest about them. We know theyÕre wrong; we know youÕre not supposed to say this. YouÕre not supposed to kill an old lady, right? 
Did you have any rules for running the room? 
The most important thing was having good food because the army travels on its stomach, and the only way to show love for your staff and your crew and everyone that works on the showÑother than being nice and paying them a reasonable wageÑwas food. Because IÕve worked on a lot of shows where the food was crap, and nobody really cared. It was just junk food. And you felt lousy afterwards. But if we took a little bit of careÑand I mean just a little bitÑand got some decent food and changed it up once in a while and made it even special once in a while, the mood was lifted. The mood was great because people felt that you cared about them. And I did care about them. And I wanted to show it. So for meÑwho doesnÕt like something good to eat! 
Another question about the room: If a writer is pleasant and friendly but they never contribute anything, do others notice? 
Yes. Because youÕre lifting something and carrying it, and you canÕt help but notice after a while that this person isnÕt carrying. And by the way, there are dif.ferent ways of lifting. Somebody may not be great at writing a script, but theyÕre great in other ways that are immeasurable. Like they can notice something in the acting and have the right note for how to fix it. Or theyÕre a great sounding board or a great cheerleader or their spirit is something that lifts you. You canÕt put a price on those. The lift of this person can be spiritual, and that helps you as much as the physical. But you canÕt just have a room full of spiritual leaders because then you wouldnÕt get very far! But as long as theyÕre not total drains on the system in other ways, having one or two is not so terrible. 
In your book, you mentioned Silly Putty and rubber balls in the room. 
You just need these tiny things, like candy or gum, when youÕre in that room all day. Silly Putty, a rubber ball, anything to keep your hand busy because this comedy, this writing, is so ephemeral. You canÕt say, ÒNow we will write ten pages,Ó because that could happen in a half hour; it could take all day; it could take a week to write those ten pages. You have no idea, and if you force it, maybe itÕs terrible. Although sometimes forcing it works. ThereÕs no rule. So those things are just a minor distraction to keep busy while youÕre thinking. But so is the fooling around and the off-color jokes and the food; itÕs all the stuff that makes it more pleasant to be locked in a room with the same people all day. 
And what was the thing about Lew humping the thermostat? 
Oh, you want to go there! These are the bits of business; this is the off-color stuff IÕm referring to. Like in a family where Dad passes gas now and then, and thatÕs the joke of the family. So in our room, one of the jokes that developed was Lew pretending to have sex with various objects, including Steve Skrovan. And because weÕre morons, this would make us laugh all the time, and we loved it. There was a lawsuit where some writersÕ assistant said how offended she was by what was said in the room. All I can tell you is, if I were being prosecuted, IÕd say, ÒIÕm sorry, IÕm a comedy writer. ItÕs what we do. The room isnÕt meant for you, for the outside world; you canÕt tell people what to say and do in the privacy of their room. And even furtherÑitÕs a comedy room. The rules are different.Ó As long as youÕre not physically assaulting anybody, anything goes. Really. Al.though Lew kind of physically assaulted Steve. [Laughs] But I think, deep down also, if weÕre gonna be totally honest, Steve allowed it. [Laughs] 
Steve might have even liked it. 
He might have even liked it. Yes. 
What question do fans ask you most often about the show? 
This isnÕt a question, but I still get letters from places like Sri Lanka saying, ÒThatÕs my mother.Ó Which is adorable and crazy because I donÕt know their motherÑI was writing my mother. But it just proves that, when you write very specifically, you have the best chance of being universal. 

Everybody Loves Raymond 
Steve Skrovan (Writer/Executive Producer): We usually went home at six oÕclock. A couple times we stayed maybe Õtil 8 or 8:30, and thatÕs just because we were goofing off, having fun because we liked each otherÕs company. But thatÕs a tribute to Phil as a showrunner. He knew what he wanted, he knew how to make a decision, and he knew the difference be.tween an acting problem and a writing problem. And thatÕs something not all showrunners can distinguish. So you didnÕt spend a lot of time rewriting jokes, where one joke is as good as the next, because that wasnÕt really the problem. It was a performance issue. 
I remember one second take we did. It was a script I wrote about a woman mistaking Robert for Ray and having sex with him and Robert see.ing what it felt like to be Ray for the first time. When he confesses this to Ray, they realize that, between them, theyÕre more powerful than they are separately. They are Raybert! And in one scene, theyÕre in RobertÕs laundry room, and a woman comes in, and they try out this Raybert thing. The woman leaves, and Ray says, ÒWith my name and your personality, weÕre unstoppable!Ó And then Ray says, ÒI got a second take for this one.Ó 
I donÕt know if heÕd worked this out with Brad [Garrett] beforehand; if he didnÕt, itÕs one of the great ad-libs of all time on BradÕs part. They shoot the scene again, and the woman leaves, and Ray turns to Robert and goes, ÒThis is amazing. With my money and your penisÑweÕre unstoppable.Ó And the crowd explodes with laughter. Brad waits for the laughter to die down, and he says, ÒYou better have a lot of money!Ó And the crowd ex.plodes again. It was nothing we could ever use on television. 
It was a running joke on Raymond that our spouses could tell when we were thinking about a story for the show. If my wife and I were having a disagreement, she would see my eyes kind of go off to the side, and sheÕd say, ÒThis is not for the show.Ó And then IÕd tell her how much money each episode was worth, and sheÕd go, ÒOK, this is for the show.Ó And sometimes weÕd artificially extend the argumentÑjust to get a second act!
