And this is writer Lew SchneiderÕs experience: He was on a ski trip with his wife and their three boys, and at the end of a long day skiing, theyÕre coming up in the elevator with all their ski equipment. And Lew drops his keys, and they fall into the crack between the floor of the elevator and the wall. HeÕs trying to fish them out, and heÕs cursing, and his wife, Liz, is holding the door of the elevator as it keeps trying to shut. She says, ÒWell, maybe youÕll get a story out of this.Ó And Lew goes, ÒNo, no. We already did an episode where Ray loses his wedding ring down a heating grate. WeÕve already done that show. This is just something shitty that happened!Ó 
Doris Roberts [as Marie] always wanted to play a victim; she always wanted to cry. And our direction to her was, ÒNo, no, do not cryÑyou have to be strident; you think you are absolutely right about everything you do.Ó Because in comedy, when you have somebody whoÕs got a skewed view of life, itÕs only funny when theyÕre absolutely certain thatÕs the right way to go. So thatÕs what we always had to keep in mind with Marie. 
Ray [Romano] was in the room most of the time; he did have a writerÕs head. In the room, we had a board that had notions of ideas and one that had the actual scripts with a color coding to show where they were in development. 
We also had the fun boards. One of them was dedicated to memorable things said in the room or inside jokes that made us all laugh out loud at once. But it wasnÕt until season 6 that we put one on the board, and we marked the date: We were having a creative debate, and Ray uttered the immortal words, ÒI agree with Phil.Ó And we all laughed because that was the first time weÕd ever heard him say that. A running joke in the room was Ray would say, ÒI would never do that.Ó And Phil would say, ÒThatÕs why itÕs funny! Because you would never do that.Ó 
And two other boards were directed at Ray; one was for his mispronun.ciations. Ray is one of the smartest people I know. His IQ is probably well above average, and IÕm convinced itÕs higher than mine. But on the flip side, Ray is one of the worst-educated people I know. So his command of the language is something all of these Ivy League and highly educated writ.ers made fun of him for. Ray would always call himself the Òsmartest dumb person.Ó When he mispronounced a word, weÕd put it up on the board. I remember two examples; he mispronounced the word mispronunciation. And the other was Albert Einstein. He said, ÒIÕm no Einsteen.Ó 
But then there was another board called ÒRayÕs surprise vocabulary.Ó And those were words we were surprised Ray knew. Ray really got into this because heÕd say something, and heÕd go, ÒPut it on the board! Put it on the board!Ó And sometimes he would object to words we thought he didnÕt know. Like weÕd say, ÒUse the word prone in a sentence.Ó And heÕd go, ÒWhat? I know prone!Ó So he was very good-natured about making fun of himself. 
Tom Caltabiano (Writer/Co-Executive Producer): I remember one thing we put on the boards that I think came from Skrovan: ÒNever bring anything to work that you donÕt want to end up your ass.Ó 
Do you feel your show changed TV comedy? Did it inspire more old-fashioned, classic-style sitcoms? 
I donÕt know that it changed anything. When I pitched it, people asked me what the hook or the gimmick of the show was. And I said the gimmick is that weÕre gonna be half-decent. ItÕs hard enough if youÕre just going to try to do a good job at the values of story and character and believability and relatability. So if we were innovative, we were innovative at just trying to be goodÑwhich is something thatÕs not so easy. I donÕt think it broke any new ground whatsoever. If anything, it was a very traditional show. In my book, I talk about an argument I had with a studio guy. He says, ÒI donÕt get this show. What are you trying to do with it?Ó And IÕm trying to do a traditional, well-made, old-fashioned, classic sitcom. 
What a concept. 
Yes! And to that he said, ÒAll words we should be avoiding! Hip and edgy is what we should be going for.Ó Well, thereÕs enough hip and edgy. And by the way, IÕm not capable of doing hip and edgy; thatÕs just my personality, right? ThatÕs who I am, so I couldnÕt do it if I wanted to. 
Phil, I notice you appear on a lot of documentaries about TV comedy. You even call yourself a student of television. 

Ray Romano and his son, Brad Garrett, Phil Rosenthal, Doris Roberts, Patricia Heaton, Peter Boyle, and Madylin Sweeten. CBS / Photofest © CBS 
Absolutely. I just did the sixties show. IÕm an expert there because, first, IÕm old enough to be an expert on the sixties, and second of all, I never went outside. That makes me an expert in television. 
What specifically did you learn from your favorite classic shows? 
That you could write about real life, and that real life was funny enough. And that the laughs come from character, and that story comes from character. These are specific writing concepts, that story illuminates character, and character then illuminates story. And as long as you stay in the real world, in the world thatÕs believable and relatable, then you really canÕt go wrong. 
Some people have theorized that all sitcoms derive from The Honeymooners. 
But The Honeymooners of course derives from whatever came before it. Radio sitcoms, right? And radio came from vaudeville, right? And vaudeville came from the Greeks! This tradition goes back a long way. You can never take 100 percent credit for anything. We stand on the shoulders of those who came before us. 
LetÕs talk a bit about comedy itself. Do you think humor comes from pain? 
I wouldnÕt say all of it does, but some of it does, certainly. Trying to make sense out of something terrible. 
Do you think itÕs related to anger? 
Some of it is, yes. I know a lot of angry comics. But I know a lot of sweet com.ics, too. ThereÕs no set rule. Whatever gets you there, right? Sometimes itÕs pure love that inspires wonderful comedy. And sometimes itÕs driven by this kind of cynicism or hate. Yes! Anger. Hate. That can fuel comedy. What you want are genuine emotions in the comedy that ring true, like when we see a comedian whoÕs fully committed to whatever heÕs playingÑand it can be joy, unbridled joy, like say Will Ferrell in Elf. ThatÕs just an example of somebody with such a loving, open heart, and we laugh at the innocence of that, at the love in that. The clash between him and the people heÕs up against who are more, quote unquote, real people and are cynical and nastyÑthat clash is very funny. 
And in Raymond, we had it in the character of RobertÕs wife, whoÕs this lov.ing, sweet girl who, when plopped down into this house of arguing, stood out because of her very sweet nature. So that contrast was funny. But thatÕs a perfect example of the two kinds of places where a laugh can come from. As long as you believe it, as long as the actor and the writer and the director are committed to doing it in a believable wayÑand we believe that Marie is freaking out over too much fruit in the house, as if it were a box of plutoniumÑthen weÕre laughing.1 
How do you think the Jewish perspective informs your comedy? 
This is something that others have spoken eloquently about. I think the Jew.ish perspective is always one of an outsider in the world; weÕre a very tiny part of the population and, for some reason, have an affinity for show business and for comedy. Maybe because thatÕs a way of dealing with persecution and otherness and a way to make yourself more acceptable to other people. So the humor is a defense mechanism, and then itÕs a way of gaining acceptance because people seem to like funny people. Listen, IÕm not one of the beautiful peopleÑso grow.ing up, I saw who the girls were more attracted to. And those people, they didnÕt need to be funny. 
Hence the title of your book. 
Yes! Because otherwise, who would look at you? So this is a way of making yourself acceptable to society. HereÕs what I can do. IÕm not going to be on the cover of a magazine, so maybe IÕll get noticed if I can be funny. 
Final question: Have you always been confident that you could write funny? 
No. IÕm still not confident. Are you kidding? WeÕd struggle every week. Every week IÕd look at the script and say, ÒI guess we donÕt have it this week.Ó Every single week. IÕm filled with self-doubt and worry at every turn. Now, that might make me betterÑI canÕt say. But sometimes I think maybe a lot of things in the world would be better if people worried about them more. I just wish that my worry was just thinking and not worrying. I donÕt need the agitation; I donÕt need the bad feeling, the depressive feeling that comes over me when IÕm stuck on a problem to solve. I think negatively, ÒOh, IÕm never gonna get this. IÕm never gonna get this. Why canÕt I get this? I hate myself. Why canÕt IÑwhy arenÕt I better?Ó And I donÕt need those feelings to be associated with the thought process, but for me, they are. So itÕs a struggle all the time. 
And even with the amount of success that we were lucky enough to have, not a day goes by when I donÕt think, ÒI donÕt know whatÕs next. WhatÕs gonna happen? Will they like this? Will they like that? I donÕt know.Ó So thereÕs doubt and worry, and then when it turns out well, IÕm happy. 
IÕd say you have a lot to be happy about! 
I do. IÕm the luckiest guy youÕll ever talk to. ThatÕs how I feel. 
NOTE 
1. In the pilot episode of Everybody Loves Raymond, RayÕs mom overreacts to his gift of a Fruit of the Month Club membership. 
13 



MIKE REISS 
M
ike Reiss is best known for his work on The Simpsons, for which he has produced and/or written more than five hundred episodes. Additional credits include ItÕs Garry ShandlingÕs Show, Sledge Hammer!, ALF, The Tonight Show Starring Johnny Carson, and The Simpsons Movie. With Al Jean, he cre.ated the animated series The Critic. 
HeÕs currently a part-time writer/consultant for The Simpsons who flies in from New York every Tuesday night and flies back Wednesday night (he hates LA)! 
Mike has lectured about The Simpsons on literally every continent. On the morning we talked, heÕd just been invited to speak in India and was celebrating the sale of his spec screenplay for $1.25 million, despite the fact that he Òhad no business writing it. ItÕs about rap music in the nineties and is something I know nothing about. But I had this silly idea and I thought, ÔOh, IÕll write this.ÕÓ 
Before our interview, Mike surprised me with some very personal questions about my dad, whom he feels had the life and career heÕd always dreamed of himself. As a kid, Mike says he Òalways wanted to be a Herb Finn.Ó I did my best to answer his probing questions thoughtfully, and he in turn promised Òto give you as good as you gave me.Ó 
From what IÕve read, it sounds like your interest in comedy writing started early. 
As a kid, I loved the Marx Brothers, and when I was maybe eight years old, I read a book about them that mentioned a guy named Al Boasberg. Al was the punch-up guy on their movies. And someone was quoted as saying, ÒI wouldnÕt give you a nickel for a script written by Al Boasberg, but he could fix a script like nobody else out there!Ó 
And it hit me then that thatÕs what I wanted to do: I wanted to fix scripts. And so I was this little boy, and people would ask, ÒWhat do you want to do when you grow up?Ó And IÕd say, ÒI want to be a script doctor.Ó And theyÕd say, ÒOh, thatÕs cute. He wants to be a doctor like his father.Ó And I was like, ÒNo, you idiotÑa script doctor!Ó It was the strangest goal in the world for a kid to have, but I sort of knew thatÕs where my talents lay. And thatÕs what I do. Ninety per.cent of my work isnÕt generating original material; itÕs fixing other peopleÕs stuff. 
Can you comment on your experience at The Harvard Lampoon? 
It was my first job, and it was really my best job. IÕd write something, and it would get printed in the magazine. There wasnÕt a lot of editorial restraint go.ing on. It was so much fun to go in every day and be surrounded by four or five really funny, extremely smart people. I donÕt think everyone realizes just how bright and educated and sophisticated most comedy writers are. It was such a great, heady experience, and it certainly cemented for me the idea that I like being in comedy. 
And what was it about Al JeanÕs writing that made you think heÕd be a good partner? 
It just happened organically. AlÕs story is pretty famous; he was a math prodigy who came to Harvard when he was sixteen. He was going to be a doctor until he saw me. Nothing about Harvard was of interest to me except I knew they had this great humor magazine. I came there just so I could join the Lampoon. Al and I were roommates, and he saw that I was having so much funÑwhich almost nobody at Harvard wasÑand that prompted him to join the Lampoon as well. And we were in bunk beds, and late at night, we just started talking our way through a parody of a magic book for kids. That was the first thing we wrote together. 
YouÕll hear people say whatÕs great in a comedy team is if the strengths of one are the weaknesses of the other and they complement each other. But our asset as comedy writers was we agreed on 90 percent of things; we agreed on things to the finest detail. We wrote together for sixteen years with very little friction. We just agreed on what would and wouldnÕt be funny. 
What was your first professional job writing together? 
Our first Hollywood job was writing jokes for Airplane 2. We got the job because our friends Max Pross and Tom Gammill turned it down and recom.mended us. A decade later, we got the Simpsons job because Max and Tom turned that down. 
What was your involvement in the creation of The Simpsons series? 
I would say a lot. Matt [Groening] and Sam [Simon] had written the pilot, but then they had nobody to write the show. Al and myself were the first writers hired, and we came in and wrote three of the first six episodes. One of the key things we did along with Matt and Sam was to write ÒThe Telltale HeadÓÑthe episode where Bart cuts the head off of Jebediah Springfield. We wrote it in three days, and that episode introduced nine big characters who came in in one episode. 
Since my dad wrote The Flintstones, IÕd love to know if that show influenced The Simpsons. 
The Flintstones was so much a part of all our lives that we never think of it as an influence, when of course it was. We were absorbing it. Absolutely no one who has written for The Simpsons had any animation background or ever stud.ied animation. But I think we learned all the tricks and the gimmicks and the pacing just by watching those shows so religiously. 
Do you think Homer Simpson compares to Fred Flintstone? 
Absolutely. The reason Homer Simpson has that muzzle, that kind of five oÕclock shadow, is a definite homage to The Flintstones. And thatÕs why his best friend is named Barney. ThereÕs a point where homage becomes plagiarism, and I hope we havenÕt crossed that line! [Laughs] 
And do you believe Homer is a descendant of Ralph Kramden? 
Absolutely. None of these were done consciously, but yeah, these guys were out there proving that this kind of character can work on TV. IÕd say yes, also, because The Honeymooners is a show we all really love. 
What do you think makes Homer such a funny character? 
Homer is a comedy writerÕs dream because heÕs got everything wrong with him: HeÕs fat, heÕs bald, heÕs angry, heÕs stupid, heÕs an alcoholic, and heÕs bad at his job. And then in season 4, Al and I wrote a joke where Homer is ap.proaching a pet shop, and the pet shop owner goes, ÒEww, whatÕs that terrible smell?Ó And then Homer walks offÑthe implication being that Homer smells worse than the pet shop. And I remember George Meyer, one of our writers, said, ÒOh, now he smells, too!Ó 
Why not? 
The characterÕs great for the show; he just literally has every fault you can write to. Something people never realize in television is that, when youÕre working with live actors who have these characteristics that youÕre supposed to make fun of, you canÕt do it because theyÕre real peopleÑand generally theyÕre your bosses! In ani.mation, itÕs a pleasure to make fun of Homer and call him every name in the book. But if it had been a real actor having to play that part, heÕd probably kill himself. 
Do you think you ever made him too dumb? 
Yes. We have done it, we knew we were doing it, and weÕve changed. I donÕt think heÕs too dumb now. There was a point, actually there were a lot of them, where IÕd see him get dumber and dumber over the seasons. ItÕs a gradual process; when you change showrunners, the new person comes in knowing HomerÕs this dumb, and they think they can make him a little dumber. But there was a point at which he was just this big baby. Fans on the internet first noted it and started calling him Jerk-Ass Homer, and we picked up the phrase from that. WeÕll say, ÒNow, this is too Jerk-Ass Homer.Ó 
Why do we laugh at him getting hurt or humiliated? 
We laugh at it because thatÕs what comedy is. What was Mel BrooksÕs line? If I get hurt, itÕs a tragedy. If it happens to you, itÕs funny. But this will tell you something about me: The most famous joke in Simpsons history is Homer falling down the cliff in ÒBart the DaredevilÓ [S2E8]. HeÕs getting hurt, heÕs not Wile E. CoyoteÑit looks very painful all the way down, and he starts to bleed out of his mouth. And then they bring him up, and he falls down the cliff again. And then we repeat the sequence. And Matt hated it. And I gotta say, I hated it, too. And Matt got that joke sort of suppressed, so as well loved as it was, the audience at home only saw 50 percent of it. And I agreed with him; it was we two against the world, and I guess we were wrong. But I like Homer; people like HomerÑand IÕm a little surprised people donÕt mind seeing him get hurt so much. 
What is your response to the criticism that Homer is damaging to childrenÕs perception of fatherhood with the strangling and all? 
I canÕt deny that. I do see a lot of kids call their father Homer Simpson, but I donÕt see any trouble with it. I donÕt see any evidence that kids respect their parents less than they did. But the strangling is one of the showÕs institutions that IÕve always thought is so wrong. IÕll think, ÒI canÕt believe we get away with this, and why has nobody complained about it?Ó 
Did you create any other memorable Simpsons characters? 
Ralph Wiggum. Al and I created him to be a small Homer, so we named him Ralph, after Ralph Kramden. At one point weÕd done him to death on the show, and weÕd gotten very sick of him. We stopped writing him for about two years. Then I became a part-time lecturer, and IÕd lecture on The Simpsons all over the world, and whenever IÕd ask people who their favorite character was, theyÕd go, ÒRalph! Ralph! Ralph!Ó I came back, and I said, ÒEverybody wants to see Ralph,Ó so we brought him back because of public acclaim. 
When creating a new character, does the writer decide which actor will do the voice? 
No, generally the showrunner will assign that as weÕre writing. Almost every one of our characters starts off as just a one-line appearance in an episode; itÕs usually a joke we need a guy to say. And then we get to a table reading, and the actors will have to make up a voice for that character, and then the animation designers will design a character that looks like that voice and fits the situation and the line, and sometimes it really works out. Sometimes itÕll be a completely nondescript line, and then the voice is so funny and they design such a funny character with it that that character becomes a star, and weÕll bring him back. And the proof of this is Hank Azaria, who has a raft of characters on the show that have no name; he was given a one-line character, and he made so much of it that the character became popular. We thought Comic Book Guy would be in one scene, and now heÕs a big part of the show. ThereÕs also Bumble Bee Man, Old Jewish ManÑthese are all HankÕs characters. And once he did the line, ÒJust stamp the ticketÓÑand he said it so funny that that became a character, and now we have a guy named Just Stamp the Ticket Man. 

Mike Reiss as a Simpson. © & TM Fox Broadcasting 
And something IÕve always wondered: Why is Krusty Jewish? 
It just came up at one of the retreats. We were all sitting there with our story pitches, and Jay [Kogen] and Wally [Wolodarsky] came in and said they wanted to do an episode like ÒThe Jazz Singer,Ó where Krusty has this rabbi father who looks down on him. And we go, ÒOh, rabbi fatherÑI guess KrustyÕs Jewish.Ó And while we were chewing on that, Al said, ÒHis real name could be Kros.tovsky.Ó And that got a giant laugh, especially from Jim Brooks. Every once in a while, all you need is one joke good enough that you realize a story is worth doing. So that was it. 
And often what youÕd consider to be established parts of characters actually came in much later. So Krusty was Gentile for fifteen years until we decided heÕs Jewish. Another example is that, for four seasons, weÕd had the characters of Ralph and Chief Wiggum. We were pitching an episode where Ralph gets a crush on Lisa, and I said, ÒWhat if we make Ralph Chief WiggumÕs son? Then youÕve got Lisa spurns Ralph, and Wiggum takes it out on Homer.Ó WeÕd never before mentioned those two existing characters being related, and suddenly they were father and son. 
Did the charactersÕ personalities change over the years? 
In some of the earliest episodes, the characters behaved very differently than they did later on. A good example is the third episode that Al and I ever wrote, ÒThereÕs No Disgrace Like HomeÓ [S1E4]. People remember the showÕs end.ing, where the Simpsons are all shocking each other in family therapy, but no.body remembers what leads up to itÑbecause absolutely nothing that happens in that episode is like the Simpsons we know today. TheyÕre at a party at Mr. BurnsÕs house, and Mr. Burns is this very benevolent soul. And Lisa says, ÒLetÕs go throw rocks at the swan.Ó Marge gets drunk and embarrasses the family, and thereÕs the whole plot where Homer is ashamed of his family, and he sells his TV to get money to buy family therapyÑnone of which Homer would do today. 
Homer parts with his TV? 
ItÕs crazy; itÕs all crazy. IÕve never heard anyone flag the episode of how wrong it is compared to what the show has become. 
Mike, do you think of the characters as human or as cartoons? 
I think of them as very human. And that explains virtually everything IÕm against on the show: Homer getting too hurt, Homer strangling Bart. But as a writer, you have to step back and realize itÕs probably good for keeping the characters pure. We all love writing the Halloween show because itÕs the one opportunity a year to kill off and maim all our characters. I think every writer on every sitcom would love to have one shot a year where they get to murder all their characters! 
WhoÕs your favorite character to write? 
My favorite character is Homer for the reasons laid out; heÕs pure comedy. The other one thatÕs very close to me is Kent Brockman because itÕs just jokes. Over the years, IÕve written many, many of those news storiesÑalways the, ÒComing up, blank blank blankÓÑwhich is just a freestanding joke that has nothing to do with the show. I also like Apu because heÕs always in a great mood, and when you write him, you have to pitch in a great mood, and youÕre always upbeat. I also like Apu because we work so hard at The Simpsons, and in all of Springfield, heÕs the only man who works hard and whoÕs good at his job. 
Do you identify with any character in particular? 
No. Almost all of the writers were Lisa Simpson growing up. We wish we had been Bart, but we were all a bunch of Lisa Simpsons! I think we were all just a little too smart and didnÕt have quite enough friends growing upÑand suddenly one day, we woke up, and we were Homer, with wives and kids and a little bit angry all the time and a little bit hungry all the time. 
An interesting thing is that, after all these years, weÕre allowed to pick our own work schedule. YouÕd think no one would want to work Friday, but they doÑbecause thatÕs Donut Day at The Simpsons. ItÕs the one day we get free donuts. So thatÕs a very Homer-y thing. This is a bunch of men who can easily afford a lot of donuts, but they come in that day to get them. 
How has the writersÕ job changed over the years? 
In the first four years, it was such a hard show to work on that, as soon as their contracts were up, every one of our writers quit. Including me. And then about season 8, a showrunner named Mike Scully came in and rebuilt the place. He fig.ured out ways to delegate authority and break it down into jobs and subjobs and to make the show work better. He also divided the writersÕ room into two dif.ferent rooms, so we could have two parallel writing teams going simultaneously. 
Since then, itÕs become a very pleasant place to work. The hours are always really good, and everybody learns a little of every job on the show. So as a result, we went from being a show where everybody quits to a show where nobody quits. Most of our writers have been at the show ten or twelve years. 
How many hours a day did you work in the early seasons? 
ItÕs no exaggeration to say that, in the beginning, when I ran the show, I worked eighty to one hundred hours a week. I had one week off a year, from Christmas to New Years. It was relentless, and it nearly killed me; I gained eighty pounds, and I was literally praying for a heart attack to get me out of there. It was really, really hard. When I finally had time off, I went to a doctor, who said to me, ÒYouÕre morbidly obese. Do you know what that is?Ó And I said, ÒThatÕs what Homer is!Ó And in fact, I weighed the same as Homer: 239. 
What advantages or disadvantages does animation confer to writers? 
ThereÕs just this tremendous pressure writing animation that you donÕt have in live action; the idea that itÕs always got to be dense with material. You have to be concerned with whatÕs going on in the background as well as the foreground. A show like Two and a Half Men isnÕt filled with funny signs everywhere they go, but ours is. So I would say an animation script packs about three times as much information and jokes into an episode as a three-camera live-action sitcom. 
You mentioned the story retreats, where you generate premises. Tell me about them. 
All the writers attend them. We started by holding them in nice hotel suites, as a way to get James L. Brooks out of his office and away from the phones. We wanted his undivided attention! With the invention of cell phones, we realized 

Homer, Bart, Marge, Lisa, and Grampa Simpson. Fox Broadcasting / Photofest © Fox Broadcasting 
that was impossible. But he does still always attend and always comes in with a great story idea of his own. 
And the retreats have gotten more impressive over the years. You used to just throw out something like, ÒHereÕs two lines: Marge takes an art class, and her paintings are very dark, and the familyÕs concerned about her.Ó That was a pitch. And now the writers come in like little Willy Lomans; everyoneÕs got a ten-minute soliloquy, and sometimes theyÕve got props, and they have the whole story laid out with jokes even. ItÕs like going to see a one-act play festival. The story retreats are the one chance individual writers get to shine, and IÕm really dazzled by seeing what every single one of them is capable of generating on his own and presenting. ItÕs a very entertaining day. I walk into these things knowing IÕm going to be the eighth-funniest man in the room; itÕs so humbling. 
WhatÕs the most important thing about writing you learned from this show? 
I donÕt know whether to thank Sam Simon or Jim Brooks for itÑprobably bothÑbut the key thing on The Simpsons is youÕve always got to have some heart in thereÑbut not too much. I think, for a lot of us, our instinct would have been ÒDonÕt have any of that stuff at allÑand hereÕs our edgy show.Ó But they told us, ÒIf you throw in twenty-five seconds of emotion right at the endÑif Homer can be a goof the whole show and then suddenly realize heÕs been badÑthat will be very powerful to people.Ó 
ThatÕs what worked on The Honeymooners. Ralph could be so wrong and so bad and so angry for so much of the show, and then there would be just that moment at the end where he would realize heÕd been a goof, and he would eat crow, and youÕd have a happy ending. 
Tell me about the Simpsons writersÕ room. 
In the early days, it was a dark, dingy room. It had three couches and two armchairs jammed into a space that couldnÕt hold that manyÑso when we had the full staff there, it was crammed, and peopleÕs knees were touching. ThereÕs a memorable story about one night when it was about ten oÕclock, and writer Jeff MartinÕs wife came by to visit. She said it was like walking into an opium denÑeveryone just looked gray and depressed and worn down. 
The funny thing about the Simpsons writers is they are such normal guys, and I think thatÕs the reason the show is so stable and has run so long. My wife used to work in real estate, and IÕd tell her, ÒYour coworkers are so much weirder than mine.Ó She worked with all these colorful guys who were secret alcoholics or closet bisexuals and having affairs and cheating around. And the Simpsons writersÑweÕre just these kinda nice working stiffs. 
One of the early writers, John Swartzwelder, smoked a lot, and heÕd go to the kitchen and come back with ReeseÕs Peanut Butter Cups. HeÕd line them up on the arm of his chair, so he had an ashtray on one arm and a geometrically perfect line of ReeseÕs on the other. ThatÕs as close to quirky or colorful as you got. 
And year after year, some news channel has wanted to film the Simpsons writ.ers at work, so theyÕve sent in a camera crew. They think itÕs going to be really wacky. And every year theyÕll sit there for two hours, and then finally give up because thereÕs nothing to see! ItÕs just people sitting in chairs, thinking all day. 
Do you remember any major arguments in the room? 
No, I canÕt remember one argument over what to do. As I always say, so much of it is a laugh-ocracyÑa democracy of laughter where people throw out lines, and if somebody makes everybody else laugh, the line will go in the script. 
And this wasnÕt a fight at all, but itÕs something that haunts me forever. Jay and Wally pitched a joke, and it made a bunch of people laugh. Al and I were show-runners, and we didnÕt get it. But we put it in the script because everyone else loved it. And then we had the table reading, and it got a huge laugh. And to date, I donÕt get it. But itÕs the kind of thing that haunts me; for all I know, there are a hundred jokes that would have been really funny that I kept out of the script as a showrunner. IÕm really embarrassed about the few jokes that I objected to that went in and then were great, including Homer falling down the cliff. 
What was the joke you didnÕt get? 
Marge and Bart are shopping for clothes and Marge picks out a shirt. Bart says, ÒIf I wear that, the kids at school are gonna beat me up.Ó And Marge says, ÒAnyone who beats you up for the clothes you wear isnÕt your friend.Ó ThatÕs the line. 
ThatÕs it? I was waiting for the punch line. 
That is the punch line, and it got a huge laugh. So there you go. And that was twenty-four years ago, and probably every week, I feel bad about it. I remember all the things I fought against. Another instance was the Mary Poppins episode [ÒSimpsoncalifragilisticexpiala(Annoyed Grunt)cious,Ó S8E13]. For probably ten years, Al Jean wanted to do it, and I fought him on it because I just hated the idea of introducing magic into the show. If Mary Poppins did magic, what was to stop us from having Homer get a genie or putting funny ghosts in the house? By the way, IÕm the only person who ever had a problem with this. 
But finally one day, we were on the show, but we werenÕt even working to.gether, and he said he was going to write the episode on his own. I said, ÒIf you do it, IÕm doing it with you,Ó just so I could have some control over the show. And we did the episode IÕd fought for a decadeÑand itÕs easily the most popular thing Al and I have done in our whole career. We did that episodeÕs DVD com.mentary ten years ago, and it opens with me just apologizing over and over to Al. I feel so bad that something I fought so hard against was so popular. 
Do you have a favorite episode? 
Of 550 episodes, my favorite was by David Cohen, where Lisa finds the bones of an angel [ÒLisa the Skeptic,Ó S9E8]. I loved that it did what The Simp.sons does: We take an issue of faith versus science, and over the course of a funny episode, weÕve debated this big issue fully and fairly. I love The Simpsons when itÕs really smart, when we can get really smart references in. I love that Stephen Hawking has become sort of a regular on our show. 
Were any shows based on your own life? 
Homer builds a hideous clown bed for Bart, which scares the daylights out of him [ÒLisaÕs First Word,Ó S4E10]. My father, the worldÕs worst carpenter, built that exact bed. Matt Groening loved that anecdote and suggested we do it on the show. I actually drew a picture of the bed and gave it to the animators for reference. 
What can you tell me about the popular episode ÒLast Exit to SpringfieldÓ [S4E17]? 
As a matter of fact, just recently Al told me that episode had been my idea. One day IÕd just come in and said, ÒThe nuclear plant should go on strike.Ó I have no recollection of it, but thatÕs what wound up kicking off the whole epi.sode. I give 90 percent of the credit for it to Jay and Wally as the writers. And again, it has a classic joke that Al and I didnÕt get! HomerÕs thinking of how a strike will get them a dental plan, and they need a dental plan for LisaÕs braces. You see the phrases going in HomerÕs head: Òdental plan, Lisa needs braces, dental plan, Lisa needs bracesÓÑand Jay and Wally wanted to repeat that like six times. We felt that was too much, and people would get bored. But we did it, and itÕs one of those classic jokes of that episode. 
Something I contributed that I was very proud of was the ending, which is a parody of How the Grinch Stole Christmas. I knew the moment we wanted, but the animators actually got storyboards from How the Grinch Stole Christmas, and the scene where Mr. Burns has a change of heart is, shot for shot, the same way the Grinch has a change of heart. ThatÕs a nice little moment. And USA Today once named that episode the number 1 best Simpsons of all time. Jay and Wally werenÕt at the show anymore, but I called them and said, ÒDid you know this was named the best Simpsons episode of all time?Ó And Jay said, ÒThat wasnÕt even the best episode we wrote that year!Ó 
What else are you most proud of, Mike? 
ItÕs funny. When I look back at what IÕm proud of contributing, itÕs all just my saying, ÒLetÕs parody this movie,Ó or ÒLetÕs steal this thing that worked in another movie.Ó So I have the Grinch moment, and in the episode ÒLife on the Fast Lane,Ó thereÕs a scene where Marge goes running back to Homer after sheÕs had an affair with the bowling instructor [S1E9, written by John Swartzwelder]. And I said, ÒLetÕs end it like An Officer and a Gentleman, where he carries her out, and he puts the hat on her head.Ó ItÕs a great ending, and I pitched it, but I didnÕt think of anything original. 
Now for a few final questions. What do you think is the hardest thing about writing comedy? 
I think the hardest thing about comedy is that you can work and struggle and get a funny line, and everyone agrees, ÒWow, we got a great line there!Ó And then youÕre on to the next line, and you have to do it all over again. 
You told me you have an affection for comedy writers. Can you elaborate on that a bit? 
I love comedy writers. I really worshipped them growing up, and IÕm so happy and honored to be one. And an interesting thing is, IÕve met guys a gen.eration older than me, and I felt right at home. I used to hang around with Joe Stein, a ninety-eight-year-old guy whoÕd written Your Show of Shows and Fiddler on the Roof. And he was like a ninety-eight-year-old version of me, and I said, ÒThatÕs where IÕm going if I can hang in that long.Ó I was more comfortable with him than with, say, an investment banker my own age. 
The personality doesnÕt change. IÕd say the people in comedy have always been pretty much the same, and they remind me of myselfÑsort of nebbish-y Jewish guys. TheyÕre kind of funny and pleasant, modest and entertaining, they have doting wives; it would shock people how remarkably normal they were. 
IÕve become one of these sort of living fossils they can talk to about TV, and IÕm on panels and stuff. But I never wanted that. The Dick Van Dyke Show was a huge influence on me, and I wanted to be Buddy Sorrell. I didnÕt want to be Rob Petrie; I didnÕt want to be the guy running the show married to Mary Tyler Moore. I just wanted to be the funny, kind of lazy guy in the room who slept on the couch and went home to pickles at night. 
Would you have preferred to have written during the sixties, when the staffs were smaller? 
IÕm perfectly happy with the way things have gone. But I was listening to a bunch of old radio shows in the car, especially Jack Benny and Abbott and Costello, and it occurred to me that I would have been better on those than I am as a TV writer. IÕd love to write just a lot of funny banter and silly stuff. And those shows were very silly. They really didnÕt care about story, and a lot of unreal things would happen on them. And I think, ÒWow, thatÕs where I really wouldÕve shone.Ó 
But youÕve had an amazing career. I mean, not that itÕs over! 
I told you I was celebrating this week. I just sold a spec screenplay for one and a quarter million dollars. IÕm at an age, fifty-four, where I thought, ÒNo.bodyÕs going to hire me.Ó So I was very heartened. I love getting the money, but I love almost as much just the affirmation that, hey, I still got something to give. 
Lastly, youÕve lectured on The Simpsons worldwide. What question do you get asked most often about the show? 
The number 1 question is, ÒWhen is it going to end?Ó And itÕs sort of a sad question. I always say, ÒThatÕs like saying to Grandma, ÔI love you, Grandma, but when are you gonna die?ÕÓ 
Nothing shocks my audience, mostly because they think IÕm kidding. Every.thing I say in my speech is true, but they all sound like jokes, e.g., that the pope is a fan of the show or that Bill Clinton told us, ÒIÕd love to do The Simpsons, but I would never do anything that would bring disgrace to the office of the president.Ó 
14 

AL JEAN 
A
l Jean wrote for such shows as Not Necessarily the News, Sledge Hammer!, The Tonight Show Starring Johnny Carson, ALF, ItÕs Garry ShandlingÕs Show, and The Critic (which he created with Mike Reiss). He also helped write The Simpsons Movie. His fans know him best from his long-running tenure as executive producer of The Simpsons. He started on the show in 1990 and is credited with writing and/or producing more than 573 episodes. What fans may not know is that he appeared in the film The Naked Monster as ÒBeer DrinkerÓ and in the TV show Mad Men as the uncredited ÒMan at Bar.Ó He has a degree in math from Harvard University, where he enrolled at the age of sixteen. 
What in your background most prepared you to write comedy? 
I was a math major, and surprisingly, many Simpsons writers come from sci.ence and math backgrounds. In high school, I read many classic works of litera.ture, dreaming that I might someday be a writer of some sort. But it wasnÕt until my college roommate, Mike Reiss, joined The Harvard Lampoon that I thought of being a comedy writer. 
One of your early jobs was writing The Tonight Show. Did you work alongside Johnny Carson? Do you remember any of the biggest laughs you got there? 
Although I worked there a year and a half, I only spent about forty-five min.utes in Mr. CarsonÕs physical presence. I donÕt remember any specific jokes. But interestingly, while writing The Tonight Show, I did discover a word that got a laugh in almost any context: McNugget. 

Al Jean as a Simpson. © & TM Fox Broadcasting 
What roles did Matt Groening, Sam Simon, and Jim Brooks play in the Simpsons genesis? 
The three are all brilliant and made huge contributions to the show. Matt with the shorts, which influenced so much of what was to come, and with his visual sense and vision of the Simpsons universe. Sam was a brilliant writer/ showrunner also with a great visual sense. Jim Brooks, perhaps the greatest TV writer ever, also enabled The Simpsons to exist as short episodes first and then as a groundbreaking TV series. Of course, their contributions arenÕt limited to what I just described. 
Which Simpsons characters were you responsible for? Who is your favorite character to write? 
Characters I helped create include Ralph Wiggum, Troy McClure, Jinko, Nelson Muntz, Apu, Mayor Quimby, and Bleeding Gums Murphy. I always have loved writing Lisa because I can relate to the character and because Yeardley Smith effortlessly imbues Lisa with such great emotional resonance. 
YouÕve been quoted as saying Ralph Kramden is the funniest TV character ever. Could you elaborate on that? 
Ralph Kramden was one of the great archetypal characters in cartoon history. His descendants include but are not limited to Archie Bunker; Fred Flintstone, of course; and Homer Simpson. Norman Lear originally offered the part of Ar.chie Bunker to Jackie Gleason, who turned it down, I believe, because he felt it would reflect poorly on his earlier work. 
Has the showÕs writing evolved since its first season? 
The writing style of the show has changed very little with time actually. There are more writers now than there were in the beginning, and we write in two different rooms. WeÕre always trying to write about the real problems of a contemporary family. The greatest difficulty, after having done 570 episodes, is coming up with fresh ideas for more. 
Which era of The Simpsons do you think is of the highest quality? 
ThatÕs not for me to judge. WeÕre always doing the best we can. 
Was Conan OÕBrien funny in the room? 
Conan was hysterical in the roomÑwhich was a room full of funny people. Mike and I hired him because we had gotten to know him when he was on The Harvard Lampoon and seemed destined for big things then. You can see a great accounting of the early days on YouTube at ConanÕs Serious Jibber-Jabber site. 
Did you create any catchphrases or running gags? How did they come about? 
Some of the ones I originated are the ÒFlaming MoeÓ for HomerÕs cocktail, LisaÕs ÒI Choo Choo Choose YouÓ Valentine, and Sideshow Bob stepping on the rakes. With each one, I was just trying to pitch a joke or an idea, and I never expected theyÕd be so popular. 
Do you have any favorite guest stars from the show? 
My favorites include Kelsey Grammer, Kiefer Sutherland, Anne Hathaway, Mr. T, and of course the late Phil Hartman. The amazing thing to me is that I have met them all, and they were excited to do our showÑeven people like Thomas Pynchon and Tony Blair. 
What are some examples of Michigan humor that youÕve included in the show? 
Having Ned Flanders insulted by being depicted as a LionsÕ fan; having Bart send Gordie HoweÕs picture to Mrs. Krabappel; and Moe saying, ÒLay off Detroit. Them people is livinÕ in Mad Max times.Ó That was a joke by Matt Warburton. 
What effect has the show had on live-action TV comedy? 
I think live-action shows have tended to incorporate the Simpsons pace and cut-away style. I believe Tina Fey once said she wanted 30 Rock as sort of a live-action Simpsons. 
How have changes in technology affected the way you write the show? 
One of the biggest technological changes thatÕs impacted the show since its inception is word-processingÑif you can believe it, we predate its wide use. Also, search engines have of course revolutionized the way we look up facts. 
What can you say about your experience creating The Critic? 
The Critic was very fun to work for but taught me two important lessons about creating a show. For one thing, donÕt do it for the wrong network. We were originally on ABC, which had no compatible show, and our ratings were unacceptable. I also learned not to sell your show to a network head who leaves the network before the show comes on! We did this at Fox, and even though our ratings were more than respectable, it wasnÕt picked up by the executive who felt no personal investment in the show. 
Finally, given The SimpsonsÕ longevity, has it gotten harder to come up with new jokes? 
I would say almost any joke I come up with is an act of desperation. IÕm not kidding! 
15 

JAY KOGEN 
J
ay Kogen is a TV writer, producer, and director known for his work on such series as The Simpsons, Frasier, The Tracey Ullman Show, and Malcolm in the Middle. Jay started out as an actorÑand still loves actingÑbut switched to writing because it was something people were willing to pay him for. The recipi.ent of six Emmy nominations and four Emmy wins, Jay is the son of comedy writer Arnie Kogen (The Carol Burnett Show, Empty Nest, Newhart). 
When did your interest in comedy start? 
My dad is a funny guy. 
You donÕt have to tell me! 
He liked me to make jokes, and I got a lot of praise when I was attempting to make jokes and be funny. So that motivated me to do it. 
Do you think your sense of humor is genetic or learned? 
I would say weÕre still finding out what exactly is genetic and what isnÕt, but thereÕs definitely a learned component to it. IÕve learned from both my parents about whatÕs funny, and IÕve learned about whatÕs funny from all the people IÕve talked to and worked with over the years. From the schoolyard to the writersÕ roomÑit all goes into one big, giant file of what can make people laugh. 
How has your dad specifically influenced your own comic sensibilities? 
As I say, comedy is learned behavior, and your parents are your first teachers of whatÕs funny. HeÕs absolutely influenced me in terms of what I think is funny and even joke construction and references and style. 
What memories stand out for you of growing up with your dad? 
I have a lot of memories of growing up with a silly guy. One of his favorite bits is always to put whipped cream on his face at a serious dinner and then pretend he doesnÕt know itÕs there. And heÕs done it my whole life. And then I used to do it with him. And now my son does it. So we have three generations of men with whipped cream on their face. 
But as I was growing up, my dad was busy writing. He had an office at home, so we had to be quiet in the house while he was working. Every now and then, I would bust into his office and make a joke or give him a hug, but most of the time, this was time for him to work and for us to leave him alone. And it seemed like a solitary kind of thing, so I have real memories of thinking, ÒWow, writing is kind of sad and boring and quiet.Ó And it turns out, I was right. Writing can be boring and quiet. Obviously when youÕre writing in a room, itÕs much more social and much more interesting. When youÕre writing alone, it can be more creative, but it is a solitary endeavor. 
How has sitcom humor changed since your dad was writing? 
TV comedy has changed a little bit in that you can say more in terms of dirty words, but you can say less in terms of subject matter. We could never do things on regular TV that Norman Lear did thirty years ago. 
What are some subjects that are currently taboo on network TV? 
ItÕs probably not great to make fun of Jesus, not great to allow your main character to be a racist or a devout atheist or a drug abuser. 
WhatÕs your take on how sitcoms of the fifties compare to those of today? 
LetÕs compare Father Knows Best with Modern Family. Although I think Modern Family is much, much funnier than Father Knows Best, I do think they basically serve the same purpose. TheyÕre trying to portray what they think is a window into what a family is all about and the troubles and tribulations of raising one. Father Knows Best was a very nuclear family of father, mother, and three kids, and Modern Family is a more wide-ranging blended family of a lot of different people and ex-wives and their stepkids and that sort of stuffÑbut the basic idea is they want to show American life. And theyÕre both pretty idealized versions of it, honestly. 
When did you decide you wanted to be a TV comedy writer? 
When I failed at acting. [Laughs] I failed at being a stand-up comedian, and then I thought I should probably try to do something else that I might not fail at. But I wanted to be in show business in some capacity since I was five. 
YouÕve acted on a few sitcoms, including one of your dadÕs Newhart epi.sodes. Did you learn anything about writing from acting on those shows? 
Every time IÕve acted, IÕve learned about writing. All writers should experi.ence acting so they can understand the process of actors and to gain respect for actors because a lot of writers think actors have the easy part. And their job is just as hard as anybody elseÕs, and to do it well is incredibly hard. So yes, I did learn that. 
Where did you learn to write a script? 
I learned to write a script by trying to write scripts and doing badly. And then writing others and rewriting and rewriting. The first time I ever tried to write a script, I was fourteen years old. I showed it to my dad, and he graciously said, ÒItÕs not greatÓÑand then I was scared to write one again. When I was sixteen, I did write another one with some friends, and I gave it to my dad. He said, ÒThis is better but still not great.Ó [Laughs] And then I kept writing some more. I sort of kept improving and working on things, and over time, you get better. ItÕs something you can learn; itÕs something you can practice. Joke structure, storyÑthose are learnable skills. 
Earlier you had compared writing alone versus in a room. What are the pros and cons of writing with a partner? 
Writing with a partner offers a few things: It offers two minds trying to solve the puzzles of a script. From story to dialogue, there are always places a writer gets stuck and could use help. It also offers a filter, which is especially handy when youÕre writing jokes. If the other person doesnÕt laugh, it might not be all that funny. Plus, you always have someone to eat lunch with. But on the other hand, there are moments when your writing is really flowing, and you feel great about it, and often a partner might not be on the same page. He or she might think that great stuff is actually pretty crappy. So now, even if you think itÕs great, you have to fight about it or change it. So thatÕs the downsideÑbut that lunch thing is pretty essential! 
LetÕs talk about The Simpsons. Can you characterize the tone and sensibility that Jim Brooks, Sam [Simon], and Matt [Groening] set for the show? 
Jim didnÕt want this show because it was a cartoonÑto be a cartoon. He wanted it to be a show about people. When they talk about the MTM years, a lot of it is about Jim Brooks and Allan Burns, who created The Mary Tyler Moore Show. And Jim Brooks was [one of the creators of ] The Simpsons. And he was always telling a story about characters. He was never telling a story about people falling down or hitting their heads; that was never the point. The point is, ÒWhat does it mean to be a family? What does it mean to love your kids but not know how to be the best dad in the world? What does it mean to want to please your wife but your instincts go against what will make her happy? [Laughs] What does it mean to sort of want to get ahead in life but be lazy? What does it mean that we have desires, we have ids that control us and are kind of the worst devils of our nature, when we ultimately want to be better and more productive and more creative?Ó I think a lot of people relate to Homer Simpson because heÕs a creature of id. He has no willpower. He doesnÕt think things through. We all do that. ThatÕs what our society is based onÑlike, ÒBoy, I hope I can get rich really easy. I hope I can lose weight without dieting.Ó All that stuff. 

Sam Simon gave a really smart overview of what a show could be. He knew what would make these characters pop. He knew what combinations would work. He knew about story development and how to arc a story, and I think a lot of that dovetailing, starting out with one story and blending into another story, was the result of a lot of what Sam did. He redrew characters from the original Tracey Ullman Show to make them more palatable to regular audiences. He was really in.strumental in helping to build an entire world that was understandable and funny and interesting. And Matt Groening, same thing. He wanted to show an American family in all its glory and all its shame. And he was very instrumental in the big picture of what the show should be and especially how the show should look. 
These cartoons clearly arouse viewersÕ emotions. How do you accomplish that? 
I remember feeling sad for Fred Flintstone, so weÕre not the first people to imbue a cartoon character with humanity. If you give appropriate and realistic motivations and realistic traits to a character and viewers can follow the story and can sympathize with his plight, you can get them to feel something. You can get people to feel bad for Homer or bad for Marge or sympathetic to the situ.ation. Boy, a lot of people feel bad for Marge and for Lisa because theyÕre both smarter than the other people in the house! I always felt bad for Lisa, especially because she was so optimistic. And Marge had a certain amount of optimism, too. Homer acting like an idiot or Bart acting mean can really dishearten some.one, but those characters remained hopeful that life would improve. 
You created a long list of Simpsons characters: Lou and Eddie, Sherry and Terry, Mr. Burns, Smithers, Krusty, Abe Simpson, and Kang and Kodos. 
Those were all characters who were first introduced in scripts we wrote. By the way, my partner Wally [Wolodarsky] and I didnÕt create these characters; we helped create them. People drew them, and we wrote a script where they were featured. A whole team of people goes into creating every episode. I always want to mention that because people are very quick to assign one person as the spark. I think itÕs natural to assume that George Lucas is the genius behind Star Wars; while heÕs one of the geniuses behind Star Wars, heÕs not the genius. Matt Groening is one of the people who helped create The Simpsons but not the person who created The Simpsons. So the group is what IÕm extolling these days, the power of the group. And I think thatÕs true of any great endeavor. The group makes it better. 
And where did your idea for some of those characters come from? 
Let me see. Lionel Hutz, Krusty the ClownÑthese are archetypes. So thereÕs nothing in particular that inspired Smithers or Burns or Krusty or Grampa Simpson or any of the ones that we helped create. Archetypes. The need in the story to have a psychiatrist; the need in the story to have a lawyer; the need to have a bus driver; the need to have best friendsÑnone of which really come sharply out of my life but all of which IÕve experienced in my life. There isnÕt one particular lawyer I met but lots of lawyers whom IÕve dealt with both in real life and those IÕve seen on TV. So we make a lawyer. Or bus drivers, or stoners, or any number of people. 
One of the great things about The Simpsons was we got a chance to highlight people from media and from ordinary life. We were doing a show about a small town, but we could also make fun of celebrities like Arnold Schwarzenegger or movie stars in various forms, rock singers, and what have you. We got to pull from our experiences from all over: from the movies and TV, from elementary school, and from our aunts and our uncles. Krusty the ClownÕs father was a rabbi. He wasnÕt based on any particular rabbi, but I interviewed my own rabbi and some other rabbis when we wrote it, just to get a sense of good torah. [Laughs] 
And you also introduced Sideshow Bob. 
Again, this is a group effort, and in the show ÒKrusty Gets BustedÓ [S1E12], we were creating a mystery. And the mystery needed a villain. And in this par.ticular case, we got one from Sideshow Bob. And it served as a funny irony of a brilliant genius clown sidekick. We loved it. 
Sideshow Bob. Fox Broadcasting / Photofest © & TM Fox Broadcasting 

When you created Smithers, was it your intent to make him gay, or did that evolve over time? 
We made him black and gay. 
Then why did you change him from black? 
I think people thought it was too much to be black and gay! Incidentally, when we wrote Mr. Burns, we called him Mr. Bellin after my friend Jay Bellin. But the name was changed by, I think, Sam Simon and some other peopleÑ and itÕs so much better than Mr. Bellin! And not the character, but the name Dr. Frink was inspired by my friend John Frink, who actually wound up writ.ing on the show. 
When you do a parody, say of The Twilight Zone, are you writing that from memory of having seen it in your childhood, or do you watch it again? 
Totally from memory. Always from memory. Because if I didnÕt remember perfectly, somebody else in the writersÕ room would! I mean, these guys are the smartest; they remember everything; thereÕs very little that gets past them. And theyÕre such creatures of media and our environment that thereÕs very little we need to look up. 
Can you think of any scenes you particularly enjoyed writing? 
One my favorite scenes we ever did was in ÒBart the DaredevilÓ [S2E8]. I love the scene where Homer has to threaten to ride the skateboard to prove to his son that itÕs dangerous. To keep Bart from jumping off the cliff, he tells him you wouldnÕt want to watch somebody you love get hurt. And Bart agrees. And right at that moment, Homer inadvertently steps on the skateboardÑand falls down the cliff anyway. I love that scene. I love it because he doesnÕt fall down the cliff Bugs Bunny style; he actually gets seriously hurt. I love it because thereÕs a lot of twists and turns; when he gets saved, he gets hurt again. ItÕs not just one gag; itÕs fourteen gags, and they all top each other. So IÕm very proud of that. 
What are the challenges of writing such a layered show with so many refer.ences? 
ItÕs no challenge at all! ItÕs a pleasure to be able to spill those references out finally. 
On a Simpsons, you can write a joke thatÕs the name of the most famous movie thatÕs out. Right now, thereÕs The Hunger Games Mockingjet. If I bothered to see that movie, IÕm sure I could write a joke about it and put it in a Simpsons episode. And I could cut to The Hunger Games set and write a quarter-of-a-page scene that takes place on The Hunger Games in the world of The Hunger Games. And then cut back away. On a regular TV show, I canÕt write a quarter-page scene that takes place in a brand-new world. So we were able to just keep the references rolling. 
The Simpsons often has celebrity guests. Do you have a favorite story about any of them? 
My favorite story is when we were trying to get Bruce Springsteen to be on it. WeÕd been calling his manager a lot, trying to book him. The message we kept getting was, ÒBruce is very interested, but we just canÕt find him right now.Ó So on this particular day, the manager had again told us they couldnÕt get ahold of him, and later that night, I saw Bruce Springsteen coming out of the same movie theater I was in. So I ran over to him, saying, ÒMr. Springsteen! Mr. Springsteen!Ó 
IÕm sure he was very happy to see you. 
He was not very happy to see me. He was with his wife, Patty. And he basi.cally stepped to protect her, as if I wereÑI donÕt knowÑan assassin coming to kill him. And I donÕt blame him one bit. I just didnÕt realize what it looked like from his point of view. I was wearing a Simpsons jacket, and I said, ÒIÕm a pro.ducer of The Simpsons, we were just trying to get ahold of youÑÓ 
And this jacket proves it! 
Exactly. A jacket that a million people could wear proves that IÕm a producer on The Simpsons. I said, ÒI just wanted to get ahold of you to find out if you were actually interested in doing this episode we did.Ó He said, ÒOh yeah, yeah, it sounds really great.Ó And of course, we never heard from him again. He didnÕt believe I was on The Simpsons. 
I remember directing Dustin Hoffman, which was a lot of fun. I went to his trailer to direct him for a few lines that he did on one of our shows. I got to work with Michael Jackson, which was interesting. Michael was an interesting fella, and he made some very interesting demands. He wanted a room full of heaters and twenty-four cases of Evian water in a room where we record The Simpsons. He never used them; he never had a sip of water or walked into the room full of heaters. And he looked like he was wearing orange makeup on his face. 
Do you have any fond recollections from The Simpsons room? 
I remember my partner Wally threw a cup of mashed potatoes onto the ceil.ing tiles, and it stuck there. It may still be there; I have no idea. WeÕd walk in every day, and weÕd see the 20th Century Fox mashed potatoes stuck to the ceil.ing. And weÕd just look at it, wondering when it would ever fall. 
IÕm curious how youÕd compare The Simpsons to The Flintstones. 
Based on the drought of prime-time cartoons between The Flintstones and us, we werenÕt expecting to be as successful as we were. 
I would say that HomerÕs nuclear power plant is not that far from the rock quarry that Fred Flintstone worked in. You can definitely see similarities be.tween Homer Simpson and Fred Flintstone. They both like to eat. They both like to bowl. They both like to get their way and are disappointed when that doesnÕt happen. They are both married to incredibly hot women who are too good for them. They both love their kid(s) and would do anything for them but sometimes, in a misplaced moment, forget about them. ÒWoo-hoo!Ó and ÒYabba dabba doo!Ó kind of sound similar. But I think Homer is a lot dumber than Fred. Fred also got more angry at Barney. 
The Flintstones seems to be more of a direct lift from The Honeymooners, and The Simpsons wasnÕt a lift of any particular family but just of America in general. I guess Springfield and Bedrock each had their own versions of America, and I would characterize them as an accurate sarcastic portrayal of what America looks like. So in The Flintstones, going out to eat at a drive-in restaurant or go.ing to a drive-in movie or being interested in technologyÑas Americans were in the sixtiesÑwas portrayed in a very funny way by Stone Age people who used animals to power things and used dinosaurs and used their feet to operate cars. Even though they were primitive, it was important to show technology. In The Simpsons, we showed consumerism in general, what to buy, what people wanted, how life is lived, what people envied, how every town has everything: Every town has an airport; every town has a bowling alley; every town has an amphi.theater; every town has a sports team. We tried to show every facet of American life that goes on and make our own version of it for Springfield. 
What differences do you notice in The Simpsons writing today compared to when you were there? 
I donÕt notice much. Man, theyÕre great. I publicly state that The Simpsons gets a bad rap for being an old, lazy show. ItÕs not an old, lazy show. ItÕs really funny, itÕs really sharp, and it makes me laugh every time I see it. Those guys have a hard job because telling a story thatÕs new and fresh is really hard after eight thousand episodes. So they do a really good job of keeping it fresh, of keep.ing it interesting, of keeping it relevant, and my hatÕs off to them. I had to leave after five years because I was exhausted! 
What advice would you give a writer new to a room, in general? 
I think your job is just to please the showrunner, to think of things theyÕd like and will agree with. If you pitch something thatÕs super funny but you know the showrunner will hate, youÕre just wasting everyoneÕs time. 
Most showrunners could and would write the shows themselves if they had the time, effort, and energy. And for the most part, what they really need is a bunch of brains working together to solve a problem, and hopefully the ideas they get are all or mostly within the range of stuff they like and might even think of themselves. Every now and then, an idea will strike a showrunnerÕs fancy that they could never possibly think of, so occasionally you may get stuff in like that. But primarily, your job is to read the room, read the show, read the showrun.nerÑand pitch towards what they doÑnot try to change the show. 
WhatÕs the worst kind of experience you can have in a writersÕ room? 
Generally speaking, the worst experiences in a room are when the person youÕre working for is very unhappy; thatÕs the hallmark of a bad experience. A showrunner who is unhappy in life, unhappy with the show, and especially unhappy at home. Because that means they never want to go home! 
Do you have any funny anecdotes from a room? 
Frasier was already a very successful show when I was brought onto it. And on my first day, I remember getting dressed, going to the Paramount store, and buying as much Frasier merchandise as I possibly could. Frasier hat, Frasier sweatshirt, Frasier sweatpantsÑand then wearing all of that into the writersÕ room the first day. I looked idiotic. And of course, I didnÕt say anything about it; I just walked in like nothing was weird. They laughed; they thought that was funny. Another thing was that the Frasier room was known for being a very stodgy, stiff, formal place. So pretty quickly into it, I brought in a fart machine. And I used it all the time. It sort of brings the level of discourse down to a much more basic level of jokes. I thought basically I would get fired within the first two weeks, or IÕd never get fired at all. And I never got fired at all. 
You said the room was stodgy. Can you elaborate a bit? 
They were known for being a very quiet room, a very contemplative room. Some rooms in Hollywood are very noisy, with people shouting back and forth; this was known for being more of a thought-provoking, quiet room. The reputa.tion of it was you werenÕt going to pitch anything until you had a fully formed pitchÑall the jokes, the wording, everything, exactly the way you wanted it, and otherwise you shouldnÕt say anything. 
David Lee says he doesnÕt know where that rumor got started, but he didnÕt agree with it. 
HeÕs absolutely right. ThatÕs not how it was at all. But that was the reputa.tion of it when I got there. Maybe because, when they brought in dinner, they served it on china, and we didnÕt eat out of cartons! And the expectation wasnÕt that you needed to have a fully formed joke every time, but you wanted to be at your bestÑbecause it was one of the best shows on TV at the time. You wanted to sort of pitch to the level of the show. 
Which type of FrasierÕs humor did you most enjoy writing? 
IÕm not a huge fan of puns. I like situational humor and humor that comes from characters, so I preferred writing a show where Frasier was in a situation that his ego couldnÕt let him out of, when you could take the character as created and put them into a situation where they could be their funniest. 
Did Frasier have a formula for plot? 
No, there was no formula. We just tried to pick out situations that were from our lives, that were interesting and could bring a lot of conflict and represent the characters well. It was just about making the funniest show we can. And trying not to repeat ourselves too much. 
Do you have a favorite episode you wrote? 
I guess my favorite is a Christmas episode called ÒMerry Christmas, Mrs. Moskowitz,Ó where Frasier has to pretend to be Jewish [S6E10]. And I like that a lot because itÕs a very waspy, white-bread family, and they all had to pretend to be Jews. But that was a product of a writersÕ room. So even though I get credited for writing the first draft of itÑwhich I didÑthe story came out of a group of people who came up with that idea. And writers in that room all revised it and worked on it and made the jokes funnier and finalized it. I just get to enjoy taking credit for the work of so many others, especially Chris Lloyd. 
Were you ever worried about going above the audienceÕs head on Frasier? 
No, never! Never. You canÕt go above the audienceÕs head. With all the great shows IÕve worked on, we never worried about talking down to an audience, ever. The Simpsons, Malcolm in the Middle, FrasierÑthey were shows where we assumed our audience was just as smart as we were, had the same reference base as we did, and if they didnÕt get the joke specifically because they didnÕt know the reference, theyÕd figure it out. 
How did you grow as a writer on Frasier, Jay? What did you learn from that experience? 
I learned that you can do an entire show in one or two sets, and you donÕt have to do short scenes; you can do scenes that go on for ten or twelve pages. It was like making a beautiful play every week. And I guess I learned the way Christopher Lloyd creates a show, and itÕs a very interesting way that I really like. HeÕs very smart, and he creates stories that have twists and turns and very interesting patterns that are unique to Chris. I canÕt really characterize it for you. He doesnÕt have one style; heÕs simply brilliant. HeÕs funny. HeÕs human. HeÕs a great observer of his life and times. HeÕs great with sophisticated plots and flawed characters. IÕm a fan. So when I say ÒlearnedÓ it, IÕm not sure I can re.peat exactly what Chris does, but I could probably come up with a Christopher LloydÐtype storyÑa poor imitation of a Christopher LloydÐtype story. 
What are some misconceptions that the average person has about your job? 
People seem to think comedy writing is really easy to do. And they also think, oddly enough, that thereÕs a market for somebody who has an idea. TheyÕll say, ÒI can provide you with the ideas; you just write them.Ó Like thatÕs the hard part. The hard part isnÕt coming up with ideas; we have lots of ideas. ItÕs executing the idea. Another misconception is that people will think a writer just writes for one character in a show. If I say I work on Simpsons, they ask, ÒWhat character do you write?Ó I tell them I write all the characters, and I do. ItÕs hard for them to imagine that one brain can imaginatively be all those different kinds of characters. 
Finally, did your dad give you any advice about writing? 
We donÕt really talk shop much, but over the years, IÕve gleaned from himÑ and from many other writers whom I respectÑto tell the truth. Find your truth and tell it. 

APPENDIX: MORE TALES FROM THE WRITERSÕ ROOM 
PITCHING 
Carl Kleinschmitt (The Dick Van Dyke Show, M*A*S*H): I was not a particu.larly good pitcher. IÕd find myself having two-thirds of a story and never being able to say, ÒAnd then, at the end, they do this.Ó I had the beginning and the middle, and I always went in hoping people could help me. 
But my partner Dale [McRaven] was not as vocal as I was. Garry Marshall has an anecdote in one of his books about how Dale kept saying, ÒYou always get to pitch the stories. I wanna pitch the story sometime.Ó And so Garry found Dale in the menÕs roomÑhe was throwing up because he was going to have to pitch the story! [Laughs] 
On another show we worked on, Dale insisted upon pitching the story. He got about ten, fifteen words out, and he turned to me and said, ÒTake it, Carl!Ó 
Kevin Abbott (The Golden Girls, Roseanne): You go in there, and you try to keep things light and funny, and you do your little patter, and you keep it short. IÕll tell you about my experience pitching for Golden Girls. They said, ÒGive us a log line that would be in TV Guide, and if weÕre doing something like that, weÕll stop you.Ó 
And the first one I did was, ÒOK, so Sophia gets a colostomy bag.Ó And thereÕs just shocked faces. I was like, ÒNah, IÕm just kidding, just kidding.Ó But it lightened up the room. And the show I thought I was going to sell didnÕt go; it was about DorothyÕs going bald. It turns out that the baldness skipped a generation of Petrillos, so Sophia had all her hair, and Dorothy was going to go bald. I had a lot of great scenes; it was really funny. And they loved it; they loved it. Then they said, ÒWe canÕt do it.Ó Why? They said, ÒWhen you see Estelle [Getty] and Betty [White] at the table, youÕll understand.Ó Because their hair was thinning, so they thought that would be too close. 
And the funny thing is the same thing had happened to me before when IÕd gone into ALF with a pitch for Willie that was also based around baldness. I pitched that it turns out the Melmacians had a cure for baldness, and Willie is talked into taking it. He wakes up the next morning, and heÕs got this luscious, full head of hair. Then he takes off his shirt, and it turns out heÕs just covered in fur. Because of course, the Melmacians are all covered in fur. And then it became an episode about vanity, and is he willing to have to shave his body every day in order to keep that full head of hair? 
And same thing: ÒWe love it, loved it. We canÕt do that.Ó Why not? Because the actor who played Willie was sensitive about his hair loss. I said, ÒHe knows heÕs going bald, right?Ó They go, ÒYeah, but he doesnÕt like to talk about it.Ó 
Stu Silver (Soap, Webster [Creator], Benson): In 1982, I cocreated a show for Brian Dennehy called Star of the Family. I was pitching it to ABC, and they were asking, ÒWhat are the legs of the show? What happens in episode 2? What hap.pens in episode 3?Ó And I got so POÕd I just walked out of the room. The head of ABC said, ÒStu, are you leaving in a huff ?Ó I said, ÒNo, IÕm leaving in a TR7.Ó And I kept walking. I went back to my offices at Paramount, and an hour later, they called and said, ÒYouÕre on the air.Ó Because I didnÕt try to grovel and I didnÕt complain; I just thought, ÒYou know what? Fuck this.Ó So apparently after I left, they were all sitting around, the head of Paramount and all the lieutenantsÑmy managerÕs twiddling his thumbsÑand finally he looks at them and says, ÒHeÕs not coming back. ThatÕs it.Ó An hour later, they told me I got picked up on a nonpitch because I didnÕt give a damn at that point. Their questions were stupid. 

LESSONS LEARNED 
Sol Saks (Bewitched [Creator], My Favorite Husband): I actually learned my most important lesson about writing in grammar school. We had a school news.paper in the eighth grade, and a kid named Frankie wrote comedy for it. And I gave him a joke that IÕd heard in vaudeville. I used names of classmates, and I said, ÒLeonard comes to see Shirley, and when he calls at her house, she says, ÔIÕm upstairs taking a shower.Õ And he shouts up to her, ÔWell, slip on something, and come on down.Õ So she slipped on a bar of soap and came down.Ó 
It got a good reception in class and around the school; the kids all loved it. And Frankie goes around proudly, heÕs getting all this adulationÑand IÕm get.ting nothing. But then a teacher asks him to stay after recess. And she tells him they donÕt want jokes like that anymore. So he came out to the schoolyard and punched me out. And thatÕs when I realizedÑand itÕs true of a TV writerÑwhen it works, youÕre anonymous. And when it doesnÕt work, youÕre fired. 
Arnie Kogen (The Dean Martin Show, The Tonight Show Starring Johnny Car.son, Newhart, The Carol Burnett Show): I learned to have patience in the writersÕ room. DonÕt make a pest of yourself. If your ÒbrilliantÓ joke is rejected, move on. DonÕt keep saying, ÒHey, this is a good spot to do that smoked salmon line!Ó Forget it. I donÕt know where or how I learned to stop. Probably from enough producers turning to me and saying, ÒKogenÑenough!Ó 
David Isaacs (M*A*S*H, The Simpsons, Frasier, Cheers): Ken and I were doing a new sitcom for Mary Tyler Moore [Mary], and we were pretty confi.dent that we could face all the challenges. And boy, we had no idea what we were in for. But we were pretty cocky walking around New York, thinking, ÒWow, weÕve got Mary Tyler Moore and thirteen episodesÑweÕre gonna con.quer the world!Ó About a year later, we were about as burned out as any two guys could ever be. 
I have a theory about the business and about lifeÑcalled Prince of the City. The theory is that, the minute you think youÕve got it conquered, the minute you think youÕve got the magic formula, the minute you think your work is going to make you suddenly stand out and you see yourself as the Prince of the City, the King of Los AngelesÑthatÕs the moment when you usually get a surprise thatÕll completely hit you between the eyes. So the lesson there is never get too carried away with yourself. Mary was a perfect example of that for Ken and me. 

COMEDY IS HARD! 
Bill Persky (The Dick Van Dyke Show, That Girl [Cocreator], Kate & Allie): The thing about writing comedy is, you do not know how you do it. So when I get up in the morning, I donÕt know that I still can do it. And until you do do it, you really arenÕt sure you can do it. And thatÕs a little frightening. ItÕs not like a brain surgeon who gets up one day and says, ÒOh Jesus, where do I make the incision?Ó And he looks it up. With comedy writing, thereÕs nowhere to look it up! 
Steve Skrovan (Seinfeld, Everybody Loves Raymond, Hot in Cleveland, ÕTil Death): Every time I finish a script, I think, ÒThatÕs it. ThatÕs the end of my career. IÕm done. I have no more stories. There are no more stories left in the world. Now IÕm gonna be found out.Ó Seriously, I think every writer has that anxiety. I know Larry David had itÑthatÕs it, weÕre done. 
Bill Masters (Seinfeld, Murphy Brown, Caroline in the City, Grace under Fire): We had a studio exec who wanted to come back to the room after the run-through just to see what we did. I said, ÒSure.Ó So we get back to the room and start to go over the script. And we were on one joke for forty-five minutes. Some.body will say something, and you change the joke, but that changes the next line. And ÒOh, we canÕt do that because that changes the scene.Ó Or ÒThatÕs a funny line, but she doesnÕt have that information until the next sceneÓÑor whatever. And you just keep going back and forth and back and forth. 
Finally, this exec said, ÒI donÕt know how you do this!Ó We said, ÒWhat did you think? WeÕre just so gifted, we come back here, and itÕs like weÕre knocking a tennis ball back and forth? This is hard work!Ó I donÕt know if we ever really get the best, but itÕs the best you can do up to a point. After forty-five minutes on that joke, we came up with a line. IÕm not saying it was worth anyone actually saying, but we got to move on. 
Steve Skrovan: This is not an easy thing to do, you know. ThatÕs why a child ac.tor can win an Academy AwardÑbut thereÕs no child that can win an Academy Award as a writer. 
Janet Leahy (The Cosby Show, Roseanne, Grace under Fire): Just as a general statement, almost every writer IÕve ever met whoÕs had longevity in this business has been on a show where, all of a sudden, somebody thinks theyÕre not funny. And youÕre like, ÒWhat happened to me? I was funny yesterday, I was funny on that show and those ten other showsÑbut you donÕt think IÕm funny here?Ó ItÕs happened to everyone I know. ItÕs very strange, but itÕs a phenomenon. 
Peter Mehlman (Seinfeld): I have a background in journalism, which helped me to become very good at observing things outwardly; I saw funny and interesting things in the world, and I always had my eyes open.
