But it was a bit of a handicap in that I wasnÕt at all used to looking inward at my own thoughts. So for television, I had to learn how to observe myself because stories you come up with from thoughts in your own head are the best stories. And interestingly, for a while, I went to the other extreme and was living basi.cally an existential life; I was so busy observing my life that I wasnÕt really living it. Life just became a research project because coming up with ideas for story lines was all that mattered. In a way, the writing was much less important than coming up with great ideas. So I was hyper-, hyperaware. 
And IÕve told the story once of how I went to a health spa to unwind after a season. I met a girl there, and after hanging out with her a few days, we started making out. And as weÕre kissing, I start to think how amazing it is that every girl has her own kissing system. Her hands go here, her lips do thisÑand sud.denly I realized, ÒOh my God, IÕm watching my own thoughts in the middle of making out! This is bad.Ó 
Sam Bobrick (Saved by the Bell [Creator], The Andy Griffith Show, The Smoth.ers Brothers Comedy Hour): You have to be immune to rejection. ItÕs like being an actor: YouÕll get turned down for a lot of shows youÕd like to do. ItÕs probably a much tougher situation for those starting out now since itÕs a struggle with the economy and suchÑand I think itÕs always good to come from a wealthy family! 

THE WRITERSÕ ROOM 
Phil Doran (All in the Family, WhoÕs the Boss?, The Facts of Life): A roomful of writers trying to come up with a punch line is a competitive sport. Everybody wants to move up the food chain and get more money and power. And youÕre really hoping that the guy ahead of you for promotion stumbles or quits. 
YouÕre cooped up with people twelve, fourteen hours a day, so itÕs like being in a submarineÑand after a while you just hate everybody. So yeah, your nerves are raw, and where people used to do funny and endearing things in the begin.ning, now you wanna kill them every time they do them. 
You know the classic story about Danny Simon, NeilÕs brother? HeÕs in a room full of writers, and theyÕre arguing over a joke. And Danny Simon says, ÒYou donÕt have to tell meÑIÕm the best writer in this town!Ó And somebody says, ÒYouÕre not even the best writer in your family.Ó So there are those kinds of stories, and IÕve been on shows where there were such strong personalities in the writing room that there was constant bickering and arguing, even hatred. 
Gene Perret (The Carol Burnett Show, ThreeÕs Company, MamaÕs Family): So much writing on TV is teamwork, so you really want to feel at home with those people. Sometimes if you throw an idea out and you get ridiculed for it, then you get tentative about throwing out another idea. So when we were head writ.ers, we tried to keep the room very positive, and weÕd encourage people to just listen and not put down jokes. One time on The Carol Burnett Show, a writer did a little joke, and another writer said, ÒYouÕre gonna go with a cheap joke like that?Ó And the gentleman looked at him and said, ÒThe next thing you say better be brilliant!Ó 
[Another time,] it was a full room. We started working at about 4 p.m. and worked until sunrise or even later. And in fact, the cleaning lady came in, and she was mopping the floors and emptying waste baskets. We had one more joke to do. Somebody pitched one, and we said, ÒType that, and letÕs get out of here.Ó And the guy at the typewriter, just as a joke, turned to the cleaning lady and said, ÒWhat do you think of it?Ó She said, ÒI think youÕre reaching a bit.Ó 
And we dropped the joke because the cleaning lady didnÕt like it; we had to think of another one! So thatÕs how insecure writers are. 
David Isaacs: ThereÕs a famous story about a kind of room you donÕt want to be in. A particular showrunner kept her writers in limbo for hours while she went back to the office and worked on rewriting. It was a punishing schedule. The writ.ers were never quite sure where they stood or what their contribution was, and sometime, very late one night, a writer got up and started walking out. The show-runner asked, ÒWhere are you going?Ó and the writer said she was leaving. The showrunner said, ÒYou canÕt leave now. WeÕre not done.Ó And the other woman goes, ÒYouÕre making me bleed from my ass!Ó Which is about as graphic and true a statement as you can make about a show thatÕs difficult to write and difficult to do every week. Because when youÕre not sure what the showÕs about or where you are in the narrative or your contribution to it, it can be a very, very difficult process. 
Adam Chase (Friends, Phenom, Mom): The process of group writing is inher.ently competitive because everyone is offering options, and the boss is picking the best options. But it doesnÕt have to be mean spirited, and you donÕt have to keep score. But some people do. Sometimes when you pitch a joke, by the middle of that pitch, itÕs obvious what the last word will be. ThereÕs a kind of person I call a Òclaim jumper,Ó who will say the last word of your pitch very loudly as though that somehow gives them coauthorship or allows them to take credit for the pitch. ItÕs incredibly irritating because you just want to present your idea and see if people like it or not! Or sometimes people will just pitch grammar. TheyÕll be watching the screen and say, ÒNeed a comma there,Ó to the writersÕ assistant. The scriptÕs go.ing to get proofread; someone else will notice that; thatÕs not what you were hired for. The writers who do these things tend not to be the most talented. 
Phoef Sutton (Cheers, Newhart, Bob): When you start out in a writing room, youÕre very precious about your contributions. You go home and say, ÒI got a joke in!Ó And when the show airs, you say, ÒI wrote that line!Ó But later on, when youÕve gotten good and when itÕs flowing and youÕre really part of the team, you donÕt remember what you wrote. It all just becomes the mind of the room; it becomes its own thing. 
You enter into the minds of the people around you, and they enter into yours; it almost sounds like science fiction, but you definitely get to that point. Many times, IÕve seen a writer pitch a setup for a joke, and then another writer pitched a punch lineÑand they wonÕt have talked about it beforehandÑit just happened, boom, boom. And thatÕs a uniquely magical process. 
Adam Chase: The Friends room had an atmosphere of friendly competition. Creators Marta [Kauffman] and David [Crane] encouraged us to be very pas.sionate; they liked it when we took a position and felt something deeply enough to dig in our heels. We argued for about two years about whether or not Monica and Chandler should get together! I mean it was crazyÑyelling, screaming argu.mentsÑfriendly but very passionate. 
Steve Skrovan: Anything goes in the writersÕ room, and you get jaded. ItÕs very hard to make comedians and comedy writers laugh because weÕve heard everything. I donÕt know if this is original with him, but I heard George Carlin say a regular person can watch somebody walking down the street and fall in a manhole and laugh. But for a comedian, itÕs got to be an old lady walking down the street and falling into the manhole. Just because thatÕs darker. 
Lila Garrett (Get Smart, Bewitched, Maude, All in the Family, Barney Miller): A well-run writing room is like an orgy. You close the door, and in a manner of speaking, everybody is naked. You donÕt let anybody in, and you donÕt really reveal what happens, and everybody is on the same drug. Everyone is locked in a competition until the bitter end. And there is no escape. So if youÕre looking for pressure, youÕll find it there. And if you havenÕt got a sense of humor, donÕt let yourself get locked in a writersÕ room! 
David Isaacs [regarding the roomÕs lingo]: ÒTen percentersÓ are insider jokes and references so obscure that we expect only 10 percent of viewers to get them. For example, one year on M*A*S*H, Ken and I named characters after an LA Dodger or Brooklyn Dodger. Names are hard to come up with, so we just used famous baseball playersÕ names for every patient, officer, and military personnel that came through. 
ÒStabbing the frogÓ means someone pitched a joke, everyone laughed, and then someone tried to improve it, and then someone tried to improve the im.provementÑand by the time youÕre down to the third or fourth generation of improving, no one is laughing anymore. ItÕs literally taking a stab at making a joke better and taking another stab at itÑuntil youÕve stabbed it to death. 
We had a few [expressions] that somehow grew out of the rooms at Para.mount. One was a ÒBono,Ó named after a restaurant on Melrose owned by Sonny Bono. Almost every seven months, the restaurant in that location would go out of business, and thereÕd be another restaurant there two weeks later. It was BonoÕs, then SylvioÕs, and then ArturoÕs. And sometimes weÕd have a spot in a script where we couldnÕt make a joke work. So a Bono was a location that just didnÕt work. And then we had ÒNakamura,Ó which was a joke that depended on a joke earlier in the script, and that earlier joke didnÕt work, so then all those jokes that were callbacks to it were now invalid. 
Phoef Sutton: And then thereÕs the locker room smell. When you get ten or so people in a room sitting on the same sofa and spending all day togetherÑeven if the room is done up nicelyÑit gets kind of rancid. IÕve heard that, at the end of Cheers, when they moved the sofa weÕd sat on for all those years, they found rats living there! 
Steve Skrovan: ÒRoom bitsÓ are handy if you pitch a joke and it bombs. Lew Schneider is the king of the room bits. HeÕd pitch a joke that bombed, and sometimes heÕd take a knee and act like heÕd just been punched in the solar plexus and he was out of breath, or heÕd start hacking, like he had something in his throat. HeÕd say, ÒAhh, I just got hold of some bad pitch!Ó And weÕd all laugh. And that enabled him to recover from the embarrassment of the joke not working. 
You need a million bad ideas to hit on the one good one, so doing room bits and savers for yourself is a way to tolerate the risk. Because youÕre still going to get a laugh. 
I used to do the thing where I would just slowly fall out of my chair. And my friend Mike Rowe taught me whatÕs called the Òget up and go take.Ó You pick up everything on the table near youÑyour computer, pens, a rubber ball, or whateverÑand just load your arms up as if youÕre leaving in a huff, as if youÕre quitting the show. And you walk out of the room. 
Sherwood Schwartz (The Red Skelton Hour, GilliganÕs Island [Creator], The Brady Bunch [Creator], I Married Joan): On The Red Skelton Hour, I had three writers in the room: Marty Ragaway, Dave OÕBrien, and my brother Al Schwartz. Jesse Goldstein was with us at the beginning, and later Marty replaced him. 
But it was a very difficult situation because Jesse Goldstein, my best friend at the time, was dying. He had a genetic form of cancer that his father had had. And he didnÕt know he was dying because his wife begged us not to tell him. So Dave OÕBrien and I would have to write funny, and sometimes he and I would just leave the room and cry. Then weÕd come back and continue with this very broad comedy. 
And it was so wonderful dramatically that I wanted to turn it into a play where a guy is dying and the other two people know it and he doesnÕt. And yet they have to write funny. It would have been a great play, but I could never write it. I tried for twenty-five years to write it. I never could. 

REWRITING 
Bill Persky: Sometimes weÕd rewrite freelancersÕ shows 100 percent. There was one writer who came in, and literally the only thing of his still in the script was his name, ÒWritten by.Ó And at the end of the show, he came to see us, and he said, ÒI gotta be honest with you. When I handed it in, I didnÕt realize it was this good!Ó He thought he wrote it. 
Stu Silver: Sometimes when the show didnÕt work at ThursdayÕs camera block.ing, youÕd tell all your writers to go home. YouÕd call your wife and say, ÒHoney, IÕm not gonna see you tonight,Ó and youÕd rewrite the whole script in one night. So on Friday, youÕd be shooting a show youÕd written the night before. I did that once on Brothers, and I did it once on Webster. That usually leads to divorce after youÕve done it for several years. 
Adam Chase: [On Friends,] because we did an incredible amount of rewriting between takes, most of the tapings were very longÑweÕd start at six and could go until 1 or 2 a.m. There was always a feeling of, ÒLetÕs not stop until itÕs per.fectÑif we can beat that joke, letÕs beat it.Ó Sometimes weÕd interrupt taping to rewrite an entire scene. To the actorsÕ credit, we could show them a page, and theyÕd look at it and go, ÒGot it.Ó And then theyÕd do the whole new scene, hav.ing instantaneously memorized it. It was like a magic trick. 
Joel Rapp (McHaleÕs Navy, Bewitched, The Donna Reed Show): My partner Sam Locke and I wrote for McHaleÕs Navy. TheyÕd hired Si Rose to be the story edi.tor; weÕd turned down the job because we figured weÕd make a whole lot more money just writing. We handed in our first script to Si, and he called us up and said, ÒBoys! These characters are all wrong.Ó I said, ÒSi, we invented those char.acters. How wrong can they be?Ó 
And weÕd get a call about every script we handed in. Every single one. ÒAw, boys! Are you really in the Writers Guild? Are you sure youÕre professionals?Ó And then weÕd go in for a meeting, and heÕd have forty changes, and weÕd get a second copy of the script, and it would be 50 percent him and 50 percent us. Then weÕd get the final script, and it would be about 95 percent what we handed in in the first place. Si Rose was a very nice guy. But this happened every script. And it was like that for three years. 
When weÕd turned in the very last script in the series, we went for a meeting with Si. He looked at us and said, ÒIÕm very disappointed, boys; this is not up to your usual standards.Ó And this was after three years of denigrating every word we ever wrote in every script. But thatÕs what he said: ÒBoys, IÕm very disap.pointed. This is not up to your usual standards.Ó 
Phil Doran: I was constantly having to leave out jokes I really liked. Some because of censorship; sometimes the actors didnÕt like them, or the show was too long and needed to be cut. Mort Lachman would come back from meetings where theyÕd discussed what changes we had to make, and heÕd say, ÒGentle.men, some of our children must die.Ó ThatÕs what it felt like; it felt like you were killing your children. But we did it. You move on. And you think, ÒWell, youÕll use it some other time.Ó But you never do. 
Joel Rapp: We had a contract for six Joey Bishop Shows, and we wrote the first one, and then we went to watch the taping. And there were about three words of our script left in what they finally shot. We asked the producer what happened, and he said, ÒJoey got a hold of the script, and he changed everything.Ó But we still got the writer credit, which was fine. So then we wrote a second script, and the same thing happened. So for the third script, we turned in thirty-six blank pages, and we were fired. And it was fine with us. We were making plenty of money and had other jobs. 
Sam Bobrick: On Andy Griffith, IÕd say 80 percent of what we wrote would go on the air. When we wrote for early television, they were our words. But as the comedy half-hours progressed, my words were used less and less. We won a Writers Guild Award for our original version of a Get Smart script. But when the show aired, it was unnerving how much theyÕd changed it. I didnÕt like that because I felt, if my name were going to be on it, it better represent my work. By the late eighties, I wasnÕt very proud of much of the stuff I did because what would happen to a script was upsetting. 
Arnie Kogen: I appreciated it when somebody made my script better in a re.write. But some writers didnÕt want their script tinkered with. I had a clause in my contract that my scripts must be rewritten! Not actually, but I didnÕt mind. 

BIGGEST LAUGHS 
Hal Kanter (The George Gobel Show, Julia [Creator], All in the Family): One of the biggest laughs All in the Family ever got was a joke I wrote about Gloria being pregnant. They were talking about it, but Archie hated the word pregnant. He said, ÒDonÕt say that. It sounds like youÕve done something.Ó And Mike says, ÒWe did!Ó I remember that laugh went on and on. That was one of the biggest laughs in history. 
Elliot Shoenman (Maude, The Cosby Show, Home Improvement): In the very first script I wrote, MaudeÕs eight-year-old grandson wanted to see an X-movie. And Maude was explaining the difference between an X-rated film, an R-rated film, and a G-rated film. And the joke was something like, ÒA G-rated film is about animals, an R-rated film is about animals having sex like people, and X-rated is people having sex like animals.Ó It was something in that comedy rhythm. It was one of the things that showed them I could write a sort of com.plex joke, and it got really big laughs. 
Peter Mehlman: In my Seinfeld episode ÒThe VirginÓ [S4E10], I got an incred.ibly long laugh when the second the girl admits to Jerry sheÕs a virgin, and Elaine comes in and starts right in on this story about how she opened her pocketbook and her diaphragm came flying out. And sheÕs going, ÒSo IÕm sitting there in the middle of all these people staring at my diaphragm,Ó and sheÕs really punching the word diaphragm. Then she looks at the girl who just said sheÕs a virgin, and she says, ÒLook, I donÕt have to tell youÓÑand the laugh went on and on. 
David Isaacs: The longest laugh on Cheers may have been when Sam and Diane kissed in the last episode of the first year. They were fighting, and Sam said, ÒAre 
you as hot as I am right now?Ó And she said, ÒHotter!Ó And then they kissed. I remember standing on the stage and being surprised when the audience just ex.ploded with cheers and applauseÑweÕd had no idea anyone cared all that much! My partner Ken said, ÒWeÕve peaked. WeÕll never be as good as this again.Ó 
Arnie Kogen: There was a series several years ago called The Invisible Man, and I once wrote a sketch for Carol Burnett called ÒThe Invisible ManÕs Mother.Ó Other mothers were talking about their son the doctor, their son the lawyer, and their son the businessmanÑand her son was the invisible man. HeÕd come over to visit, and sheÕd complain to him, ÒWhy canÕt you be like other people?Ó HeÕd say, ÒThis is the way I am.Ó And sheÕd yell at him, ÒStand up straight when IÕm talking to you!Ó And heÕd ask, ÒHow do you know IÕm not standing up straight?Ó And sheÕd say, ÒA mother knows!Ó 

CENSORSHIP 
John Rappaport (Rowan & MartinÕs Laugh-In, The Odd Couple, M*A*S*H): Laugh-In was very, very way out there and highly advanced. We never really did anything dirty, but the producer, George Schlatter, loved to sneak stuff in, to try to get away with it. And when he learned one day that there was a Lake Titicaca, he went berserk: ÒOh my God, I can say that on TV, and thereÕs nothing they can do about it!Ó 
I remember one thing we did that I wrote a lot of, and the network never caught on. We had a guy called Mervyn the Magnificent; he wore a big black cape, and you would only see him from behind, and all of a sudden, he would spread the cape out. And whoever was looking at him would say, ÒMervyn, that was magnificent.Ó And the networks never seemed to understand that this was a guy flashing! They allowed it on the air because they just didnÕt know what we were doing. And nowadays you can say, ÒHey, look at the big pack!Ó But Mervyn the Magnificent was much more clever. 
Hal Kanter: On one of my early shows, a censor ruled that the joke ÒI was a tall baby. I was born on December 18, 19, and 20Ó was too graphic to use on the air. 
Gene Perret: In one sketch on the Burnett show, Carol was being interviewed at a nudist camp. It was all very strategically done; the journalist was outside, and Carol was behind some bushesÑbut you got the idea she was nude. The jour.nalist asked, ÒHow do you dance at a nudist colony?Ó Her response was, ÒVery carefully.Ó And the censors objected to that. So we said, ÒWell, you come up with a line.Ó And the censors came up with, ÒHow do you dance at a nudist colony? Cheek to cheek.Ó That sounded worse to us, but they approved it. 
Another was about a Santa Claus sketch. They told us we couldnÕt say that about Santa Claus; itÕs disrespectful. One of the network execs said, ÒPeople might think itÕs the real Santa Claus.Ó And we said, ÒYou still believe in Santa Claus?Ó So we got notes like that. 
Sherwood Schwartz: [On GilliganÕs Island,] I came home one day after IÕd been having trouble with the censors over Dawn and Tina. They were worried about TinaÕs cleavage and DawnÕs navel because in those days you couldnÕt show a navel. My wife asked me what was wrong, and I said, ÒBetween TinaÕs tits and Mary AnnÕs navel, I canÕt get a minute of peace!Ó 
Joel Rapp: In those days, we didnÕt have a problem because we knew the rules, and there really wasnÕt a show where you could get in trouble! Some.times weÕd write the filthiest scenes you can imagine with, say, Donna Reed and her husband, but that was our way of having a little fun. As a joke, we wrote a couple of scenes for that show that were pornographic, for GodÕs sakeÑand we did it just to get our own rocks off. Then weÕd hand it in, and theyÕd call and say, ÒThatÕs hysterical. Now letÕs talk about the script.Ó And weÕd say, ÒFine,Ó and that would be that. But once in a while, we needed to do something weird because it can get awfully boring. 
Kevin Abbott: On Roseanne, we had to change a joke where Darlene was sup.posed to be doing cocaine. Roseanne confronts her, and Darlene says, ÒI only tried it once, and I didnÕt like it.Ó And Roseanne says, ÒOh come on. Nobody doesnÕt like it the first time!Ó That one got dung by the Standards; I donÕt re.member what we changed it to, but it wasnÕt as good. 
Stu Silver: On Star of the Family, there was a line I thought IÕd never get away with, but the censor kid who came from the studio let it go because he had no idea what I was talking about. The episode took place at the fire department. A fireman goes into a house and rescues a kidÕs hamster by giving it mouth-to.mouth resuscitation. The night before I wrote this, there was a fireman on the Carson show whoÕd actually done that, and he started getting such a ribbing from people that he was going to quit the department. 
So in my script the guy says, ÒIÕm outta here. I saved this kidÕs pet, but I canÕt take the ribbing anymore.Ó And one of his firefighter partners says, ÒYou can save a million people, you can save homes from destruction, but suck one hamsterÑÓ and that was the line. ItÕs a takeoff on an old dirty joke. And the audience went crazy. But the kid didnÕt understand the reference, so he let it go. 
Jim McGinn (Julia, ValentineÕs Day): IÕll tell you about a Julia episode thatÕs most meaningful to meÑbecause it damn near didnÕt get done! It was about a black burglar. He steals two TVs in plain sight since everyone assumes that, because heÕs black, he must be JuliaÕs friend. Her neighbor asks him, ÒWhat are you going to do with JuliaÕs TV set?Ó He says, ÒIÕm gonna take it into the shop and repair it.Ó ÒOh, thatÕs so nice.Ó The landlord says, ÒHey, where are you go.ing with that? Are you giving Julia a good price on fixing it?Ó ÒYes, sir.Ó And the landlord gives him his set to get repaired. 
So the script goes over to NBC, and Standards and Practices says, ÒWe canÕt have a black burglar; weÕre taking this thing too far.Ó Hal [Kanter] is trying to de.fend it. And they decide to send it to a man named Stanley Robinson, who was a VP at NBC. They said, ÒIf StanleyÕs comfortable with it, weÕll do it.Ó Stanley reads it, and he puts a little note up on the front page that says, ÒApproved. Not all my people are brain surgeons!Ó He was black. He was the only black execu.tive in network television at the time. And so thatÕs how the script was approved. And of course, I loved Stanley Robinson from that time forward! 
Gene Perret: On The Bob Hope Show, we once went to a place like Sea World to do a sketch. Bob went into the penguin area, and we did jokes that were words or thoughts from the penguins. And the park objected. They said they donÕt ap.prove of anthropomorphizing animals. I was at the front of it, and I said theyÕre not talkingÑthese are just thoughts they might have, and we donÕt know what penguins think! They called the head office and finally gave us permission to do the sketch. I donÕt know if it played well or not. 
John Rappaport: On M*A*S*H, I discovered that, when youÕre successful, the networks donÕt bother you too much. And because our show was so successful, we could basically do anything we wanted, outside of a couple swear words. WeÕd get a call saying, ÒPotter has five hells and three damns. Can you make it two hells and one damn?Ó That was how it went. 
I suppose now itÕd be, ÒWe got seven fucks and two cocksuckers. Can you make it eight fucks and nine cocksuckers?Ó 

THAT ODD PERSPECTIVE 
Elliot Shoenman: I certainly think comedy writing comes from angst or anger, and for me growing up, it was a survival mechanism. I watched all the sitcoms because our house was very dark. And my parentsÕ relationship was dark. To me, it was a tremendous escape, and I picked it up and obviously had a knack for it. I think also itÕs in your bonesÑand why you see things a certain way and why your comic sensibility is what it is are inexplicable. 
Bill Persky: You have to be able to write about painful thingsÑbut in a way that allows for the humanity of the experience rather than the tragedy of it. ItÕs like when my mother died; she was in her nineties, and on a Friday, she went in and left her dentures with the dentist because they were hurting her. She died on Sunday, and she was being buried on Monday. And my sister and I thought, ÒMy God, if sheÕs buried without her teeth, sheÕll come back and haunt us!Ó 
I guess maybe in a way, when things are tragic, youÕre looking for light in them. YouÕre looking for a little air to creep in. So I went and picked up my motherÕs teeth, and I had them in my pocketÑand all the way to the funeral parlor, my mother was biting me! So you have to be able to look for the absurd. Because in the worst situation, we behave in absurd waysÑand weÕre all assholes when these things happen. 
Carl Kleinschmitt: I attribute my being funny or having a sense of humor and seeing funny stuff to the fact that I felt like an outsider as a kid. Because I had a name like Kleinschmitt during World War II, OK? And when I played war with other kids, they got to be the Americans, and I was the Nazi all the time. And I think, if you feel like an outsider, you start observing. And when you observe, you see things from different angles than the obvious. And everyone I know who was a successful comedy writer always had a way of looking at things from the outside rather than inside out. 
I think all the great black comics came out of pain and persecution. Richard Pryor, with whom I worked briefly, was raised in a whorehouse. His mother was a prostitute, and for his whole life, he was the outsider. 
Elliot Shoenman: A comic person has a fascination with the way people speak and the way people sound and the way people think. When I was in New York and struggling to write, IÕd take the train back home late at night. And there were times when I was listening to a conversation next to me and I wouldnÕt get off at my stop because I just had to hear the rest of what they were saying. And I re.member when IÕd ride to the end of the line and have to go all the way backÑbut I couldnÕt not listen to the rest of their conversation; it had so much color to it. 
Bill Persky: I see [the world] as absurd. Most of what I write is based on my view of things and my willingness to be an assholeÑwhich is what I am. IÕm very unprotected, and I just go flying into things and then find myself saying, ÒHow the hell did this happen?Ó Most people are afraid to laugh at themselves and to acknowledge and love their weaknesses. And I acknowledge and love all of the things that are wrong with me. Except for my backÑI donÕt know. 
Janet Leahy: Most people can walk into a room and see a room, but a comedy writer would see the fly doing some sort of spinning thing on the wall, you know what I mean? The comedy writer usually sees more [of the world] because theyÕre often sensitive, even though theyÕll try to hide it with humor. But thereÕs a brilliant capacity for pulling out words and language and memories from one place and adding a word and memory from another place and coming up with a joke. 
And with the good ones, thereÕs a great perspective. They can look at situ.ations where other people are taking it all so seriously, and something in them says, ÒWe really donÕt have to.Ó And by the way, sometimes that can be inappro.priate! [Laughs] But thatÕs what you get. 
Elliot Shoenman: I once did a play about suicide. This is a true story. My father committed suicide when I was eighteen, and in the early 2000s, I wrote a book about him. I tracked down a lot of his story, and I discovered that he took a cab to the Hudson River from where he worked and then killed himself. And I duplicated that journey. I took a cab from there just to see what it felt like. And on the way, one of my thoughts was that my father was notoriously cheapÑand what kind of tip does a guy leave on the way to his suicide? And I used that in my play, and thatÕs real stuff. That really is where my mind went, and I believe in that kind of comedy as a survival mechanism. 
Phoef Sutton: You can make any subject funny. If youÕre close enough to it, itÕs naturally funny. I had a brain aneurysm, and I wrote a pilot about thatÑ and itÕs hilarious. 
Arnie Kogen: YouÕre shaped for comedy at an early age. You have funny family, a nutty uncle, witty buddiesÑchances are strong you might turn out to be funny. 
Somewhere along the way, people like George Carlin, Chris Rock, and Albert Brooks just started thinking funny. ItÕs not just Jewish and Italian kids in big cit.ies. Not just an urban thing. IÕm sure Garrison Keillor probably was an amusing kid somewhere in Minnesota. Probably the funniest kid at the lake. 
My son, Jay, was the funniest eleven-year-old IÕve ever seen, throwing lines out all over the place. Perhaps too many. Once he was playing with some friends at our backyard in Encino, and I heard my wife, Sue, shout out a warning to him: ÒJay, didnÕt I tell you? No one-liners at the pool!Ó Like it was a safety thing. Wait one hour before throwing a quip. 
John Rappaport: When I lecture at comedy writing classes, people usually ask if you can teach a person to be funny. And IÕll say no. I can teach you some of the tricks of the trade, but if by the age of five you werenÕt out to dinner with your parents and putting two straws in your mouth and going Òarf arf arf,Ó then youÕre not gonna be funny. Certainly, you can learn writing and crafting and all that, but I think just a slight tinge of insanity is required from day 1. 
Gene Perret: Someone once wrote a book on comedy, and on the first page they said, ÒYou canÕt teach comedy. No one can learn that.Ó And on the third page they said, ÒI want to thank the following people, who taught me everything I know about comedy!Ó 

FAMOUS FEUDING 
Kevin Abbott: IÕll give you an anecdote about my first Golden Girls taping. The producers and writers sat up in a separate booth; there were phones so you could call and get jokes and whatnot, but they werenÕt down on the floor at all. And before the taping started, I wandered around onstage where the sets were because this was the first good show IÕd ever worked on. And IÕm just kind of soaking it all in and in awe of this. IÕm walking behind the kitchen set, and Bea [Arthur] and Betty [White] are running lines supposedly, and they start scream.ing at each other. And Bea yells at her, ÒJust learn your lines, you fucking bitch!Ó But she didnÕt say Òbitch.Ó 
And I said Òfucking,Ó so you know itÕs worse than that! So I run up to the pro.ducersÕ booth, and I say, ÒOh my God, thereÕs a huge problem down on stage.Ó TheyÕre like, ÒWhatÕs goinÕ on?Ó And I tell them whatÕs going on, and they say, ÒUh no no, that happens every show. DonÕt worry about it.Ó 

CAREER HIGHS AND LOWS 
Jim McGinn: It was the first day of shooting Julia, and Hal [Kanter] had told me they were filming on stage 19. So I walked over there to deliver my first draft, and I saw Diahann Carroll standing alone. I went up and said, ÒMs. Carroll, my nameÕs Jim McGinn. IÕm one of your writers, and I just want to wish you well.Ó She thanked me, and I left. 
And Hal goes over to her, and she tells him that one of the writers stopped by and wished her well. Hal asks her which one, and she says she didnÕt catch his name. Hal says, ÒWhat did he look like?Ó She thinks for a moment and she says, ÒWell, heÕs pink.Ó And both of them started to laugh. So it became a running joke, and as I got to know her, IÕd say, ÒListen, weÕre taking this skin color thing a little far. Black, maybe, but pink?Ó That Christmas I bought an ad in Daily Variety, and I said, ÒDear Hal and Diahann. Thank you for a green Christmas. Love, your pink writer.Ó They got a kick out of that. 
And the last time I saw Hal was at the Writers Guild Theater, when they had a memorial service for Allan Manings. Hal came in, and he was very frail; he was on a cane, standing near the door. I went up and softly hugged him. And he whispered to me, ÒHowÕs my pink writer?Ó 
He died two or three months later. I attended his memorial service there, and it was the biggest IÕd ever seen. If that theater has 500 seats, there had to be 490 people. He was just such a nice man. If you did something he didnÕt like, heÕd usually handle it with a joke. One time I was supposed to have lunch with him, and I was late getting there. So heÕs waiting for me at the commissary at Twentieth, and heÕs obviously a little peeved. I said, ÒHow are you, Hal?Ó He said, ÒPrompt.Ó And that took care of that. 
Arnie Kogen: I was the head writer [on Mary]. There were some funny bits and pieces. We had Mary Tyler Moore singing ÒDead Skunk in the Middle of the Road.Ó We had a musical production number featuring the Ed Asner dancersÑ six guys in shirts and ties looking like Ed Asner doing a disco dance. Mary did a monologue saluting Òdriftwood.Ó It was fresh. It was different. It was offbeat. 
The series was canceled after three episodes. America didnÕt like Mary doing anything but the Mary Richards character they were used to. 
Sherwood Schwartz: The president of CBS was a very strong-minded guy named Jim Aubrey; he was very successful, very powerful. I presented the idea for GilliganÕs Island, and he said, ÒNo oneÕs going to keep coming back and watching these same seven people. And how many stories can you do?Ó Well, I already had lots of stories; I knew how I wanted it to go. A lot of steps were involved, but finally, after I reedited the film, it tested very well. And Aubrey knew he had to put shows on that tested well, so when he called me, he said, ÒSherwood, I still hate your fucking showÑand IÕm putting it on the air!Ó 
Sam Bobrick: Joe Barbera was one of the Flintstones producers whom I liked very much. One time he said he wanted to have lunch with me, and I was very excited, since IÕd never had a Hollywood lunch. I arrived at his office, and we were sitting there talking; I think I was pitching a show. And he says, ÒLetÕs have lunch.Ó And IÕm all dressed to go to lunch. He says, ÒWhat kind of sandwich do you like?Ó He had a vending machine where you put in fifteen cents and you get your sandwich. 
Elliot Shoenman: I was doing a rewrite and was in a kind of panicked spot in a script, and I used an old Honeymooners joke. I could not today tell you what it was. And Rod Parker, the producer, singled that one out. He said, ÒI like the rewrite, and I especially like this joke.Ó And I was very excited and happy, and I was about to tiptoe out of there. And then he said, ÒI like it so much because I wrote it originally!Ó 
So those kinds of things happen, but I got away with it because I was just kind of winging it. I also think they got a kick out of the fact that I was this twenty-six-year-old guy who could steal an old jokeÑthat I knew the old jokes. 
Arnie Kogen: IÕd write the Tonight Show monologue on the subway. I lived in Brooklyn, and I used to get on at Sheepshead Bay Road and write the mono.logue on the BMT going into Rockefeller Center. I usually got very funny around Newkirk Avenue. 
It was tough for me to get more than three jokes in the monologue. IÕd always watch the cue card guy write the jokes before the show to see if mine were in there. And then one day, I see him writing a fourth joke. It was another career milestone for me. I remember the date, it was July 8, 1966. I sometimes forget my anniversaryÑbut never the day I finally landed four jokes in the Carson monologue. 
Phil Doran: After Doug and I had signed [to write Hot L Baltimore], we came in to meet Norman Lear officially for the first time. And you have to understand, this was a guy with eight or nine hit shows running. So we walk into his office, and it must have been like what MGM was like back in the Golden Age: people coming and going, movie stars and this and thatÑa lot of action. I walk in, and the first person I see is Mike Wallace. And itÕs a crew from 60 Minutes, and theyÕre filming Norman. We say, ÒWeÕll come back later,Ó but Norman says, ÒNo, no, come in, boys, come in.Ó So we sit down on the couch, and theyÕre shooting over our shoulder and Norman is saying, ÒNow boys, the essence of comedy is when the conflict and the resolution,Ó and heÕs going on and on. Later that day I called my parents and said, ÒYouÕre not gonna believe it. IÕm on 60 Minutes! You only get to see my earÑbut IÕm actually there.Ó And thatÕs the kind of life it was; these wonderful amazing things would happen that happened nowhere else. 
Lila Garrett: I was brand new in town, and IÕd pitched a wonderfully charm.ing story, a perfect satirical situation. When I tried to explain the premise to the producer and tried to discuss it with him, he just said, ÒNo thanks.Ó So I said, ÒIÕll go back to the drawing board.Ó And that producer told my agent, ÒLila Gar.rett argues.Ó The next thing I knew, that producer had spread my reputation as a person who argued. That went through the industry like wildfire. Because in this industry they love you and they hug you and kiss you and they adore you, but the thing that they hate most is to have someone challenge them. So it was very hard after that to get a job, for a very long time. 
Arnie Kogen: I met Dean Martin onceÑthe day of the first showÕs taping. The producer, Greg Garrison, introduced us, ÒDean, these are the four new writers this year. This is Ed. Weinberger.Ó And Dean said, ÒHello, Ed.Ó ÒThis is Stan Daniels.Ó ÒHello, Stan.Ó ÒThis is Treva Silverman.Ó ÒHello, Treva.Ó ÒThis is Arnie Kogen.Ó ÒHello, Marty.Ó 
I never corrected him. I shook his hand and never spoke to him again in my life. A few weeks later, my wife, Sue, was having dinner with Steve Lawrence and Eydie GormeÕs associate, Judy, in a restaurant on Sunset Boulevard. Dean Martin was with his manager two tables away from them, and he said, ÒThereÕs Judy Tannen and one of the writerÕs wives. Come on over!Ó My wife got to spend an evening with Dean Martin, and I got, ÒHello, Marty.Ó 
Bill Persky: I did a comedy TV special for Mary Tyler Moore called How to Survive the 70s, and there was a dance number in it that was based on jog.ging. We had this whole track laid out, and there were dancers running while they were dancing. And everybody was sitting on the floor as the dancers were rehearsing. I was drinking a beer, and the piano player and the drummer were banging. Mary came over, and she was all sweaty, and she sat down and took a sip of the beer. She said, ÒBilly, what are all the other people doing?Ó And I said, ÒTheyÕre waiting for their break.Ó

ABOUT THE AUTHOR 
Daughter of Honeymooners writer Herbert Finn, Paula Finn grew up in the culture. She is a summa cum laude/Phi Beta Kappa UCLA graduate and a former college English teacher and TV documentary researcher. She is a vet.eran inspirational writer, and her work has been featured on ABC-TVÕs 20/20 show. She has authored ten gift books, including Believe in Yourself, When Love IsnÕt Easy, and Make This Your Day.
